# Facts

Every fact the Architecture stage's answers wait on, stated once, with what it would take to get it and what a rented machine would have to be.

This is a **live tracker**: keeping it current is its whole job, so it describes changing state where every other document stays timeless. It is part of `Coverage.md` rather than a document beside it — that file holds the rows and the count, this one holds the facts the rows point at, because the row list and the whole of this register in one file is unreadable and unusable both *(this named a row count of 426 until 2026-08-23; the count is `Coverage.md`'s and is stated once there, so this sentence no longer restates it — the same mechanism this file already applies to its own total)*. *(This said 123 facts until 2026-08-22; the number is stated once, immediately below, and nowhere else.)*

## The runner's rulings on the deduplication, 2026-08-13

The draft flagged nine merges and splits it was unsure about. **Eight stand as drafted. One is overturned.**

**Overturned — Fact 5 is split, and the rewrite becomes Fact 77.** The draft merged reading a `.blend` on the machine with rewriting its recorded locations, on the ground that the capability is one: Smoozy driving a headless Blender on a rental and receiving a result. The two sweeps that raised it disagreed — Rendering named one fact, Dependency Upload & Remap named two — and the tie goes to the split, for a reason the draft's own rule supplies: **a wrong split costs a rental and a wrong merge costs an answer resting on a question nobody posed**, and here the split costs no rental at all, since both sit on the same box. Beyond that, a yes on reading does not carry a yes on rewriting: reading changes nothing, while a rewrite reaches the file on disk only if something saves it, is owed on **both** routes the research says are needed together, and has no one-pass route in 4.5. An answer proving the read and declaring the capability settled would leave the whole remap resting on a question nobody asked.

**Upheld, and the one worth recording why.** Fact 68 — a render told to end by something on the machine itself, with nobody connected — stays apart from Fact 2. The decisive argument is the draft's third, not Telegram's own: a yes to Fact 2 may rest entirely on the connection's own session control, which is exactly what is absent when nobody is connected. Both are posed on the same box at the same moment, so keeping them apart costs one command.

**Upheld as drafted:** Fact 7 absorbing what a listing yields (genuinely one experiment); Facts 11 and 12 kept apart as identity against name, with the warning that the identity answer must never be inferred from the name one; Facts 28, 43 and 46 kept as three against the Queue & History sweep's own recommendation, since three commands with three machine requirements cost nothing to keep separate and one yes must not close three promises; 17 against 18; 18 against 72; 21 against 30; 48 against 61; and 25 against 75.

**Fact 5's payloads stay merged after the split**, and the requirement that an answer be posed with all of them and reported per payload stands as the draft wrote it. **There are three, not four** — the fourth the draft counted was the rewrite, which is now Fact 77.

Rule 1's plain-English rule is suspended for this document, per the brief. Each fact is stated in the exact technical word.

## How to read an entry

- **Named by** — every sweep that raised it, with the wording that sweep used, so the merge can be checked rather than trusted.
- **Answered by** — a rented machine, reading a thing's own documentation, a throwaway on the Creator's own computer, or the Creator's own knowledge. Some need more than one.
- **Machine** — exactly what the machine has to be, where one is wanted. This is the part that decides what gets rented.
- **Shape-deciding** — whether a whole arrangement would be wrong if the answer came out the other way, and whether that is the stage's shape or one module's.
- **Posable** — whether the question can be put at all today.

## Facts 1 to 8 — the numbers already fixed in `Coverage.md`

### Fact 1 — Whether a program Smoozy puts on a rented machine can outlive the connection: hold a queue and a job waiting for a person with nobody connected, come back by itself after a reboot or a crash, read back the line of work and the state it was holding, produce a reading nobody is there to receive, and register that boot return under the non-root login Smoozy holds.

**Named by, seven sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 1 — "hold a queue with nobody connected, come back by itself after a reboot or a crash, and read back the line of work it was holding". This is the wording `Coverage.md` carries.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact A — same words; adds that fifteen rows want it and that the **disk-full form** must be asked too, since a job killed by a full disk sits in exactly that form.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 12 — same words, wanted by the ordering row and the removal warning.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 6 — "holds a queue with nobody connected, comes back by itself, and can be read for the queued and running work sitting on it"; says outright it is Rendering's first.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 1 — "run with nobody connected, come back by itself after a reboot or a crash, and read back what it was holding"; says one answer serves both documents.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Access.md` Fact 7 — "hold a state handed to it and go on honouring it with nobody connected and Smoozy closed", for the licence lock reaching the Telegram piece; says it should be answered with the queue's rather than separately.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/What a job writes.md` Fact 1 — "hold a job that is waiting for a person, work things out at the start of a render with nobody connected, and put what it found where a queue entry will read it later"; widens the fact from *holding a queue* to *holding a wait and producing an unreceived reading*.

**Answered by:** a rented machine in three parts — the plain level on Batch A; the narrow non-root boot-registration and reboot-return clause on Batch C; the remaining promise level after the shape is written on Batch E.
**Machine:** Batch C poses boot registration and reboot return under the non-root login Smoozy holds; Batch A ran as real root and cannot answer that clause. Batch E uses two ordinary rentals — a virtual machine and a container, each one card, no screen, an ordinary connection — for the installed supervisor, adapter, durable state and workers, including the disk-full form. **Two kinds rather than one**, because the boot arrangements differ between them and this clause is the one that differs most.
**Shape-deciding:** yes, for the whole stage. `Preparation/Architecture/Method.md` names it as one of the two answered first.
**Posable:** the plain half is done. The narrow boot-registration clause needs no Smoozy throwaway and rides on Batch C. The remaining promise half needs the shape and rides on Batch E.

**ANSWERED at the plain level, 2026-08-13** — yes, on all four clauses. See `Preparation/Architecture/Batch A — the shape throwaway.md`. A program left detached outlives the connection that started it, holds and advances a queue with nobody connected, has its line of work read back over a fresh connection, and comes back by itself after a reboot when arranged as an ordinary service — resuming from where it was rather than starting over. **Still owed:** the narrow non-root boot-registration clause on Batch C, and the remaining promise level on Batch E once the shape exists.

**ANSWERED AT THE PROMISE LEVEL, 2026-08-18, on both machine kinds — and the disk-full form is answered NO.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §1. Posed with a job running, a job held and the role worker enabled.

**The reboot half is yes on both kinds**, and **crash return is answered for the first time**. On the virtual machine a real kernel panic was induced, proved unclean by the next boot's filesystem recovery in the kernel's own words; the supervisor came back with a fresh run identifier, the held job still held with its hold byte-identical and unreleased, the running job at attempt 2 with `re-run once` journalled, and the role worker reading forward from its durable cursor. The container reproduced the same shape over two reboots by a completely different arrangement — **and on that provider a clean restart and a crash are the same event**, because its first process discards the runtime's polite stop and the forcible kill is what ends everything, which the record proves rather than the reasoning.

**The disk-full form is answered no, and it is the batch's heaviest negative.** Every write is refused loudly and correctly with errno 28, nothing moves behind it, and every read still answers truthfully with free space `0` rather than a stale figure. **But the supervisor cannot start on a full state mount** — its first act needs a directory entry, the endpoint bind on a container and the fresh run identifier on a systemd machine, and there is none. On the container the keeper loops forever and **heals by itself the moment space returns**; on the systemd machine **the unit did not recover merely because space was freed** — three minutes of watching, nothing. **Two of the four named negatives fired:** *an attempt count that does not advance*, in its strongest form, because reconciliation never runs at all; and *an ending no reader learns* — **the arrangement's own death is recorded nowhere Smoozy writes**, the machine's own log had `-- No entries --` for that boot, and from outside it looks exactly like a machine that is briefly busy. The two that did not fire are the safe ones: no stop was recorded that reconciliation would then dishonour, and no hold was left unreleased.

**One trap worth carrying: a writability test is not a write.** `os.access(W_OK)` answered `True` on a filesystem with literally zero bytes free while every real write to the same areas failed.


### Fact 2 — Whether a thing Smoozy started on a rented machine — a render, a setup step, the piece of Telegram — can be reached over a connection and told to end, with the interrupt reaching that thing's own process rather than a shell around it.

**Named by, five sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 2 — "a render running on a rented machine can be reached over a connection and told to end". `Coverage.md`'s wording.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact B — same words; wanted by stopping a job, and stop-and-clear.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 7 — same words; wanted by stop-and-clear.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 16 — "something Smoozy **is running** on a rented machine can be reached over the connection and told to end", for the stop-a-step row and the Telegram piece; states outright it is the same fact Rendering labels unresolved for a render.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/What a job writes.md` Fact 2 — adds "whether the interrupt reaches the render's own process rather than a shell around it".

**Merge note:** widened from *a render* to *a thing Smoozy started*, on Server Setup's own argument. Any answer is owed for a setup step and for the Telegram piece as well as for a render, and for the process-versus-shell clause.
**Answered by:** a rented machine, twice, as Fact 1.
**Machine:** as Fact 1. Nothing special.
**Shape-deciding:** yes, for the whole stage. The Method's second named first-answer.
**Posable:** the plain half now; the promise half once the shape exists.

**ANSWERED at the plain level, 2026-08-13** — yes. Reached from a new connection against a process whose starting connection was gone; it ended cleanly and lost no work. **The stop is not instant**: it took effect on the program's next cycle, about 2.3 seconds, so anything waiting on a stop needs a limit of its own. **Still owed at the promise level.**

**ANSWERED AT THE PROMISE LEVEL, 2026-08-18, on both machine kinds — yes, both halves.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §3. Posed against a **real Blender render** rather than a stand-in, under all three signals, on both boxes.

**The interrupt reaches Blender itself and never a shell.** Checked by hand on both: the execution group is the worker, its child is the recorded target — process name `blender`, 44 threads on the virtual machine — and the launch material `exec`s itself away so nothing sits between them. Under a polite interrupt, a terminate and a forcible kill, the signal went to the recorded render process every time; the journal recorded the request, the issue and the outcome in order; and **nothing restarted the render**. Time to a recorded outcome was 1–3 seconds on the virtual machine and 1.9–14 seconds on the container.

**The container's reduced capability set touches none of it, and there is a reason to expect none:** the one relevant missing capability is the one that permits tracing another process, and nothing in the stop uses it — because the shape forbids finding anything by process search, nothing in the stop depends on a capability a container lacks.

**The orphaned-worker half is yes with one line of the boot registration corrected**, and the first attempt failing is a finding rather than a machine answer. Written as the architecture describes it, the user unit carries no kill-mode line, so the service manager's default takes the whole control group down with the supervisor — **a supervisor restart destroys a paid render every time**. Three side-by-side probes separated the builder's unit from the machine and proved the machine permits a worker to outlive its supervisor **two independent ways, both unprivileged**. With the correction, the case the capture arrangement exists for was seen working for the first time: supervisor forcibly killed under a live 100-frame render, worker and Blender both survived, the render **finished under a supervisor it never met**, disposition `resumed`, attempt count still 1, **514,253 bytes byte-for-byte identical to the uninterrupted run** and matching Smoozy's own count exactly. **No frame re-rendered.** That correction is a repair to `Rented-server runtime` [1.2](Rented-server%20runtime.md#12-on-a-systemd-machine) rather than a fact about the world.


### Fact 3 — Which card-technology names the Blender build on a rented machine will accept.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 3 — "every card on the machine". `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Render Settings.md` Fact 4 — same words, wanted by how the scene's own device setting is imposed; says it is Rendering's and should be answered once.
Both record that the research places this with the operating system rather than the version, so it does **not** go to Blender Research.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** a rental with a working card of the make in question. One card is enough — but one rental answers one make. If the Creator expects users on more than one make, that is more than one rental.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED for the NVIDIA make, 2026-08-15/16, by the six-machine batch.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), *The cards — Facts 46, 3 and 43*. The Cycles device setting takes `NONE`, `CUDA`, `OPTIX`, `HIP`, `ONEAPI` and **refuses `METAL` outright** — `enum "METAL" not found in ('NONE', 'CUDA', 'OPTIX', 'HIP', 'ONEAPI')` — while `blender --help` prints *"Valid options are: 'CPU' 'CUDA' 'OPTIX' 'HIP' 'ONEAPI' 'METAL'."* identically in 4.5.12 and 5.2.0. **`--help` prints one list for every platform and is wrong about this build.** At launch there are two distinguishable refusals, both exit 1 with nothing written: a name the build does not know gives `Error: BOGUS is not a valid Cycles device.`, a known name no hardware answers to gives `Error: Found no Cycles device of the specified type`. **What is not distinguishable is a broken driver from absent hardware** — both give the second line. Also: the processor appears inside each technology's own device list, so `get_devices_for_type('OPTIX')` returns 3 on a two-card box.

**Still owed:** every make other than NVIDIA, as the fact's own machine note allows. AMD, Intel and Apple hardware are unposed and want another rental.

### Fact 4 — Whether a broken or mismatched graphics driver produces the same loud refusal when the card technology is named at launch, rather than the quiet processor render it produces when the device comes from a script.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 4 only.
**Answered by:** a rented machine. Partly answered for the script route by `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md`; the launch-named case has never been run.
**Machine:** a rental with a working NVIDIA card that **nobody needs afterwards** — answering it means purging or mismatching the driver. Run it last on whichever box is being given up. The Creator has authorised destroying a machine for exactly this before.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-13** — yes, it still refuses loudly. With the driver packages genuinely purged and OPTIX named at launch, Blender 4.5.3 ended at **exit 1**, wrote no file, and printed `Error: Found no Cycles device of the specified type`. So the Rendering spec's decision does convert the quiet processor render into a refusal with the driver broken, not only with the hardware absent. **Not covered**: the other three Blender versions, and the driver *mismatched* rather than removed — which is the state an unattended upgrade produces.


### Fact 5 — Whether Smoozy can drive a Blender on a rented machine to open a `.blend` and hand a reading back over the connection it holds.

**Named by, four sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 5 — "drive a read ... of a `.blend`'s recorded locations on a rented machine, over the connection it holds". `Coverage.md`'s original wording, which also covered the rewrite; the rewrite is now Fact 77.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Fact 1 — "read of a `.blend` for what it points at ... with a named Blender that is already there ... and read the answer back".
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Render Settings.md` Fact 1 — "open a `.blend`, read **every value this page shows** out of it, and hand the reading back".
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/What a job writes.md` Fact 5 — read "a `.blend`'s destinations, its compositor tree, its gating conditions and its per-frame set"; calls itself the reading half of Rendering's Fact 5.

**Three payloads, and an answer that proves one does not close the others.** Any answer must be posed with all three and reported per payload:
1. the file's recorded locations (Dependency Upload & Remap);
2. every value `Preparation/Specs/Render Settings.md` shows (Render Settings);
3. destinations, compositor tree, gating conditions and per-frame set (What a job writes).

**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with a Blender on it. Worth posing **beside a render already running**, since the research settles that the two do not disturb each other but says nothing about a rental's limits.
**Shape-deciding:** for one module — `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Render Settings.md` states a negative answer takes the whole file half of Pre-load Settings with it.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-17, on all three payloads and in Blender 4.5.9, 5.0.1 and 5.2.0** — yes. See [One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §1. One SSH command, a named Blender already on the machine, headless, no display server, and **all three payloads out of one read**, returned as one JSON document between sentinels. **About 3.2 to 3.8 seconds end to end including a fresh connection; the reading itself 0.09 to 0.12 seconds; 15 to 26 KB back.** The reading was checked against a real render rather than trusted: it predicted 105 files over 21 frames and the render wrote 105. The per-frame view-layer set came out of the same single read, which is the claim `Render Settings` calls a bigger one than knowing which view layers a file holds. **The bulk path means miss every write location and a walk over Blender's own path marks reaches all of them**, which is the reading Row 119 rests on.

**Three limits.** **Blender 5.1 was not installed and is not spoken for.** A file naming an engine the chosen Blender lacks could not be posed, because Cycles is enabled on all three builds. And **whether a read disturbs a running render is not answered** — five reads during a render all succeeded and the render finished whole, but three testers shared four cores and the loads cannot be separated.

### Fact 77 — Whether Smoozy can drive a Blender on a rented machine to rewrite a `.blend`'s recorded locations so the change reaches the file on disk, on both routes and in every supported version.

**Created by the runner on 2026-08-13**, splitting the rewrite out of Fact 5. The two sweeps that raised it disagreed about whether it was one fact or two, and the split costs no rental because both sit on the same box.

**Named by:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 5 — the rewrite half of "a read and a rewrite".
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Fact 2 — names it separately and adds what the answer is owed for: **each of the two rewrite routes** the research says are needed together, and in 4.5 **without** the one-pass route the other three versions have.

**Why it is not Fact 5.** A read changes nothing. A rewrite shows in the running Blender and **never reaches the file on disk unless something saves it** (*Reading a file 9*), which is the only reason it can be done at all given the spec forbids writing to the artist's own file. No single route reaches every recorded location in any version (*Reading a file 5, 9*; *Dependencies 17*), so the answer is owed per route and per version rather than once. An answer proving the read and declaring the capability settled would leave the whole remap resting on a question nobody posed.

**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** the same ordinary rental as Fact 5, with a Blender on it — and with **4.5 and one of 5.0, 5.1 or 5.2 both present**, since the routes differ across that boundary.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but a negative answer is a **hole in the spec rather than a narrowing** — `Preparation/Specs/Dependency Upload & Remap.md`'s sources file says so outright, and `Preparation/Specs/Rendering.md`'s row 13 rests on it.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-17, per route and per version.** See [One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §2. **The per-setting route works in all three versions and reaches every recorded location in the fixture, write locations included**, proved on disk by a separate process opening the file after the save. **The property name for a file output node's folder differs across the version boundary and the rewrite must know which.** The artist's own file was digested before and after all seven runs and never changed, with the two no-op runs standing as the control that the check would have noticed.

**The one-pass route carries the negative the question named.** It is **absent in 4.5.9**, so 4.5's answer is owed without it. **In 5.0.1 and 5.2.0 it rewrites the images, the text and the library and silently leaves every write location alone** — nothing raised, nothing printed, the save succeeding. **A redirect built on that route alone would appear to work and leave every place the job writes pointing at the artist's own machine.** That is the more striking result because the route's own release notes name render farms as the reason for it.

**Two traps recorded for Build.** Saving remaps paths relative by default, so whatever writes the copy Smoozy renders from must turn that off. And from 5.0, adding an output to a file output node rebuilds its socket list, so a held socket reference goes stale and links land on the trailing extend socket with no error.

**Limits:** 5.1 untested. Whole kinds of recorded location not posed at all — a font, a sound, a volume, a point cache, a bake folder, a geometry-nodes bake folder, a sequencer proxy folder, and **a path in a custom property**.

### Fact 78 — What a rented machine's filesystem guarantees for the package commit and transfer publication: whether the active-package pointer switch and matching boot-registration activation can be indivisible or deterministically recovered to one matching pair, and whether publication into a final name is atomic.

**Named by:** the third audit round of `Preparation/Architecture/The shape.md`. The shape's package transaction and transfer staging-and-publication contract each rest on this outside behaviour.
**Answered by:** a throwaway on a rented machine, using the exact operations the package commit and transfer publication choose.
**Machine:** any rental batch with the non-root login Smoozy holds. Batch A's filesystem was ext4, but that machine is destroyed and no package replacement or publication interruption was run on it. This fact rides on the first suitable later rental and justifies none of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no. A negative answer keeps the arrangement unresolved until the transaction demonstrates deterministic recovery to a matching pointer-and-registration pair and publication cannot expose part-arrived bytes at the final name.
**Posable:** once the package commit and publication operations are concrete enough to interrupt at each boundary.

**A third filesystem entered the evidence on 2026-08-17 and nothing has posed this on it.** The Blender batch ran on a container whose root is **`overlayfs`**, where this entry connects only ext4 and xfs. `Job files and outputs` rests the publication of a job's material and of a derivative on single-rename atomicity, so the gap is load-bearing rather than tidy-up. Raised by that document's writer; **the entry is not closed and nothing about overlayfs is claimed either way.**

**ANSWERED on ext4, 2026-08-15/16.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 78. Replacing a file by rename is atomic — the content became the whole new string and **the inode changed**, a whole-file swap that was never partial — and the system's own manual says so: *"If newpath already exists, it will be atomically replaced, so that there is no point at which another process attempting to access newpath will find it missing."* A symlink pointer switches atomically by the same means, and two names can be exchanged atomically. **So publication into a final name cannot expose part-arrived bytes.**

**But no single operation updates two independent files atomically.** The matching pointer-and-registration pair is **a design obligation built on single-rename atomicity plus ordering, not a filesystem primitive** — which is what the fact's own wording asks about, and it is answered as *deterministic recovery to one matching pair* rather than as indivisibility.

**Still owed, and it is load-bearing.** No crash injection at the rename boundary was run, and durability rests on the journal plus an explicit directory sync. **And a third filesystem entered the evidence on 2026-08-17 that this entry does not connect** — the Blender batch ran on a container whose root is `overlayfs`, where `Job files and outputs` rests publication of a job's material and of a derivative on single-rename atomicity. **Nothing about overlayfs is claimed either way.**

**THE INTERRUPTION BOUNDARIES ARE ANSWERED, 2026-08-18, ON ALL THREE FILESYSTEMS — and the overlayfs gap above is closed.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §8. The virtual machine posed **ext4** and **xfs**, the container posed **overlayfs**, and every death was a real forcible kill without unwinding — which is the crash injection this entry recorded as never run, at every one of the four commit boundaries.

**None of the four named negatives occurred on any of the three.** **No part-arrived bytes at a final name, ever.** **The pointer always named exactly one of the two versions — never neither.** The phase record always said which branch to resume from, and every retry under the same identity resolved correctly. The container's checks returned empty mismatch, missing, unexpected-temporary and staging-leftover lists at every boundary.

**The hold record's publication behaves the same way on all three.** Interrupted before the rename, the record is left under a temporary name with **nothing at a final name** and the machine answering zero holds. Interrupted after the rename and before the journal entry, the record is complete at a final name and **carries its reason and its subject** — so **no hold ever reads as a hold with no reason** — but **the hold stands with no journal entry**, so a reader following the journal never learns it exists while the hold silently blocks every package commit. That is the case the document's own machinery already names, arriving live on all three.

**Two things it does not close, and both are named rather than glossed.** **The rollback boundary could not be posed**, blocked twice over — the masking route is refused by the image, and once the health exchange was made to fail honestly the test root would not enter a commit at all. A real rollback *was* observed and never interrupted mid-flight. And **the `pointer` and `pointer-done` boundaries leave the same phase** and are told apart only by reading the pointer, so *the retry can tell from the phase record which branch it is on* is true only of the phase record **plus** the pointer.

**One divergence that is not a filesystem difference and must not be read as one.** The xfs pre-health retry rolled back where ext4 and overlayfs went to verified. **It is one run each**, and the tester attributes it to the health-exchange deadline on a box contending for one shared unit.

**And litter nobody swept.** The temporary file left by an interrupted hold publication survived hours, several supervisor restarts and full reconciliation, on all three filesystems.

### Fact 79 — What each of Mac, Windows and Linux guarantees for the exact desktop-side operation chosen to publish a staged download into its final name: whether part-arrived bytes can remain hidden until publication, and whether an interrupted publication can recover without making an incomplete file look complete.

**Named by:** the close gate for the third audit round of `Preparation/Architecture/The shape.md`. Fact 78 covers publication on the rented machine; it cannot answer publication of a download on the user's different filesystem.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own filesystem and application-file documentation, with exact words, addresses and dates, plus a throwaway using the chosen operation on a computer running each supported operating system.
**Machine:** a Mac, a Windows computer and a Linux computer — none rented for this purpose. A rented Linux server does not answer for the desktop application merely by sharing an operating-system family.
**Shape-deciding:** no. A negative answer leaves the desktop publication operation unresolved; otherwise Rows 208, 209, 212 and 281 can expose part-arrived bytes under the final name or present an incomplete download as complete.
**Posable:** Mac and Linux now. **Windows was recorded as *not at all today because the project has no Windows computer*, and that was wrong for the documentation route — corrected 2026-08-21**, since this entry itself names two routes and only one of them wants a machine. The documentation route is now read; the throwaway route still wants Windows.

**THE MAC DOCUMENTATION ROUTE IS ANSWERED, 2026-08-17**, while writing `Preparation/Architecture/Server protocol and transfers.md`. Apple's own `rename(2)` manual page, macOS 26.5, dated 2021-06-03, read 2026-08-17: *"guarantees that an instance of new will always exist, even if the system should crash in the middle of the operation."* So on that system an interrupted publication cannot leave the final name absent, which is the half of this fact the download promise leans on hardest.

**THE OPERATION IS CHOSEN, 2026-08-18, by `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md`**, which is what the throwaway route was waiting on: **the platform's own atomic rename onto the final path, staged on the same filesystem** — `rename(2)` on macOS against Apple's crash guarantee, `rename(2)` on Linux against the kernel's own manual, **Windows read on the documentation route from 2026-08-20 and unread on the throwaway route.** **The documented answer is real and it is negative.** Windows publishes two operations: `MoveFileExW` with `MOVEFILE_REPLACE_EXISTING`, *"If a file named lpNewFileName exists, the function replaces its contents with the contents of the lpExistingFileName file"* — **quoted truncated here until 2026-08-23; the page's sentence continues *", provided that security requirements regarding access control lists (ACLs) are met."*** — with a `MOVEFILE_WRITE_THROUGH` flag whose own words are about flushing to disk before returning; and `ReplaceFileW`, *"Replaces one file with another file, with the option of creating a backup copy of the original file. The replacement file assumes the name of the replaced file and its identity."* **Neither page carries anything like Apple's crash sentence, and `ReplaceFileW` documents the opposite** — among its named failure states, `ERROR_UNABLE_TO_MOVE_REPLACEMENT`: *"If lpBackupFileName was specified, the replaced and replacement files retain their original file names. Otherwise, the replaced file no longer exists and the replacement file exists under its original name."* **So a Windows publication can genuinely leave the final name absent, and Windows documents no crash guarantee for either route.** **Both addresses were supplied on 2026-08-23**, this entry having carried the words and the date and no address: `MoveFileExW` at `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/winbase/nf-winbase-movefileexw` and `ReplaceFileW` at `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/winbase/nf-winbase-replacefilew`, both pages dated 2018-12-05, both re-read 2026-08-23 by two implementers independently. **The negative was confirmed by the reading rather than carried**: neither page contains the word *atomic*, *crash*, *power* or *interrupt* anywhere. That is an answer rather than a silence, and it is the third system's honest half. That document states what would demonstrate it — repeated forcible kills at the publication boundary, reading both paths after each — and the exact negative result, and says outright it is **not claimed demonstrated**. **The Linux run wants a desktop Linux computer**, which this project does not have; a rented Linux server does not answer for it.

**The two routes are now at different places, and the fact keeps both.** The **documentation route** stands answered for Mac and, through Fact 78, for Linux — the operating system's own `rename(2)` manual is a permitted source under `Method.md`'s reading rule whatever machine displayed the page. Windows is answered negatively on the documentation route and unread on the throwaway route. The **throwaway route** is untouched on all three: nothing has been run on any computer, and the *Machine* line above is why a rented Linux server does not close the Linux throwaway. **Nothing here rides on a rental** — what it wants is a desktop computer running each system.

**THE THROWAWAY ROUTE IS PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED ON ALL THREE SYSTEMS, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** It wants a desktop computer running each system; the Windows third and the Linux desktop third are ones this project does not have, and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental to answer a question. **The documentation route stands where it is** — answered for Mac, answered through Fact 78 for Linux, and answered **negatively** for Windows — and nothing above is weakened by this line. **This is Windows-list item 9, and `Desktop app and delivery` §22 item 4, `Download` §15 item 18, `Server protocol and transfers` §12 item 2 and `State, identity and recovery` §13 item 3 must all say one thing.**

**Breaks:** every download landing on the user's own computer rests on it — S18, and with it Rows 208, 209, 212 and 281. **What holds:** staging on the same filesystem, no route to the final name but the publication step, and Apple's own crash guarantee on the one system where a guarantee is published. **The silence is already half-removed and that sentence is the load-bearing one:** `Desktop app and delivery` **F18** states what would demonstrate the operation and **says outright that it is not claimed demonstrated**. That sentence stays, and it is what keeps an undemonstrated operation from being read as a proved one.

**What this fact underwrites was narrowed on 2026-08-22, and the register is corrected rather than left to be read against the documents.** **On macOS the update no longer replaces the executable inside the bundle at all**: it replaces the **complete signed bundle at the installed path the user already approved**, never a member inside it, because a signature seals a bundle's resources and a signed bundle is to be treated as read-only — `Desktop app and delivery` Z5 now quotes Apple's own technical note for both rules, and the **directory form of the named-backup replacement is unread**, which that document carries as its own unresolved item. **And the Linux half no longer describes the update route at all**: the Linux release and update artifact is a signed, dependency-declaring package installed through the distribution's own package mechanism (E4, Z10), so this fact's `rename(2)` answer underwrites **ordinary data publication** there — `Server protocol and transfers` S19 and `Desktop app and delivery` F15 — and nothing about how a new version arrives. **The Windows twin is Fact 97 and is untouched.**

### Fact 80 — Whether the concrete driver work chosen for the ready action can require the rented machine to restart.

**Named by:** the required changed-spec re-sweep of `Preparation/Specs/Server Setup.md`. The spec labels this outside behaviour an assumption. Fact 51 answers whether Smoozy can restart a rental over its connection; it does not answer whether the driver work ever requires that restart.
**Answered by:** the chosen driver's own documentation, with exact words, address and date, and by a throwaway on a rental when the ready action is concrete.
**Machine:** the first suitable rental on which the ready action's exact driver operation can be run safely; it rides on B1 and justifies no rental of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no. A negative answer simply leaves the restart rule unused; an answer showing a restart is required makes Rows 88, 105 and 295 inherit the interruption consequences.
**Posable:** only once the ready action's driver operation is chosen.

**ANSWERED: yes on both machines that posed it, 2026-08-15/16, by two different mechanisms — and they disagree about the signal, which is the useful part.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 80. Sesterce needed a restart because the provider left the card bound to `nouveau`: the install finished at **exit 0 in 85 seconds, printed no reboot demand**, left `nvidia-smi` failing at exit 9, and the kernel said why — `NVRM: GPU 0000:00:06.0 is already bound to nouveau.` Hyperstack needed one for a different reason — the install pulled a whole new kernel and **did** print its own demand.

**So Ubuntu's own reboot-required flag is not a reliable signal — it was never set on the machine that needed a restart most.** The rule that survives both: **run `nvidia-smi` after installing and restart only if it fails.** Never unconditionally, and never on the package manager's word.

**Avoiding the restart by hand was possible and left the machine worse.** A full module unload and reload brought the card up and it **broke again on the very next command** (`Failed to initialize NVML: Unknown Error`), because the reload left device nodes with the wrong majors; the state then oscillated, working and inconsistent at once. **No ready action should ship in that condition, and no reading taken in it can be trusted — including card detection.** NVIDIA's own documentation, read 2026-08-15: *"You will need to reboot your system after adding these configuration directives in order for them to take effect."* **Both alternatives it offers are boot-time changes; it offers no route that avoids a restart.** **That quotation answers a different question and this conclusion does not follow from it — corrected 2026-08-20.** It sits at the end of NVIDIA's answer to *"How do I prevent Nouveau from loading and performing a kernel modeset?"* and is about the two lines written into a modprobe configuration file taking effect at the next boot. **The sentence immediately before it says the opposite in NVIDIA's own words**: *"Without the kernel modeset, it is possible to unload Nouveau's kernel module, in the event that it is accidentally or intentionally loaded."* (same page, re-read 2026-08-20). **The decision does not change** — run the card tool after installing and restart only if it fails, which is sound on its own merits and costs 9 to 28 seconds on these boxes — and neither does the observation. **What changes is the reason given, and the flat claim that no reading taken in that state can be trusted**, which is written as a permanent property of what is an ordinary repairable state: the repair NVIDIA does document is that `nvidia-modprobe` creates the device files, and only *"when ... the NVIDIA character device files do not yet exist"*, so a stale file that does exist is not replaced. **The repair itself is undocumented and unposed here**, and wants a Linux machine with an NVIDIA card.

**Restart cost varies by an order of magnitude and that is load-bearing.** Measured eight times across three machines: **9, 11 and 12 seconds** on one, **21, 22 and 28** on another, **85 to 90** on a third. **Anything that budgets a fixed wait for a machine to return will be wrong on most of them.** Bare card to a rendered image was about **4 minutes 15 seconds** on one machine and **6 minutes 27 seconds** on another, and **nothing on either needed a human**.

**Still owed:** driver *upgrades* and *removals*, and the `.run` installer route. **The Secure Boot risk this batch exposed is now Fact 81** and is not covered here.

### Fact 81 — Whether a rented machine with Secure Boot enforcing will take the driver work the ready action needs, and what it does when the module cannot be signed.

**Exposed by** the six-machine batch of 2026-08-16, which recorded it as *a new fact, not yet numbered*. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-16**, on the Creator's instruction to rule: the register's own rule is that one fact takes one number and keeps it, an unnumbered fact is invisible to the count and no row can wait on it, and this one carries its own machine requirement that no other fact carries. Fact 80 does not cover it — that fact asks whether the driver work needs a restart, this one whether it can complete at all.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from a machine.
**Answered by:** a rented machine with Secure Boot enforcing, plus the driver's and the operating system's own documentation for what signing an out-of-tree module requires.
**Machine:** a rental whose firmware is EFI **with Secure Boot on**. None of the seven machines this project has rented had one; the Sesterce box had legacy BIOS with no EFI at all and its driver build logged `EFI variables are not supported on this system` while trying to sign the module, which is what raised the question.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but it fails the same way Fact 80 can: the ready action may need **a human at the machine's console enrolling a key**, which Smoozy cannot do over the connection it holds. If so, that is not an architecture choice but a machine Smoozy cannot make ready, and Server Setup's promise has to say so.
**Posable:** ~~only on a machine that has it. Rides on any later batch where such a rental can be found, and justifies no rental of its own.~~ **Not at all. Struck 2026-09-02 with the ruling below — there is no later batch.**

**PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02, and this entry carried no status line of any kind until that date.** It wants a rental whose firmware is EFI with Secure Boot on, and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental to answer a question. **None of the seven machines this project has rented had one, and there will be no eighth. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one**, and no reader may leave a space in the code or the record for the answer.

**Breaks:** where the module cannot be signed, the ready action needs a human at the machine's own console enrolling a key, which Smoozy cannot do over the connection it holds — **not an architecture choice but a machine Smoozy cannot make ready**, and `Server Setup`'s promise has to say so rather than discover it. **What holds:** every machine this project has rented booted without it, so the observed setup route is the ordinary one, and `Server Setup` §13 item 1 already carries the failure as a named clause of an answered row rather than as a reason to leave one open.

**The silence is removed by the failure's own loudness rather than by anything new, and no second channel is opened for it.** The driver build that raised this question logged `EFI variables are not supported on this system` while trying to sign; a signing failure states itself in the machine's own words and is carried into the setup fault the user already reads.

### Fact 82 — How the host key a machine offers is obtained and compared without authenticating to it first, and what an unmatched one yields that a program can act on.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Security and licence.md`, which asked for the number. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-16.**

**Named by:** no sweep. Rows 36 and 37 in `Preparation/Architecture/Rows/Servers.md` rest on it and no fact covered it.
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own manuals for whatever obtains a host key without completing an authentication, and a throwaway against any reachable SSH server. Fact 37 already establishes why parsing the client's ordinary text is not an alternative: **no OpenSSH manual documents a single one of the diagnostic strings the tools emit**, so a version change can break a text match silently.
**Machine:** none rented; any reachable SSH server will do. It should ride on a batch rather than justify one.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but a negative answer leaves only two routes and both are bad — parse an undocumented banner, or accept whatever key is offered, connect anyway, and warn the user after the fact. **Corrected 2026-08-21: this route was described as *authenticating to an impostor* or *proving Smoozy's identity to an impostor*, and that cannot happen.** SSH checks the host key inside key exchange, before authentication is even asked for — RFC 4253 §8 step 3, read 2026-08-20: *"C verifies that K_S really is the host key for S (e.g., using certificates or a local database)."*, and §10 makes `ssh-userauth` a service requested *"After the key exchange"*; RFC 4252's abstract puts the authentication protocol *"on top of the SSH transport layer protocol"*. **Run with a control on the Creator's Mac, `OpenSSH_10.2p1`, 2026-08-20:** with a deliberately wrong stored key the client exited 255 at *"Host key verification failed."* and the whole trace contains no `userauth`, no `SERVICE_ACCEPT`, no key offered; with the true key in the same scratch file the trace reaches *"debug2: service_accept: ssh-userauth"*. **No credential can go first.** **What the bad route actually is** — accept whatever key is offered, connect anyway, and warn the user after the fact, which is a weaker and different harm.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-17** — and **neither bad route is forced.** `ssh-keyscan` obtains the offered key with no credential asked for and none supplied, its own manual saying so in as many words — *"For scanning, one does not need login access to the machines that are being scanned"* — and the comparison is then a string comparison on that output, against what Smoozy remembers itself. Observed against the German rental from the Creator's Mac: a scan returned all three key types at exit `0` with nothing on stderr, two independent scans gave **byte-identical** key material, and comparing that material separated the real key from a generated impostor. So the decision is made **before** any connection that would present Smoozy's own credential to whatever is at the far end.

**Two limits, and both are Fact 37's shape again.** Where a connection *is* attempted against a wrong stored record, ssh exits **255** — its generic failure status, which separates a host-key mismatch from nothing at all; only the thirteen-line warning text does, and Fact 37 established that no manual documents it. And **`ssh-keyscan(1)` has no EXIT STATUS section**: the `0` on success and the `1` on a port with no server are observed on this version on this system and documented nowhere. **Not established:** whether a scan can be defeated by a middle that answers the scan honestly and the session dishonestly, which one vantage point cannot settle. See [The host key](The%20host%20key%20—%20obtained%20without%20authenticating,%20and%20whose%20it%20turns%20out%20to%20be.md).

### Fact 83 — Whether the host key recorded at a provider that fronts SSH with its own proxy belongs to the rented machine or to the proxy.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Security and licence.md`, which asked for the number. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-16.**

**Named by:** no sweep. **Kept apart from Fact 82**, which asks how a host key is obtained at all; this asks whose key it is.
**Answered by:** a rented machine at a proxying provider, reading the offered host key, then destroying that rental and renting another behind the same proxy address and reading it again.
**Machine:** a rental at a provider that fronts SSH with its own address — Vast.ai does, and every Vast rental this project has taken was reachable **only** through `sshN.vast.ai`. **Two rentals in sequence behind one proxy address**, which is what makes the comparison possible. Nothing in any input document records anything about host keys at all, so there is nothing to read back from the seven machines already destroyed.
**Shape-deciding:** no. If the key belongs to the proxy, the remembered identity changes when the proxy changes and does **not** change when the machine is replaced — so Row 36's changed-identity warning fires on the wrong events in both directions, and the promise that a machine is recognised again is being kept against something that is not the machine.
**Posable:** now, on any batch that takes two Vast rentals in sequence.

**ANSWERED IN PART, 2026-08-17, and it opened a sharper question than it asked.** The proxy half is answered **no**: the three keys offered through `ssh3.vast.ai:28452` are **byte-for-byte the machine's own `/etc/ssh/ssh_host_*_key.pub`**, so the proxy forwards the connection rather than terminating it with an identity of its own. That was settled on **one** rental by comparing the scan against the files, rather than by the two rentals in sequence this entry proposed. **One honest limit:** the files were read over an authenticated session through the same proxy, so a middle lying consistently on both would not show up; what is ruled out is the ordinary case the fact was filed against.

**ANSWERED IN FULL the same day, and the answer is the negative one.** The German box was destroyed and **a fresh rental taken on the same physical machine `141036` from the same offer**. It is beyond doubt a different container — hostname `149e28227de9` against `a1a84bc7fa93`, started 2026-08-17 13:44:45 against 2026-08-16 13:27:02, and **the twenty-four-hour record the old rental had been writing is gone** along with everything else Smoozy put there, on a host that had not rebooted between the two. It reaches **a different proxy and port**, `ssh9.vast.ai:27488` against `ssh3.vast.ai:28452`. **And it presents byte-identical host keys** — all three fingerprints, the same `root@f077078e58b1` comment and the same 2026-08-04 08:14:13 file date. **The host key survived the rental it belonged to.**

**A second machine confirms the mechanism rather than the value.** A container on machine `55691` carried keys commented **`root@buildkitsandbox`**, dated 2026-08-07 — a container-image build artefact. **The mechanism inferred from that comment is wrong, and Test 19 of the multi-provider batch disproved it on 2026-08-22.** This read *So keys are baked at image-build time and a rental inherits whatever build its machine holds* — which predicts that one image gives one set of keys. It does not: **a container's keys are the host's**. The same offer re-rented gave three identical keys with the proxy port moved; **a different machine on the same image gave three different ones**. The `root@buildkitsandbox` comment is a build artefact of the image the host was built from and says nothing about which host serves the rental. **What survives, and is what the fact was for: nothing generates a host key for a rental, ever** — on the two providers observed, which is the scope this sentence has in practice and did not carry until 2026-08-22. **The consequence is `Servers` §16 item 5's**: a host key cannot tell *my machine, still mine* from *the same machine, re-rented after mine was destroyed*. See [The host key](The%20host%20key%20—%20obtained%20without%20authenticating,%20and%20whose%20it%20turns%20out%20to%20be.md).

**What it does to Row 36.** The changed-identity warning **cannot fire on the event it exists for**: a user whose rental is gone and who rents again on the same hardware gets the same key and no warning, while everything Smoozy installed has been destroyed — the same blind spot the container recycle has, reached by a second door. **And it fires when nothing is wrong**, because re-renting on different hardware changes the key. So the harm this fact named is real and arrives by neither route it guessed: the proxy is honest, the key is the machine's, and **the key is simply not a name for the thing Smoozy needs to recognise** — not the rental and not the installation. What can tell those apart is the **installation identity** that `State, identity and recovery` introduced for the recycle case, and this is a second, independent argument for it. **Also observed, unasked:** the proxy address is not a stable name either — `ssh3` became `ssh9` and the port changed, for the same machine hours apart. **Not established:** whether a machine's keys ever change without the hardware changing. See [The host key](The%20host%20key%20—%20obtained%20without%20authenticating,%20and%20whose%20it%20turns%20out%20to%20be.md).

**THE VIRTUAL-MACHINE HALF IS ANSWERED, 2026-08-18, and the answer refutes the question's own premise.** This entry's own rider had said a container's answer could not carry the case because **a virtual machine generates its own host keys at first boot**. It does not, on this provider. See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §17.

The virtual machine's four host keys were recorded before anything else was done and again immediately before a **recycle**, unchanged across five reboots and one kernel panic in between. After the recycle they were **identical: every fingerprint, every file size, every file timestamp to the nanosecond**, and the scanned public keys matched byte for byte. **The recycle was thorough in every other respect** — both logins gone, Blender gone, the throwaway gone, the lingering flag gone, and the image's own broken driver back. **The keys are shipped in the image, generated 2025-11-06 with `root@ubuntu` baked into every comment, months before the rental existed.**

**So an unchanged set after a recycle is the finding rather than a failed test, and the consequence is worse than the container's.** Every rental of that image, on every host, presents the same four keys — **so anything keying on them to recognise *the same machine* would also recognise every other machine from the same image.** **That *on every host* is an inference from one machine watched across a recycle, not from two rentals on two hosts — marked 2026-08-22, where it stood as settled.** **The question it opens, and it is Fact 83's:** whether the same virtual-machine template on a second physical host presents the same keys, and therefore whether **the private half travels with the image**. A machine cannot present a host key whose private half it does not hold — RFC 4253 §8, read 2026-08-20: *"S generates a random number y … and signature s on H with its private host key."* — **so if two rentals of one image present the same public key, both hold the same private key, anyone who can rent that image holds it, and comparing scanned keys buys nothing against a middle for that image.** It rides free on any batch that takes two rentals. **And the recycle changed the connection's port and not its address**, so a reconnection cannot key on the port either and the address alone is not specific to an instance. **Whatever survives a recycle for identification purposes, it is neither the endpoint nor the host key** — which is the third independent argument for the installation identity.

### Fact 84 — Which mechanism yields a caller's local identity at a rented machine's own filesystem endpoint, and whether it survives a container's reduced capability set.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Security and licence.md`, Section 14 item 6. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-16**, at the round close: it is a machine question with an answer, it rides free on any Linux rental, and left unnumbered it is found only by someone already reading that document's unresolved list. Section 14 item 15 was considered at the same time and **deliberately not numbered** — it asks what an elevation route yields, and no document has yet chosen that route, so it is a constraint handed to `Server Setup`, `File Browser` and `Server protocol and transfers` rather than a fact about the world.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from writing the supervisor's endpoint boundary.
**Answered by:** a throwaway on a rented machine — one process holding the endpoint, another connecting to it as a different ordinary login, on both a virtual-machine rental and a container rental. The pair is the point, as it was for Fact 1's narrow clause: a container holds a reduced capability set and an answer taken only on a virtual machine can be silently wrong there.
**Machine:** any rented Linux machine, and the second half wants a container. Rides on a batch and justifies no rental of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no. It is the endpoint boundary's **second** layer only. The first layer — the endpoint and its containing directory owned by the supervisor's login and permitting that login alone — was observed on both machines where Fact 1's narrow clause was posed and holds without this.
**Posable:** now, on any batch that takes a Linux rental.

**ANSWERED for a container, 2026-08-17.** The mechanism is **peer credentials read from the kernel on the accepted connection** — `getsockopt(SOL_SOCKET, SO_PEERCRED)` on an `AF_UNIX` stream socket. On the German container rental, a server running as root at an endpoint deliberately left mode `0666` was told `pid=1628 uid=1000 gid=1000` for a caller running as the ordinary login `f84user`, and `uid 1000` resolved to that login; the caller's own independent view of itself agreed exactly. **It survives the container's reduced capability set** — `CapPrm`/`CapEff`/`CapBnd` are all `00000000a80405fb`, which does not include `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, and the mechanism worked unchanged.

**ANSWERED IN FULL later the same day, both halves and both machine kinds.** On a genuine **virtual machine** — Vast KVM rental, `systemd-detect-virt: kvm`, PID 1 `systemd`, full capability set `000001ffffffffff` — the identical test returned `pid=1643 uid=1002 gid=1002` for a caller running as `f84user`, with the caller's own view agreeing exactly. **So the mechanism behaves the same on both machine kinds and the fact's paired requirement is met.**

**And the endpoint cannot be lied to.** Posed on both kinds: an unprivileged caller attaching `SCM_CREDENTIALS` had its claim of **`uid 0` refused with `errno 1 Operation not permitted`**, and its claim of another process's identifier refused the same way; only a truthful claim was accepted. **A datagram sent with nothing attached still reached the receiver carrying the caller's true identity**, because the kernel fills the credentials in itself. A `uid 0` claim arriving as `uid 0` would have been the negative result. **So the endpoint may trust what it is told as well as what it asks.**

**One thing fell out of it that belongs to Fact 91:** on both machine kinds the reported process identifier was already absent from `/proc` by the time the server looked it up. See [The host key](The%20host%20key%20—%20obtained%20without%20authenticating,%20and%20whose%20it%20turns%20out%20to%20be.md).

### Fact 85 — Whether the chosen host behaves as its own documentation says, for the four clauses the licence service rests on: that an unreplicated database routes every read and every write to one primary, that a batch is atomic, that the always-on point-in-time restore restores, and that request logging is genuinely off when it is turned off.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Licence service.md`. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17.**

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from choosing a host on documentation nobody has ever exercised.
**Answered by:** an account with the chosen vendor, a throwaway service deployed and called, and the four clauses posed against it — a concurrent pair of slot claims on one key, a batch with a deliberate failure in it, a restore of a deliberately damaged record, and a request carrying a marker value looked for afterwards in the vendor's own log panel. What a negative would look like is written into the document: a third slot granted, or a second trial record written for a computer that already has one.
**Machine:** none. **No rented machine, and no machine of the Creator's beyond the one they already use.**
**Shape-deciding:** no, but it is the whole confidence of `Licence service` Sections 1, 6, 7, 10 and 11. The failure it guards against is silent and permanent rather than loud.
**Posable:** the Creator supplied their own Cloudflare account on 2026-08-17 and ruled the same day that agents act on their behalf with their keys, so the bar this entry was written under is gone.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-17, on the Creator's own account — the first time this stage observed the licence platform rather than read about it.** A throwaway Worker, six idle copies and a database, all destroyed afterwards, with the account inventoried before and after and proved identical line for line. **Nothing was spent**: no purchase, no upgrade, no payment method.

**Three clauses held. One is the reason the fact was worth posing.** Batch atomicity **held** — a failure mid-batch left nothing, where the same three writes outside a batch left two rows. The always-on restore **held** — a deliberately damaged value came back in about 3.5 seconds, with one in-flight request refused inside a window under half a second. The logging switch **held for the stored log** and **did not hold for the live stream**: with logging on, the request body's marker was **absent** and the header's marker **present**, along with the caller's address and rough location; turning it off produced no event at all; but the live tail keeps streaming every header and the address with logging off and with observability off entirely.

**The single-primary clause held as routing and did not hold as the thing the two-slot ceiling rests on**, which is the finding. Every read and write reported itself served by the primary. But 30 to 50 simultaneous claims against a two-slot key granted **19 of 30, 45 of 50 and 40 of 50** with separate statements, and **30 of 30** with the read and the write in separate batches. **Only a guard inside one SQL statement gave exactly two**, over four runs. The trial record never doubled under 40 simultaneous claims in either shape, because its primary key holds it. **So `Licence service` §7 as written — one statement sequence in one transaction — is not implementable**, since a batch returns its results only after committing, and the runner carries that correction.

**Four more questions were posed while the account was open.** The primitives all **held**, including a signature made in the service from a stored key and verified **off the platform**, with a one-character alteration rejected. One licence check costs **0.87 ms of processor time at the median and 1.64 ms at the 99th**, against the free plan's documented 10 ms ceiling — so **the free plan's processor ceiling is not a reason to take the paid plan**, and `Licence service` §1's third reason falls away while its first two stand. The clock **held**, frozen across computation and agreeing with a public time source **to within roughly a tenth of a second**. **Corrected 2026-08-23 at the stage-close gate: this read *accurate to about 1.5 ms against a public source*.** The `+/-` term in the `sntp` reading is that query's own error bound, not a spread the 170.9 ms offset can be taken through — the same instrument re-run on 2026-08-20 returned error terms of 33 to 59 ms while the offset barely moved — so a quantity known only to within tens of milliseconds cannot be subtracted from another and the difference stated to 1.5 ms. **The conclusion is unchanged**: the boundaries this clock feeds are measured in days, and there is no accuracy problem here of any size. `Licence service` §1 and §12 have carried the corrected figure since 2026-08-22 and this entry had gone stale against them. And two documented refusals were reached at no cost: the processor ceiling above about 200 ms of computation, and a size limit above about 2.2 MB — **and the 10 ms figure and the 200 ms refusal are not in conflict, which this paragraph carried both of without saying until 2026-08-22.** The 10 ms ceiling is a billed limit with documented flexibility rather than a hard wall, and the refusal arrives only when a Worker hits it consistently: Cloudflare, *Limits*, `https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/platform/limits/`, read 2026-08-20 — *"Each isolate has some built-in flexibility to allow for cases where your Worker infrequently runs over the configured limit."* and *"If your Worker starts hitting the limit consistently, its execution will be terminated according to the limit configured."*

**What could not be posed:** how far back the restore reaches. The database was minutes old and the account is on the free plan, so neither the 7-day nor the 30-day window could be tested. **That half stays open.** The answers are in [The host itself](The%20host%20itself%20—%20the%20licence%20platform%20run%20rather%20than%20read.md).

### Fact 86 — How long a caller waits for the first request to a host's idle service, and whether a durable write and a signed response fit inside the seller's first webhook retry.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Licence service.md`. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17.**

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from putting the seller's revocation route on a service that sleeps.
**Answered by:** the same throwaway Fact 85 needs, called cold and timed, against the retry window the seller's own documentation states. A negative is a first call that does not answer inside that window, which sends the revocation down the daily reconciliation path instead of the immediate one.
**Machine:** none. Rides entirely on Fact 85's account and justifies nothing of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no. It decides whether Row 237's immediacy is the ordinary path or the exception.
**Posable:** it was posed with Fact 85 on 2026-08-17, on the Creator's own account.

**ANSWERED OUTRIGHT, 2026-08-17.** Twelve first calls to services left genuinely idle: **0.108 s at the fastest, 0.128 s at the median, 0.567 s at the worst**, and every one of them included a durable write and a signed response. Warm calls ran 0.091 to 0.185 s, and a first call after a fresh deployment showed no penalty at all. **The worst reading is about one ninth of the gap before the seller's second delivery attempt**, so the revocation route is the ordinary path rather than the exception, and the daily reconciliation stays the backstop it was written as. **Corrected 2026-08-21: the seller's five seconds is the gap before its second delivery attempt, not a deadline the receiver must answer inside** — the vendor's sentence governs what happens *"If the response status is anything else"*. **No response deadline is published**, on any of its three webhook pages, searched 2026-08-20 with a working control. The measured figures are unchanged and are small against any plausible value; `Licence service` §15 item 4 carries the reopened question. **This fact was retired on the wrong framing and the numbers survive it**, which is why it is corrected rather than reopened. The answers are in [The host itself](The%20host%20itself%20—%20the%20licence%20platform%20run%20rather%20than%20read.md).

### Fact 87 — Whether the seller's refunded-order event distinguishes a full refund from a partial one, and what the order record carries about the amount refunded.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Licence service.md`. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17.**

**Named by:** no sweep, but it sits directly on Fact 10, which records that the refund event fires on a full **or** partial refund and that a chargeback becomes a full refund in most cases.
**Answered by:** reading the seller's own pages — the refunded-order event and the order record's own fields. **No account, no key, no request, no machine.** The same standing warning applies as to every reading of that seller: its help pages carry no date of any kind, so the answer is worth exactly its own date.
**Machine:** none.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now, by any agent, on documentation alone. **What breaks without it** is written into `Licence service`: with the fallback as written, a small partial refund revokes a paying user's licence and locks them.

**ANSWERED OUTRIGHT, 2026-08-17, the same day it was numbered.** The **event** does not distinguish — the vendor's own event list gives `order_refunded` as occurring on a full **or** partial refund — but **the order record it delivers does**, and the vendor states generally that an order event is sent an order object. That object enumerates six refund attributes, and the one the answer rests on is defined in exactly the terms the fallback needed: `refunded` is true **only** where the order has been fully refunded, with `refunded_at` non-null beside it, and `refunded_amount` measurable against `total`. The vendor's own worked example is a **partial** refund of 100 cents against a total of 1199 and returns `refunded` false. **So a small partial refund need never lock a paying user**, and it does not need the one contested field: `partial_refund` is enumerated as an order status while that same worked example returns `paid`, a vendor-versus-vendor disagreement recorded and not adjudicated. Three silences stand: no example payload for the event exists anywhere in the set, nothing states how many times it fires, and a sentence-level search of all 232 pages for any sentence carrying both a refund word and a licence word returned **zero** — the refund-to-key silence Fact 10 records is confirmed rather than narrowed. Read anonymously, nothing run. The answer is the dated later section at the end of [Lemon Squeezy](Lemon%20Squeezy%20—%20the%20licence%20service%20read%20against%20what%20Access%20needs.md), which the citation check of 2026-08-16 did not cover. **It contradicts `Licence service` Sections 9 and 15 item 5**, which say no page read enumerates those fields; the page that does was in that reading's own address list. The runner carries that correction.

### Fact 88 — Whether a test-mode key at the seller can see live-mode data, and what the store's live/test toggle does to a key that already exists.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Exposed by** the work of getting an agent near the Creator's live Lemon Squeezy account at all. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17**, after the observation had already been made and had nowhere to live: the board carried it as a placeholder saying the write-up and the number were owed.

**Named by:** no sweep. It is not a fact any promise waits on — **it is the fact every other seller observation stands on.** Without it, no agent may act in that account at all, because the account also holds live data, and the whole of `Method.md`'s bound *nothing that is already the Creator's is touched* turns on whether a test key can reach live data.
**Answered by:** the vendor's own documentation for what it states, and a controlled test for what it does not. The vendor states only that *"API keys created in test mode will interact with test mode store data only."* — **nothing anywhere says what the dashboard's live/test toggle does to a key that already exists**, which is the half that matters, because the Creator switched the store back to live after issuing the key.
**Machine:** none.
**Shape-deciding:** no. It gates permission rather than design.
**Posable:** it was posed on 2026-08-17.

**ANSWERED BY OBSERVATION, 2026-08-17, on the Creator's own account.** **The toggle and the key's world are independent**, and live-mode data are not visible to this key. Three limits ride with it: **one account, one day, the Creator's own**; the live-mode key was never opened; and nothing was created, written or refunded anywhere.

**The first sitting's evidence was partly wrong and the second sitting corrected it, so the check is written here as it actually stands.** The first sitting reported running `GET /v1/users/me` and `GET /v1/stores` either side of the toggle and finding `meta.test_mode: true` on both. **`GET /v1/stores` carries no `test_mode` field at all** — not in `meta`, not on the store object, on either the list or the single store — so that half of the check cannot be run as described by anybody, in either mode. `GET /v1/users/me` does carry it and did return it.

**What replaces it is stronger than what it replaces.** The second sitting ran with the store in **live mode throughout, the toggle never touched**, and every object the key returned across twelve collections carried its own `test_mode: true` — one product, one variant, twenty orders, twenty order items, eleven licence keys, three files, four customers, and every webhook it created. Beside that: the store reported `"total_sales":0` on the live side of an account, and all twenty orders carried obvious junk buyer addresses. **A negative would have been one object anywhere reading `test_mode: false`, one non-zero sales figure, or one real customer address.** None appeared. The write-up is [The seller itself](The%20seller%20itself%20—%20the%20store%20observed%20rather%20than%20read.md), whose Section 0 carries both sittings side by side.

**What it does not licence.** The live-mode key is revoked and `~/.smoozy-keys/lemonsqueezy.txt` says so; **no agent authenticates to that account in live mode at all**, whatever this fact says, until the Creator rules otherwise for a specific piece of work.

### Fact 89 — Whether the seller behaves as its own documentation says, on the three clauses `Licence service` leaves unresolved: what the delivered refunded-order body actually carries, whether the unauthenticated validate response carries the order identifier, and whether a chargeback produces any signal at all.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Licence service.md`, Section 15 items 5, 6 and 9. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17**, once Fact 88 made the account reachable. It is the seller half of what Fact 85 did for the host, and it is grouped as one fact for the same reason Fact 85 is: one account, one sitting, one answer, and the three clauses only mean anything together.

**Named by:** no sweep. Every seller claim in `Licence service` is documented assertion with **zero observation** — no account, no product, no key, not one request — and these three are where that costs something.
**Answered by:** a test-mode store. Issue a key, call the **unauthenticated** validate endpoint against it and record the whole body; register a webhook, cause or simulate a refund, and record the whole delivered body against the clause that `refunded` is true only for a full refund; and take the store's actual subscribable event list as the answer on the chargeback silence. **A negative on item 6 is a validate response with no order identifier in it**, which leaves a refunded key accepted until the next reconciliation pass.
**Machine:** none. A throwaway endpoint somewhere to receive the webhook, destroyed afterwards.
