**Shape-deciding:** no. **Item 6 is the one with a cost** — without it Row 237's immediacy has a day-long hole in that one case.
**Posable:** now. The Creator put the store into test mode, supplied `~/.smoozy-keys/lemonsqueezy-test.txt` and switched the store back to live on 2026-08-17; Fact 88 establishes the key is confined to test data regardless.

**TWO PARTS ANSWERED, ONE NOT POSABLE, 2026-08-17, the day it was numbered.** The store was observed for the first time on this project. The account inventory matched exactly before and after — twelve collections, same totals and same identifiers — and all eleven licence keys still carried their original `updated_at`, which is what proves the validate calls changed nothing. The answers are in [The seller itself](The%20seller%20itself%20—%20the%20store%20observed%20rather%20than%20read.md).

**THE ROUTE TO ITEM 5 IS NOW READ, 2026-08-17.** The Creator ruled they do not want a second seller account, so the two routes that use the store they already have were read against the vendor's own documentation — 264 addresses crawled to closure, every claimed silence checked against all 264 retrieved bodies rather than against the pages quoted. The reading is in [The refund event](The%20refund%20event%20—%20the%20routes%20to%20it,%20read%20rather%20than%20tried.md) and **nothing was run for it**: no authentication, no request to the seller's API, no checkout, no webhook registration.

**The recommendation is a test order and a refund of it, not the dashboard's event simulation**, and the reason is not fidelity. A simulation changes no record, so a refunded-order event simulated against an unrefunded order delivers an order whose refund fields are all in the unrefunded state — **it can report which field names appear and nothing about what any of them say after a refund**, which is the half this fact actually needs. Three vendor locations also describe the simulator as subscriptions-only against one page listing the event, and the vendor is silent on fidelity everywhere.

**What the fuller route closes and what it costs.** It closes what the delivered body carries and the status contradiction outright, most of the firing count, **and the refunded-key and disabled-key validate responses that could not be posed at all** — which is the only item in the batch with a named user-facing cost, since it decides whether Row 237's day-long hole closes. **Nothing in 264 pages discusses deleting test data**, so the test order and its key are permanent. **Two things go to the Creator with it:** entering the test card is their hands rather than an agent's, and **the vendor nowhere states that refunding a test order has no payout effect** — a strong inference on an account with live data, and still an inference. **If they decline, this item stays where it is and that is defensible**, because each remainder is covered by the daily reconciliation rather than being a broken promise.

**Item 6 — answered, and it closes the mechanical half of its hole.** The unauthenticated validate call **does** carry the order identifier: `meta.order_id`, matching the order the authenticated API attributes the key to, alongside the store, order-item, variant, product and customer identifiers. So the first-presentation check already knows which order a key belongs to and needs nothing further to identify it. **What it does not carry is anything about that order's refund state**, and **the response for a refunded or disabled key could not be posed** — no such key exists in the store and making one means writing to the Creator's records. So *is this key's order refunded* still cannot be answered from the unauthenticated call, and Row 237's day-long hole is narrowed rather than closed.

**Item 9 — answered, and the answer reopens a question the plan had closed.** The store accepts two webhook events that appear on **no page** of the vendor's documentation: **`dispute_created` and `dispute_resolved`**. Both registered and read back intact, against sixty-eight other names refused with one identical sentence, which is the control that makes it a test. `Licence service` §15 item 9 and §9 both rest on *no chargeback or dispute event exists in the vendor's set, confirmed across all 232 pages* — the pages still carry none, and the store does. **What is still unobserved is everything that would let anything rest on them**: whether they ever fire, whether they fire in test mode, what object they deliver, and whether that object reaches an order and therefore a key. ~~A dispute cannot be caused to order.~~ *(Struck 2026-08-29. The question was reopened on 2026-08-22 — a dispute **can** be caused to order in test mode, by the card number the payment processor publishes for it, on Route B — and `Preparation/Architecture/Licence service.md`, `The seller itself`, `The refund event` and Row 237 all carry the reopening. **This register was the last copy still saying it could not**, which matters because the register is the authority its own preamble makes it and the gates read their labels out of it. Found by the `Licence service` stage-close gate.)* **So the caution stands, the reason first given for it was wrong, and what is still unobserved is what the sentence before this one lists rather than the causing.**

**Item 5 — not posable in this account at all, and the bound is what stopped it rather than effort.** A delivered refund event needs a refund; refunding one of the twenty pre-existing test orders is forbidden. Making an order of the tester's own is impossible, because **the seller's API has no endpoint to create a product, a variant, an order or a licence key, and none to delete any of them either** — so even an object that could be made could never be removed. The vendor's manual event simulation is dashboard-only. **What it would take is a throwaway seller account that is not the Creator's**, which no agent here may create.

**Two things came back that nobody asked for and a builder needs.** The unauthenticated response carries the **customer's name and email address** — so that exposure is not behind the service's own credential, and anyone holding a licence key can read the buyer's address. And the same count is named `activation_usage` on the unauthenticated API and `instances_count` on the authenticated one, for the same key.

### Fact 90 — How much of the licence record one reconciliation pass can cover: the paid plan's per-invocation processor ceiling for a scheduled handler, the platform's limit on outbound calls in one invocation, and the seller's own rate limit against a pass of unknown size.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Licence service.md`, Section 15 item 13. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17**, at the round close. The close gate confirmed independently that **no input document reads a per-invocation outbound-call limit** — so it is genuinely unread rather than merely unquoted.

**Why it is numbered, when the design does not rest on it.** Item 13 says outright that the pass is bounded and resumable and that nothing in it should be changed on a guess at any of the three figures. That is true and it is not the test. The register's own rule is that a fact takes a number so a row can wait on it, and Row 237 gets weaker without these three as the record grows — the further down the ordering a key sits, the longer a refund whose webhook never arrived goes unrevoked. Left unnumbered it is findable only by someone already reading an unresolved list, which is the argument Fact 84 was numbered on.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from putting a periodic pass over a growing record on a platform that bounds every invocation.
**Answered by:** three different things, which is why the entry names them separately. The processor ceiling is documented as *"5 min"* for the paid plan and is **unobserved** — the account Fact 85 ran on was the free plan and no scheduled trigger was run at all. The outbound-call limit is **read by nothing** and wants the platform's own documentation first. The seller's limit is documented at *"60 requests per minute"* for its licence API and has never been tested; Fact 89's sitting can pose it against a test store for free.
**Machine:** none.
**Shape-deciding:** no. All three are sizing figures for an arrangement already chosen.
**Posable:** the outbound-call limit now, by reading. **The processor ceiling needs the paid plan**, which is a spend the Creator has not been asked for and which `Method.md` puts on the list that still stops.

**The seller's limit was not tested when the store was open on 2026-08-17, and the write-up says so plainly rather than letting silence read as a result.** Five calls were made to that API in the whole sitting, nowhere near the documented *"60 requests per minute"*. ~~**So all three parts stand open.**~~ **Two of the three stand open; the outbound-call limit was answered by reading on 2026-09-02.**

**PARTLY ANSWERED BY READING, PARTLY UNSCHEDULED, 2026-09-02.**

**The outbound-call limit is answered, from the platform's own published limits, at no cost and with no machine.** *Limits*, `https://developers.cloudflare.com/workers/platform/limits/`, page dated 2026-07-28, read 2026-09-02. **Subrequests per invocation: 50 on Workers Free, 10,000 (up to 10M) on Workers Paid**; subrequests to internal services, 1,000 on Free and *"Matches configured limit (default 10,000)"* on Paid. The page defines the term and it is wider than the entry assumed: *"A subrequest is any request a Worker makes using the Fetch API or to Cloudflare services like R2, KV, or D1."* — **so the pass's own D1 reads and writes are counted in the same budget as its seller calls**, which is the figure a builder actually sizes against. Two further published bounds ride with it: *"Each subrequest in a redirect chain counts against this limit. The total number of subrequests may exceed the number of fetch() calls in your code. You can change the subrequest limit per Worker using the limits configuration in your Wrangler configuration file."*, and a concurrency bound rather than a total — *"Each Worker invocation can have up to six connections simultaneously waiting for response headers."* Label: **documented**, unobserved. **The citation is carried here in full — exact words, address, page date and date read — and a companion entry is owed at `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Licence service.sources.md`**, not written from here because that document was in another implementer's hands on the day and this project's own record of two agents editing one sentence is why.

**And the processor ceiling's documented figure is not what this entry recorded — corrected 2026-09-02 on the same reading.** The same page gives, for the Paid plan, **CPU time per Cron Trigger of *"30 seconds (< 1 hour interval) 15 min (>= 1 hour interval)"***, and the *"5 min"* this entry carried is the **HTTP-request** ceiling's maximum, *"you can raise the limit from the default 30 seconds up to 5 minutes (300,000 ms)"*. **A scheduled handler's processor ceiling therefore turns on how often the schedule runs, which is a choice the service makes rather than a constant.** The figure is still **unobserved** — that half needs the paid plan.

**The two that remain are not closed by either ruling of 2026-08-31, and neither is scheduled.** The processor ceiling wants the paid plan, a spend the Creator has not been asked for; the seller's own limit wants a pass of unknown size against a live store. **Neither is a rental and neither is Blender research, so nothing here is written permanent — but nothing may be written as though a figure were coming either.**

**Breaks:** Row 237 gets weaker as the record grows — the further down the ordering a key sits, the longer a refund whose webhook never arrived goes unrevoked. **What holds, and it is the silence already removed:** `Licence service` §15 item 13 states outright that the pass is bounded and resumable and that **nothing in it may be changed on a guess at any of the three figures**, so a pass that runs out of its invocation stops at a recorded cursor and resumes rather than reporting a sweep it did not make.

### Fact 91 — Which mechanism pairs a recorded execution group with a value a later process cannot present, on a rented Linux machine, and whether it survives a container's reduced capability set.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/State, identity and recovery.md`, Section 13 item 5. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17**, on the same grounds as Fact 84: it is a machine question with an answer, it rides free on any Linux rental, and left unnumbered it is found only by someone already reading that document's unresolved list.

**What it is really about.** `The shape` already forbids discovering or stopping a worker by executable name, command-line pattern or process search, on Batch A's observation that a pattern selected more than one process and failed to stop the intended worker. That prohibition is necessary and is not sufficient: a bare process identifier is reused by the machine, so a recorded identity a later process can present is a pattern with extra steps. What is missing is the pairing value — something recorded when the group starts that a later, unrelated process holding the same identifier cannot present.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from writing the operation lifecycle and the deliberate stop.
**Answered by:** a throwaway on a rented machine — record a group's identity and whatever the mechanism records at its start, let the group end, cause a later process to take the same process identifier, and check that the pair still separates the two. **A negative result would be a later, unrelated process validating against a recorded pair.** Posed on both a virtual-machine rental and a container rental, because a container holds a reduced capability set and an answer taken only on a virtual machine can be silently wrong there — the same pairing Fact 1's narrow clause and Fact 84 both needed.
**Machine:** any rented Linux machine, and the second half wants a container. Rides on a batch and justifies no rental of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no. The run identifier that document records at start settles the reboot, container-restart and recycle cases by construction and needs no such mechanism, and the prohibition on a bare process identifier, a name and a pattern stands whatever the answer is. What is exposed without it is the *resumed* branch of reconciliation and the validation step of the deliberate stop — a stop could reach a process that is not the intended operation's, which is the failure Batch A observed arriving by another door.
**Posable:** now, on any batch that takes a Linux rental. **It belongs with Fact 84**, which wants the same two machine kinds and comes from the same boundary.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-17.** The mechanism is **the group leader's identity recorded together with its start time — field 22 of `/proc/<identifier>/stat`**, written by the kernel at fork. **It cannot be chosen by the process it describes**: `/proc/self/stat` is read-only and a write was refused with `errno 22 Invalid argument` on both machine kinds.

**The reuse case was forced on a virtual machine, which is the only place it can be.** A group leader was recorded as the pair **(identifier 1493, start time 19186)** and killed; the next identifier to be handed out was then set, and **the next process created received identifier 1493 — the very same one**. Validating the recorded pair against that new, unrelated process gave **`exists=True matches=False`**. **A later process validating against the recorded pair would have been the negative result**, and it did not happen: the pair separates them.

**A container cannot pose this, by three independent refusals**, and that is worth recording for the next batch: `pid_max` is 4194304 so natural wrap is untestable; writing the next-identifier control is refused with **`errno 30 Read-only file system`** because `/proc/sys` is mounted read-only; and making a namespace of one's own fails with **`unshare: unshare failed: Operation not permitted`**. The *mechanism* is a kernel property and reads identically on a container — only the demonstration needs the virtual machine.

**What it settles.** `The shape`'s prohibition on finding or stopping a worker by name, pattern or process search is **completed rather than contradicted**: a recorded identifier alone is a pattern with extra steps, because identifiers are re-handed-out; the identifier **paired with the kernel-set start time** is not. **What it does not settle:** start time is measured in ticks since boot, so it is meaningful only within one boot — the run identifier `State, identity and recovery` records at start remains what settles the reboot, container-restart and recycle cases, exactly as that document already had it. See [The host key](The%20host%20key%20—%20obtained%20without%20authenticating,%20and%20whose%20it%20turns%20out%20to%20be.md).

**RE-POSED AGAINST THE THROWAWAY'S OWN RECORDED PAIR, 2026-08-18 — same answer, and now against the real arrangement rather than a bare process.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §5. The pair the supervisor itself recorded when it started a worker was preserved verbatim, the identifier was forced onto a new unrelated process, and the throwaway's own validation gave **`exists=True matches=False`** — for the execution group and for the recorded render process alike. **The container's three refusals were re-confirmed in the machine's own words**, so *the demonstration wants a virtual machine* is now established rather than assumed.

**And a hole came out of it that nothing had noticed.** **The supervisor never waits on the workers it starts**, so a killed worker persists as a defunct entry whose start-time field still reads exactly what was recorded — **the recorded pair validates for a process that no longer exists.** The rule that a pair which no longer validates means the group has ended therefore **never fires for a dead worker while its parent lives**. It is accidentally protective, since the identifier cannot be reused while the entry stands, and it is also a hole: **the machine believes a dead group is live.** It blocked this demonstration until the supervisor was killed so the service manager could reap the entry, and it produced the same wrong reading under Fact 49. **This is a property of the throwaway's reaping rather than of the pairing mechanism**, and it is recorded here because the pairing is what it defeats.

### Fact 92 — Whether a rented machine's clock stays stable over the length of a render, rather than only over the half hour anyone has watched it.

**Exposed by** the Round 1 repair of `Preparation/Architecture/State, identity and recovery.md`, Section 13 item 10. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17.** It came from an auditor noticing that an accurate duration rests on a clock nobody has watched for as long as the thing it measures.

**Why it is not already covered.** Fact 13 is the *user's computer* — what each operating system offers as a reading of elapsed time that winding the clock back does not defeat. Fact 7 asks which clock a folder listing's times are on. Neither asks whether a rented machine's own clock holds steady across hours, which is the stretch a render actually runs for.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from computing a duration about work on a machine from that machine's own readings.
**Answered by:** a reading pair taken hours apart on a rental — the machine's clock against a real time source at the start, and again after several hours, with the difference recorded. **A negative result would be a drift large enough to show in a duration a user reads**, growing with the length of the job. `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` already has the half-hour half: within about a hundredth of a second on two checks thirty minutes apart, with anything longer untested.
**Machine:** any rented Linux machine, and it wants **hours rather than a visit** — so it rides free on any rental already up for something else, which is how it should be posed rather than by renting for it.
**Shape-deciding:** no. Nothing in the plan's shape moves either way.
**Breaks if negative:** [Queue & History](../Specs/Queue%20&%20History.md)'s recorded start, end and duration for a job. A duration is a cost the user reads, and a drifting clock states one that is not true. **What holds regardless:** the ordering of events, which comes from the journal's sequence numbers rather than any clock; and the prohibition on subtracting readings taken on different clocks, which stops the machine's drift being compounded with the user's computer's own undefended one.
**Posable:** now, on any batch whose machine stays up for a few hours.

**STILL NOT ANSWERED, and 2026-08-17 added fourteen minutes to it.** See [One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §8. A round-trip-compensated client against three public time sources ran **44.1 minutes and produced 45 samples**, because the container carries no time client and no service manager of its own. **The clock never left a 9.4 ms band**, and the apparent trend is smaller than the sample spread, so it is noise rather than drift. **The fact asks about the length of a render and 44 minutes is not that.**

**The limit that matters: a container has no clock of its own.** The reading is the host kernel's and nothing inside can discipline it, so what was measured is that physical machine's clock and a different host is a different answer. **It still rides free on any rental up for something else and still justifies no rental of its own.**

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-18 — yes, and the qualifier matters more than the number.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §13. On the virtual machine the realtime clock was sampled once a second against the monotonic clock **for the whole of a real 400-frame Cycles render** — 1,499 samples over **25.0 minutes**, 371 frames rendered inside it. **It stayed inside a band of 0.0129 ms**, with a largest single step of 0.0124 ms. **No jump, no slew, nothing a reading would notice.**

**And nothing is disciplining that clock.** `NTP=no`, `NTPSynchronized=no`, server `n/a`, **packet count 0**, the alternative daemon absent. **Zero time-service packets, ever.** So the band is tight **because nothing is stepping it**, and it says nothing whatever about whether the clock is *correct* against the outside world — only that it does not lurch during a render. **A box that later starts synchronising is a different box for this question**, and that is the honest reading of the number.

**THE RESIDUE IS PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** What is answered is a 25-minute render and a 44-minute container run; what is not answered is **a machine whose clock is being stepped by a time service across the length of a long job**, and settling that wants a rental the Creator ruled out on **2026-08-31**. The band measured is realtime-minus-monotonic and **says nothing about drift against true time**; **no time-service packet was ever seen** on either box.

**Breaks, and the break needs relocating.** `Job files and outputs` §14 item 16 says *"Breaks: nothing"*, which is true of that document and **not true of the figure the user is billed against**: a stepped host clock under a container **states a duration that is not true, silently, in figures the user reads as cost** — `Queue & History`'s recorded start, end and duration. That is where the break lands and it should be said there. **What holds:** the ordering of events, which comes from journal sequence numbers rather than any clock; the prohibition on subtracting readings taken on different clocks; and the two observations, at the widths they were taken. **The silence is removed by the qualifier being written into the answer rather than by any new reading** — the entry states outright that the band is tight because nothing is stepping the clock, so a box that later synchronises is a different box and no reader may carry this figure onto one.

**Two things were tightened on 2026-08-21 and neither changes the answer.** **What the band is a band of:** it is realtime **minus monotonic**, which by `clock_gettime(2)`'s own wording is insensitive to frequency drift by construction and moves only when the clock is **stepped** — so it says no step occurred in those 25 minutes and **no drift figure against true time can be recovered from it at all**. A reading of it as a parts-per-million drift figure was put and refuted. **How far it generalises:** the *nothing disciplines it* label is four readings on **one** rental, and Ubuntu ships a time service whose own manual says it *"will step the system clock for large offsets"* — **the address and the whole sentence were supplied on 2026-08-23, this entry having carried neither**: `systemd-timesyncd.service(8)` as Ubuntu publishes it, `https://manpages.ubuntu.com/manpages/noble/man8/systemd-timesyncd.service.8.html` and `.../jammy/...`, read 2026-08-23, identical on both releases — *"This minimalistic service will step the system clock for large offsets or slowly adjust it for smaller deltas."* The half this entry quoted omitted the slow-adjustment clause. So other rentals may well be disciplined and the honest form is *the one rented clock this project has watched*. **Widening it is free and wants no rental of its own:** run `timedatectl show` on the next two or three rentals that come up for anything else. **Also recorded, and it is a design lever rather than a fact:** a monotonic reading taken beside each realtime stamp would make a step visible and make a duration immune, which `Queue & History` §18 item 10 now states and declines to decide.

**The container's own answer is the same and the reason is different, which closes the earlier entry's limit properly.** It has no clock of its own, established four ways: its time namespace is the initial one at zero offset; nothing inside can discipline it — setting the date is refused even as root, the hardware clock is unreachable, and the capability that would permit it is absent from root's bounding set; no time daemon runs inside; and its monotonic and boot-time clocks read the **host's** uptime, 8.21 days on a container created that morning. **Both restarts left that uptime and the boot identifier unchanged, so nothing inside can tell from them that it was restarted at all.** Against an outside synchronised clock over 9.6 minutes spanning a live render, the corrected offset spread was 0.039 s with drift within noise — **and the container had nothing to do with that: if the host stepped its clock the container would follow silently and could neither prevent nor detect it.**

### Fact 93 — What a rented machine's own file-transfer server actually offers: its extension set, its announced operation limits, and the session cap that decides how many transfer channels one connection can carry.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Server protocol and transfers.md`, Sections 1.1 and 1.2. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17.**

**Why it is not already covered.** Nothing in the register asks it. Fact 17 asks whether a part-sent transfer can be resumed and Fact 72 whether damage is caught, both of which were posed with whatever tools the two ends happened to carry. This asks the prior question the whole protocol rests on: **what the far end announces it can do.** The distinction is not academic — the publication step, the resume offset, the per-path free-space reading and the digest check are each written against a named extension, and a machine that does not offer one takes a different path through the document.

**And the evidence to date is all from the wrong end.** Every extension, limit and behaviour that document quotes was observed on **the Creator's own Mac** — macOS 26.5, `OpenSSH_10.2p1` — while every rental on record announces **8.9p1**. So the caller's capabilities are established and the machine's are assumed from them. The document says so itself and prices it both ways.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from writing a protocol against a far end nobody has interrogated.
**Answered by:** running the transfer server's own capability enumeration on a rental and recording what comes back word for word — the announced protocol version, the extension list, the reported maximum packet, read, write and open-handle figures — and reading the machine's own manual pages for the request-ban and read-only flags and the session cap. **A negative result would be a rental announcing a materially smaller extension set than the caller's** — in particular one lacking the POSIX-rename, fsync or statvfs extensions the publication, durability and free-space steps are written against.
**Machine:** any ordinary Linux rental under the non-root login Smoozy holds. **It rides free on any batch and justifies no rental of its own.**
**Shape-deciding:** no. Every path the answer could take is already written; what moves is which path is the ordinary one and which is the degraded one.
**Breaks if negative:** nothing outright — the document already carries the absent-extension branch for each of the three. What a negative answer does is make the degraded path the ordinary one, which puts the publication step on a rename that can fail over an existing target and the free-space reading on a different route.
**Posable:** now.

**The silence that was recorded here was not one, and it is struck — 2026-08-21.** This entry said no manual page on any system documents what the POSIX-rename extension performs on the server, and called that Fact 37's shape a third time. **The premise was true and the conclusion was false.** There is no such manual page because OpenSSH does not put its extensions in manual pages; it puts them in `PROTOCOL`, which ships in every source release and opens *"This documents OpenSSH's deviations and extensions to the published SSH protocol."* Section 4.3, read 2026-08-20 at `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openssh/openssh-portable/master/PROTOCOL`: *"On receiving this request the server will perform the POSIX operation rename(oldpath, newpath) and will respond with a SSH_FXP_STATUS message. This extension is advertised in the SSH_FXP_VERSION hello with version \"1\"."* Joined to Linux's own `rename(2)` — *"If newpath already exists, it will be atomically replaced"* — two first-party sources state what this project had recorded as unstated. **It is not Fact 37's shape at all:** Fact 37 is about diagnostic strings no source specifies, and this is a wire operation OpenSSH specifies. Fact 82's exit statuses are untouched and stay Fact 37's shape. **What the miss cost is the lesson** — a manual page is not the boundary of a project's documentation, which is now `Method.md`'s standing note, and `PROTOCOL` Sections 4.3 to 4.12 specify every extension `Server protocol and transfers` leans on.

**Round 1 of that document parked two more things against this fact on 2026-08-17, and both ride on the same rental.** **The free-space route.** The document no longer makes the file-transfer extension the only route to a per-path reading: where it is absent, a per-path command-channel reading against the exact path is used, which is the route Fact 28 was actually answered by on a rental. **What this fact now also settles is which route a real machine puts Smoozy on**, and the negative result to watch for is a machine offering neither. **The fallback operation limits.** Where a machine announces no limits, the document uses 32,768 bytes for both the read and the write size. **That was labelled Smoozy's own conservative choice rather than anything documented, and the label was wrong — corrected 2026-08-21.** `draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02` Section 3, which is the specification of the very protocol version the project observed announced, says *"All servers SHOULD support packets of at least 34000 bytes … This should allow for reads and writes of at most 32768 bytes."* So the number is the protocol's own recommendation and `sftp(1)`'s 32KB default comes from the same place. **What survives is the caution and not the invention:** SHOULD is not MUST, so the size is recommended and guaranteed by nobody, and a SHOULD is not an observation — this half of the parking stays open. **In the event the branch was never reached**: the rental announced `limits@openssh.com` with real figures. Nothing was written into the document about what a rental actually announces, and whoever poses this must not be told what the plan hopes to see.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-17, with one half unposable.** See [One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §3, read raw off the wire by a protocol speaker written for the purpose rather than by trusting a client, and byte-identical under the non-root login.

**The three extensions the document is written against are present** — POSIX-rename, fsync and statvfs — in a set of **eight**. **But the set is smaller than the caller's eleven**, and the shortfall matters: the rental lacks **server-side copy**, which the Mac announces. Anything designed by watching the caller's end finds it there and finds it absent on the rental.

**The announced limits are 262144 for a packet, 261120 for a read and for a write, and 1019 open handles.** Recorded as the machine's own words and compared to nothing, as this fact requires.

**The session cap is 10 and it is per connection** — not set in configuration, present only as a commented default, and then posed rather than trusted: ten of fourteen concurrent sessions on one connection succeeded, with the first ten as the control, and a second connection gets its own ten. **The refusal never says *too many sessions*** — it says the session was refused and then appends `Host key verification failed.`, which is Fact 37's shape again and would send a builder after a host-key bug that is not there.

**Both free-space routes are available under the non-root login**, so the outcome to watch for did not occur — and **both report the whole container overlay rather than a per-path allowance**, which was proved by reading a path the login cannot even list and getting the same figures at success.

**The manuals document none of it, because there are none.** No OpenSSH manual files exist on this image; the request-ban flag, the read-only flag and the session cap are all undocumented on the machine. **And nothing on that image describes what POSIX-rename performs on the server** — the protocol description files are not shipped there and the string exists only as a bare name in the server's own request enumeration. **That is a gap in the provider's image and not a silence of OpenSSH's, and this entry read it as the latter until 2026-08-21.** The file that is missing from the image is `PROTOCOL`, and it specifies the operation in full; see the struck silence above. **What stays true and is worth keeping** is that a builder standing on such a machine has no local documentation for the request-ban flag, the read-only flag or the session cap, so those must be read from the project's records rather than from the box.

**COULD NOT POSE: the proxy against the direct route — and the reason is itself the finding.** The provider's recorded direct address answers with an identical banner and **its host keys match nothing on the container**, while the proxy route's three match the container's own exactly, and inside the container every connection arrives on loopback. **So a recorded direct endpoint at this provider is not verified to reach that rental**: connecting can succeed at the TCP and banner level while being a different machine, and only the host key distinguishes them. It follows that **the container cannot see the caller's real address** and that the proxy-against-direct comparison remains unestablished in both directions.

**RE-POSED ON THREE MORE BOXES ACROSS THREE SELLERS, 2026-08-22, and the load-bearing half did not vary.** The multi-provider batch drove machines A (Vast container, Ubuntu 24.04, OpenSSH `9.6p1`), C (Hyperstack virtual machine, Debian 12, OpenSSH `9.2p1`) and D (Sesterce virtual machine, Ubuntu 22.04, OpenSSH `8.9p1`). **Protocol version 3 on all three. Limits byte-identical on all three** — max-packet 262 144, max-read and max-write 261 120, max-open-handles **1 019**. **The exclusive create refused on all three with the plain create succeeding as the control; the base rename refused on all three; `posix-rename` succeeded on all three; `fsync` and `statvfs` succeeded on all three; `f_namemax` 255 on all three; the session cap was 10 on all three and a second connection was permitted on all three.**

**One reading did vary, and it is the useful part.** **The announced extension set is 11 on the OpenSSH 9.x boxes and 8 on the 8.9p1 box**, which lacks `copy-data`, `home-directory` and `users-groups-by-id@openssh.com`. **Every extension Smoozy leans on was present on all three**, so the variance is entirely in the tail. **This is the first observation that the set varies at all on a rental**, and it is what makes capability negotiation load-bearing rather than a courtesy.

**And the max-open-handles figure differs from the caller's own end** — 1 019 on every rental against 1 048 571 on the Mac — so that number is a per-machine reading and must never be carried across.

**Still not reached: the 32 768 fallback branch.** Four boxes across three sellers have now all announced `limits@openssh.com` with real figures. The branch remains **untested rather than disproved**.

### Fact 94 — Whether Smoozy's own byte loop behaves over a real network connection as it does over a local pipe.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Server protocol and transfers.md`, Section 12 item 4. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-17.**

**Why it is not already covered.** Fact 25 measures what the link carries and Fact 75 whether a rate cap holds; both were posed against whole transfers run by ordinary tools. This asks about **the loop the document has Smoozy write itself** — offset writes, a stop at a chosen point, a resume from a recorded offset, a digest that matches afterwards — every part of which was exercised over a pipe on one computer and none of it over a network.

**Why it matters more than it reads.** The loop is what the document reaches for wherever an ordinary tool cannot be relied on: it is how the partial is held off the final name after an interruption Smoozy performs itself, and that hazard's whole answer is that the loop is Smoozy's own rather than a tool's. A loop that behaves differently once a real link and its buffering sit in the middle takes that answer with it.

**Named by:** no sweep.
**Answered by:** running the same loop against a rental over the ordinary home connection and comparing what it does at each boundary with what it did over the pipe. **A negative result would be a resume that starts over, an offset write landing somewhere other than the offset, or a digest that matches over a pipe and not over a link.**
**Machine:** any ordinary Linux rental, with **the Creator's own Mac at the other end** — the same requirement Facts 17, 72 and 25 carry, and for the same reason. **It rides with Fact 93 on the same rental and the same sitting.**
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Breaks if negative:** the resume and integrity halves of Row 281, which would fall back to the tool-driven route the document already describes.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-17 — the link behaves as the pipe does.** See [One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §4. The loop was written rather than borrowed and the pipe case was run **in the same sitting**, so the comparison is like for like. 400 MB, a stop at 32.7%, and all three channels — pipe, Mac to rental, rental to Mac — resumed from the recorded offset rather than starting over, and **all three ended at the same digest as the source**. An offset write lands exactly at its offset on both channels, and the hole is genuinely sparse on the rental.

**One thing established deliberately and it is not good news.** After a stop the loop performs itself, **the bytes sit at the final name with ordinary permissions and nothing on either machine marks them as partial** — no suffix, no flag, no attribute. The only thing that knows the file is incomplete is the recorded offset, which lives outside the filesystem. **This is the failure already recorded for an ordinary tool, now recorded for Smoozy's own loop.**

**Cost:** upload about 0.7 MB/s, download 2.3–2.8 MB/s, **roughly three to four times faster down than up**, against about 500 MB/s over the pipe.

### Fact 95 — Which start-up mechanism a container provider runs when a rented container starts: whether it is a file at a path or a stored field, under which identity it runs, whether it runs again on a restart or a recycle, and what becomes of a customer-written hook.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Rented-server runtime.md`, Round 1, as an **unchecked fact** parked for Batch E. **Numbered by the architecture runner on 2026-08-18**, at the write-up, on the same grounds as Facts 84, 90 and 91: it is a question about the world with an answer, several documents lean on it, and **left unnumbered it is findable only by somebody already reading one document's unresolved list.** It is numbered even though it is now answered, because a number is what lets a row and a document's repair both point at the same thing.

**Why it is not already covered.** Fact 1's narrow clause established *that* a boot return works on a container, on one provider, by the provider running a hook of its own. Fact 61 asks about gateways and ports. Nothing asked **what the mechanism is on any other provider**, which is the question the container branch of the boot registration rests on entirely.

**Why it is the worse half of a pair.** The systemd branch reads back a **machine-set** value — the lingering flag — so a machine whose policy refuses it fails the placement loudly. The container branch reads back **Smoozy's own content**, so a hook written at a path a provider never runs **reads back as confirmed** and nothing ever runs it.

**Named by:** no sweep.
**Answered by:** reading each provider's own documentation — quoted, with the address and the date read, and nothing else admitted — and then a restart on a rented container. **A negative result would be a provider offering no path at which a customer may leave a script, so that a hook Smoozy writes sits unexecuted with nothing about writing it failing.**
**Machine:** rides on any container rental and justifies none of its own. The reading half needs no machine.
**Shape-deciding:** no, and it changes no arrangement — but it changes what may be claimed for the container branch on any provider but the one observed.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED IN PART, 2026-08-18, and the premise the question was testing does not survive.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §12 for the reading and §2 for the observation.

**A container rental's boot hook is not one mechanism.** **Vast alone documents a file at a path** — `/root/onstart.sh`, *"This script runs automatically on container startup."* **RunPod, Novita and Paperspace Deployments instead hold a stored field that replaces the image's start command.** There is no path in that shape, **so a file a builder wrote expecting the first shape would sit unexecuted and nothing about writing it would fail** — which is exactly the negative result this entry was filed against, arriving for three of the four container providers that document anything at all. **Lambda and Hyperstack rent no containers at all**; their equivalent is cloud-init, and Hyperstack states its timing outright: *"The script runs when the virtual machine first boots."* **Sesterce and Paperspace Notebooks are silent on all five questions.** **SaladCloud refused every documentation fetch and nothing is claimed about it.**

**Not one provider — Vast included — documents whether the hook runs again on a restart.** That is the half that matters most for the boot return and the silence is universal, without a single exception. **Nor does any provider read document the identity the hook runs as**, or what becomes of a customer-written hook at a recycle.

**The one documented fact that bears hardest.** RunPod, of a Pod's own storage: *"It's created when a Pod launches and is cleared when the Pod stops"*, against the volume, where *"Data stored in the `/workspace` directory survives Pod stops and restarts"*. **So on RunPod a hook written as a file outside the persistent volume is erased by exactly the event a boot hook exists to survive** — and a read-back of Smoozy's own file at placement would have confirmed it happily, minutes before the wipe.

**What the observation adds, on the one provider where the documentation is silent about restarts.** **Vast does run `/root/onstart.sh` on every restart of an existing instance, as root, proved three independent ways** — the hook chain in the process table, a log file only the hook writes created at the restart moment, and a witness file gaining one line per reboot. It is handed to a shell rather than executed, so the execute bit and the first line are irrelevant; it is backgrounded and detached; and it is **not overwritten if it is already there**, so Smoozy's content survives. `/etc/rc.local` is a second hook run by the same line, **and its directory is world-readable where `/root` is not.**

**Still owed:** whether a **recycle** replaces a hook Smoozy wrote — the tester was forbidden to recycle and did not, and reading the provider's own script is not an observation.

**THE RESIDUE IS PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** Two things are left and neither has a route. **The reading route is exhausted:** three of four container providers hold a stored field rather than a file path, and **not one provider — Vast included — documents whether the hook runs again on a restart**, so there is no page left to read. **The observation route wants a container rental**, and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental to answer a question.

**Breaks, and it is the sharp one:** a container rental that **reports a good placement and never returns after a restart, with nothing in the confirmation to reveal it** — because that read-back is of Smoozy's own content and never of the provider's behaviour. **What holds:** Vast observed on every restart three independent ways, as root, not overwriting Smoozy's content; and the systemd branch entire, which reads back a machine-set value and fails loudly.

**The silence, stated plainly rather than papered over.** **No reading taken at placement can reveal it, and none exists that would** — the confirmation is a read of Smoozy's own file, so on a provider that never runs it the confirmation is true and useless. **What is readable, and it costs nothing:** the **first connection after any restart** finds no supervisor and reports the machine as not ready rather than as holding work, so the failure is loud at the moment it matters even though it is invisible at the moment it is made. **The rule that follows, and it is what the disclosure has to carry:** a placement confirmation may be recorded and reported as *the content was written* and never as *this machine will come back*, on any provider but the one observed. `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 16 is where that belongs; it is named here and not written here.

### Fact 96 — Which desktop application shells can host Smoozy on Mac, Windows and Linux from one codebase, and what a real published release artifact of each actually weighs on each of the three.

**Numbered by the architecture runner, 2026-08-18.** **It waits on no sweep** — no sweep named it, because it is not a promise's dependency but a **standing Creator ruling's**: `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md` records that when Architecture chooses the desktop technology it compares **real release artifacts** for all three systems and prefers an operating-system web view or another materially smaller shell, that a route bundling a complete browser engine is not chosen merely because it keeps more promises, and that if the smaller route cannot keep a promise Architecture **stops** and brings the exact user outcome and its smaller replacement to the Creator. Label on the ruling: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-13).

**Numbered rather than left in one document's list**, on the Fact 95 precedent: an unnumbered fact is findable only by somebody already reading the document that parked it, and this one is the **first decision `Desktop app and delivery` takes**.

**Answered by:** reading each candidate's own documentation, site and repository, and reading **published asset sizes from each release listing's own metadata**. Nothing downloaded, nothing built, nothing installed.
**Machine:** none rented. **Shape-deciding:** for one document, in the escalating sense the ruling names.
**Posable:** now, and it was.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-18, by the reading batch, and the choice is deliberately not taken here.** See [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §6. Six candidates across three kinds — a bundled browser engine, the operating system's own web view, and two routes that are not web-based at all. **The six are Electron, Tauri, Wails, Neutralinojs, Qt 6 and Flutter**, and three of them publish no end-user artifact of their own, so those figures are real applications built with them. *(Runner, 2026-08-18: the answer of record described the candidates without naming them, which made it unusable for the one decision it exists to serve — `Desktop app and delivery` could not name the route it chose, because the names sat in the trail and the trail is outside this stage's reading. The names are restored in that file from the findings' own comparison tables, with a dated note there. No figure and no claim changed.)* **All 51 byte figures were independently re-read by the citation check against their listings' own metadata and every one matched to the byte.**

**The spread, per system, macOS / Windows / Linux.** **Electron, shell alone before any app: 122.1 / 144.4 / 125.6 MB**, and a real app on it (YouTube Music) 124 / 287 / 142. **Tauri v2, on Yaak: 66.4 / 42.9 / 79.9.** **Wails v2, on October: 6.4 / 8.8 / 5.7 — the smallest real figures in the comparison.** **Qt 6, on KeePassXC: 34.4 / 35.9 / 49.2.** **Flutter, on LocalSend: 52.5 / 25.8 / 24.6.** **Neutralinojs** ships a single 8.1 MB bundle for all three and its per-system share cannot be got without downloading it, so it cannot be placed in an order.

**What the figures are and are not, which the naming correction made plain.** **Tauri and Wails are both compiled**, so the gap between 66.4 and 6.4 is **October against Yaak — two applications — and not one framework against another.** Three of the six publish no end-user artifact of their own, so **no figure here predicts what Smoozy would weigh on any route.** `Desktop app and delivery` §22 carries that as its own open item and says what closes it: building one throwaway on both and reading the two artifacts, which is a Build-stage measurement.

**Three qualifications, all in the projects' own words.** A system-web-view artifact **grows back toward engine scale** under two documented options — one project's Windows offline installer adds "~127MB" and its fixed-version option "~180MB", and a self-contained Linux bundle "bundles all dependencies", which is why one app's self-contained Linux artifact is **149 MB against its 80 MB package**. **The three system web views are three different engines** — one self-updating, one frozen to the operating-system version, and one whose picture its own project calls "very hard to compile accurate information about" — and that is the documented cost of the small shell. **Installed and unpacked sizes are documented by none of the six.**

**The negatives matter more than the sizes, and two of them touch Row 280 directly.** **Electron documents no automatic update on Linux at all** — accurately quoted from its own page, *"There is no built-in support for auto-updater on Linux"* (read 2026-08-20). **Corrected 2026-08-21: this register entry carried that negative without the sentence that disposes of it, so a reader of the register alone got an eliminating version the document does not have.** `Desktop app and delivery` H9 says the same argument disposes of it — the update promise is kept below the framework on every route, so Electron's larger size buys nothing here. **And Linux auto-update on Electron is documented**, outside Electron's own docs: electron-builder's page, `https://www.electron.build/docs/features/auto-update/`, read 2026-08-20 — *"Linux is supported (not only macOS and Windows)"*, with AppImage, DEB, Pacman and RPM as auto-updatable targets. **Wails documents no self-update anywhere**, and every page of its current major version carries a notice that the next one is in beta. **Two candidate negatives are struck, 2026-08-21, because each was contradicted by that candidate's own documentation.** **Flutter** was recorded as documenting *store distribution rather than a signed downloadable binary*: its own Windows page says *"You are not required to publish Windows apps through the Microsoft Store"* and its build page says *"Distributing your application by self hosting it on a website requires a certificate signed by a Certificate Authority known to Windows"*; its Linux build page documents a plain bundle directory and names no store; its macOS page documents distributing the `.app` *"perhaps from your own website"* (all read 2026-08-20). **And this project's own Flutter figures were read off LocalSend v1.18.0's `.dmg`, `.zip` and `.deb` — 52.49 / 25.80 / 24.58 MB, which is the row above to two decimals. It measured the thing it then said did not exist.** **Qt 6** was recorded as the candidate whose GPL-versus-LGPL module split *could not be resolved*: Qt publishes it on one page, `https://doc.qt.io/qt-6/licensing.html` (read 2026-08-20), under the heading *Modules available under GNU General Public License v3*, and every module page repeats its own terms. What LGPLv3 costs — a relinkable binary and installation information — is real and is a different question. **Neither strike changes the choice**: `Desktop app and delivery` H7 now excludes both on property 3, since neither is a system-web-view route on this comparison's own rows, and Wails is the smallest on every reading. **Only Tauri addresses cross-platform signing head-on**: macOS signing needs an Apple device, and cross-compiled Windows installers need an external tool and are "not tested as much".

**Two hazards in the evidence itself.** **A release listing is not frozen** — one asset was added to a listing days after publication — so a byte figure is durable and *that listing holds only these assets* is not. And **one same-app comparison of a bundled engine against a system web view, 209 MB against 80 MB, rests on a filename alone** because no documentation explains those assets; it is labelled inferred and must not be leaned on.

**This fact is the fastest-changing in the register after Fact 10.** Every figure and every version is worth exactly its own date, 2026-08-18. A comparison read in August says nothing about the choice made in October.


### Fact 97 — Whether Windows' reputation warning fires on a build the application downloads and launches itself, as distinct from a build a person downloads from a website.

**Exposed by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md`, 2026-08-18, while answering Row 280. **No sweep named it and no register entry held it.**
**Why it is its own fact rather than part of Fact 74.** That fact establishes both halves separately and **no page read joins them**: Microsoft documents the warning for a *newly created binary*, and documents the unattended update outcome for an unpackaged per-user application. Whether the first applies to the second is stated nowhere.
**Why it matters more on Windows than the macOS question it is the twin of.** **The Windows update shape is side-by-side by construction** — Microsoft documents that replacing the running image at its own path fails — so every Windows update puts a new build at a new path, and **it cannot take the precaution the macOS answer takes** (replacing the executable inside the already-approved bundle).
**Answered by:** a Windows computer, a signed build with no accumulated reputation, and an update the application performs on itself. **This entry said *Reading cannot close it — the pages that would answer it are the ones already read*, and that was false — corrected 2026-08-22.** At least three first-party Microsoft pages bearing on it were unread, and reading has since narrowed the question without closing it: *Microsoft Defender SmartScreen overview*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/security/operating-system-security/virus-and-threat-protection/microsoft-defender-smartscreen/`, page dated 2026-04-23, read 2026-08-20, pulls both ways in its own words — *"SmartScreen protects against malicious files from the internet. It doesn't protect against malicious files on internal locations or network shares"* against *"It checks any files an app (including 3rd-party browsers and email clients) that attempts to download and run."* **What no page read states is the negative**, that an untagged file is not checked.
**Machine:** a Windows computer. **None exists on this project and none is scheduled.**
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** the reading route now, and largely spent; the throwaway route not at all today, no Windows machine having been rented for it. **This line said *not at all today*, flat, until 2026-08-22.**

**And on Windows 11 the cost may not be a warning at all — recorded here 2026-08-22, out of the first sanity sweep's `raw-facts-head` finding, which had reached this project's own audit trail and never this register.** *SmartScreen reputation for Windows app developers*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/package-and-deploy/smartscreen-reputation`, page dated 2026-05-04, read 2026-08-20: *"On Windows 11 devices, the Smart App Control feature may supersede SmartScreen Application Reputation. Smart App Control will block execution of unsigned files unless the file has a positive reputation. Smart App Control signature checks apply to all executable files, not just those downloaded from the Internet."* **Smart App Control is gated on a clean install and is not opt-in** — *Smart App Control*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/develop/smart-app-control/overview`, page dated 2025-11-18, read 2026-08-20: *"In most cases Smart App Control automatically turns on"*, *"it can only be enabled on a clean install of a version of Windows that contains the Smart App Control feature"*, and in enforcement mode *"Apps cannot be run unless they are recognized by Microsoft's app intelligence services, or they are signed with a trusted certificate."*

**NOT ANSWERED. What breaks if it fires:** the Windows user meets a warning on an update the app performed for them, which is a cost no spec mentions and which would reach `Method`'s third escalation. **What holds without it:** Row 280's promise, which is about the user not having to fetch or remember an update, and which the documented unattended outcome keeps either way. `Desktop app and delivery` §19.2 states the warning plainly rather than letting the download promise be read as promising a silent first run.

**PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** The reading route is spent — the two Microsoft pages quoted above pull both ways in their own words and **no page read states the negative**, that an untagged file is not checked. The throwaway route wants a Windows computer and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental to answer a question. **And on Windows 11 the ending may not be a warning at all:** Smart App Control may supersede reputation and **block** execution, which is an ending different in kind rather than a worse warning.

**Breaks, unchanged:** the Windows user meets a warning — or a block — on an update the app performed for them, a cost no spec mentions, reaching `Method`'s third escalation. **What holds:** Row 280's promise, which is about the user not having to fetch or remember an update and which the documented unattended outcome keeps either way. **The silence is already removed:** `Desktop app and delivery` §19.2 states the warning plainly rather than letting the download promise be read as promising a silent first run, and **Z17** states that the side-by-side shape cannot take the macOS precaution because the new build is at a new path by construction. **This is Windows-list item 10, and `Desktop app and delivery` §22 item 6 must say the same thing.**

### Fact 98 — How each of Mac, Windows and Linux provides an application menu bar.

**Named by:** Row 305, in `Preparation/Architecture/Rows/Design handoffs.md`, which labels the Design document's arrangement **an assumption** and hands this stage the confirming. The row named no numbered fact for it and the register held none; **numbered by the runner on 2026-08-18** on the Fact 95 and 96 precedent, so that the reading is findable by somebody who is not already reading the document that got it.
**Answered by:** reading each platform's own documentation. **No computer of any kind** — it is a documentation question and the missing Windows computer does not block it.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now, and it was.

**ANSWERED ON ALL THREE, 2026-08-18**, by `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md` §18.1, which carries each quotation with its address and the date read. **The assumption is confirmed on all three and corrected in its reason on one.**

**macOS — confirmed.** Apple's own *macOS User Guide*: *"The menu bar runs along the top of the screen on your Mac."* The bar is the system's and belongs to the screen rather than to a window, so the only question is what goes in it.

**Windows — confirmed in conclusion, corrected in reason.** Microsoft's *About Menus* documents a **window-owned** menu bar the system positions just below the title bar, and that *"Only an overlapped or pop-up window can contain a menu bar"*. So there is no screen-level bar — but the platform's own alternative is not nothing, and **the Design's one-button arrangement is an application choice rather than a platform necessity.** Nothing about the five menus changes.

**Linux — no *platform* specification defines one, and it is the same shape as its downloads folder. This said *nothing is provided*, and that is the wrong word — corrected 2026-08-22.** freedesktop's specification index lists 33 specifications and **none is an application menu bar**; the one named `menu` is the Desktop Menu Specification, which is the desktop's list of applications rather than an application's own bar. **What does exist is a toolkit's rather than the platform's, and it travels over D-Bus, which is not what a specification index lists** — GTK's own `GtkApplication`, `https://docs.gtk.org/gtk4/class.Application.html`, read 2026-08-20: *"GtkApplication will automatically load menus from the GtkBuilder resource located at 'gtk/menus.ui', relative to the application's resource base path."* and *"The menu with the ID 'menubar' is taken as the application's menubar."*, exported on the session bus as a `GDBusMenuModel`. **That route belongs to GTK and is not available to a system-web-view shell**, which is what this stage has chosen, so the menus are the application's own to draw and no platform provision is relied on.

**What it does not close:** what the chosen shell exposes, which is a property of a candidate rather than of a platform and is a required property in that document's own list.

### Fact 99 — Whether assigning a non-library recorded location in a running Blender is enough on its own for the render that follows to read the new location, per kind, and which kinds need what reload.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Section 12 item 1, its sharpest unresolved, raised while that document was written. **Numbered by the runner on 2026-08-18.** It is the untested remainder of Fact 77 rather than a new subject: that fact answered whether a rewrite driven on a rented machine reaches the file **on disk**, proved by a separate process reading the file after a save. This one asks whether an assignment that is never saved is in force for the render started in the same session, which is the only form the remap can take — the artist's file is never written to.

**Why Fact 77 does not close it.** The library case is answered in all four versions on the rented Linux machine: assignment records at once, the old data stands until the library is reloaded, and the relocate command does both in one act. For every other kind the spec's own sources file says a write on its own is untried, and **no observation anywhere renders from an in-session rewrite of a non-library read location.**

**Answered by:** a rented machine. **Machine:** the same ordinary rental Facts 5 and 77 used, with 4.5 and one of 5.0, 5.1 or 5.2 both present. **It rides one rental with Fact 100 and with the seven inferred kinds of that document's Section 12 item 2**, which want watching in the same sitting.
**The test, and what a negative looks like:** **the fixture's original location absent at load**, so the load resolves nothing; then assign each kind's location in the running headless Blender to a second file that does exist, reload where the kind has a reload, render, and read which file the render used. **A negative result is the render using the original location, or nothing at all, for some kind** — which is the harm the document names: a finished render, wrong, saying nothing. **The two-valid-files form does not pose it**: it starts from a reference that resolved, which is not the state a remapped file is in, and answered in that form it would read as though it had closed the question while leaving the real case open. **Corrected by Round 1's must-do D3, 2026-08-19** — the recipe this entry was numbered from was the one that cannot answer the module's case.
**Shape-deciding:** no. A negative for a kind means that kind's pair needs a reload step, which the mechanism is already shaped to take.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED FOR THE IMAGE KIND, 2026-08-22, and the other kinds are named as unposed rather than counted.** Run on the multi-provider batch's machine A in **4.5.12** and **5.2.0**, on a fixture whose recorded locations were **absent at load** — the state the corrected recipe requires. Evidence, the reasoning and the five kinds that were not posed are in [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20—%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md).

- **The image kind needs no reload.** Assigning `filepath` alone put the render on the new file: the unresolved load rendered the missing-texture colour `[0.741, 0.400, 0.745]`, the assignment alone rendered `[0.145, 0.557, 0.000]` — the alternate file's green — and a subsequent `reload()` changed nothing. **A freshly loaded datablock in the same slot rendered the identical colour**, which is the control that makes the green a read of the new file rather than a coincidence. Identical in both versions.
- **One trap, and anything that gates on it will report a working remap as broken:** `image.has_data` read **`False`** throughout — before the assignment, after it, and after the reload — while the render was reading the file correctly.
- **NOT POSED, and not claimed: sound, font, movie clip, volume and cache file.** Sound was assigned and read back but has no render to observe. The `CacheFile` datablock had no user after the Alembic import and was dropped on save, so it was not present at load. Blender's default font is built-in and carries no path, and no external font, movie or VDB fixture was built. **The fact stands answered for one kind of the several it names.**

**PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED FOR THE FIVE, 2026-09-02.** Settling cache file, sound, font, movie clip and volume needs a rental with Blender on it and a fixture of each kind, and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental and no further Blender research. **This is not a pending item and must not be read as one, and no reader may leave a space in the code or the record for the answer.** Sound's case is structural rather than an omission and stays so: a sound has no render to read it from, so *is it in force for the render* cannot be asked of it at all.

**Breaks, in the carrying document's own words:** *"a remapped reference of a kind that silently needs a reload renders as missing — a finished render, wrong, saying nothing."* It is the sharpest break in the register, because M9a's trap forbids gating on `image.has_data` or any flag of the kind, so the marker D16 reports **what was assigned** and never what the render read — success, with the silence complete. **What holds:** the image kind, proved by render and not by a flag; the library kind with its reload (Fact 101); and M9b's refusal to extend either to the five.

**The silence-removal is `Dependency Upload & Remap` §12 item 1's and is named there rather than restated here** — a further reading on the marker D16 already writes, saying per applied pair whether assignment-alone is **established** for that pair's kind. **It is not written into this register**, which holds facts rather than clauses, and it must not be duplicated here.

### Fact 100 — On what Smoozy recognises the recorded location pointing into the Blender application the file was saved with.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Section 12 item 8. **Numbered by the runner on 2026-08-18**, and **Row 171 waits on it** — the only row of that document not answered.

**What is in hand, and it is one observation rather than a mark.** A file saved on the Mac and opened on Linux listed, among its own paths, *"a path into the Mac's own Blender application — `/Applications/Blender 4.5.12.app/…/essentials_brushes-gp_draw.blend`, a folder that exists on no Linux machine"*, and Blender's own missing-file report does not name it (*Dependencies 1*); the same entry is recorded as *"one entry nothing in the file pointed at"* in all four versions (*Reading a file 5*). **So it sits inside the very lists Smoozy's own enumeration reads, in every version, and nothing anywhere says what marks it.**

**What is owed:** what identifies the entry **as data** — which data-block kind and which property it arrives under, whether it is the library weak reference, whether more than one such location can exist, and whether the mark is stable across versions.

**Answered by:** a rented machine, in the same shape as Facts 5 and 77 — one file, each version, the reading Smoozy already drives. **It rides the same rental as Fact 99.** **The route through Blender's own printed missing-file report is not the answer** and the document says why: it is printed lines in every version including 5.2, a name holding a newline shatters line-reading, and mining printed wording is a trap two closed documents already name.
**The test, and what a negative looks like:** read a file saved by a known Blender for its own paths, in each version, and look for a mark separating that entry from every reference the user made. **A negative result is no mark that separates them** — in which case Row 171 cannot be kept as written and what the user gets instead goes to the Creator.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but **Row 171's promise cannot be kept without it**: unexcluded, the check never leaves nothing unfound, so `Rendering`'s warning never stops for any file the user made. The document declines to guess, because the over-broad guess silently drops a user's real missing file.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-22, and the mark is `library_weak_reference`.** Run on the multi-provider batch's machine A in **4.5.12** and **5.2.0**, each saving a file from its own factory startup and touching nothing. Evidence in [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20—%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md).

- **Which data-block kind and which property.** Any datablock, through **`library_weak_reference.filepath`**, with `library_weak_reference.id_name` beside it. Observed on the two Materials `Dots Stroke` and `Material`, carrying `MADots Stroke` and `MAMaterial`, pointing at `…/datafiles/assets/brushes/essentials_brushes-gp_draw.blend` under the running application's own folder.
- **Whether it is the library weak reference: yes, exactly that.**
- **What separates it from every reference the user made.** It appears in the flat list `bpy.utils.blend_paths()` returns — the list Smoozy's enumeration reads — **and it has no datablock in any path-carrying collection.** `bpy.data.libraries` is empty, and nothing in `images`, `sounds`, `fonts`, `movieclips`, `cache_files`, `volumes` or `texts` carries the path. A user's own reference always has such a datablock. **That is the mark, and it is a presence test rather than a string test** — nothing is matched against a path prefix.
- **Whether more than one can exist: yes.** Two here, one per datablock, both naming the same file — so a deduplicating reader sees one and a per-datablock reader sees two.
- **Whether the mark is stable across versions: yes**, 4.5.12 and 5.2.0 identical.
- **And Blender's own report is confirmed useless for it, as this entry predicted:** `report_missing_files()` printed `Info: No missing files` in both versions.

**A negative would have been no mark that separates them**, in which case Row 171's promise could not stand. **There is one. Row 171's promise can be kept**, and the answer costs the enumeration a presence check rather than a path comparison.

### Fact 101 — Whether assigning a library's path and reloading populates a library that never loaded.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Section 12 item 17, opened by that document's Round 1 on 2026-08-19 (must-do D3). **Numbered by the runner the same day.** It is the library half of Fact 99 rather than a separate subject, and it was hidden inside a claim of *proven* until the round pulled the observation apart from the case it was of.

**Why the existing observation does not close it.** *Reading a file 5* records that *"Assigning a library's path changes what is recorded at once but leaves the old data in place until the library is told to reload"* — **old data in place is data that resolved**, so what was watched is a working library being re-pointed. The same source lists under *Not established*: *"Whether writing a path reloads anything other than a library."* **Every remapped file is in the other state**: at launch the load resolves the top file's own recorded values, the library path names the artist's computer, nothing loads, and the material then re-points and reloads what the load did not resolve.

**Answered by:** a rented machine. **Machine:** the same ordinary rental Facts 99 and 100 ride, and **item 1's corrected recipe poses this one too** — the fixture's original location absent at load, then assign, reload, render, and read which file the render used.
**The test, and what a negative looks like:** **a negative is the linked data still missing after the assignment and the reload** — the pair assigned, the marker reading it back as applied, the stand-in empty, and the render finishing at success with the linked data absent and nothing to see.
**Shape-deciding:** no. A negative means the library pair needs the relocate form rather than assign-and-reload, which the mechanism is already shaped to take and which Blender's own repair path uses on a broken library.
**Posable:** now, on the rental Facts 99 and 100 already want.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-22, and positively.** Run on the multi-provider batch's machine A in **4.5.12** and **5.2.0**, on the same fixture as Fact 99 and in the state this entry insists on rather than the one Fact 77 watched. Evidence in [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20—%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md).

- **The library genuinely never loaded.** At load: `Warning: Unable to open '…/orig/lib.blend': No such file or directory`, `Info: Cannot find lib`, `LIB: Object: 'LinkedThing' missing`, `is_missing=True`, and the linked object present as an `EMPTY` with `data = None`. **Nothing resolved, so this is not a working library being re-pointed.**
- **Assignment alone did not populate it:** after setting `library.filepath`, the object was still `('LinkedThing', None, -1, -1)`.
- **Assign plus `library.reload()` did:** `('LinkedThing', 'Cone', 4, 4)` with `is_missing=False`, and the mesh is the **alternate** library's cone rather than the original's plane, so the data came from the newly named file.

**So the library kind needs the reload and the image kind does not** — which is the per-kind difference Fact 99 exists to find, observed rather than inferred. **The relocate form is not required**, so the mechanism keeps its assign-and-reload shape for this kind. Identical in both versions.

### Fact 102 — Whether the update a pin produces carries the pinned message itself, and whether a pin made with the notification suppressed leaves anything visible in a private conversation.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Telegram.md` Section 21 items 10 and 11, on 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the runner the same day.** Two halves of one bot's behaviour, posed together because they ride the same pin.

**Why it is owed.** The whole machine-to-holder channel is built on a machine pinning its own re-used message and the holder hearing about it. *A pin produces an update* is observed; that the update's service message carries the **pinned message itself** is Telegram's own documented `pinned_message` field and the join between the two was never recorded.
