And every machinery pin is made with the notification suppressed — a documented option that was never exercised — so whether the conversation shows the user a service line for each one is unknown, and a conversation littered with them is a cost nobody has weighed.

**Answered by:** a throwaway against a live bot. **Machine:** none — a bot and one Telegram account.
**The test, and what a negative looks like:** a negative on the first half is a pin update arriving with no pinned message in it, which costs a step rather than the design — the holder falls back to reading the reported pin, which `Telegram.md` B11 already writes. A negative on the second is a visible service line per machinery pin, which is a Design cost and possibly a third-escalation question.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now — a bot token has been in `~/.smoozy-keys/telegram.txt` since 2026-08-19, and this needs one conversation with it.

~~**NOT ANSWERED.**~~ **ANSWERED 2026-08-29, both halves**, against the live bot with the Creator at the client. **No machine and nothing spent.** The record is [The pinned line](The%20pinned%20line%20—%20what%20a%20conversation%20actually%20shows%20for%20a%20pin,%20and%20the%20filter%20that%20had%20been%20eating%20the%20bot's%20messages.md), which carries the calls, their answers word for word, and every limit.

**First half — yes.** Each pin's update carried a **`pinned_message`** holding the whole original message: id, sender, chat, date and text. Observed three times. **So B11's fallback is insurance rather than the ordinary route**, and the negative that would have cost a step did not happen.

**Second half — there is a line, and it is not the line the spec describes.** Suppressing the notification removes nothing from the conversation: the two suppressed pins drew the same line as the unsuppressed control, so **`disable_notification` silences the notification and nothing else** — §21 item 17's ruling, until now reasoned, is now observed. *(Noted 2026-10-18, Creator-stated: a phone announced every pin made with `disable_notification` in a private chat, so a suppressed pin is not silent; `Telegram` B24 and Sources entry 41. No auditor has seen it.)* **But the line quotes the message rather than naming an act**: `smoozy_example pinned "Fact 102 test — message A"`, the bot's own name and the pinned message's own text in quotation marks. **It is cut by the window's width rather than by any character count** (Creator-stated, 2026-08-29, by resizing), so a wide window shows more. **And it follows the message's later edits** — a message pinned and then edited draws its *edited* text — which matters because B4 makes the board's heartbeat an edit.

**Three things fell out that nobody posed.** **Re-pinning an already-pinned message costs nothing** — no update, no second line — so a line is written when a message goes from unpinned to pinned and at no other time, which prices the machinery far below what Row 506's table assumes and turns the real rate on B18's janitor. **Editing produces no update and no line.** **And unpinning produces no update at all and leaves the line standing** — what it removes is the pin marker beside the message; the lines accumulate and nothing takes one away.

**What was owed to Row 506 and not settled here** — how much of that the user meets under B9 to B15 and B18, and whether the spec's account of it can stand — **was taken up the same day.** The rate is priced at `Telegram` B20 and **the observed cheapness turned out not to accrue to the machinery at all**: B11's janitor pops every machinery pin, so each utterance is a fresh unpinned-to-pinned transition and the one-line-per-utterance rate stands. **Whether the spec's account can stand is the Creator's**, stopped at `Telegram` §21 item 28 and put as `Audits/Architecture/Row 506 — for the Creator.md`.

**One thing nobody posed at all, and it is in the same record.** The bot's token was carrying a leftover **`allowed_updates` filter naming `my_chat_member` alone**, silently dropping every ordinary message; `getUpdates` answered `{"ok":true,"result":[]}` while the Creator had genuinely written. **The setting lives on the token every machine shares**, is invisible in an ordinary call's answer, and is revealed only by `getWebhookInfo`. **So an empty update queue has a second cause nobody had recorded** — which is the reading Fact 129's entry records the runner meeting on 2026-08-19, though whether it was this cause is not established. ~~**Nothing in `Telegram` covers it**, and it is routed to Row 506's implementer to place or to refuse.~~ **Placed the same day at `Telegram` §7 P6**: every receiving call Smoozy makes names its wanted update kinds explicitly, on every call, never relying on the token's standing filter — so the fleet's hearing is re-asserted at every ask and a leftover filter from any earlier holder is displaced by the first one. Two residuals at that document's §21 item 29: the kinds' published identifiers are in no record of this project's, and whether a webhook registration carries or resets the filter is unrecorded.

### Fact 103 — What the documented webhook-removal call does against a registration that is genuinely stale, and what a machine can tell from the answer.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Telegram.md` Section 21 item 11 and Section 4 J3, on 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the runner the same day.**

**Why it is owed.** J3 is the one piece of the listening machinery `Rented-server runtime` could not write, and it is the only thing standing between a destroyed webhook holder and **every held job on every machine being unanswerable from a phone**. The rule joins a staleness reading to the webhook-form `409` description text and then makes the documented removal call — **a call this project has never run**, on a registration that is genuinely dead rather than merely unreachable.

**Answered by:** a throwaway against a live bot, and Telegram's own documentation for what the call promises.
**Machine:** none for the removal itself — a bot and one Telegram account are enough to register a webhook at an unreachable address and then remove it. **A provider that permits a real webhook** is wanted only for the rest of Fact 63's owed remainder.
**The test, and what a negative looks like:** a negative is the removal call refusing, or succeeding while the next ask is still refused for conflict — either of which leaves J3's rule unable to recover the slot and turns `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 8's named break into a live one.
**Shape-deciding:** no; the asking route is complete without the webhook route at all.
**Posable:** now, on the same bot as Fact 102.

~~**NOT ANSWERED.**~~ **ANSWERED, 2026-09-02, on the removal call and on the conflict text, against the live bot and Telegram's own documentation. No machine, nothing spent, and nothing left registered.** Run from the Creator's own computer on the bot whose token has been in `~/.smoozy-keys/` since 2026-08-19 — the same bot Fact 102 used. **The bot's webhook state was read before the run and restored after it**: no webhook either side, and the token's standing update filter recorded and returned to the identical six kinds.

**The sequence, in the answers' own words.**
- **The registration.** `setWebhook` at an address that resolves and will never accept an update returned `{"ok":true,"result":true,"description":"Webhook was set"}`, and `getWebhookInfo` then carried that address at `max_connections` 40.
- **The refusal J3 joins to, word for word.** `getUpdates` while the registration stood returned **HTTP 409** and `{"ok":false,"error_code":409,"description":"Conflict: can't use getUpdates method while webhook is active; use deleteWebhook to delete the webhook first"}`.
- **The removal.** `deleteWebhook` returned `{"ok":true,"result":true,"description":"Webhook was deleted"}`.
- **The slot came back in the same second.** `getWebhookInfo` returned an empty `url`, and `getUpdates` answered `{"ok":true,"result":[]}` — success, not conflict.
- **And the answer separates the two cases, which the documentation does not.** `deleteWebhook` called again with nothing registered returned `{"ok":true,"result":true,"description":"Webhook is already deleted"}`. **`ok` and `result` are `true` in both**; the only discriminator is the `description` string.

**The documentation, read the same day at `https://core.telegram.org/bots/api`.** `deleteWebhook` is *"Use this method to remove webhook integration if you decide to switch back to getUpdates. Returns True on success."* — **it promises the switch back, says nothing whatever about staleness, and publishes neither description string.** `setWebhook`'s own note is the other half: *"You will not be able to receive updates using getUpdates for as long as an outgoing webhook is set up."* **The run and the citation are carried here in full; a companion entry is owed at `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Telegram.sources.md`**, not written from here because that file was in another implementer's hands on the day.

**So neither negative occurred.** The removal did not refuse, and the next ask was not still refused for conflict. **J3's rule can recover the slot**, and `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 8's named break does not become a live one.

**One rule for a builder falls out of it and it is the useful part.** The two description strings are the only thing separating *there was a registration and it is gone* from *there was nothing to remove*, and **Telegram publishes neither**, so a rule that turns on them is turning on an unpublished string. **What is published and sufficient is the pair of readings around the call** — `getWebhookInfo`'s `url` before, and `getUpdates` succeeding after. J3 should decide on those two and may record the description without depending on it.

**Two limits, stated rather than glossed.** **The staleness reading is still unobserved:** the far end would never have accepted an update, but the bot's queue was empty, so Telegram made no delivery attempt and `last_error_date` and `last_error_message` were never populated. **That half is not closed by either ruling of 2026-08-31** — it wants one message written to the bot while a registration stands, no machine and nothing spent — and it is recorded here rather than left as an implied yes. And this is one bot, one account, one day.

**THE STALENESS HALF WAS RUN AGAIN LATER THE SAME DAY, 2026-09-02, three ways, and it is still unobserved — but for a reason that is now established rather than assumed, and two of the three ways produced answers of their own.** Same bot, same token, from the Creator's own computer. **The bot's state was read before and restored identical after: no webhook either side, `pending_update_count` 0 either side, and the standing update filter untouched throughout** — `allowed_updates` read `message`, `edited_message`, `callback_query`, `my_chat_member`, `chat_member`, `chat_join_request` before, in every reading during the run, and after. No call of this run ever named `allowed_updates`, and `setWebhook`'s own published rule is that an unnamed filter keeps the previous setting. **No machine, nothing spent, nothing left registered.**

- **A registration cannot be made stale by an address that cannot be reached at all, because Telegram refuses it at `setWebhook`. Two refusals, in Telegram's own words.** A reserved address — `https://192.0.2.1/…`, RFC 5737's TEST-NET-1 — returned `{"ok":false,"error_code":400,"description":"Bad Request: bad webhook: IP address 192.0.2.1 is reserved"}`. A host that does not resolve — `https://smoozy-f103.invalid/…`, the reserved top-level name — returned `{"ok":false,"error_code":400,"description":"Bad Request: bad webhook: Failed to resolve host: Name or service not known"}`. **So the address is validated when it is set**, and a genuinely stale registration is one that was valid at registration and died afterwards, which is the only shape J3 ever has to recognise.
- **Telegram does not probe a registered endpoint, so the staleness reading is produced by a delivery attempt and by nothing else.** A registration at an address that resolves and answers nothing useful — `https://example.com/…`, accepted with `{"ok":true,"result":true,"description":"Webhook was set"}`, `getWebhookInfo` then carrying the resolved `ip_address` and `max_connections` 40 — **stood for three minutes with `pending_update_count` 0, read every six seconds, and `last_error_date` and `last_error_message` never appeared at all.**
- **That narrows a reading this project has carried since 2026-08-16 and it is routed, not corrected here.** [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 63, says the error field *"appeared within sixteen seconds"* and that *"Telegram appears to make it once on registration"*. **The error there was delivery-driven rather than probe-driven** — that same write-up records that the Creator had sent many messages to the bot and that something in the run consumed them — so updates existed to be delivered. **Nothing that rests on it moves**: the health test is still the presence of the error field, and `Security and licence` B26 states it as such. What changes is that the field is evidence of a **failed delivery** and never of a registration merely standing, so **an endpoint that has never been sent anything is indistinguishable from a healthy one**.
- **The removal call's two description strings were reproduced** — `{"ok":true,"result":true,"description":"Webhook was deleted"}` against a standing registration and `{"ok":true,"result":true,"description":"Webhook is already deleted"}` against none, with `ok` and `result` both `true` in each — so today's earlier discriminator holds on a second run.

**What is still not done, and why it is not this stage's to do.** The reading itself wants **one update to exist while a registration stands**, and an update is made by a person writing to the bot or by the bot acting in a conversation whose identifier it holds. **The bot's queue is empty and no conversation identifier is recorded anywhere in this project** — [The pinned line](The%20pinned%20line%20—%20what%20a%20conversation%20actually%20shows%20for%20a%20pin,%20and%20the%20filter%20that%20had%20been%20eating%20the%20bot's%20messages.md) states outright that the Creator's own numeric identifier is *"deliberately not written down"* — so the bot can address no conversation and can make no update of its own. **This is not a rental, not a Blender question and not a spend, so neither ruling of 2026-08-31 closes it: it wants one message typed to the bot by a person, and it belongs on the Creator's list beside Fact 69.** **It must not be written permanent.**

**Breaks, and it is narrower than it was.** J3 joins a staleness reading to the conflict text before making the removal call. **The removal half and the conflict text are observed and the recovery works** (above), so what an unobserved staleness reading costs is only J3's ability to tell *this registration is dead* from *this registration has simply never been sent anything* — and the run establishes that Telegram gives no signal at all in the second case. **What holds:** the pair of readings around the call, which J3 decides on and which are published and observed — `getWebhookInfo`'s `url` before, and `getUpdates` succeeding after. **The silence is removed by the pair rather than by the error field**, so J3 needs no staleness reading to recover the slot; the reading would only let it say why.

### Fact 104 — Whether Blender's own moving-picture route honours the file's view transform the way the still-image routes were observed to, and whether it takes a card.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Telegram.md` Section 21 item 7, on 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the runner the same day, and it is numbered here only to keep it from being lost.**

**This one is not this stage's to answer.** It is a question about what Blender does, and Rule 2 and the [Blender Research method](../Blender%20Research/Method.md) both say a Blender fact is looked up rather than tested here — and no question in the register asks it. The still copies' colour treatment is settled by `Job files and outputs` B25 and B27 **on stills-only evidence**; the moving-picture route was never observed, and whether the tool touches a card at all is unobserved beside a render on a machine the user is paying for.

**Answered by:** Blender Research, as a question added to the appropriate topic — **not by an agent here, and not from any agent's memory.**
**What breaks:** the media feature's colour and its cost, never a render's correctness. `Telegram.md` M5 declares the media worker cancellable, last-ranked and never blocking, and M5 says outright that one change to that declaration excludes media from running beside a render with nothing else moving. A wrong colour is the obvious failure B25 observed rather than a subtle one.
**Posable:** ~~not here. It goes to [Open Threads](../Groundwork/Open%20Threads.md) for the Creator to schedule as research.~~ **Not at all. Struck 2026-09-02 with the ruling below.**

**NOT ANSWERED. PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02, and the route it was parked on is closed.** This entry sends the question to Blender Research for the Creator to schedule, and **the Creator ruled on 2026-08-31 that there will be no further Blender research.** So it is not parked — **it will not arrive, this is not a pending item, and nothing may be written as though a round were coming.**

**Breaks, unchanged: the media feature's colour and its cost, never a render's correctness.** **What holds:** `Telegram` M5's declaration that the media worker is cancellable, last-ranked and never blocking; and B25's observation that a wrong colour is the obvious failure rather than a subtle one — **which is the silence already removed**, since a media file the user can see is wrong is not a silent failure at all. **`Telegram` §21 item 7 is where it is carried, and it is the only one of that document's four that is permanent.**

### Fact 105 — Whether the machine-side reading of a `.blend` returns the running Blender's own version identifier as one of its payloads.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Rendering.md` L14, on 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the runner the same day.**

**Why it is not already covered.** Fact 5 is answered on all three payloads and none of them is the version: the reading was run in 4.5.9, 5.0.1 and 5.2.0 and the observation is that the values it returned were version-correct, which is not the same as the reading naming its own version in its answer. L14 composes the version confirmation out of that same machinery and rides it on the reading where one runs, so **the confirmation costs nothing extra exactly when the payload is there and costs one short invocation when it is not.**

**Answered by:** a rented machine, riding free on any rental already up with a Blender on it — the same box shape Fact 5 used. **A negative result is the reading answering without a version field**, which costs the confirmation its free ride and nothing else.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with a Blender on it. No rental of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Breaks if negative:** one clause of L14's cost, not its mechanism — the worker makes a short invocation of the same machinery asking for the version alone on every attempt rather than only on attempts with no reading. `Preparation/Architecture/Rendering.md` Section 10 already prices that invocation and declines it as an escalation. **What holds:** the confirmation itself, which is a read of the binary about to be launched and never of a path, a folder name or an installation record.
**Posable:** ~~now, on any batch whose machine carries a Blender.~~ **Not by anything this stage will have. Struck 2026-09-02 with the ruling below.**

**NOT ANSWERED. PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** It rides on a rental with a Blender on it and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental to answer a question. **Breaks:** one clause of L14's cost and not its mechanism — the worker makes one short extra invocation on every attempt rather than only on attempts with no reading, which `Rendering` §10 already prices and declines as an escalation. **What holds:** the confirmation itself, which is a read of the binary about to be launched and never of a path, a folder name or an installation record. **There is no silence to remove and that is a property of the shape rather than luck:** a reading that answers without a version field costs the free ride and nothing else, so no wrong answer is taken here quietly. **A builder takes the paid route and never assumes the free one.**

*(Flagged for the runner, not decided here: whether the payload is named in Blender's own published API reference is something a reading could in principle reach. It is not taken, because a Blender fact in this project comes only from the dedicated official-docs research and that route is closed. Recorded so nobody re-opens it as an oversight.)*

### Fact 106 — How long a heavy frame takes to honour an interrupt.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Rendering.md` K9 and Section 9 item 7, on 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the runner the same day.**

**Why it is not already covered.** Fact 2 is answered in full and measures **time to a recorded outcome** — 1 to 3 seconds on the virtual machine and 1.9 to 14 on the container, against a real Blender render. What it does not measure is the other end of the distribution: those runs were a small scene, and nothing anywhere observes a frame heavy enough that Blender takes a long time to reach the point where it honours the interrupt. **K9's 60-second deadline is the one number this document invents and it is bounded from below by Fact 2 and from above by nothing.**

**ANSWERED 2026-08-25, on the Pile 3 rental. The result is positive, and the reason it is positive is narrower than the number.** The answer of record is [The Pile 3 batch](The%20Pile%203%20batch%20%E2%80%94%20a%20full%20mount%20under%20a%20running%20supervisor,%20an%20interrupted%20heavy%20frame,%20and%20an%20hour%20of%20silence.md) §3. A scene Blender itself estimated at **ten and a half hours for one frame** was interrupted four times: latencies of **2.666, 8.142, 13.437 and 29.946 seconds**, against a sample batch of **37.3 seconds**. Label: **observed in a test**, Blender 4.5.3 on one rented container.

**The mechanism is the whole answer: the latency is the time left in the current sample batch and nothing else.** Blender announces the break at once, finishes the batch in flight, then cancels. All four measurements fall inside one batch and are spread across it as a signal arriving at an arbitrary moment would be.

**How deep the frame is does not matter, which is the opposite of what this entry expected.** The deepest interrupt of the four — twelve and a half minutes in — gave the **fastest** exit, because the signal landed near the end of a batch. **What a small fast scene changes is the batch length, not the mechanism**, so Fact 2's small-scene runs were not measuring a different thing after all.

**SUPERSEDED 2026-08-31: K9's deadline is now 120 seconds, and this paragraph's *confirmed* was never the whole of the evidence.** A second body of measurement — *Frames 15*, sixteen tests~~ on one scene at 320 by 180~~ — records **longest stop waits of sixty-five to seventy-three seconds in all four supported versions**. That is **above** the sixty this paragraph confirmed, so on the very shape *Frames 15* tested the waiter would have expired routinely on renders that were stopping normally. **The two bodies are not reconciled**: this fact's mechanism — the wait is the remainder of the current sample batch — predicts a *short* wait on a small scene~~, and those long waits are on a small scene~~. Nothing here explains it and nothing invents an explanation. **Corrected 2026-10-01: the long waits were not on a 320 by 180 scene, which is the Cycles scene most of *Frames 15* used.** They are its entry 13: an EEVEE render at 1920 by 1080, frames 1 to 500, on the rented Linux machine, whose viewport is drawn by its processor and where each frame took about ninety seconds — 72.84 s in 4.5, 66.37 in 5.0, 68.04 in 5.1, 65.04 in 5.2, against 0.54 to 2.35 s for the same render on the Mac, and the research says the two machines' figures are not a comparison of anything (`Findings/4.5/Frames — Architecture questions, second pass.md` §13, and the same entry for 5.0, 5.1 and 5.2). Label: **observed in a test**. So the disagreement as first stated here rested on a misreading; why those waits were long is still not established. `Rendering` K9 was corrected the same way the same day. The runner ruled the deadline to 120 on 2026-08-31; `Rendering` K9 now carries both bodies and states the disagreement, and §9 item 7 carries the enlarged residue. **The paragraph as written follows, because what it measured is unchanged and only its conclusion about the deadline is not.**

~~**So K9's 60 seconds is confirmed at the width exercised and no wider, and the residue is named rather than closed.**~~ The worst case on this scene is one batch — 37.3 seconds — about 23 seconds inside the deadline. **But that headroom is a property of the scene's per-sample cost, not of Blender**, and nothing observed says a batch cannot exceed 60 seconds. A scene that pushed it there would make K10's premature sentence the ordinary case on a stop that is working. `Rendering` §9 item 7 carries that residue.

**And an interrupted frame left nothing**, on all four runs — no part-written image, no empty file, no output folder at all.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with a card, and a scene chosen to make one frame slow. Rides free on any batch already running a render.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Breaks if negative:** `Preparation/Architecture/Rendering.md` K10's sentence fires before the render stops, so the user is told the render could not be stopped and then it stops — a premature sentence corrected by the entry when the outcome lands, costing trust and never state. **What holds:** the definition of *not taking* (K8), the honesty of the sentence (K11), and the value being one named constant changed by one edit.
**Posable:** now.

*(A leftover `NOT ANSWERED.` line stood here until 2026-08-29, below this entry's own **ANSWERED 2026-08-25** and its four measurements. Removed by the architecture runner at the stage close, found by the `Rendering` gate. Nothing rested on it — `Rendering` K9 and §9 item 7 both read the entry as answered.)*

**THE RESIDUE IS PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** Two things are unestablished and both want a rental the Creator ruled out on **2026-08-31**: **that a sample batch cannot exceed the deadline**, and **why the two bodies of evidence disagree at all** — this entry's mechanism predicts a short wait on a small scene and *Frames 15*'s sixteen tests recorded sixty-five to seventy-three seconds~~ on a small scene~~. **Nothing here explains it and nothing invents an explanation.** *(Corrected 2026-10-01: those waits were on an EEVEE render at 1920 by 1080 on the rented Linux machine, not a small scene — see the superseding note above. Why they were long is the half still unestablished.)*

**Breaks:** where a batch runs past 120 seconds, `Rendering` K10's sentence fires before the render stops — the user is told the render could not be stopped and then it stops. **The case a builder watches for is a scene whose sample batch runs long, not a heavy frame**, which is the opposite of what this fact was numbered to test and is what the Pile 3 measurement established. **What holds:** the definition of *not taking* (K8), the honesty of the sentence (K11), the deadline being one named constant changed by one edit, and the four measurements themselves. **The silence is removed by `Rendering` K9 and §9 item 7, which now carry both bodies and state the disagreement rather than resolving it** — a reader meets the conflict rather than a false settlement.

### Fact 107 — What naming `CUDA` rather than `OPTIX` at launch costs a render in speed, on the same NVIDIA hardware.

**THE NVIDIA VALUE IS `OPTIX` FROM 2026-10-03, on the Creator's ruling (`Rendering` L23).** This entry was written while it was `CUDA`; its sentences calling `CUDA` the choice, or saying why the choice does not move, are the record of the decision before that day. The measurements are unchanged, and `Rendering` L23 and its Section 9 item 3 read them now as the costs and gains of `OPTIX`.

**Named by:** `Preparation/Architecture/Rendering.md` L23 and Section 9 item 3, on 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the runner the same day.**

**Why it is not already covered.** Fact 3 establishes which technology names the build accepts and that both render at exit 0; the ready-action findings establish that one provider's own driver route omits the OptiX library, so `OPTIX` fails with the found-no-device line **while `CUDA` works**. That is why L23's NVIDIA table row names `CUDA` — it is the value that does not refuse a machine which can serve the cards. *(This said *L23's one filled table row* until 2026-08-22; that table has more than one filled row.)* **This entry said *nothing in this project's evidence measures what the choice costs* until 2026-08-21, and that was false — the fact was numbered over an answer the project already held.** `Preparation/Blender Research/Findings/5.1/What a render demands — second pass.md`, hand test 28, on a rented Linux machine with one NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4080 SUPER, driver 580.173.02, Ubuntu 22.04.5, Blender 5.1.2, the same 640×480 file at 1024 samples and the same settings folder, run back to back: `OPTIX` at 4.14 s on Blender's own `Time:` line and 5.82 s wall; **`CUDA` at 3.62 s and 4.45 s wall — the faster of the two.** **The direction is the opposite of what the choice is usually assumed to cost**, and the magnitude is nothing like a doubling either way.

**It does not settle the general question and is not offered as settling it**, in the findings file's own words: the machine was *"shared with another topic's tests"* while these ran — a second Blender rendering on the same card — so it is one busy machine, one card of 16 GB, one small scene, one day, and a scene small enough that kernel-build time is a large share of it. **The two are still not equivalent work on the hardware.**

**And the choice is not only about speed, which nothing had recorded.** The same block compared the pictures pixel by pixel: `CUDA` matched the card reference to 2.9e-06 while **`OPTIX` matched neither reference** — 0.0618 against the processor and 0.0532 against the CUDA card. *"Three devices, three pictures."* So a change of value is a change to what the user gets back, not only to how long they wait.

**RE-POSED, 2026-08-21, on what is actually missing.** **Answered by:** the same back-to-back comparison **on a scene big enough that kernel-build time is not a large share of it, on an uncontended machine**, with a working OptiX library present, comparing the times. **A negative result is `CUDA` being materially slower there**, which makes the safe default a bill the user pays.
**Machine:** an ordinary NVIDIA rental whose driver route carries the OptiX library, which the omitting provider's does not. Rides free on any batch with such a card. **It wants a scene rather than a rental.**
**Routed alongside it, and not this fact's:** whether `OPTIX` beats `CUDA` in general, on RTX-class hardware and at scale, is a question about what Cycles does and goes to **a fresh blind Blender researcher** — who must be asked for the measurement fresh and must not be shown the pair above.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Breaks if negative:** a user on a machine that could have served `OPTIX` pays for the slower technology, silently, because L23 names one value per make. **What holds:** the mechanism entire — the table is one value per make and changing it is one edit, and a per-machine probe would be a different mechanism rather than a different value. **The safe direction is stated:** naming `OPTIX` refuses a machine that can render, and naming `CUDA` renders on every machine either would serve.
**Posable:** ~~now.~~ ~~**Not on either route. Struck 2026-09-02 with the ruling below.**~~ **The re-posed measurement was already taken. Only the threshold's portability is unposable. Corrected 2026-09-02, later the same day.**

~~**NOT ANSWERED. PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02, on both of its routes, and this is not a pending item.** The measurement wants an uncontended machine with a working OptiX library and a scene big enough that kernel-build time is not a large share of it — a rental, ruled out on **2026-08-31** — and the general question was routed to **a fresh blind Blender researcher**, which the same day's ruling closes.~~

**ANSWERED ON THE RE-POSED HALF, 2026-09-02, from a reading this project already held, and the answer runs against the direction the entry assumed.** *What a render demands 7* — this project's own blind Blender Research, joined across all four supported versions — was **run under exactly the conditions the re-posing named**: the one rented Linux machine this project ever **checked** to be uncontended, one NVIDIA card, three repeats of every measurement, scenes at 322, 10,485,762 and 125,829,122 triangles. **Nobody had read it against this fact until the `Rendering` implementer did on 2026-09-02**, and `Rendering` L23 and [Section 9](Rendering.md#9-unresolved) item 3 now carry it.

- **At 322 triangles the two are within a tenth of a second of each other, `CUDA` marginally ahead** — both under three quarters of a second, against about six seconds on the processor.
- **At 10,485,762 triangles `OPTIX` is about four times faster than `CUDA`** — around one and a half to two seconds against six to eight and a half; three to five times on a curve-heavy scene, the same way round.
- **At 125,829,122 triangles the choice decides whether there is a render at all. Every `OPTIX` run finished and wrote its frame, in all four versions; every `CUDA` run was killed by the system part-way through building its structure, in all four versions, with no frame written.**

**So the direction turns on the size of the scene and changes sign, and the negative this entry named fired harder than it was worded.** *A negative result is `CUDA` being materially slower there, which makes the safe default a bill the user pays* — above some scene size it is not a bill at all: **`CUDA` costs the render**. Label: **observed in a test**, all four versions, three repeats, one machine.

**NOT ANSWERED, AND PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02, on what is left: where that threshold sits on any other machine.** The one measurement of it met the **host machine's** memory ceiling and not the **card's** — `OPTIX` never used more than about eight of the card's ten gigabytes and `CUDA` died well below that — so the size at which `CUDA` dies is a property of the box as much as of the scene, and no number here may be carried to another machine. **Bounding it wants a rental and the Creator ruled on 2026-08-31 that there will be no further rental to answer a question. It is not a pending item, and nothing may be written as though a threshold were coming.** The general *does `OPTIX` beat `CUDA` at scale* question was routed to a fresh blind Blender researcher, and the same day's ruling closes that route too.

**Breaks, restated on the answer and larger in kind than before:** a user whose scene is heavy enough meets a failed attempt with nothing written, on a machine `OPTIX` would have rendered on, because L23 names one value per make and nothing adapts. **What holds, and it is why the choice does not move:** `OPTIX` was observed refusing outright on a provider's own image whose driver route omits the OptiX library — a refusal on **every** scene rather than on a heavy one, so the other value fails renders this one completes; the failure is loud and total either way, a non-zero status with no frame, never a paid render that is wrong and says nothing; the pixel comparison makes the value a promise about what the user gets back — *"Three devices, three pictures"* — so a per-machine probe would change the product rather than a number; and the mechanism is one table value changed by one edit. **The silence is removed by the failure's own shape rather than by anything new:** a killed `CUDA` render writes no frame and returns a non-zero status, which `Rendering` K14 classifies *failed* with the machine's own reason recorded verbatim. **`Rendering` checked this against the third escalation on 2026-09-02 and declined it**, on the ground that naming `OPTIX` moves the cost onto a larger set of machines rather than removing it.

**The earlier measurement this entry carries is not superseded and must still never be read as settling anything** — hand test 28's `CUDA`-ahead figures are one busy machine, one 16 GB card, one small scene, one day, and they agree with the new reading only at the smallest of its three sizes.

### Fact 108 — Whether a rental's own elevating command carries the validate form, and whether it behaves there as its manual documents.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md`, 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the architecture runner** the same day: it is the one thing the separable authentication result rests on, `Security and licence` §14 item 15 says outright that no fact in the register covers it, and an unnumbered fact is invisible to the count.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from a document being written.
**Answered by:** a rented machine, plus that machine's own `sudo` manual for the version it ships.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, posed **under the login Smoozy runs as** rather than as root, on both machine kinds. Rides on any later batch; justifies none of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Breaks if negative:** the whole-app rule that a password that stops working is forgotten rather than retried — with no separable result, a password that has genuinely stopped working is kept and the user meets a failure that repeats until they clear it themselves. **What holds:** the invariant `Security and licence` §4 already states — a failed operation is reported as a failed operation and forgets nothing, so a good password is never discarded on a failure that was not an authentication failure.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED ON THE CALLER'S END ONLY, 2026-08-19.** `sudo(8)` at **Sudo 1.9.17p2**, manual dated June 7 2025, read on the Creator's Mac: the validate form *"does not run a command"*, the stdin form reads the password from standard input, and the exit value alone conflates an authentication failure with a command that could not be executed — which is why the separation is by the validate-first order and never by the number. The citation is in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Server Setup.sources.md`.

~~**Still owed, and it is the whole fact:** **the form has never been run on any rental.** No rental's `sudo` version or policy has been read, and whether a rental's policy supports cached credentials at all is unread. That is the gap `Server Setup` Section 13 carries.~~

**RUN ON A RENTAL, 2026-08-22, and the gap is closed.** The multi-provider batch's machine A, **under a non-root login made for the purpose** — because the delivered login is already root and no elevation question can be asked of it. `sudo` **1.9.15p5**, sudoers as Ubuntu 24.04 ships it (`Defaults env_reset, mail_badpass, secure_path, use_pty`; `%sudo ALL=(ALL:ALL) ALL`). Evidence in [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20—%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md).

- **The validate form exists and behaves as the manual documents.** With the right password over standard input: the prompt, then **exit 0**, and no command run.
- **The three refusals are distinguishable by their own words**, which is what the separable result needed. **Wrong password:** `Sorry, try again.` … `sudo: no password was provided` … **`sudo: 1 incorrect password attempt`**, exit 1. **No password offered:** **`sudo: a password is required`**, exit 1. **Not an administrator:** **`Sorry, user smoozynoadmin may not run sudo on a0e7a496ff47.`**, exit 1.
- **A negative would have been a refusal indistinguishable from a wrong password.** It was not.
- **Cached credentials are supported and the window is 15 minutes** — `sudo -V`: *Authentication timestamp timeout: 15.0 minutes* — and `sudo -n id -u` returned `0` after a successful validate, so one authentication does carry across later commands inside it.
- **One caution that must reach whoever matches the text:** the not-an-administrator message **names the host**, so it is per-machine and cannot be compared as a constant.

**Still not posed:** the same questions on the virtual-machine kind under a non-root login. Both virtual machines in that batch delivered a login with **passwordless** sudo, which cannot pose a password refusal either.

### Fact 109 — Whether the package-system repair sequence the ready action's library step falls back on is itself safe to repeat.

**Exposed by** `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md`, 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the architecture runner** the same day: Row 114's *running a step again is always safe* is kept step by step, and this is the one step whose own re-run rule rests on a sequence observed once.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from a document being written.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** a rental **nobody needs afterwards**, whose package system has been deliberately poisoned the way Fact 48's driver install poisoned one — the bind-mount failure leaving `dpkg` in a broken state. Run it last on a box being given up, as Fact 50 was.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Breaks if negative:** the libraries step's re-run rule, and with it one branch of Row 114 — a recovery run resuming at the `tooling` or `libraries` boundary could leave the package system worse than it found it. **What holds:** the other steps' re-run rules, each of which rests on its own observation, and the admission refusal and take-over recovery, which are about the state rather than about any step.
**Posable:** now.

**NOT ANSWERED.** Observed once, on one container, on one failure shape — that a driver install fails on the bind mounts and poisons the package system afterwards (Fact 48). Whether the repair sequence is safe to run twice was never posed.

**PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** It wants a rental deliberately poisoned and then given up, and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental to answer a question.

**Breaks, unchanged:** the libraries step's re-run rule and one branch of Row 114 — a recovery run resuming at the `tooling` or `libraries` boundary could leave the package system worse than it found it. **What holds:** every other step's re-run rule, each on its own observation, and the admission refusal and take-over recovery, which are about the state rather than about any step. **The silence to remove is `Server Setup`'s and not this register's, and it is already removed:** §13 item 5 records that the repair operation has **never been run by this project on any machine** and is taken from dpkg's own manual and from the instruction apt itself prints. **That label must not be quietly upgraded by a builder who runs it once and it works.**

### Fact 110 — What a removal performed by the machine's own software manager does to a render already running from that build.

**Exposed by** Round 1 of `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md`, 2026-08-19. **Numbered by the architecture runner** the same day: B14's *a job already rendering will finish* is the sentence Smoozy reads to the user deciding, and the observation behind it is a **folder deletion** rather than a manager-driven removal.

**Named by:** no sweep. It comes from an audit round.

**Split, and half of it is answered. *(Corrected 2026-08-31. This entry had gone on describing the whole manager route as owed to a rented machine, and `Server Setup` §13 item 17 had said otherwise since 2026-08-20 — the register was the last copy still reading the old way.)*** The question is two questions and they have different answers.

**The package-manager half is answered, by the standard, and wanted no machine at all.** A package removal unlinks, and POSIX settles what an unlink does to a process holding the file: the link goes at once and the file's contents survive until the last reference to them does (`unlink()`, The Open Group Base Specifications Issue 8, read 2026-08-20; the address is at `Server Setup` §13 item 17). That is the same rule behind B14's folder-deletion observation — permitted at once, the render carrying on to a normal end, all four versions, on Linux and on a Mac. Label: **documented**.

**The snap half is open**, because a snap build is a read-only squashfs mount rather than ordinary files and removing it must unmount it, and nothing this project has read or run covers what that does to a render in flight. **No answer is guessed at here.**

**Answered by:** ~~a rented machine.~~ **Nothing further will answer it.** What the snap half wants is one Ubuntu machine with snapd — any hardware, no card and **no rental** — to install the Blender snap, start a long render from it, issue the removal, and record whether the removal is refused and whether the render survives. **This project does not have such a machine**, and the Creator ruled on 2026-08-31 that there will be no further rental to answer a question. **So the snap half is permanently unresolved rather than pending**, and it is written that way here rather than left reading as though a batch were coming.

**Machine:** ~~a rental with a card … it rides on any later batch.~~ **Struck 2026-08-31 with the ruling above.** The half that needed a machine needs a free one this project does not hold; the half that seemed to need one never did.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Breaks if negative:** ~~the manager half of B14's removal warning~~ **the snap half of it** — the user is told a job in flight will finish and it does not, which is the one place this module speaks to somebody making a decision. **What holds:** the folder-deletion route, observed on four versions on Linux and on a Mac; the package-manager route, now settled by the standard; the warning's other half; and B14 itself, which labels the snap route as not established rather than asserting it, and whose predicate reaches both routes so no removal happens quietly.
**Posable:** ~~now.~~ **Not by anything this stage or Build will have.**

**THE PACKAGE-MANAGER HALF IS ANSWERED BY THE STANDARD, 2026-08-20; THE SNAP HALF IS PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-08-31.** The snap route was never taken on a running render and nothing will take it. `Server Setup` §13 item 17 carries it on that document's side. *(This line read a bare `**NOT ANSWERED.** The route was never taken on a running render.` until 2026-09-02, contradicting the body above it — the exact fault this register caught at Facts 131 and 57.)*

### Fact 111 — Whether the caller-side keepalive cadence `Servers` chose produces a false break on a real link to a rented machine while a transfer runs at the negotiated maximum write.

**Named by:** exposed by `Preparation/Architecture/Servers.md` and numbered by the runner on 2026-08-19. It waits on no sweep. `State, identity and recovery` §8.5 fixes the **mechanism class** — a break reported without the caller having to use the connection first — and that is a requirement rather than a choice; what is unobserved is the pair of numbers `Servers` K3 chose against it, a 15-second interval and a count of 3.
**Why it is not covered by Fact 57.** That fact's 56-second detection was measured with keepalives on and **the interval and count behind that figure are not in the record**, so it establishes that the route works and not that this cadence is safe.
**Answered by:** a rented machine. **Machine:** any ordinary Linux rental with a transfer running at the link's negotiated maximum write; it rides free on any batch that has one and justifies no rental of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now, on any later batch.

**What a negative result would look like:** a break declared on a healthy connection under load, which would tear down a live connection and start a repair against a machine that never went away. **What holds without it:** the mechanism class, which is §8.5's and not this figure's; the announced-ending route, which needs no keepalive at all and covers both ways a machine goes away; and that the two figures are named constants changed by one edit. `Servers` Section 16 item 11 carries it.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-22, and no false break occurred.** Run on the multi-provider batch's machine G, a Vast container in Norway, with the connection carrying **the 15-second interval `Servers` K3 chose** while a **419 430 400-byte** file moved at the link's full speed in both directions — **680.52 s up, 108.07 s down, and a second full download straight after at 97.28 s**, about eleven minutes of continuous saturated transfer. A watcher checked once a second whether the transport had been declared dead while bytes were still moving.

- **`connection still active after all of it: True`.**
- **`false breaks reported while bytes were moving: NONE`.**
- The round-trip digest matched the source exactly, so the transfer was real rather than stalled.

**The cadence stands observed rather than assumed.** **Not posed:** the same cadence against a link that genuinely degrades mid-transfer rather than one that stays healthy — this run had no loss to meet. Evidence in [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20—%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md).

### Fact 112 — Whether a rented machine at a low session cap permits a second connection to the same machine under the same login at all.

**Named by:** exposed by `Preparation/Architecture/Servers.md` and numbered by the runner on 2026-08-19. `Servers` 1.4 gives a connected period **one additional connection, transfer channels only**, opened at the first refused channel admission — which is the route `Server protocol and transfers` T2 and its §12 item 5 hand to that document. **The whole route depends on a second *connection* being permitted where *sessions* are capped, and that is a different setting from `MaxSessions`.**
**Answered by:** a rented machine. **Machine:** rides with Fact 93 — what a rented machine's own file-transfer server offers and its session cap — and justifies no rental of its own.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now, on any later batch that poses Fact 93.

**ANSWERED, and it was answered before it was numbered — 2026-08-17, by Fact 93's own rental, three days before this entry was written as open. Closed 2026-08-21.** **Documented:** `sshd_config(5)`, read 2026-08-20, defines `MaxSessions` as *"the maximum number of open shell, login or subsystem (e.g. sftp) sessions permitted **per network connection**"* and says nothing whatever about how many connections a login may open. The two settings that do bound connections bound something else and say so — `MaxStartups` is *"the maximum number of concurrent **unauthenticated** connections to the SSH daemon"* and `PerSourceMaxStartups` is *"the number of **unauthenticated** connections allowed from a given source address"*. **Neither caps established connections.** **Observed**, on a real rental under the login Smoozy holds, posed with a control rather than trusted: ten of fourteen concurrent sessions on one connection succeeded with the first ten as the control, and **a second connection gets its own ten** (Fact 93). **So the route `Servers` 1.4 rests on is not in doubt**, and this fact was numbered over an answer the same batch had already returned. **What a negative result would have looked like:** a machine at a low session cap on which transfers cannot run at all, with no route left, which is the outcome the additional connection exists to prevent. It did not occur. **What is still not observed is a machine at a low cap at all** — every machine on record is at the documented default of 10 — so what is answered is that the *mechanism* is sound, not that the case has been met.

**RE-POSED ON THREE BOXES, 2026-08-22, and still no machine at a low cap.** The multi-provider batch found **the documented default of 10 sessions per connection on all three** of its group-1 machines — a Vast container, a Hyperstack virtual machine and a Sesterce virtual machine, across OpenSSH 9.6p1, 9.2p1 and 8.9p1 — and **six parallel connections opened on each with no refusal**. **So the mechanism is sound on three more machines and the case the additional connection exists for has still never been met**: every machine this project has seen sits at the default.

### Fact 113 — Whether a read of a `.blend` and the render still agree about a view-layer switch for driver and stored-script shapes beyond the two `Render Settings` posed.

**Named by:** exposed by `Preparation/Architecture/Render Settings.md` and numbered by the runner on 2026-08-19. **This stage owns the question by an explicit ruling** — [Open Threads](../Groundwork/Open%20Threads.md) sends *whether one read of a file answers for a view layer switched by a driver or by a script* to Architecture rather than back to Blender Research, to be *"answered where it is needed, or … unresolved and the design says so"*. **It is answered on the shapes posed**: a scripted-expression driver and one stored-script handler shape, in 4.5.12 and 5.2.0, read and render agreeing frame for frame, under both states of the stored-script preference. **What is left is the residue** — a Python-function driver, which 5.x documentation says needs the automatic-running permission, and stored-script shapes other than the one posed.
**Answered by:** a rented machine, or any computer carrying two supported Blenders and a fixture of each shape.
**Machine:** no card needed; the answers already taken were on a four-core container at 64×64.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now, on any later batch, and cheaply.

**What a negative result would look like:** a shape where the read's stepping and the render diverge, which makes the view-layer boxes and `Queue & History`'s plan total wrong for that file, silently. **What holds without it:** the keyframed case, established in all four versions by the research; the two posed shapes, observed twice over; and the plan total's *a plan rather than a promise* shape, which `Queue & History` B1 is already built on. `Render Settings` Section 13 item 7 carries it.

**ANSWERED ON THE SHAPES POSED, 2026-08-19; THE RESIDUE IS PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02.** The residue — a Python-function driver, and stored-script shapes other than the one posed — is a question about what Blender does, and the ruling of **2026-08-31** closes both a rental and a research round. **The entry's *or any computer carrying two supported Blenders* route is not an exception:** wherever it runs it is a Blender behaviour test, and this stage will not run one. **This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.**

**Breaks:** a shape where the read's stepping and the render diverge makes the view-layer boxes and `Queue & History`'s plan total wrong for that file, silently. **What holds:** the keyframed case, established in all four versions by the research; the two posed shapes, observed twice over; and the plan total's *a plan rather than a promise* shape, which `Queue & History` B1 is already built on — **which is the silence removed**, because a total offered as a plan is one the user was never told was a guarantee.

**Why this status line matters, recorded rather than glossed.** With none, this entry read as *no answer at all* to a reader deriving the register entry by entry and as *answered in part* to `What Build inherits`, and it was counted in both of those mutually exclusive lists at once. **The line above settles which it is: answered in part.**

### Fact 114 — Whether a rented machine's saved Blender preferences can pull a scene set to the processor onto the cards when no technology is named at launch.

**Named by:** exposed by `Preparation/Architecture/Render Settings.md` and numbered by the runner on 2026-08-19. The research establishes the redirection in the other direction only — a scene asking for the cards on a machine left as it comes renders on the processor with Blender saying nothing at all (*The machine 5*, *Settings 9*). **Row 158's warning promises no outcome by the spec's own bound, so this narrows what the page can honestly say rather than breaking it.**
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** a rental with a working card and a Blender, with the machine's saved preferences set to the cards and a scene set to the processor, run with nothing named at launch. Rides free on any batch that already has a card.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**What a negative result would look like:** a user's processor choice costing them a cards render, unannounced. **What holds without it:** the override case is already mitigated, since a typed processor choice is delivered as a launch-time pair rather than left to the preferences; what is unmitigated is the file's-own case, where Smoozy imposes nothing by design. `Render Settings` Section 13 item 11 carries it.

**PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02, and this entry carried no status line of any kind until that date.** It rides on a rental with a card and a Blender on it; **it never had a batch of its own and there is now no batch**, the Creator having ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental to answer a question. **This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.**

**Breaks:** a user's processor choice costing them a cards render, unannounced. **What holds:** the override case is already mitigated, since a typed processor choice is delivered as a launch-time pair rather than left to the preferences; **what is unmitigated is the file's-own case**, where Smoozy imposes nothing by design, and Row 158's warning promises no outcome by the spec's own bound. **The silence is not fully removable and that is stated rather than papered over:** nothing Smoozy reads after a render distinguishes a scene that rendered on the cards because the file asked from one that rendered on the cards because a stored preference overrode it. `Render Settings` §13 item 11 is where that limit is written.

### Fact 115 — Whether `ssh-keygen` honours the askpass route when it asks for a **new** key's passphrase at generation, on each supported system.

**Named by:** exposed by `Preparation/Architecture/Keys.md` and numbered by the runner on 2026-08-20. **The question exists because a closed document closed the documented route**: `Security and licence` §3.0 rule 2 and §13 forbid a secret as a process argument, and §4 states that `ssh-keygen`'s `-P` is used for nothing but a local probe with an empty passphrase — so `-N` cannot carry a real passphrase and Row 248's *protected at creation* has no other named route.
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own manuals first, then a throwaway on a computer of each supported kind. **No rental of any kind** — Keys never talks to a server.
**Machine:** a computer running each supported system. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** Mac done; Linux wants a desktop computer this project does not have, and Windows wants a rental nobody has taken for it.

**THE MAC HALF ANSWERED, 2026-08-20, and the manual answers nothing.** Observed on the Creator's own Mac, OpenSSH 10.2p1, by the writing agent's own throwaway: a generation run with no terminal, `SSH_ASKPASS` naming a helper and `SSH_ASKPASS_REQUIRE=force` **invoked the helper exactly twice** — the enter and the confirm — exited 0, and wrote the pair, with the key verifiably protected afterwards (`-P ''` probe at exit 255 `incorrect passphrase supplied to decrypt private key`; an askpass-fed load at exit 0 printing a public half byte-identical to the `.pub`). **`ssh-keygen(1)` documents no askpass behaviour at all** — its ENVIRONMENT section names only `SSH_SK_PROVIDER` — so this is observed behaviour on one build and not a documented contract. Evidence in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Keys.sources.md`.

**Still owed: Windows and Linux**, neither of which has a computer behind it on this project. **What that costs is bounded and is stated where it is used**: `Keys` G4 verifies the protection reading against what was asked before any row is written, so a system that ignores the route produces a **failed generation said plainly** rather than a key quietly weaker than the user asked for. `Keys` Section 13 item 3 carries it.

**THE WINDOWS AND LINUX HALVES ARE PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** The reading route is spent — **`ssh-keygen(1)`'s ENVIRONMENT names only `SSH_SK_PROVIDER`, so the manual answers nothing** — the Linux half wants a desktop computer this project does not have, and the Windows half wants a rental the Creator ruled out on **2026-08-31**.

**Breaks:** Row 248's *protected at creation* on two of the three systems, and nothing else — the Mac half is observed. **What holds:** the Mac observation, and the whole mechanism, which never depends on the route succeeding. **The silence is already removed and `Keys` §13 item 3 says so:** G4 verifies the protection reading against what was asked before any row is written, so a system that ignores the route gives **a failed generation said plainly** rather than a quietly weaker key.

### Fact 116 — Whether each of the four added write places can be given a location from outside the file where the artist never gave it one, and whether the render then honours it, in each supported version.

**Named by:** exposed by Row 313 at the stage-close re-sweep and numbered by the runner on 2026-08-21. **The question exists because the one test that established the four as findable at all posed it on the wrong file**: the fixture behind *Reading a file 9* was built *"with every one written out in full from the drive root"*, so every one of the four had been given an explicit path by hand. **The ordinary artist's file gives none of them a path**, and *Dependencies 10* documents — identically in all four versions — that a physics cache with no location given lands in a folder named for the file **beside the `.blend`**, and that a geometry-node bake may be packed into the file instead of written to disk.

**The four:** the bake output path, the physics point cache, the geometry-nodes bake folder, and the sequencer proxy folder. **Two halves per place, and both are needed:** does an assignment from outside take where nothing was recorded, and does the render write where the assignment says.

**Answered by:** one rental with Blender on it, in 4.5 and one 5.x, on a file where none of the four carries a path — the same shape Facts 5 and 77 used, and it rides with Facts 99 and 100's rental rather than justifying one of its own. **A test that cannot fail is not a test:** the negative result is a render that finishes, reports success, and leaves the cache beside the `.blend` rather than under the job's folder, which is exactly D22's observed silent case and is what the run must be able to show.

**Machine:** a rental with Blender on it. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** yes, and cheaply, on a rental already up for Facts 99 and 100.

**What rests on it:** `Job files and outputs` D21b to D21d, and through them Rows 313 and 118. **What holds without it:** the promise `What a job writes` makes is *not left on a machine that is about to be destroyed*, kept exactly when the redirect reaches — so a negative here is a promise broken silently rather than a promise weakened, which is why the row stays waiting rather than closing unresolved.

**ANSWERED FOR THE PHYSICS POINT CACHE, 2026-08-22, in both versions — and the first answer was the negative this entry was written to be able to show.** Run on the multi-provider batch's machine A in **4.5.12** and **5.2.0**, on a file where the artist gave none of the four a path. Evidence in [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20—%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md).

**As the artist left them:** point cache `use_external=False`, `use_disk_cache=False`, `filepath=''`, `name=''`; geometry-nodes `bake_target='PACKED'`, `bake_directory=''`; sequencer `proxy_storage='PER_STRIP'`, `proxy_dir=''`; bake output `'//'`.

- **The negative, reproduced deliberately.** Setting `point_cache.filepath` to the job folder and `use_disk_cache=True` — the obvious assignment — **was read back exactly as assigned**, the bake ran, **the render finished and reported success**, and the cache was written to **`blendcache_artist/` beside the `.blend`**: 250 files, nothing under the job folder but the frames. **That is D22's silent case, and the mechanism is worse than a refusal — the value is stored, returned on read, and ignored at write time.**
- **What actually takes is four values together — corrected 2026-08-22; this said *three* and then listed four, and four is right**, and with them both versions wrote the cache under the job folder and left nothing beside the `.blend`: `use_disk_cache = True`, **`use_external = True`** — without which `filepath` is ignored — `filepath = <job folder>`, and a **non-empty `name`**, which is the stem of every cache file. The directory must exist first.
- **NOT ESTABLISHED for the other three, and the attempts are recorded rather than the conclusions.** The **geometry-nodes bake folder**: `bake_target='DISK'` and `bake_directory` both read back as assigned and the bake operator returned without raising, but **no bake folder appeared anywhere** — the fixture's simulation zone passed geometry through unchanged and appears to have produced nothing to bake, so the assignment was never tested against a real bake. The **sequencer proxy folder**: both values read back, but the fixture has no movie strip so **no proxy was ever built**. The **bake output path**: assigned and read back, but **no image bake was run**.

**So one of this fact's four places is answered.** *(This read *So this fact is one of four answered* until 2026-09-02, which mis-scopes what Row 313 waits on if read literally — the four are the write places named in this entry, not four facts.)* The general hazard the one answer exposes — **a location stored, returned on read, and ignored at write time** — is what the other three must be tested against rather than assumed past. **Row 313 does not close on this**; what it gains is the point cache's exact four-value condition and a demonstrated failure mode. *(This sentence said *three-value* until 2026-08-23, contradicting the bullet above it, which was itself corrected from three to four on 2026-08-22. The four are `use_disk_cache`, `use_external`, `filepath` and a non-empty `name`.)*

**This is not a Blender Research question and must not be sent as one.** It asks what Smoozy's own construction does to a file it wrote the launch material for, on a machine it rented; the general behaviour it leans on is already answered in *Dependencies 10* and *Reading a file 9*, and is cited above rather than re-asked.

**CLOSED 2026-08-31 AS ONE THAT WILL NOT BE COMPLETED, and Row 313 closed with it.** The three unanswered halves — the geometry-nodes bake folder, the sequencer proxy folder and the bake output path — need a rental with Blender on it, **and the Creator has ruled that there will be no further rental and no further Blender research.** So this entry does not sit waiting; it is answered for the physics point cache and permanently unanswered for the other three.

**What was written instead, because a fact that will not arrive is not a reason to leave a failure silent.** `Job files and outputs` gains **D21f**: at the ending walk, an added write place that was assigned and produced nothing under the job's folder is **disclosed on the completeness statement** rather than passed over. **D21g** states what that does not claim — nothing anywhere asserts a cache *was* written, so a place that produced nothing is not by itself a fault and the statement says what was observed rather than declaring a failure. **D21b-ii** promotes this entry's own sharpest finding into a standing rule over all four places: **a location may be stored, returned on read, and ignored at write time**, so no clause may treat a read-back as evidence that the redirect took. The residue is `Job files and outputs` **§14 item 22**, written as closed-unresolved-permanently rather than pending.

**So what rests on it changes.** It now rests on D21b–D21e's naming construction, **D21f's disclosure** and §14 item 22 — and Row 313, which closes **unresolved with the promise standing** rather than waiting. **What still breaks for the three:** a job's bake, proxy or image bake may land beside the `.blend` in the asset area, be destroyed with the machine, and the render report success — **but no longer silently**, which is the whole of what D21f buys.

### Fact 117 — How the application learns which of light or dark the operating system is set to, at start and again when the user changes it while Smoozy is running, on each of the three systems and in each of the three system web views.

**THE SHELL CHANGED UNDER THIS ENTRY, 2026-09-02. What is read below is Wails v2's facility, and the selected shell is Tauri v2** — `Desktop app and delivery` H5b was filled by Build and Tauri came out materially smaller. **The entry stands as a reading of Wails and is not struck**, because a dated reading is not falsified by a later choice. **What actually carries this fact is the page rather than the shell** — `Desktop app and delivery` H17's `prefers-color-scheme` reading and H19's declaration are the application's own, on whichever shell hosts them — and **the shell half is answered on the selected shell at H6b**, whose window theme *"Defaults to the system theme. Only implemented on Windows and macOS 10.14+"* is the same two-of-three shape read here. **Nothing is owed.**

**Named by:** Row 321 at the stage-close Design sweep, 2026-08-20 — *"how each of Mac, Windows and Linux exposes its light-or-dark setting to an application, whether a change made while the application is running is delivered to it, and whether the system web view the shell puts under Smoozy carries either."* Numbered by the runner on 2026-08-21. **The register held no entry for it**, and outside `Preparation/Design/` the word *dark* did not occur anywhere in the project.

**Answered by:** reading the standard's, the three engine projects' and the shell's own publications. **No rental** — Smoozy does not run on the rented machine — and **no Windows or Linux desktop computer ran it**, which by `Desktop app and delivery` Y3 is the standing limit; a Windows machine can be rented and none was rented for this.

**Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no; it decides one visible behaviour and no arrangement. **Posable:** yes, as documentation.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-21, and the answer is one reading rather than three.** The reading is the page's own `prefers-color-scheme` media query, which every one of the three system web views carries, and the later change arrives as an event on it rather than by anything polling.

- **The standard, and it is narrower than the promise.** W3C, *Media Queries Level 5*, Working Draft 19 February 2026, `https://drafts.csswg.org/mediaqueries-5/#prefers-color-scheme`, read 2026-08-21: *"The prefers-color-scheme media feature reflects the user's desire that the page use a light or dark color scheme."* Values `light | dark`. And: *"The method by which the user expresses their preference can vary. It might be a system-wide setting exposed by the Operating System, or a setting controlled by the user agent."* **So the standard does not promise the value is the operating system's setting.** That each engine takes it from the system is the per-engine evidence below, not the standard's.
- **The change while running.** W3C, *CSSOM View Module*, `https://drafts.csswg.org/cssom-view/#evaluate-media-queries-and-report-changes`, read 2026-08-21: *"When asked to evaluate media queries and report changes for a Document doc, run these steps: For each MediaQueryList object target that has doc as its document, in the order they were created, oldest first, run these substeps: If target's matches state has changed since the last time these steps were run, fire an event named change at target using MediaQueryListEvent"*. **It is an event, not a schedule** — which is why the answer costs `Desktop app and delivery` P10 nothing.
- **macOS.** The WebKit Project, *Dark Mode Support in WebKit*, 7 May 2019, `https://webkit.org/blog/8840/dark-mode-support-in-webkit/`, read 2026-08-21: the engine matches *"the current system appearance"*, and of the media query — *"auto switching with any appearance change"*, usable *"anywhere media queries are supported, such as in `<picture>` elements or `window.matchMedia()` for script triggers."*
- **Windows.** Microsoft, *CoreWebView2Profile.PreferredColorScheme Property*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/dotnet/api/microsoft.web.webview2.core.corewebview2profile.preferredcolorscheme`, page updated 2026-08-04, read 2026-08-21: *"This sets the color scheme for WebView2 UI like dialogs, prompts, and menus by setting the media feature `prefers-color-scheme`. The default value for this is `CoreWebView2PreferredColorScheme.Auto`, which will follow whatever color scheme the OS is currently set to."*
- **Linux, and this is the one that is answered with a limit.** The WebKitGTK Project's own release announcement, *WebKitGTK 2.25.1 released!*, 27 May 2019, `https://webkitgtk.org/2019/05/27/webkitgtk2.25.1-released.html`, read 2026-08-21: *"Improve rendering of form controls when GTK theme is dark and enable prefers-color-scheme media query."* Underneath it, the GNOME project's own GTK 4 reference, `https://docs.gtk.org/gtk4/property.Settings.gtk-interface-color-scheme.html`, read 2026-08-21: *"The color scheme used for rendering the user interface. This setting communicates the system-wide preference."* — **default value `GTK_INTERFACE_COLOR_SCHEME_UNSUPPORTED`**, which the same project's enumeration page glosses *"The system doesn't support color schemes."* **So on Linux a system may have no such setting at all**, and there is nothing to read.

**And the window frame is not the web view, so it is answered separately, out of the shell's own documentation.** Wails, `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/options.mdx`, read 2026-08-21 (wails.io returns 403 to a fetch; this is the site's own documentation source). Windows `Theme`, minimum Windows 10 2004/20H1, default `SystemDefault` — *"The theme will be based on the system default. If the user changes their theme, the application will update to use the new setting"*. macOS `Appearance`, default `DefaultAppearance` — *"DefaultAppearance uses the default system value"*. **And the Linux options are Icon, WindowIsTranslucent, WebviewGpuPolicy and ProgramName** — **no theme or appearance option of any kind**, so on Linux the frame is the window manager's and the shell offers nothing to set.

**What rests on it:** `Desktop app and delivery` 2.6 and Row 321. **What is still owed:** nothing that stops the row. Two limits are carried at `Desktop app and delivery` Section 22 — a Linux system whose colour scheme reads *unsupported*, and that none of this is observed anywhere, on any of the three, by anybody on this project.

### Fact 118 — Whether a declared colour scheme reaches the surfaces the operating system draws rather than the page — scrollbars, form controls and focus rings — inside each of the three system web views.

**Named by:** Row 327 at the stage-close Design sweep, 2026-08-20 — *"what each of the three system web views does with a declared colour scheme, and whether the declaration reaches the operating-system-drawn controls inside it"* — which `Preparation/Design/Sources/Design values.sources.md` had already handed to this stage in as many words: *"Anything about the window Smoozy will actually be built in. The mockup is a page in a browser, and whether the same rule holds in whatever Architecture chooses is that stage's to find out."* Numbered by the runner on 2026-08-21.

**Answered by:** reading the standard's and the engine projects' own publications. **No rental, no machine.**

**Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** yes, as documentation.

**ANSWERED FOR THE STANDARD AND FOR macOS, AND CARRIED WITH A LIMIT FOR LINUX, 2026-08-21.**

- **The standard says the declaration is exactly what does it.** W3C, *CSS Color Adjustment Module Level 1*, Candidate Recommendation Snapshot 16 December 2025, `https://drafts.csswg.org/css-color-adjust-1/#color-scheme-prop`, read 2026-08-21. The used colour scheme must affect *"the default colors of scrollbars and other interaction UI"*, *"the default colors of form controls and other 'specially-rendered' elements"*, *"the default colors of other browser-provided UI, such as 'spellcheck' underlines"*, *"the value of system colors"* and *"the value of the light-dark() function"*; on the root element it must additionally affect *"the surface color of the canvas"* and *"the viewport's scrollbars"*.
- **macOS, and it states the negative the Design observation is about.** The WebKit Project, *Dark Mode Support in WebKit*, cited above: *"Specifying the values light and dark on the root element lets the engine know both modes are supported by the document. This changes the default text and background colors of the page to match the current system appearance. Also standard form controls, scrollbars, and other named system colors change their look automatically. Without this declaration, it would not be safe for the engine to use dark form controls or a dark color scheme, since many documents are designed with an assumed light color scheme."*
- **Linux, and the engine's own words tie the controls to the desktop's theme rather than to the page's declaration.** WebKitGTK 2.25.1's announcement, cited above, pairs the two in one line — *"Improve rendering of form controls when GTK theme is dark and enable prefers-color-scheme media query"*. **Read plainly that is the GTK theme deciding how a form control is drawn**, which is not the same thing as the page's `color-scheme` deciding it.
- **Windows is claimed by no page read here.** The WebView2 property above is about *"WebView2 UI like dialogs, prompts, and menus"* and the media feature, and says nothing about the page's own `color-scheme` reaching a scrollbar.

**What rests on it:** `Desktop app and delivery` 2.6 and Row 327. **What is still owed, and it is cosmetic rather than a promise:** whether the declaration reaches operating-system-drawn controls on Windows and on Linux. **What a negative costs** is a light scrollbar or a light dropdown inside a dark window — the exact thing the Design observation says gives it away — and never a wrong reading, a lost file or a silent success. Carried at `Desktop app and delivery` Section 22.
