### Fact 119 — What each of the three platforms resolves the interface face and the fixed-width face to, whether the system web view resolves them at all, and whether figures line up down a column on all three.

**Named by:** Row 322 at the stage-close Design sweep, 2026-08-20 — *"what each of the three platforms names as its interface face and its fixed-width face, whether the system web view resolves those names, and whether the fixed-width face gives figures of equal width on all three."* Numbered by the runner on 2026-08-21. **The register held no entry for it**; the nearest was Fact 96, which is about download sizes.

**Answered by:** reading the standard's own publication. **No rental and no machine** — and the question turns out not to be a per-platform lookup at all.

**Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** yes, as documentation.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-21, and the question was posed one level too low.** The mockup does not name a platform's face; it names **generic families**, and the standard says what a generic family is and what it is guaranteed to do. W3C, *CSS Fonts Module Level 4*, `https://drafts.csswg.org/css-fonts-4/`, read 2026-08-21.

- **`system-ui`, which is the interface face.** *"This generic font family lets text render with the default user interface font (which, as with all Generic font families, may be a composite font) on the platform on which the UA is running. A cross-platform UA should use different fonts on its different supported platforms. The purpose of system-ui is to allow web content to integrate with the look and feel of the native OS."* **So it is the platform's own face by definition, and no per-platform name has to be known by anybody.**
- **`ui-monospace`, and it carries the one real caveat.** *"This font family is used for the monospaced variant of the system's user interface."* And: *"Note: ui-monospace is not expected to map to any font on platforms without an appropriate system font."*
- **The generics are three kinds and the difference is the whole of the answer.** *"Generics which apply to all Unicode characters and will always match a locally installed font. For example, monospace. Generics which apply to all Unicode characters but may not match to a locally installed font on some systems. For example, ui-rounded."* `system-ui` and `ui-monospace` are of the second kind; `sans-serif` and `monospace` are of the first. **The spec's own instruction follows:** *"Authors are encouraged to append a generic font family as a last alternative for improved robustness."*
- **The column promise is guaranteed by the standard rather than by any face.** *"The sole criterion of a monospace font is that all glyphs have the same fixed width."* And: *"monospace must always map to at least one matched font face."* **So on any conforming engine a run set in the `monospace` generic lines up down a column**, whatever the platform resolved it to, and the question *do figures come out equal width on all three* is answered without reading three platforms' pages.
- **A name an engine does not know is skipped rather than fatal.** `font-family`'s value is `[ <font-family-name> | <generic-font-family> ]#`, and an unquoted identifier an engine does not recognise as a generic keyword is a **family name** — one that matches nothing, so matching moves to the next entry. **The declaration is not invalidated**, which is what makes a stack ending in a first-kind generic safe on an engine that has never heard of `ui-monospace`.
- **`font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums` is an OpenType feature and not a guarantee.** *"Enables display of tabular numerals (OpenType feature: tnum)."* **It is not what the column promise rests on** — a monospaced face lines figures up by the definition above, with or without the feature — so a face lacking `tnum` costs nothing that matters here.

**What rests on it:** `Desktop app and delivery` 2.7 and Row 322, and through them `Keys` F3, which requires a fingerprint's display line in the fixed-width face and cites the Design document for it.

**What is still owed:** **which face each of the three platforms actually resolves the two second-kind generics to is unread and does not need to be**, and nothing here is observed on any engine. What a failure looks like is bounded and stated at `Desktop app and delivery` Section 22: the interface degrades to a platform sans-serif or a platform monospace that is not the system's own, which is a look rather than a broken promise, and **the column goes on lining up either way.**

### Fact 120 — Whether an application on each of Mac, Windows and Linux is handed the paths of files **and folders** dropped onto its window, and what is available about a drop before it is accepted.

**THE SHELL CHANGED UNDER THIS ENTRY, 2026-09-02, AND THIS ONE IS OWED A READING. What is read below is Wails v2's facility, and the selected shell is Tauri v2** — `Desktop app and delivery` H5b was filled by Build and Tauri came out materially smaller. **The entry stands as a reading of Wails and is not struck**, because a dated reading is not falsified by a later choice. **But this is a shell facility rather than a page one**, so the answer below does not carry across on its own. **What is owed is one reading of the selected shell's own publication on the same question**, and it wants no machine and no rental. **When:** at the step that consumes it, which is the file browser's, not before. **What must not be done:** assume the selected shell behaves as the one read here, in either direction.

**THE OWED READING WAS TAKEN, 2026-09-04, at the step that consumes it, and it carried half the answer across and not the other half.** `File Browser` H3a is re-based onto it and that document's sources entry **I2a** holds the two quotations with their addresses. **What carried:** what arrives is paths and nothing else. **What did not:** whether a folder may be dropped at all, which the selected shell's reference states in neither direction — `File Browser` §13 item 19. See *What rests on it* below, which is now nothing.

**Named by:** Row 341 at the stage-close Design sweep, 2026-08-20 — *"whether an application on each of Mac, Windows and Linux receives the paths of files and folders dropped onto it, whether a dropped folder arrives as a folder rather than as nothing, and what is available about the drop before it is accepted."* Numbered by the runner on 2026-08-21. **The register held no entry for it**, and the sweep noted its nearest numbered neighbours — Facts 22, 23, 24 and 73 — are all the same shape.

**Answered by:** reading the shell's own documentation. **No rental and no machine.** **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** yes, as documentation.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-21, and it is the shell's own facility rather than three platforms' separate ones.** Wails, read 2026-08-21 (wails.io returns 403 to a fetch; these are the site's own documentation sources).

- **The capability, and it is one option, off by default.** `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/options.mdx`: *"EnableFileDrop enables wails' drag and drop functionality that returns the dropped in files' absolute paths."* Default `false`. *"The event returns the coordinates of the drop and a file path slice."*
- **Folders are named by the project itself.** `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/runtime/draganddrop.mdx`: *"This part of the runtime handles dragging and dropping files and or folders in to the window."* And the handler's shape: *"Calls the callback function with the coordinates inside the window where the drag was released and a slice of absolute file paths."*
- **What arrives is paths and nothing else.** No sizes, no counts, no walk. **So nothing about what is *inside* a dropped folder is known at the drop**, and a count shown at that moment can only be a count of what was dropped.
- **The drop target is the shell's too.** `CSSDropProperty` (default `--wails-drop-target`) and `CSSDropValue` (default `drop`) mark an element as a valid target, and the runtime adds `wails-drop-target-active` to it while the pointer is over it — so the border state is a class the shell sets rather than something Smoozy computes.
- **`DisableWebViewDrop`** exists separately, *"to prevent accidental opening of dragged in files in the webview"* — a real hazard on a route built over a web view, and named here because a build that enables the first and not the second can open a dropped `.blend` in the interface.

**What rests on it: nothing, as of 2026-09-04, and the entry still stands.** The reading this entry is owed on the selected shell was taken at a Build sitting on **2026-09-04**, and everything that used to rest here was moved onto it or struck. **`File Browser` H3a was re-based** onto Tauri v2's own reference and now rests on that document's sources entry **I2a**; **H4's clause that *"dragging onto the list, which Design already carries, takes any mix everywhere"* was struck rather than re-sourced**, the chosen shell's reference speaking of files throughout and stating nothing about folders in either direction; and **Row 341's head-table line no longer reads *on Fact 120***. The whole account is `File Browser` **H3a**, its **§13 item 19**, and **`Preparation/Architecture/Sources/File Browser.sources.md` entry I2a**; the same file's **I2** keeps this entry's own citation and is marked superseded. **This entry is not withdrawn and must not be** — it is a true reading of Wails' own documentation on the date it was taken, and a dated reading is not falsified by a later choice. What changed is only what leans on it. *(This line read *`File Browser` H3a and Row 341 … and through them H4's existing clause* until 2026-09-04, which was true until the shell changed. It was corrected 2026-08-22 before that, having cited an H4a that does not exist.)*

**What this entry settles for the selected shell, and what it does not.** The question in this entry's own heading is answered for Wails and **not** for the selected shell: the reading of 2026-09-04 settles the payload — the drop event and its `paths` — and **does not settle whether a folder may be dropped at all**, which `File Browser` §13 item 19 holds with what it breaks. **Whether the facility differs by platform is `Desktop app and delivery` [Section 22](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#22-unresolved) item 38's**, re-written in the selected shell's terms the same day.

**What is still owed, and it is one sentence rather than a gap.** **The pages state no per-platform difference and this project has observed none**, on any of the three. Wails' own version notice applies (`Desktop app and delivery` H10): nothing here rests on a facility announced for the next major version, and this one is documented for the current one. `Desktop app and delivery` Section 22 carries both.

### Fact 121 — Whether Windows has a documented call that returns a file's on-disk capitalisation, and on which filesystems it works.

**Named by:** Row 406 at the stage-close Design sweep, answered 2026-08-21. **The register held no entry for it**, because Fact 16 recorded the opposite as settled: *no documented call returns a file's on-disk capitalisation*, after thirteen pages were read twice. **Those thirteen were manual and reference pages.** `Method`'s standing note of 2026-08-21 — a vendor's own protocol specification is the same authority as a manual page — is what re-opened it.

**Answered by:** reading Microsoft's own published protocol specification. **No rental and no machine.** **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** yes, as documentation.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-21, and it reverses Fact 16's Windows half.**

- **The sentence Fact 16 records as unwritten.** `[MS-FSCC]` §2.4.35, **FileNormalizedNameInformation**, read 2026-08-21 at `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/openspecs/windows_protocols/ms-fscc/20bcadba-808c-4880-b757-4af93e41edf6`: *"A normalized name is an absolute pathname where each short name component has been replaced with the corresponding long name component, and each name component uses the exact letter casing stored on disk."*
- **The Win32 surface** is `GetFileInformationByHandleEx` with `FileNormalizedNameInfo`, carried in the `FILE_INFO_BY_HANDLE_CLASS` enumeration, **which gives it no description of its own** — read the same day at `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/minwinbase/ne-minwinbase-file_info_by_handle_class`. That silence is why thirteen reference pages missed it.
- **The bound is the specification's own product-behaviour note**, footnote 139 to that section: *"This information class is implemented on ReFS and NTFS file systems. Other file systems return STATUS_INVALID_DEVICE_REQUEST."* **So FAT32, exFAT and the rest refuse it, and they refuse loudly with a status code rather than answering wrongly.**
- **Two adjacent pages were read and answer nothing**, recorded so the next reader does not read them again: Microsoft's WSL *Case Sensitivity* page, which settles case-insensitivity and the per-directory flag and says nothing about returned casing; and the archived *Configuring case sensitivity for file and folder names*, whose `ntfscase={upper | lower | preserve}` belongs to Server for NFS and **argues the other way if anything**, showing that a serving layer may hand back a folded spelling.
- **What is still not written down anywhere** is what a directory enumeration's own casing does on NTFS, and NTFS's folding rule itself. `Dependency Upload & Remap` F14a is written so that **neither is load-bearing**: candidates by fold-equality, exactness from the normalized name's last component alone.

**What rests on it:** `Dependency Upload & Remap` F14a and Row 406, and through them the Creator's capitals ruling of 2026-08-13.

**What is still owed: nobody has run it.** `Dependency Upload & Remap` §12 item 21 *(repointed 2026-08-22 — that section carried two entries numbered 20 and the second was renumbered)*. The throwaway is minutes on a Windows computer this register already wants: create a mixed-case file on an NTFS volume, open it, query the class, compare the last component against the bytes created. **A negative looks like a folded or uppercased component, or a refusal.**

### Fact 122 — Whether a case-folding Linux directory stores the name it was given, or a folded form.

**Named by:** Row 406 at the stage-close Design sweep, answered 2026-08-21. It settles a contradiction this project recorded against itself on 2026-08-20 — `Dependency Upload & Remap` F8 and F14 said opposite things about a folding disk and **neither reading had a citation**, which is why it was put on a machine rather than decided.

**Answered by:** reading the Linux kernel's own documentation. **No rental and no machine.** **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** yes, as documentation.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-21, for ext4 and for no other filesystem.** `admin-guide/ext4`, read 2026-08-21 at `https://docs.kernel.org/admin-guide/ext4.html`:

- *"The case-awareness is name-preserving on the disk, meaning that the file name provided by userspace is a byte-per-byte match to what is actually written in the disk."*
- *"The Unicode normalization format used by the kernel is thus an internal representation, and not exposed to the userspace nor to the disk, with the important exception of disk hashes, used on large case-insensitive directories with DX feature."*

**So one name is stored, it is the one that was given, and the folded form exists nowhere an enumeration could return it.** **The F8/F14 contradiction settles in F8's favour** — for ext4, and the citation says nothing about any other folding filesystem.

**What rests on it:** `Dependency Upload & Remap` F14a and F8, and Row 406.

**What is still owed:** which filesystem a distribution presents is not established (`Dependency Upload & Remap` §12 item 11), a folding filesystem that is not ext4 has no citation, and **nobody has run it** (§12 item 21, repointed 2026-08-22 from a duplicate item number) — create a case-folding directory, create a file with mixed capitals, enumerate, see which spelling comes back.

### Fact 123 — Whether the application can give up the operating system's native window frame and draw the title bar itself — the window buttons, the drag region and the bar's height — on macOS, Windows and Linux, and whether the chosen shell exposes it.

**THE SHELL CHANGED UNDER THIS ENTRY, 2026-09-02, AND IT IS ALREADY ANSWERED ON THE NEW ONE.** What is read below is Wails v2's facility, and the selected shell is Tauri v2 — `Desktop app and delivery` H5b was filled by Build and Tauri came out materially smaller. **The entry stands as a reading of Wails and is not struck.** **H6a made this exact check conditional — *whether Tauri publishes the same facility has deliberately not been read; that check is required if H5b places Tauri first* — and it was run the same day: [H6b](Desktop%20app%20and%20delivery.md#22-the-route-chosen-and-the-properties-it-is-held-to).** The frame is given up by one setting, the drag region is a page attribute, the three buttons are wired to published calls, and macOS keeps the same second route. **Two differences a builder carries and neither costs a promise:** the drag attribute does not reach child elements on the selected shell, and its macOS custom title bar publishes a cost Wails left unstated. **Nothing is owed.**

**Named by:** Row 425 at the stage-close Design sweep, 2026-08-20. **The register held no entry for it.** The sweep records that the nearest numbered neighbours — Facts 12, 13, 22, 23, 24, 73 and 98 — are about menu bars, download folders and update replacement, and **none of them reaches the frame**.

**Answered by:** reading the shell's own documentation and its own release notes. **No rental and no machine.** **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** yes, as documentation.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-21, and it is the shell's own facility rather than three platforms' separate ones.** Wails, read 2026-08-21 (`wails.io` returns 403 to a fetch; these are the site's own documentation sources, the route Facts 117 and 120 took).

- **The frame.** `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/options.mdx` — **Frameless**, type `bool`: *"When set to `true`, the window will have no borders or title bar."* The guide it points at, `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/guides/frameless.mdx`, opens *"Wails supports application that have no frames."* **The option carries no per-platform table**, unlike `WindowStartState` on the same page — which is why the release notes below matter.
- **The drag region.** Same guide: *"Any HTML element that has the CSS style `--wails-draggable:drag` will act as a \"drag handle\". This property applies to all child elements. If you need to indicate that a nested element should not drag, then use the attribute '--wails-draggable:no-drag' on that element."* Configurable via `CSSDragProperty` (default `--wails-draggable`) and `CSSDragValue` (default `drag`). **And the carve-out**: *"If you allow your application to go fullscreen, this drag functionality will be disabled."*
- **The window buttons are the application's own and each act is published.** `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/docs/reference/runtime/window.mdx` — `WindowMinimise` (*"Minimises the window"*), `WindowToggleMaximise` (*"Toggles between Maximised and UnMaximised"*), with `WindowMaximise`, `WindowUnmaximise`, `WindowIsMaximised` and `WindowIsMinimised` beside them. Closing is `Quit` (*"Quits the application."*). **No shell-supplied button set exists in frameless mode.**
- **All three systems, from the project's own release notes** — `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/wailsapp/wails/master/website/src/pages/changelog.mdx`, read 2026-08-21. **Linux**, v2.1.0, 2022-10-18: *"Add missing resize for frameless window on Linux"* and *"Fixed Linux frameless window drag"*. **macOS**, v2.0.0-beta.43, 2022-08-08: *"Allow MacOS frameless window to be miniturisable"*. **Windows**, v2.4.0, 2023-03-08: *"Frameless resize now sets the cursor on documentElement…"*, and v2.10, 2025-02-15: *"[windows] Fixed frameless window flickering when minimizing/restoring by preventing unnecessary redraws"*.
- **Windows' one option, left at its default.** `DisableFramelessWindowDecorations` — *"Setting this to `true` will remove the window decorations in Frameless mode. This means there will be no 'Aero Shadow' and no 'Rounded Corners' shown for the window."* Default `false`.
- **macOS's second route**, named because it is a real alternative rather than a variant: `mac.TitleBar` with `TitlebarAppearsTransparent`, `FullSizeContent` and `HideTitleBar`, and the preset `mac.TitleBarHiddenInset()` — **which keeps the system's own window buttons in the system's own place.**
- **The bar's height is not the shell's at all.** With the frame gone the web view is the whole window, so the mockup's `--title-h: 38px` is one row of the page's own grid and **no platform figure is read or reconciled.**

**What rests on it:** `Desktop app and delivery` 2.2 **H6a** and Row 425 — and through it R15's achieved-rate reading, **which had an established value and no established place to sit.**

**What is still owed, and none of it stops the row.** `Desktop app and delivery` §22 items 39 and 40. **The shell names the standard window buttons and the native frame's own behaviours nowhere** — nothing is published about drag-to-edge arrangement, double-click-to-zoom or the system window menu on any system, and **Linux is documented thinnest of the three**, its frameless resize and frameless drag both arriving as release-note fixes. **None of it is observed by anybody on this project.** Nothing promised breaks either way, because Smoozy draws its own three buttons on every system.

### Fact 6 — Where things Smoozy puts on a rented machine sit: which disk, whether a rental offers a choice of disk at all, and whether what is written there survives a reboot and the machine's own unattended housekeeping.

**Named by, six sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 6 — "where a job's folder sits on the machine: which disk, and whether a rental offers a choice of disk at all". `Coverage.md`'s wording.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact F — same words; says it is Rendering's Fact 6.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/What a job writes.md` Fact 3 — same words; says it is Rendering's Fact 6.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 9 — same words; says it is the fact Rendering waits on.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Fact 4 — "where Smoozy's **asset area** sits ... and whether what is written there survives a reboot and the machine's own unattended housekeeping"; states outright it is the same question and that the two must be answered together, because a write place mirrored under the landing rule would sit in the asset area, outside the job's folder, where Download would never see it.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 11 — "where a **Blender** is put on the machine: which disk"; says it is the same question.

**Merge note.** Merged on two sweeps' own explicit argument. The answer is owed for **three placements** — a job's folder, Smoozy's asset area, a Blender install — plus Dependency Upload & Remap's survival clause, which no other sweep asks.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** a rental that actually **has more than one disk**. The one snapshot in `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` had a single 126 GB disk with a 40 GB memory-backed folder beside it, so posing the choice half may need a differently specified rental.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but Open Threads notes it may turn out to be Specs' if the user should be offered the choice.
**Posable:** now, given a rental of the right specification.

**THE CHOICE HALF ANSWERED, 2026-08-16** — yes, by attaching a volume, which the runner ruled permitted the same day. It arrives mounted at the path the rental asks for and is a genuinely separate filesystem: `/`, `/root`, `/tmp` and `/var` all report device 53, the volume reports 2052. **But it is not a second disk** — `lsblk` shows one 1.8 T disk and the volume is a 20 GiB quota on a partition of it, so it shares whatever that disk can do. **And it arrives `root:root 755`, refusing the non-root login Smoozy holds** until a setup step hands it over, after which that login writes and reads free space normally. **Both disks survived a stop and a start** with markers byte-identical and the volume remounted in place. **Still owed:** a machine with more than one **physical** disk, and survival across the machine's own unattended housekeeping over days. See [Two more boxes](Two%20more%20boxes%20—%20the%20volume,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20a%20day%20left%20running.md).

**THE MORE-THAN-ONE-PHYSICAL-DISK HALF ANSWERED, 2026-08-22, and the answer reverses the line above it.** One seller ships two real virtio devices as standard — `/dev/vda1` at 97 GB on `/` and `/dev/vdb` at 787 GB on `/ephemeral` — so a rental's second filesystem can be a genuine second device and not only a quota on the same disk. **Both shapes are now observed and neither may be expected.** See [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20—%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md); `Job files and outputs` L4 and its §14 item 6 carry it, and `Telegram` §21 item 8 reads the same. **Still owed, and narrower than before:** whether that large mount survives a stop, a start or a restart was not posed, its name is the seller's own claim rather than an observation, and survival across the machine's own unattended housekeeping over days is untouched. *(Recorded here 2026-08-23. The entry had stood at the 2026-08-16 line for a day while two documents carried the newer answer.)*

### Fact 7 — What listing a folder over the connection under whatever login Smoozy holds yields, and which failures can be told apart: name, size, last-changed time and kind for every entry, which clock the time is on, and whether *this login may not read it* can be told from *the folder is gone* and from *the machine did not answer*.

**Named by, five sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 7 — "whether a job's output folder can be listed ... and whether *the folder is gone* can be told from *this login may not read it*". `Coverage.md`'s wording.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 5 — same, **plus each file's size**; says plainly it is the same fact Rendering names and asks the runner to give it one number.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/What a job writes.md` Fact 4 — same, plus the size; calls itself Rendering's Fact 7 widened by the size, and says the zero-byte rule the Creator ruled on 2026-08-12 is unbuildable without it.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 2 — adds the **third case**: *the machine did not answer*. Insists any test carry a control proving the refusal in the same run — Open Threads records two Linux passes that read as successes and should have read as failures for want of one.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 3 — "what a folder listing yields ... name, size, last-changed time and kind for every entry, and which clock the time is on", plus what a listing reports for a file whose contents are a lie, such as the sparse file that claimed 500 GB on a 126 GB disk.

**Merge note — flagged.** File Browser 3 is not obviously the same question as Rendering 7: one asks *what fields come back*, the other *whether it can be got and which failure it was*. They are merged because they are one command, one sitting, one answer — and because Download 5 and What a job writes 4 already push Rendering 7 into the field question by adding the size. If the runner disagrees, split File Browser 3 back out; it costs no extra rental.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, posed under a **non-root login** or with the root capability dropped for the command. On the box in `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` root ignored file permissions, so the refusing case could not arise at all.
**Shape-deciding:** no. But File Browser calls it among the widest-reaching in its document.
**Posable:** now.

**THE FIELDS HALF ANSWERED and THE FAILURE HALF ANSWERED IN PART, 2026-08-17.** See [One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §5, posed throughout under the non-root login.

**All four fields come back in one reading on both routes, and the size costs no second round trip.** That is settled at protocol level with the server's own logging: a 15-entry listing cost **zero per-file stats**, and 5010 entries cost **52 round trips rather than 5010** — which would have been 651 seconds against the 18.22 measured. On the command channel the same four fields cost **about a quarter of a second for 5010 entries**. **So the zero-byte rule the Creator ruled on for 2026-08-12 is buildable**; the zero-length file reports 0 on both routes at no extra cost.

**Which clock, established four ways.** **No timezone is configured at all on this container** — the `+0000` is the C library's fallback in the absence of configuration, so a provider image that set one would render those times in its zone, and **the epoch form is the unambiguous one**. Nothing inside disciplines the clock. The two ends **agree to better than half a second and no more finely than that**, bounded by a ±0.3 s round trip. A hand-set time round-trips exactly at nanosecond resolution.

**The sparse file's lie is catchable on one route only.** A 500 GB sparse file on a 60 GB allocation reports its full apparent size with **0 blocks allocated** — one extra field in the same command-channel reading. **The file-transfer route carries no allocated-block count**, so it cannot be caught there.

**The failure half is partial, and the reason is exact.** On the file-transfer route the three cases carry **distinct protocol statuses**, with a control either side. *The machine did not answer* is exit **255** with a client-written message in all four shapes tried. **But on the command channel the plain listing command returns exit 2 for both refusal and absence** — only the English message separates them, and that is stable here only because the image carries no locale beyond the default. **What separates them on that channel is a walk of the parent components, not a test on the path itself — corrected 2026-08-22, where this said *Adding a stat or a directory test does separate them*.** Run with a control on macOS, a file test and a `stat` **on the path itself** both exit 1 in either case, and `test`'s own specification carries no third value for *I was not allowed to look*; `One box with Blender on it`'s own Linux table shows `test -d` at exit 0 for the denied and the absent path alike. **The Linux form of the path-under-an-unreadable-parent case is unposed** — one command set on a Linux rental, `stat` and `test -e` on a path under a mode-000 parent against a path that does not exist, with the exit statuses written down.

**And one finding a builder most needs.** **Partial output is delivered before a connection dies**: a truncated listing and a genuinely short one were both four lines, separable **only by exit status**. Anything treating *output arrived* as *listing complete* is wrong on this machine.

**The trap was demonstrated first:** root lists a root-owned private folder at exit 0, so a question posed as root reads as success and proves nothing.

**Limits:** one container, `overlayfs`, the proxy route only, one unprivileged login shape with no groups, access lists or read-only mounts.

### Fact 8 — What a connection dropping mid-submission leaves on the machine: whether a job can arrive while its acknowledgement is lost, and whether Smoozy can afterwards tell that job from one that never arrived.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Rendering.md` Fact 8 only.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental. The plain half — what a cut connection does to a command in flight — rides on the Fact 1 and 2 throwaway.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** the plain half now; the full question **only once the shape exists**, since there is nothing on the machine yet to take a submission.

**ANSWERED IN FULL, 2026-08-18 — yes, over 312 trials, of which 230 are the sound base, and the window is about three milliseconds.** The input document's three rounds are **82, 120 and 110**; the first 82 were cold and are worthless on the tester's own word, leaving the two warm rounds — 120 and 110 — as what the answer rests on. See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §6. The connection was cut mid-submission with the operation identity chosen at the desk before the request was sent. **No disagreement between the machine's answer and the queue in any of the 312.**

**The transition sits at about 3 ms**: every trial cut at 0.000–0.002 s answered *no record of that identity* with the job absent; every trial from 0.004 s upward answered *a recorded outcome* with the job present; 0.003 s split. **The third answer, *still in hand*, was never once returned** — the window in which a submission is accepted but has no recorded outcome is narrower than a shell can aim at.

**One of the three rounds was worthless and the tester says so.** The first 82 trials, on cold connections, all answered *no record* only because the handshake to that box costs 1.6–1.9 seconds, so a cut at one second lands before the request has been sent at all. The middle 120, over a warm connection, were consistent and all landed past the window; the third round's 110, warm and cut between 0.000 s and 0.018 s, is where the transition above was found.

**One process fact worth keeping: cutting a connection does not guarantee the request never lands.** In 2 of 202 warm trials the submission arrived between the tester's two reads, so the machine looked as though it disagreed with itself; re-asked, both answered correctly. That was the harness and not the machine.

---

## Facts 9 to 14 — the licence and the user's own computer, from `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Access.md`

### Fact 9 — What the licence service must be able to do: hold two computer slots against a key; release one on request from a computer that holds no slot and about a computer that may no longer exist; report a key as no longer valid **and why**; tell an invalid key from a licence already on two computers from a check that could not be reached; and hold whether a given computer has already had a trial.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Access.md` Fact 1, wanted by nine rows. Labelled an assumption in the spec and its sources file.
**Answered by:** reading a candidate service's own documentation, quoted with its address and the date read — or by a decision to build one, in which case it stops being a fact and becomes a shape question.
**Machine:** none.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but the build-or-buy fork sits directly on it.
**Posable:** now, once a candidate service is named. Standing constraint: no account, no registration and no email inside Smoozy, so the key is the only thing slots can be held against.

**THE FORK IS RULED, 2026-08-16. A component the Creator runs exists.** Shown that the reading below leaves no branch without one — the vendor cannot hold the trial record at all, and the release and revocation routes both need a credential its own documentation forbids shipping in a client — the Creator answered: **"Sure, I'm okay with that … I am more than happy to have something extra for this."** Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-16). Lemon Squeezy still sells and issues the keys; the component holds the trial record and does the release and the switching off.

**Two things the ruling does not decide, and both are this stage's.** **Where it runs** — the Creator named Cloudflare as a guess and said outright that agents decide it, so no host is chosen here. And **what it costs**, beyond the modest recurring figure the question was put with. Both belong to `Licence service`, which is now unblocked. The standing constraint from `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md` holds over the whole of it: **nothing may bind to one seller**, because the Creator expects the seller to change.

**ANSWERED AGAINST THE NAMED CANDIDATE, 2026-08-16, and the candidate cannot carry it alone.** The Creator named **Lemon Squeezy** as the seller that issues licence keys; its own documentation was read against each clause and citation-checked by a fresh agent, which ruled **84 claims — 76 supported, 6 narrowed, 2 struck, 0 not found** — and then searched **all 232 pages** of the documentation set for the load-bearing silences. Clause by clause: **holding two slots against a key is documented**, with the activation call taking the key and a name and nothing else, so the standing constraint holds — but **a slot is bound to an activation event, never to a computer**, and the instance name is documented as a label with no uniqueness, matching or de-duplication anywhere in the set. **Release is documented but out of reach**: it needs the instance identifier of the computer being released, and the only documented way to learn another computer's identifier is an authenticated call the vendor's own *Requests* page forbids shipping — *"Do not share your API keys in publicly accessible areas such as GitHub, client-side code, and so forth."* The three licence endpoints are the only API pages whose examples omit authorisation, and **there is no unauthenticated route to list a key's instances**. **Reporting a key as no longer valid, and why, is narrower than the clause**: a status field and an error field exist, the vendor does direct the reader to the status, but **no page enumerates the error strings**, none of the four statuses means *refunded*, and an example shows a key valid and active over four years past its own expiry date. **Telling the three cases apart rests on one example string** for the limit-reached case; an invalid key is documented nowhere in the set; and a documented rate-limit refusal and a documented server error are neither of the two outcomes the reading first framed. **Holding whether a computer has already had a trial is not stated at all, and the negative is total**: every record descends from an order, neither licence object carries a free-form field, and the vendor's own free trials are subscription-bound while its one free-access route trades on the user's email address, which the standing constraint forbids. **Fact 11's two consequences both land badly**: nothing documented stops the same key being activated repeatedly under fresh computer names, since only the count defends and the release call reduces it without authorisation; and the service holds its own clock for a purchased key's expiry but **holds no trial record to put a clock against**. **The fork therefore does not turn on preference.** Nothing this vendor documents can hold a record against a computer that has bought nothing, and both the release route and the revocation route below need a component the vendor's own words forbid putting inside the app. **Three limits stand over the whole reading:** nothing was run — no account, no key, no request, so it is documented assertion with zero observation; it is one day's reading of a live set whose help pages carry **no date of any kind**; and the documentation **refuses one common retrieval route with a 403 while serving the identical addresses at 200 on another**, so a later reader on the failing route would wrongly conclude it is unavailable. See [Lemon Squeezy](Lemon%20Squeezy%20—%20the%20licence%20service%20read%20against%20what%20Access%20needs.md).

### Fact 10 — Who handles the sale, and what that seller can do: take the payment, send the key by email, and make a refund or a chargeback reach whatever the licence check reads.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Access.md` Fact 2. Creator-stated 2026-07-29 for the seller's identity; Open Threads records **Lemon Squeezy**, with the Creator expecting it to change.
**Answered by:** reading the seller's own documentation, with the date read; the seller's identity itself is the Creator's own knowledge.
**Machine:** none.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now, but this is the fastest-changing fact in the register — a page read in August says nothing about September.

**ANSWERED IN PART, 2026-08-16, from the seller's own documentation, citation-checked.** The seller is **Lemon Squeezy**, Creator-stated, with the Creator expecting it to change — so nothing may bind to its shape. **Taking the payment and delivering the key by email are documented** and happen entirely outside the app, which is what Row 226 needs a destination for. **A refund or chargeback reaching whatever the licence check reads is not stated in any direction checked**: the refund pages never say *licence*, the licence pages never say *refund*, the licence-updated event names no trigger, the only documented cause of a disabled key is a person disabling it by hand in the seller's dashboard, and **there is no chargeback or dispute event anywhere in the seventeen-event list**. **But it is reachable rather than impossible, and the citation check found the missing link:** a refunded-order event carries an order identifier, licence keys can be listed by that identifier, and a key can then be disabled — a documented, automatable chain from refund to revocation. **A chargeback is documented to become a full refund in most cases**, and the refund event fires on a full **or partial** refund, so the seller can be learnt from. **The chain needs the same Creator-side component the release route in Fact 9 already needs, so it is paid for once rather than twice.** One shape the clause wants does exist and is closed to us: the vendor documents exactly one automatic billing-to-licence propagation, from an expired **subscription** to its key, which Row 301 forbids. See [Lemon Squeezy](Lemon%20Squeezy%20—%20the%20licence%20service%20read%20against%20what%20Access%20needs.md), and the register's warning above stands — **this reading is dated 2026-08-16 and the seller's help pages carry no dates at all**.

**ANSWERED FURTHER FOR ROW 471, 2026-08-27, from the seller's own documentation.** Lemon Squeezy's current pages show UUID-shaped, 36-character examples, but they call the value only the *full licence key*: **they promise no invariant format and expose no field by which a client discovers one**. The documented `key_short` is the literal prefix `XXXX-` followed by the last twelve characters of the real key, so it reveals part of the secret and says nothing dependable about the full key's form. **There is therefore no ground for the mockup's `SMZ-XXXX-XXXX-XXXX` placeholders or `SMZ-••••-••••-••••` masks.** The focused reading, exact quotations, addresses and date are in [Lemon Squeezy](Lemon%20Squeezy%20—%20the%20licence%20service%20read%20against%20what%20Access%20needs.md) under *Row 471 — the issued key's form, whether it is invariant, and key_short*.

### Fact 11 — Whether each supported operating system offers something that identifies a computer again from away, how far it survives a reinstall, a move and a person with full control of the machine, and what it takes to read it.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Access.md` Fact 3, wanted by the tamper row, the trial record, and a key entered after a lock.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation, plus a throwaway on a computer of each kind.
**Machine:** a computer of each supported operating system — **not a rental**. A rented Linux machine answers the Linux third and nothing else, and Smoozy does not run on the rented machine anyway.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** the Mac third now; the Windows third **not at all** — no Windows machine has run it, and none is rented today. See the split against Fact 12 below.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16, and the answer is negative on all three.** **Every identifier an ordinary downloaded application can read is a value the operating system hands over on request, and each vendor documents that it can be changed or spoofed.** Apple: any I/O Registry property can "be spoofed by knowledgeable persons", and `gethostuuid(2)`'s own manual says "the hardware identifiers that gethostuuid() uses to generate the UUID can themselves be modified". Microsoft: "someone can easily spoof a software KSP as a TPM KSP with local administrator credentials". systemd: `systemd.machine_id=` on the kernel command line "has higher priority and will be used instead of the ID stored in /etc/machine-id" — **the override is a documented feature, so no forgery is required at all**, and systemd asks that the ID be treated as "confidential" and hashed with `sd_id128_get_machine_app_specific()` before it leaves the host. **Every route that does resist forgery demands something a website-distributed binary does not have.** Apple's Managed Device Attestation needs MDM enrolment or an organisation's ACME CA, Apple silicon and macOS 14+; App Attest is documented off on Mac and confirmed so on the machine (`DCAppAttestService.shared.isSupported = false`); Windows' AD CS key attestation needs an enterprise CA ("not supported for a standalone CA") and the ASHWID is package-scoped; Linux's TPM route needs an EKCert that not all TPMs have, the manufacturer's chain out of band, group `tss` membership, and a verifier the developer writes and runs. **One live lead, undocumented rather than closed:** `DCDevice` works on macOS from an ad-hoc-signed, bundle-less binary — observed, token returned, nil error — but Apple documents no forgery resistance for it anywhere; the check enumerated all eleven DeviceCheck pages from Apple's own index and "unforgeable", "tamper", "spoof" and "forge" occur **zero** times. **Stability is documented only in patches:** Microsoft's `GetSystemIdForPublisher` carries the strongest contract of the three but "will be identical for all unpackaged (Win32) apps"; systemd's machine ID "does not change based on local or network configuration or when hardware is replaced"; Apple documents nothing about a reinstall either way. **Forgeability itself was never tested on any system** — all three are documented assertions rather than demonstrations, and settling them wants three disposable machines and permission to modify them. See [The computer itself](The%20computer%20itself%20—%20its%20name,%20its%20identity,%20and%20its%20clock.md).

**One part of this became load-bearing on 2026-08-20 and is named so it is not lost among the rest.** `Preparation/Architecture/Access.md` §4 chooses **`GetSystemIdForPublisher`** as the Windows local computer identity, on the ground that it carries the only documented stability contract of the three systems. **What is not established is which `Source` an unpackaged, website-distributed Win32 binary would receive.** **Corrected 2026-08-21: this also carried *whether it can call it at all* and *nothing read establishes the call succeeds from that position*, and both are now answered.** Microsoft's Note is a positive statement about what such a caller gets — *"The system identification string is created using the calling app's publisher ID and an identifier derived from the system's firmware/hardware. The system identification string will be identical for all unpackaged (Win32) apps and all packaged apps with no publisher ID."* (`https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/uwp/api/windows.system.profile.systemidentification`, read 2026-08-20) — **it names the value returned to that caller rather than shutting a door.** Supporting it: Microsoft's *WinRT APIs not supported in desktop apps* (`https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/apps/desktop/modernize/winrt-api-desktop-app-support`, read 2026-08-20, page dated 2026-07-22) lists the two sets that are unsupported or restricted, and `Windows.System.Profile.SystemIdentification` is on neither — while two of its immediate namespace neighbours, `AnalyticsInfo` and `AnalyticsVersionInfo`, **are** enumerated as package-identity-only. **The weakness, stated:** that page says *"The list might not be comprehensive"*, and this round exists because absence from a page was read as absence from the world — so the confirmation rests on the first page, with the second as support. **The exclusivity clause costs only app-scoping**, which this use does not need. **The `Source` half no reading will settle**: the method page conditions nothing on the caller's packaging. What would settle it is running the call from an unpackaged binary on a Windows machine and reading `SystemIdentificationInfo.Source` — one Windows computer, which belongs in whatever Windows sitting eventually happens rather than being rented for this alone. **This is not a new fact**: it is inside this one's existing negative and its unresolved items, and it needs a Windows computer, which this project has not got. It is written here because a whole system's identity choice now rests on it where before it was inventory.

### Fact 12 — What each supported operating system calls the computer and the login account, how a program reads them, how steady each is across a rename, a network move, a reinstall and a hardware change, and whether two computers can carry the same computer name.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Named by, three sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Access.md` Fact 4 — "whether each supported operating system offers a name the computer answers to, whether Smoozy can read it, and how steady it is when the user renames the machine or moves it between networks"; wanted by the name each computer is shown by.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 6 — "what the login account and the computer are each called on Mac, Windows and Linux, how a program reads them, and whether either can change while Smoozy is installed"; wanted by the default key name.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 8 — "what name each of Mac, Windows and Linux gives a computer; whether it survives a rename, a reinstall and a hardware change, and whether two computers can carry the same one"; wanted by the licence-service row, and it decides whether the promise that a third computer is refused *by name* can be kept at all.

**Split note — flagged, and the one I am least sure of.** Fact 11 and Fact 12 are kept apart. Access itself draws the line: Fact 11 is an identity a person must not be able to forge; Fact 12 is a name shown to a person. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 8 straddles the line — its uniqueness and survives-a-reinstall clauses read as Fact 11's, its wording as Fact 12's. It is filed under Fact 12 because it says *name*, with this warning attached: **the answer to Fact 11 must not be inferred from Fact 12.** A name that is unique and survives a reinstall is still not an identity, because Fact 11 also asks whether a person with full control can forge it, and Fact 12 does not.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation, plus a throwaway on a computer of each kind.
**Machine:** a computer of each supported operating system. Answerable in the same sitting as Fact 11.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** Mac now; Windows not at all.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16, and the collision clause is answered yes on all three.** **Two computers can carry the same computer name on every one of the three**, and no system documents a hostname as derived from hardware. On macOS and Windows it takes a user choosing it or a vendor setting it — Windows' out-of-box default is documented as *random*, so it is not itself a collision source, but an OEM can pin a fixed name in `Unattend.xml`. **Linux is the worst case:** the compiled-in fallback is `localhost` on every machine that reaches it. What each system does about a collision differs sharply and none of it protects a licence check: macOS renumbers the **local network name only**, per link, leaving `ComputerName` untouched; NetBIOS marks the name in conflict on any node receiving the demand and returns `NAME CONFLICT` with no renumbering; Linux documents nothing at the hostname layer at all. **A computer has more than one name on all three.** macOS has three by Apple's own `scutil(8)` — `ComputerName`, `LocalHostName`, `HostName` — and on the observed machine `HostName` was unset, so `gethostname(3)` falls through to a **fourth** string that is neither of the two actually set; `LocalHostName` was observed **consistent with** derivation from `ComputerName` by character-class filtering, which a single static reading cannot establish as derivation. Windows has the NetBIOS-era name (15 bytes) *derived from* the DNS host name by re-encoding and truncation, and the two Microsoft pages **contradict each other** on whether a name may exceed 15 characters. Linux has systemd's pretty, static and transient forms, static beating transient beating the fallback. **The login account's name is not one thing either:** on the observed Mac `id -un` and `logname` **disagreed**, `getlogin()` returning `root` at effective uid 501, so a program reading the account name that way can get the wrong account. **Steadiness across a reinstall or a hardware change is documented by nobody, on any of the three** — that is the largest unresolved cluster. The rename clause is documentation-only everywhere, macOS included, since observing it would have meant renaming the Creator's computer. See [The computer itself](The%20computer%20itself%20—%20its%20name,%20its%20identity,%20and%20its%20clock.md).

### Fact 13 — What each supported operating system offers as a reading of elapsed time that winding the computer's clock back does not defeat, and whether any of it survives the app being closed for weeks.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Access.md` Fact 5 — the trial's seven days, the key entered offline with days held in reserve, the three-and-seven-day count.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation, plus a throwaway on the Creator's own computer that moves the clock.
**Machine:** a computer of each supported operating system. Nothing rented.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** Mac now; Windows not at all.

**ANSWERED IN PART, 2026-08-16, and the answer is a clean negative on the half that matters.** **Nothing on any of the three systems was shown to survive both a reboot and an administrator.** Every monotonic reading each vendor documents as immune to the clock resets at boot — Microsoft is the plainest, with `QueryPerformanceCounter` "completely independent of the system time and UTC" and counting only "since the Windows operating system was started", and the interrupt-time counters "not subject to adjustments by users or the Windows time service"; Linux's `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` is suspend-inclusive and unsettable and resets at boot. **Every cross-reboot record is wall-clock-derived**, and each vendor documents the administrator's power over it: `SeSystemtimePrivilege`'s holder can make event-log and file timestamps inaccurate, `SetFileTime` sets creation time too, `CAP_SYS_TIME` covers the hardware clock as well as the system clock, and `utmp(5)` says of the boot record "if it is removed, record-keeping is turned off". **The one mechanism anywhere designed against a backwards clock is systemd's clock epoch** — at boot "the local clock is advanced to the epoch if it was set to a lower value" — and it is Linux-only, distribution-dependent, and defined from file modification times systemd never claims are protected. **Two clock names are the reverse of Linux's on macOS**, which will invert a decision if read carelessly: `CLOCK_UPTIME_RAW` is the sleep-**exclusive** one and `mach_continuous_time` the sleep-**inclusive** one. **What is unanswered is the observed half, deliberately:** the brief forbade changing the Creator's clock, so **no claim about what a wound-back clock does to any reading is observed on any system, this Mac included**. Settling it wants a disposable machine, a recorded before-reading, the clock moved back, a reboot, and a further reading. **Two leads remain open:** the TPM's own clock, which Microsoft acknowledges in a single sentence and otherwise does not document, and an undocumented macOS counter reading 3.73 years on a machine up 34 days, which cannot be told apart from a recomputation per read without moving the clock. **One claim the check withdrew outright:** the first answer said a Mac's sleep-inclusive monotonic clock and `kern.boottime` are two views of one quantity; neither run supports it and the two runs disagree with each other, so **anything downstream that rested on that identity needs re-deciding**. **Ruled 2026-08-31: where the two runs disagree — the size of the gap, not whether there is one — the re-run's 0.775668 s stands, being stable to 2 µs across six interleaved rounds against one unrepeated 0.319 s sample. It will not be run again, and nothing rests on it** (`Access` T5 and `Security and licence` L11 both say so). See [The computer itself](The%20computer%20itself%20—%20its%20name,%20its%20identity,%20and%20its%20clock.md).

### Fact 14 — Whether the licence check is reachable from where users actually are: a company network, a country that blocks it, and an outage at the service's own end.

**No longer relied on for paid access — decision 2026-10-04, marked 2026-10-26.** The licence service, seller, trial, computer identity and licence-clock uses are withdrawn. The fact, its evidence and limits below remain unchanged as the record; no outside fact is re-decided here.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Access.md` Fact 6. Labelled an assumption in the sources file.
**Answered by:** reading the chosen service's own documentation, and the Creator's own judgement of what it costs to be wrong.
**Machine:** **none that can be rented for it.** A rented server sits in a data centre with an unobstructed connection, which is the one place the question cannot be posed. Do not put it in a rental batch.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now, partly. It may not be fully answerable at all, in which case the row closes as unresolved with the harm the sources file already names written down.

**NOT ANSWERED, and the row it exists for is closed unresolved with the promise standing.** This entry carried no closure line until 2026-08-20, when the `Access` gates found three documents recording the outcome while the register said nothing — the register's own preamble makes it the authority, so it says so here. **The hosts reading searched all fifty-four addresses** for `firewall`, `corporate`, `blocked`, `blocking`, `censor`, `proxy` and `restricted network`: *"Not one vendor says anything about a network between the user and the endpoint."* **No further reading will close it**, and it cannot be posed on a rented machine, which is the one place the question does not exist. **Row 236 is closed unresolved with the promise still standing** at `Preparation/Architecture/Licence service.md` §10 and §15 item 2. **What breaks:** a paying user on a permanently blocking network is locked at seven days, which is the exact harm the three days exist to prevent. **One documented instance exists rather than none** — Cloudflare states that traffic to Mainland China from outside faces *"significant latency and reliability issues"*, with an Enterprise subscription and an ICP filing as the remedy. **What holds:** an ordinary HTTPS POST on the standard port to an address on the Creator's own domain, movable without changing installed copies. **What would close it is a change to what `Access` promises**, which is Specs' and the Creator's alone. **And the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-31 reaches this entry too, so a reader who knows the ruling need not re-derive the permanence from the argument above: there will be no further rental to answer a question, and this one could not have used one anyway.** *(Added 2026-09-02.)*

---

## Facts 15 to 26 — files, transfers and the connection

### Fact 15 — What is available on a rented machine to tell two files apart by their contents, and what reading a large asset through costs at each end.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Fact 3 — telling two files apart, the part-arrived file, the warning row.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, and the question posed with a **genuinely large asset** rather than a test file — the promise is argued from size, and the cost is the reason the spec puts the work on each machine rather than between them. `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` gives the tested box's disk at 600 to 900 MB a second written and 2.0 GB a second read, against a link at 1.3 MB a second down and 0.48 MB a second up.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-15/16, by the six-machine batch, on a genuinely large asset as the fact requires.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 15. On a real 669,966,622-byte `.blend`: machine warm md5 0.89 s, sha256 2.24 s, sha512 1.53 s, a plain read 0.34 s cold at about 1.97 GB/s; Mac md5 1.26 s, sha256 2.22 s. **Every digest identical at both ends.**

**The finding that decides the design is a five-fold difference inside one machine**: `openssl dgst -sha256` took **0.46 s** against `sha256sum`'s **2.24 s**, same file, same box, and the same gap in the same direction on the Mac. **The cost is processor rather than disk**, so an end-to-end check is available at about a gigabyte a second — **on processors with the instructions that make it fast, which both ends here had and a rental may not.**

### Fact 16 — Whether a file the operating systems Smoozy runs on report as a match is the file that was asked for, capitals included, on a disk that does not mind capitals — and whether a case-insensitive near-miss can be got out of the same search.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Fact 5 — the exact-match search, what makes two references one row, the searched folders.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation, plus a throwaway on the Creator's own computer against a disk of each kind.
**Machine:** **none rented.** The Creator's Mac is one of the two ends the research already found disagreeing.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** Mac now; Windows is untested for every part of it, and the artist's own computer may be one.

**ANSWERED FOR MAC, 2026-08-18, by the reading batch, and the Windows half is the largest hole it leaves.** See [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §3.

**A near-miss is detectable in one call on macOS.** `getattrlist(ATTR_CMN_NAME)`, `fcntl(F_GETPATH)` and the C `realpath(3)` all return the name **as stored**; comparing those bytes against the bytes asked for is the whole test, and Apple's own `realpath(3)` documents `F_GETPATH` as the mechanism. **The trap is any canonicaliser that manipulates strings rather than calling the system** — the first run reported `realpath` echoing back what it was given, which was an artefact of one language's own pure-string implementation, and the citation check caught it. **APFS stores names byte for byte** and normalises only its lookup hash. **Folding is Unicode *full* folding**, tested against the folding table and predicting all fifteen observed pairs where simple folding mispredicts three — so a one-character name and a two-character name can be one file.

~~**STILL OWED, and it needs a Windows computer.** **No Microsoft page documents a call guaranteed to return a file's on-disk capitalisation** — thirteen pages read across two runs, none closes it~~ — **corrected 2026-08-21 and now [Fact 121](#fact-121--whether-windows-has-a-documented-call-that-returns-a-files-on-disk-capitalisation-and-on-which-filesystems-it-works).** **Microsoft's own published protocol specification does document one**: `[MS-FSCC]` §2.4.35's `FileNormalizedNameInformation` uses *"the exact letter casing stored on disk"*, bounded by its own footnote to NTFS and ReFS. **The thirteen pages were manual and reference pages**, and the Win32 enumeration that carries the class gives it no description of its own — which is exactly the failure `Method`'s standing note of 2026-08-21 was written for: **a manual page is not the boundary of a project's documentation.** **The Linux half moved the same day and is [Fact 122](#fact-122--whether-a-case-folding-linux-directory-stores-the-name-it-was-given-or-a-folded-form).** **What is genuinely still owed here** is that **NTFS's folding rule is undocumented** on every page read, where exFAT's is fully specified. Also owed: whether an exact-comparison lookup can be had on a folding Windows volume; which Unicode version macOS folds by; a case-sensitive APFS volume, HFS+, and network mounts, all of which need a machine that may be changed.

**THE CAPITALISATION RESIDUE IS PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** Every clause left here wants a computer this project does not have and will not get: the Windows folding rule and the folding-volume lookup want a Windows machine, and the macOS residues want a volume the Creator's own Mac may be changed to carry. **The Creator ruled on 2026-08-31 that there will be no further rental to answer a question.** This is Windows-list item 1.

**Breaks:** on a Windows filesystem other than NTFS or ReFS, an exact match stays indistinguishable from a case near-miss, so the wrong-file harm cannot be excluded. **What holds:** everything on macOS, observed; the documented exact-case route on NTFS and ReFS (Fact 121); and the fold-equal candidate rule. **The silence is already removed and it is loud by construction:** the class refuses on any other filesystem with `STATUS_INVALID_DEVICE_REQUEST`, so a filesystem that cannot answer **says so** rather than returning a wrong spelling, and the mirror reports the limit instead of a false guarantee. `Dependency Upload & Remap` §12 items 3 and 21 and `Desktop app and delivery` §22 item 10 are where it is carried.

### Fact 17 — Whether a part-sent transfer can be carried on from where it stopped over the connection Smoozy holds, **in both directions**, and whether the part that arrived can be held somewhere that is not the final name.

**Named by, three sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 3 — the fetching direction; "one answer serves both directions and it should not be asked twice"; adds the clause about holding the part away from the final name on the user's disk.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 6 — the **sending** direction, from the user's own computer. "Shape-deciding in the escalating sense" — the sources file states a negative answer is a hole in the spec to be fixed here rather than reinterpreted.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Fact 6 — "whether one can be carried on from where it stopped with whatever Smoozy holds at both ends", for uploads.

**Merge note — flagged.** Merged into one fact **posed twice, once per direction**, which is what both sweeps actually ask for. Download says do not ask it twice; File Browser says the half already answered is the wrong half — a *fetch* was resumed with `rsync`, and the **send has never been tried** — and the Creator's Mac carries Apple's own `rsync` at protocol 29, which refused the usual resume flags and resumed anyway with none. Both are satisfied by one fact whose answer is owed per direction. **An answer for one direction does not close the other.**
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, with **the Creator's own Mac at the other end** for the sending direction, since the two ends carry different tools.
**Shape-deciding:** for one module, in the escalating sense File Browser names.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED in both directions, 2026-08-15/16, by the six-machine batch.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 17. Resume was exact — 119,424,000 bytes where 157,286,400 minus 37,862,400 was owed, and 436,786,462 where 669,966,622 minus 233,180,160 was owed — with matching checksums; on a second provider an interrupted transfer resumed in **12.85 s against a from-scratch control of 30.97**. **The part that arrived can be held off the final name**, which is the clause the specs lean on. The fetching direction was posed on one machine with two tools, the sending direction on two providers with one.

**Two conditions ride with it.** Apple's `rsync` now accepts every resume flag but one, unlike this project's earlier record — **and the flag that holds a partial off the final name accepted the flag and resumed nothing on the sending direction on one box**, re-sending in full. And **the partial directory cannot be relied on after a crash**: a graceful interrupt produced it on both boxes, a hard kill produced it on one and on the other left rsync's own **randomly named temporary file** in the destination, a name Smoozy cannot predict. **What can be relied on is the partial directory after an interruption Smoozy performs itself.**

### Fact 18 — What a stopped, dropped or app-closed transfer leaves on the machine, and whether anything on the machine distinguishes a part-arrived file from a whole one.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 7 — a part-arrived file, the already-exists check. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Dependency Upload & Remap.md` Fact 6, first half — "what an interrupted upload leaves on a rented machine".
**Split note — flagged.** Kept apart from Fact 17. *Can it resume* and *what does an interruption leave behind* are two questions, and a yes to the first says nothing about the second. Kept apart also from Fact 72 (a whole arrival told from a **damaged** one), which is corruption rather than truncation. All three share one box and one sitting, so the split costs no rental.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental. **Already answered against the promise on one box on one day**: in both directions a killed copy leaves a piece under the final name with no marker of any kind, and a full disk leaves the same. What a fresh rental is for is whether that repeats — because if nothing on the machine distinguishes the two, the whole promise rests on Smoozy's own record and the shape has to carry it.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but it decides whether the shape must carry a record.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-15/16, by the six-machine batch, and it is worse than the earlier record says.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 18. Killed and dropped transfers leave bytes **at the final name** with **identical mode, permissions, extended attributes, inode flags, allocation and file-type output to a whole file**. Nothing on the machine distinguishes them.

**And the new case is sharper than the old one.** An interrupted **overwrite** is not truncated: 40,000,000 bytes of one file overwritten by a killed transfer of another left a **40,000,000-byte hybrid** — 10,444,800 new bytes spliced onto 29,555,200 old ones. **Neither version, plausible size, no marker.** So anything relying on the size looking wrong is defeated, and the whole promise rests on Smoozy's own record.

### Fact 19 — Whether a change on a rented machine can be noticed over the connection Smoozy holds while it stays connected: a file arriving in a watched folder, and a `.blend` changing under a read — what counts as changed, and what the watching costs while a render runs.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 1 — "whether a folder on a rented machine can be watched over the connection ... so that a file arriving in it is noticed, and what that costs while a render runs"; says it is closely related to the Open Threads item *how Smoozy notices a file change on a machine it is watching* and should be answered with it. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Render Settings.md` Fact 3 — "whether a `.blend` changing on a rented machine can be noticed over the connection ... and what counts as changed"; also points at the same parked item.
**Merge note — flagged.** Merged because both sweeps route to the same parked Open Threads item and both describe one mechanism. If the runner splits them, it costs no rental. Any answer is owed for **both payloads**: a new file appearing in an output folder while a render runs, and a `.blend` replaced by a fresh upload, saved on the machine itself, and left part-written by a killed transfer.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with a Blender on it and a render long enough to write frames while being watched.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED for both payloads, 2026-08-15/16, and the watching is free.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 19. A render took 43.64 seconds watched against controls of 43.47 and 43.79, and the watcher cost **0.08 seconds of processor over 55**. A file arriving by transfer is **indistinguishable in event shape** from a frame being written.

**One trap: a watch on a file follows the inode**, so a save-by-rename kills it silently, and a replaced folder gives one ignored event and then nothing. **Still owed:** the render was deliberately run on the processor, so the card case is unposed.

### Fact 20 — Whether a file on a rented machine can be told to be finished rather than still being written, over the connection, without opening it and without changing anything on the machine.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 2. Every route through the file itself is already closed off by the research.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with a Blender, **posed at a large picture size** — the window is about a fifth of a second at 1920 by 1080 and about three and a half seconds at 10000 by 10000, and a question posed at the small size can pass by luck.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED by two routes at a large picture size, 2026-08-15/16, as the fact insists** — 10000×10000, where the window is 1.2–1.6 seconds rather than a fraction of one. See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 20.

**And the clause the fact named as most likely to break did break, silently.** Watching the writing process works as root and **fails completely across a user boundary**: a non-root reader watching a root-written file got **160,151 refusals, zero matches, and "held by nobody" from an 18 MB file all the way to 1.6 GB** — the same answer the route gives for a finished file. **A Smoozy applying that rule would have called the frame finished at 18 MB.** The same-user control found 42 matches, so it is the boundary and not the route. Two related tools fail the same way and return exit 0 with no rows.

**The other route watches the folder rather than the process and is unaffected — so only one of the two survives a non-root Smoozy, and it is the one that cannot answer for a file finished before Smoozy connected.** And **size stability is a trap**: a frozen transfer sat at 17,233,920 bytes for 11.1 seconds while unfinished. **Still owed:** a `/proc` mounted to hide other users' processes, which some providers do, was not posed.

### Fact 21 — Whether Smoozy can tell that a file **on the server** has not changed since a transfer of it began.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 4.
**Split note — ruled, on the sweep's own argument.** Download states plainly this is **not** the same fact `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 8 parks, which asks it of a file on the user's own computer. Kept apart: different machine, different mechanism, different answering route (this one wants a rental, that one does not).
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-15/16, by the six-machine batch.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 21. **Yes, from size, inode, modification time and change time together** — a save-by-rename changes the inode, and a hand-restored modification time still moves the change time, which cannot be set back. Posed live, a byte changed mid-fetch and **the copy returned 0 and delivered a different file**, with only the timestamps revealing it. **The answered line was missing from this entry until 2026-08-17**, though two architecture documents already read it as answered: `Server protocol and transfers` §7.4 S13 records all four values when a download is arranged and compares all four before a resume, and `Job files and outputs` §4.4 uses the same four as a `.blend`'s file identity.
