**One thing this entry does not record and should be read with, added 2026-08-21: which channel the four values were read on.** It matters, because **the file-transfer surface cannot supply two of them.** Revision 3 of the file-transfer protocol — the revision OpenSSH implements, by its own `PROTOCOL` file (*"Note that OpenSSH's sftp and sftp-server implement revision 3 of the SSH filexfer protocol described in:"*, `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openssh/openssh-portable/master/PROTOCOL`, **address and reading date of 2026-08-23 supplied that day, this addendum having carried neither**) — **and the words *"and the one this project's sessions negotiate"* were struck the same day**, that being the unrecorded `Remote version: 3` observation struck from `Dependency Upload & Remap` Q5 route 1 and `Server protocol and transfers` §7.4; what stands in its place is protocol version 3 announced by every rental posed, four boxes across three sellers — carries flags, size, owner and group, permissions, access and modification times, and extensions. **No inode, no change time, and the modification time is a 32-bit count of seconds.** So the change time, which is this answer's whole point, is not on that surface, and the reading is a **command-channel** one — unestablished ground on an arbitrary rental, `File Browser` Section 13 item 9. **The answer itself is unaffected**; what was missing is the route, now stated in the three documents that use it.

### Fact 22 — Which folder each of the three operating systems Smoozy runs on uses for downloads, and how it is found.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 6.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation, with the exact words, the address and the date read.
**Machine:** none.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now, on all three — this is a documentation question and the missing Windows computer does not block it.

**ANSWERED on all three, 2026-08-18, by the reading batch.** See [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §1, which carries the three routes side by side.

**The three systems are three different mechanisms rather than three spellings of one.** **Windows is the only one with a real relocation API whose default read follows the relocation** — so the default-path flag must never be passed, because it defeats the user's own choice. **Linux has no specification at all**: the XDG Base Directory Specification contains the string `download` zero times and freedesktop's index has no entry for it; what governs it is a tool, its **documented fallback is `$HOME` rather than `$HOME/Downloads`**, and its update tool resets a deleted downloads folder to `$HOME` at next login. **Linux is also the only system where the on-disk name is genuinely translated.** **Apple forbids silent defaulting** in its own words — an app "must not write files to the preceding directories unless explicitly directed to do so by the user" — so proposing the folder is permitted and writing into it unasked is not.

**One trap:** `.NET`'s `Environment.SpecialFolder` has **no Downloads member** and Microsoft's own table records no older equivalent, so there is nothing to fall back on in that route.

**STILL OWED:** whether the macOS route looks the location up or composes it, which wants a compiled Foundation call.

**THE WINDOWS ON-DISK NAME IS ANSWERED BY READING, 2026-08-22, and it is no — corrected here, where this entry carried it under STILL OWED as *whether Windows localises the on-disk name, which wants a Windows computer*.** A known folder has two names in Microsoft's own structure. *KNOWNFOLDER_DEFINITION (shobjidl_core.h)*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/shobjidl_core/ns-shobjidl_core-knownfolder_definition`, read 2026-08-20: `pszRelativePath` *"refers to the physical file system path, and is not localized"*; `pszLocalizedName` is *"the default localized name resource used when the folder is created … in this form: Module name, Resource ID"*, and *"When the folder is created, this string is stored in that folder's copy of Desktop.ini."* **So the physical path is not localized and the translation is a display name held as a resource reference.** The instrument the reading table guessed at is display-only in Microsoft's words too — *SHGetLocalizedName function (shellapi.h)*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/shellapi/nf-shellapi-shgetlocalizedname`, read 2026-08-20: *"Retrieves the localized name of a file in a Shell folder"*, returning *"a pointer to a string resource"* and never a path. **The `desktop.ini` key name is not confirmed** — Microsoft's *How to Customize Folders with Desktop.ini* page lists only `ConfirmFileOp`, `NoSharing`, `IconFile`, `IconIndex` and `InfoTip` — and the answer does not need it.

### Fact 23 — What each of the three operating systems reports when a write to the destination fails, and whether a full disk, a folder that refuses writing, a folder that has been deleted and a drive that is not plugged in can be told apart.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 7.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation, plus a throwaway on a computer of each kind.
**Machine:** none rented. Needs Mac, Windows and Linux.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** Mac and Linux now; the Windows half has no computer behind it.

**ANSWERED FOR MAC BY OBSERVATION, 2026-08-18, and it is the batch's most dangerous finding.** See [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §2.

**Two of the four collapse and a third is worse than a collapse.** At `open()` a full volume gives `ENOSPC` and a refusing folder `EACCES`, both clean; **a removed directory and a detached volume both give `ENOENT`** where the mount point went with the volume, so Smoozy cannot separate them and must not claim to. **Where the mount point survives the detach, `open()` succeeds and the frame lands on the underlying volume with no error anywhere** — reproduced twice each way, neither adjudicated as the normal case.

**A directory deleted underneath an open descriptor reports success at every call** — write, `fsync` and `close` — while the bytes go to an unlinked inode that vanishes at close. **Smoozy will report a frame written that does not exist**, and the only defence is to re-check the destination by path after closing. A detached volume gives `EIO` **once, at the write**, with `fsync` and `close` then returning 0, so an arrangement inspecting only the last call sees a clean finish.

**Free space separates full from refusing** — about 61 GB against about 1 MB — which confirms the direction of the rented-Linux finding. **But it never reads zero: the genuinely full volume reported 1,068 KB**, so a check written as *available equals zero* will never fire. Compare against the volume's size.

**Three corrections to what this project already believed.** *Leaves no file behind* holds only where the volume was **already full at open time**; filling during a write left a truncated file on every byte-loop run and an empty file on every copy-tool run. `ERROR_NOT_ENOUGH_QUOTA` is **not** a disk quota — it is the process's working set. And `EDQUOT` is **69 on macOS and 122 on Linux**. A write to the sealed macOS system volume returned `EPERM` rather than the `EROFS` Apple's own `open(2)` documents; observed, unadjudicated.

**STILL OWED:** **Windows' detached-volume error is not established** — six candidate codes and Microsoft's `WriteFile` page names none — and **Linux's manual pages name none either**. Quota behaviour and blocking writes are documentation-only.

**PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02, and *still owed* is the wrong reading of it. This is not a pending item.** The reading route is exhausted on both systems — no page names a value — and the throwaway route wants a Windows computer, which the Creator's ruling of **2026-08-31** against further rentals will not supply. This is Windows-list item 8.

**Breaks:** `Desktop app and delivery` F3's per-system classification on those two systems, and that document's F4a fourth class with it. **What holds:** F1 and F2, which do not depend on which value comes back, and the whole macOS reading, which is observed. **The silence is already removed by the classification's own fallback:** where a system returns something this project has not read, the adapter degrades to *taken* without a name (`Desktop app and delivery` C18) rather than inventing a class, so nothing is asserted about an ending nobody has seen. `Desktop app and delivery` §22 item 9 is where it is carried.

### Fact 24 — Which characters a file or folder name can carry on each of the three operating systems Smoozy runs on, and what becomes of a name the rented Linux machine accepted and the user's own computer will not.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 8 — a downloaded folder arrives as a folder.
**Split note.** Kept apart from Fact 29, which asks the same of the **rented machine**. This is the receiving end and needs no rental; Fact 29 is the sending end and does.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation; the render side is already answered by the research.
**Machine:** none rented.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** Mac and Linux now; same Windows gap as Fact 23.

**ANSWERED FOR MAC AND LINUX, 2026-08-18, by the reading batch.** See [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §3.

**The project's existing byte-versus-character finding is confirmed from the vendor's own words** — ext4's kernel documentation and POSIX's own definition both give 255 **bytes** — but the unit story is **two units rather than three**: Windows and macOS both count 255 UTF-16 code units, and **Linux stands alone with the binding limit**. The macOS figure is **measured, not documented**, and **Apple's own two manual pages contradict each other** — `pathconf(2)` says bytes, `getattrlist(2)` says characters — with a 765-byte name accepted and a 128-character name refused.

**Three silent failure modes, and only one is on a Mac.** **Windows silently strips trailing periods and spaces** from any path not prefixed with exactly `\\?\`, documented by Microsoft, no error, file lands under a different name — **this is the one that will bite, because Linux produces such names happily**. **Windows silently collapses a reserved stem** — `NUL.png` "is equivalent to NUL", a device rather than a file, so the frame is discarded. And **a Linux case-folding directory silently loses case-insensitive lookup** for a name not valid in the volume's encoding. Everything else measured on the Mac is a visible hard error.

**And one thing directly on Smoozy's path: macOS refuses invalid UTF-8 outright with `EILSEQ`, which Apple documents nowhere, while Linux permits any byte but `/` and NUL.** So a frame can come back carrying a name that cannot be written to an APFS volume at all, and that must be caught before the transfer rather than during it. Separately: **a path-length ceiling limits the pathname handed to one call, not the file's depth** — a fetched tree can go deeper than any absolute path macOS will accept while staying reachable by relative descent.

**STILL OWED, all wanting a Windows computer:** what Windows returns for a forbidden character, a reserved stem, an unpaired surrogate, and an over-long component; and whether a name that survives the long-path prefix is reachable by ordinary Windows software afterwards.

**PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02, on all five. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** All five want a Windows computer and the Creator ruled on **2026-08-31** that there will be no further rental to answer a question. **These are five of the eleven Windows readings** — items 2 to 6 — and this entry had no breaks text of any kind until this line.

**Breaks:** `Download` P4 and P6's per-file honesty on Windows, which rests on `Desktop app and delivery` C7 and C18's documented halves — where Windows returns something this project has not read, the outcome cannot be classed, so the per-file account of what happened to a name is thinner on that system than on the other two. **What holds:** the Mac and Linux readings entire, both observed; Microsoft's own documented silent transformations, which are what C7 is written against; and the mechanisms, which are the adapter's. **The silence is already removed twice over, and both sentences are load-bearing:** C7 **refuses** a name that would be silently transformed, so the transformation cannot happen quietly; and C8 says outright that **nothing is claimed about what Windows returns** and the adapter degrades to *taken* without a name (C18) rather than inventing a class. **What stays genuinely unestablished is only the long-path clause** — whether a name that *does* survive the prefix is reachable by ordinary Windows software afterwards — and no unresolved item in any answer document named it before 2026-09-02.

### Fact 25 — What the connection actually carries in each direction, how many transfers at once against one machine stop helping, and what the number should depend on — measured per server and against the one home line every server shares.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 10 — the per-server pool and the transfer-priority order. *(This line also credited this fact with **the unit being the file** until 2026-09-02. `Rows/File Browser.md` Row 190 records **"Waits on: nothing to get"**, so the unit is not taken from here and the credit made a row look as though it waited on a measurement it does not.)* `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 9 — "how many transfers should run at once against one rented machine over one connection, and what the number should depend on", taken against the user's own speed caps.
**Merge note.** One measurement with two consumers. Both figures in `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` — about 1.3 MB a second down, about 0.48 MB a second up — were taken with other work running and are recorded there as "a floor, not a measurement of the link at its best", so neither is a number to tune against.
**Split note — flagged.** Kept apart from Fact 75. This one is a **measurement**; Fact 75 asks whether a total cap can be **held** across machines and whether the achieved rate can be read. They share a batch, not a number.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with files of several sizes, **and more than one rental at once for the sharing half**, since the pool is per server while the bottleneck measured is the Creator's home line.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED in full including the sharing half, 2026-08-15/16, and it corrects this project's own record about tenfold.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 25. `The rented machine.md` gives 1.3 MB/s down and 0.48 up; measured against the same home line, **12–15 MB/s down and 1.2–1.3 up**. **Down is 11–12 times up, and that asymmetry is the dominant fact about this connection.**

**Concurrency against one machine:** the knee is **two streams** for download — one to two buys 53%, two to four buys 3%, four to eight buys 11% — and around **four** for upload, saturating near 1.85–1.9 MB/s. Most of the gain is hiding a flat 1.0–1.4 s connection setup rather than adding bandwidth.

**The sharing half is the decisive result.** One machine alone gave 13.9–15.2 MB/s, a second alone 14.7–15.0, **both at once 16.5–18.3** — a whole second rented machine bought about **20%, not 100%**, and each machine's throughput roughly halved. **So the limit is the home line and not the server, and a pool that scales with machine count measures the wrong thing.** A per-server number is still needed for a different reason: one provider could not fill its share at all (4.24 MB/s down, 0.35 up), and with three machines running **the slowest set the finish time** — 112 seconds for a job whose fast half finished in 28.

**Still owed:** one home line on one evening over Wi-Fi, and two of the three rentals were the same provider.

### Fact 26 — The method for working out how many output files a job will produce, from the `.blend` and the settings the job was submitted with.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Download.md` Fact 10 — the count of what the job will produce, and the list of formats offered. Open Threads says the Creator holds the method and it is Architecture's to record.
**Answered by:** **the Creator's own knowledge.** The only fact in the register answered that way, and the only one nobody in this stage can get on their own.
**Machine:** none.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now — carry it with the batch request rather than discovering it late.

---

**ANSWERED by the Creator, 2026-08-13.** For each frame the job covers: which view layers render, what the regular output path writes, which file output nodes run and what is connected to each, less what a collision swallows; added up over the frames. Written into `Preparation/Specs/What a job writes.md`.

**The one residue was closed the same day**: a view layer that does not render at a frame writes nothing there, so a node input fed only by it owes no file. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-13). It wanted no machine after all, and does not ride with any batch.

**The Creator's own statement of the method was corrected against the research before being recorded**: view layers do not multiply the count. A frame with several active view layers writes one set of files from the regular output path, never one per layer.


## Facts 27 to 31 — the rented machine's own behaviour, from `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md`

### Fact 27 — Whether a rented machine permits elevated access at all, and how it is obtained on a given machine.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 1 — elevated access, telling a permission refusal apart; records that a negative answer removes the retry offer entirely. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 9 — "whether a machine permits the elevated access **making it ready** needs"; labelled unresolved, owned and cited by `Preparation/Specs/Sources/File Browser.sources.md`.
**Merge note.** Clean merge — Server Setup names the same fact and leans on it far harder than browsing does. The answer is owed at both weights: browsing a folder, and running the ready action.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, posed **under a non-root login**. On the box in `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` root was real root with every capability held, so the refusing case could not arise.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** the browsing weight now; the ready-action weight once the ready action exists.

**ANSWERED across the six-machine batch, 2026-08-15/16, rather than in a section of its own — and the answer differs by machine kind.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), *The machines*, Fact 51 and Fact 48. **On a virtual-machine rental elevated access is ordinarily present and needs no password**: the Sesterce login was uid 1001 **non-root with passwordless sudo**, the Hyperstack login uid 1000 the same with **root login refused by the provider**, and on the Vast boxes the handed-over login was **real root** with a second account (uid 1001) holding passwordless sudo. One trap: the Vast ordinary account was **in the `docker` group**, which is silent root-equivalence.

**On a container rental a non-root login's elevation route demands a password Smoozy does not hold — corrected 2026-08-22, where this said *there is no elevation route at all from a non-root login*** — `sudo -n` demands a password and `/root/onstart.sh` and `/etc/rc.local` are both `Permission denied`. **Nothing downstream changes**: a password Smoozy does not have is as good as no route for the placement's purposes. And **root inside a container is not root**: it holds **13 of 41 capabilities** (`CapPrm/CapEff/CapBnd = 00000000a80405fb`), without `CAP_SYS_MODULE`, `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, `CAP_SYS_PTRACE` or `CAP_SYS_BOOT`, so elevation obtained there still cannot load a module, mount, unmount or read the kernel buffer.

**What it costs where it is absent, observed rather than argued.** Restarting the machine is refused to an ordinary login — `Failed to reboot system via logind: Interactive authentication required.` — because an SSH session is not a local seat (Fact 51); stopping another login's render is refused with `Operation not permitted` (Fact 60); and a non-root Blender search found three of four and was refused the fourth outright (Fact 45).

**THE READY-ACTION WEIGHT IS ANSWERED, 2026-08-18, and it differs by machine kind.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §10. **The fact is now closed on both weights.**

**On a systemd rental the answer is in two halves and the split is the useful part.** *No elevated access is needed for the placement at all* — writing the user unit, enabling it, granting lingering to its own login, the linger read-back and the registration confirmation all ran unprivileged and all succeeded. *Root is needed for everything about preparing the machine to render*, and on this rental that was not hypothetical: the card arrived unusable and the repair was a driver package plus a reboot, refused outright to the supervisor's login. Also refused to it: reading the root home, writing the system configuration directory, and mounting. **Passwordless root exists on the image and is held by the cloud-init account rather than by the login Smoozy runs as** — `ubuntu ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD:ALL` in the image's own drop-in — so the elevated half must be performed as that account, or as root over the connection the rental hands over, and privilege dropped for the placement.

**On a container nothing is open at all**, confirming the earlier answer at the ready action's weight: elevation demands a password the supervisor's login does not have, switching to root fails authentication, the capability set is empty, and each of the three things a ready action needs is refused — installing software, installing the boot hook, and writing anywhere system. **So the container arrangement requires a root step the arrangement itself cannot perform.**

**How the lingering grant actually succeeds, which nobody had established.** The image's own shipped policy reads as though the by-name grant should be refused — it demands administrator authentication in all three columns. It is not refused, because the machine routes a request whose target is the caller's own login to the *self* action, which the image allows outright. **The boundary was proved by asking for another login's:** `Could not enable linger: tester2 … Interactive authentication required.` **A build that ever grants lingering for a different login than the one it is running as is refused on this image.**

### Fact 28 — Whether the machine's free disk space can be read over the connection under whatever login Smoozy holds, what a reading has to exclude to be true, and whether a folder that refused a write can be seen to be taking files again.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 4 — "the widest-reaching fact in the document": a negative answer takes two promises made in other specs with it, and leaves a render killed by a full disk with no diagnosis available from inside the app at all. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 6 — the disk-full carve-out and the failure-clears-itself row, adding the taking-files-again clause. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact H, disk half.
**Split note — flagged, against a sweep's own recommendation.** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact H says the three hardware reads are "one reading with three consumers" and should be answered together. They are kept as **three facts** — 28, 43 and 46 — because they are three different commands with three different machine requirements (more than one disk; more than one card and one with no card; a card under load) and three different consumers. They are answered **in one batch on one box**, so the split costs no rental, and it prevents a yes on one read closing the other two.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, posed under a **non-root login**, and ideally one with **more than one disk**. The box tested had a single 126 GB disk with a 40 GB memory-backed folder beside it that must not be counted.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**THE MORE-THAN-ONE-DISK CLAUSE ANSWERED, 2026-08-16**, on a rental with a volume attached. Free space reads true, by root and by the non-root login, before and after the disk filled. **What a reading has to exclude, in numbers: a plain `df` returned 51 filesystems, 48 of them `tmpfs`** — including one per CPU thermal-throttle folder — and of the three that are not, one is **the host's own 32 GiB disk bind-mounted in to deliver the driver files, showing 20 GiB free that the rental may not use**. So the free-space question is not a reading but a choice of which mount to read, and total size, biggest, and sum all pick the wrong one. **A full disk refuses identically everywhere** — `No space left on device`, errno 28, from a shell redirect, from `cp`, and from a flushed and fsynced write — and **leaves no file behind where the volume was already full when the write opened**. **Corrected 2026-08-21: this said *leaves no file behind*, flat, and Fact 23 in this same file had already narrowed it on 2026-08-18.** That entry's words: *"Leaves no file behind holds only where the volume was **already full at open time**; filling during a write left a truncated file on every byte-loop run and an empty file on every copy-tool run."* **The Linux run only ever posed the already-full case** — the volume was filled with `dd` first and every write tested afterwards opened onto a full volume — so **the partway-fill case is not posed on Linux at all**, and the flat sentence generalised past what was run. **This is the correction that can lose a frame**: anything written against the flat form reads *no file at the destination* as *nothing was written*, and on a partway fill there is a **truncated file sitting at the final name**. **Two architecture documents already carried the narrow form and one register entry did not** — `Desktop app and delivery` F9 states the defence outright, *"no local writer ever reads the absence of a file as evidence that nothing was written"*, and `The user's own computer` §2 agrees; this entry was the last place still disagreeing with them. **The Linux half will not be settled, and is recorded permanently unresolved 2026-09-01**, on the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-31 that no further rented machine will be taken. *(The test it wanted is kept for the record: start a write larger than the free space onto a volume not yet full, let it fail, list the destination; a negative is an empty listing; one hour on any Linux machine riding a batch already going. **It is not owed and nobody should re-open it as though it were pending.**)* **What breaks, and it is a frame:** `Job files and outputs` C12 to C15 count a file present at the final name as already there, so on a partway fill of the rented machine's mount the boundary frame — the one being written when the mount filled — is either absent, in which case a carry-on re-renders it and nothing is lost, or **short but not empty, in which case the carry-on opens at the next frame and the user keeps a broken picture under a finished frame's name for good, with the frame count coming up short as the only signal and no signal at all where the file has bytes.** Scenario replay *The server disk fills up during render*, finding N-b, whose arithmetic puts the scenario's own frame 40 exactly there. `Job files and outputs` §14 item 23 carries it on that document's side. **A folder that refused a write can be seen taking files again by both signals**: the reading recovers the instant the space is freed, and a copy into that same folder succeeds. See [Two more boxes](Two%20more%20boxes%20—%20the%20volume,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20a%20day%20left%20running.md).

### Fact 29 — What a rented machine's filesystem **and its tools** accept in a name and a whole path: capitals, spaces, characters outside the Latin alphabet, the longest single name and the longest whole path, what a write over the limit does, whether an arbitrary name from the user's computer reaches the machine unmangled, and what happens to two jobs given the same label.

**Named by, three sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 5 — "whether an arbitrary file or folder name from the user's computer reaches the machine unmangled: capitals, spaces, characters outside the Latin alphabet, and length"; wanted by a folder going up as a folder, and collisions judged by name and place.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact E — "what a rented machine's folder names will take: whether any label a user might type can become part of one, and what happens to two jobs given the same label"; insists it be posed against **the machine's tools as well as its file system**.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/What a job writes.md` Fact 8 — "the longest single name and the longest whole path a rented machine accepts, and what a write over that limit does"; wanted by the shared-run rule, which deepens every foreign path by construction.

**Merge note — flagged.** Three sweeps, three angles on what the machine takes in a name. Merged because one run answers all three: send hostile names from the Mac, write them, list them, and run the machine's own tools over them. `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` recorded 255 characters for a name and about 4096 for a path on one box, now destroyed, and **one space in a path breaking three tools three different ways**. What a job writes records that a name too long is refused at the moment of writing on the regular output path, and that the same case for a node's output was never asked.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, posed **from the Creator's own Mac** — the whole question is what happens between two filesystems that disagree. Nothing outside the Latin alphabet has been tried on any rented machine by this project.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**THE OPEN HALVES ANSWERED, 2026-08-17, and they found the negative.** See [One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §7.

**Longest single name 255 bytes; longest whole path 4095.** Both refuse **at open, before any byte is sent**, and leave nothing behind.

**The unit is bytes and not characters, and this entry said characters until 2026-08-17.** The 2026-08-17 tester posed the length with ASCII names and reported 255 characters; the six-machine batch had already established **255 bytes, which is 85 CJK characters**, and the runner's first wording of this entry carried the tester's word rather than the earlier measurement. **A length check written in characters passes a name the machine refuses**, and the writer of `Job files and outputs` caught it against that document. Corrected here; the two batches do not disagree about the machine, only about how the first report was worded. **The refusal for both is `Bad message`**, which reads as protocol corruption rather than *name too long* — anything surfacing that string to a user reports the wrong problem entirely.

**Two jobs given the same label.** The file-transfer channel refuses the second with a **generic** failure code, so **that protocol gives no way to tell *already exists* from any other failure** without a separate stat and the race that comes with it. The command channel names it exactly. **And the make-parents form — what a builder usually writes — returns success against the existing folder**, which is the silent collision path where two jobs land in one folder with nothing reported.

**The machine's own tools held.** Quoted, a path with spaces and a 4039-character nested path were listed, found, archived and removed cleanly. **This box did not reproduce the earlier box's one-space-breaks-three-tools behaviour.**

**The negative, and it is silent.** All seventeen hostile names were written on the rental and **fourteen of seventeen came back to the Mac**. Three files were destroyed on arrival: names differing only in capitals collapse to one file holding the **last** payload fetched, and two spellings of the same accented name collapse the same way. **Every fetch reported success — no error, no warning, no exception.** A control in the same sitting proves the loss is at the Mac end rather than in transit, and everything else survived byte for byte including spaces, Cyrillic, Japanese, emoji, colon, backslash, quote, newline, tab and a leading dash.

**What that means beyond this fact: frames rendered into distinct names on a rental can arrive on the user's computer as fewer files than were made, with nothing indicating anything was lost.** It bears on Fact 16 and Fact 24 from the other side, and on Row 303's receiving-filesystem collision judgement.

**Limits:** one Mac, one case-insensitive volume at default settings — **a case-sensitive volume would behave differently and was not posed.**

### Fact 30 — Whether Smoozy can tell that a file on the user's own computer changed while it was being read, on each operating system it runs on, including the case of a program that saves by writing a new file and swapping it in.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 8 — telling that the file has not changed, and carrying on inside a single file.
**Answered by:** a throwaway on the Creator's own computer, and on a computer of each other supported kind.
**Machine:** **none rented.** The one thing it does need is a real Blender save and a real texture export to test against, not a synthetic write, since the swap-in case is exactly what an application does and not what a test script does.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** Mac now; the other operating systems need only that operating system.

**THE MAC HALF ANSWERED, 2026-08-20, and both shapes were found — the fear in the spec's own sources is real.** Posed for `File Browser` on the Creator's own Mac (macOS 26.5.2, APFS at default settings) with Blender 4.5.12 and 5.0.1, each launched by the throwaway itself; **nothing pre-existing was touched** and the Creator's open Blender instance was left alone. The evidence, the control and the limits are in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/File Browser.sources.md`; the answer of record is `Preparation/Architecture/File Browser.md` 9.3.

**The register's own requirement was honoured — a real save, not a synthetic write — and it is what made the answer worth having.** **A `.blend` save is a swap-in**: a temporary `name.blend@` renamed over the final name, with size, inode and modification time all moving in **one atomic transition** caught inside a 100 ms poll, and **the handle held open across it going on delivering the pre-save file whole**. That is exactly the case the sources file said *"may slip past detection"* — it does not slip past a **path** reading, and it defeats anything that trusts the open handle. **An image export writes in place**: truncate to zero and rewrite under the **same inode** at a **byte-identical size**, with only the modification time moving. **So one application produces two shapes, and each defeats the check the other suggests** — inode and size both miss the export, and a held handle misses the save.

**Control:** a run with no save produced zero identity-change events and three equal digests. **What a negative would have looked like:** a real save moving none of size, modification time or inode at the path. It did not occur.

**Still owed: Windows and Linux**, which have no computer behind them in this project, and applications other than Blender. **What is owed there was narrowed on 2026-08-20, and one half came back worse rather than merely unknown.**

**The granularity of each system's timestamps and identifiers is not part of this fact's machine debt at all — most of it reads.** **Windows values:** *"A *file time* is a 64-bit value that represents the number of 100-nanosecond intervals that have elapsed since 12:00 A.M. January 1, 1601 Coordinated Universal Time (UTC)."* **Windows identifiers:** *"The identifier (low and high parts) and the volume serial number uniquely identify a file on a single computer."*, with a 128-bit form on ReFS through a named call, and with the page's own two cautions — identifiers may be reused over time, and a replace operation keeps the **replacement's** identifier. **macOS:** posed on the Creator's Mac, nanosecond modification and change times and a real inode on APFS. **Linux:** ext4's own kernel documentation widens the times to nanoseconds only *"If the inode structure size … is larger than 128 bytes"* — **and the same document states the default outright, which this entry said was documented nowhere.** *(Corrected 2026-08-23; the publication is right and the register was wrong, found by the fresh reader's pass over `File Browser`.)* `Documentation/filesystems/ext4/inodes.rst`, read 2026-08-23: *"By default, ext4 inode records are 256 bytes, and (as of August 2019) the inode structure is 160 bytes (``i_extra_isize = 32``)."*, and `mke2fs.conf(5)` at e2fsprogs' own source sets `inode_size = 256` under both `[defaults]` and `ext4`, with `128` under `floppy` as the override case. **So what is open is not the default but which filesystem a given Linux machine actually carries**, which one `stat` settles and which no publication can. Addresses and exact words at `Sources/File Browser.sources.md`.

**And Windows' *earliness* half is documented to be unreliable rather than untested, which is worse than parked.** Microsoft's own *File Times* page: *"The only guarantee about a file time stamp is that the file time is correctly reflected when the handle that makes the change is closed."* and *"When writing to a file, the last write time is not fully updated until all handles that are used for writing are closed."* A watch that re-reads the path identity **while** a file is being written is reading, on Windows, exactly the value Microsoft declines to guarantee — for any writer. **Size may still move; the modification time is not promised to.** The obvious narrower repair fails too: the page draws no distinction between reading the value by opening the path and reading it by enumerating the directory, so preferring one has no first-party basis. **What genuinely remains this fact's and wants a fresh blind Blender researcher** is whether every real application save moves the path identity on those two systems. **What that costs is bounded and is stated where it is used**: `File Browser` K9 gates every publication on a contents digest rather than on any stat value, so a change no path reading catches is still caught before anything is published, and **the no-corruption promise holds on all three systems**. *(Round 1 of `File Browser` corrected the second half of this line, which had read "never the promise": what is unestablished on the other two systems is how early the watch fires, and with it **Row 201's third clause's saying and offering** — where a real save moves no path-visible value the resume either starts over silently or carries on silently, and in neither case does Smoozy say the file changed and offer the new version. That is a promise not firing, not a wording. `File Browser` K13 and Section 13 item 6 carry it.)*

### Fact 31 — Whether a file deleted on a rented machine is recoverable by anything on that machine.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/File Browser.md` Fact 9 — deleting is permanent. Labelled an assumption in the spec; the answer changes a sentence the user reads before an irreversible act.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental. Cheapest question in the register; rides on any box.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

---

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-13** — no. ext4, no `trash-cli`, no trash folder for root, no snapshotting filesystem. What is deleted is gone.


## Facts 32 to 39 — keys and secrets, from `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md`

None of these wants a rented machine. `Preparation/Specs/Whole app.md` states that Keys works only on the user's own computer and never talks to a server at all. All are facts got by reading — OpenSSH's own manuals at man.openbsd.org for the SSH ones, each operating system's own documentation for the rest — each answer carrying the exact words quoted, the address, and the date read.

**Windows is the standing gap in five of them.** Reading answers a documentation question without a computer, so none is blocked; what is blocked is **confirming** any of them against a real Windows installation.

### Fact 32 — Which SSH key types are current, which of them a Smoozy-supported OpenSSH accepts, and which is the sensible modern default.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 1 — the key type row, what makes a key usable, what generates the key. Cross-named by `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 10 ("answer it with the Keys questions Open Threads parks — which key types Smoozy offers") and by `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 1 ("settle with the key-type question ... since the answer may differ by type").
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own manuals.
**Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16.** The documented `-t` set is ecdsa, ecdsa-sk, ed25519, ed25519-sk, mldsa44-ed25519 and rsa, with **ed25519 the type generated when no argument is given**; the client's and server's default `PubkeyAcceptedAlgorithms` lists are quoted in full in the answer. **DSA appears nowhere in any of the six pages** — an absence of documentation, which is not the same as a documented removal, and the answer says so. **One caveat governs every OpenSSH answer here:** `man.openbsd.org` serves OpenBSD-current and runs ahead of shipping systems — it documents an `mldsa44-ed25519` type the observed OpenSSH 10.2p1 does not offer — so **the accepted type list must be read from the installed binary at runtime, never hardcoded from the manual**. *Which type is the sensible modern default* is a judgement rather than a documented fact; the manual states only its own no-argument default and labels SHA1 `ssh-rsa` not recommended. See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

### Fact 33 — Where each of Mac, Windows and Linux keeps its SSH keys, and whether each has exactly one such place.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 2 — "the heaviest fact in the document"; four promises rest on it and one decides whether delete destroys files. The spec states the one-place premise as something to confirm rather than assume, so the answer has to rule on it.
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own manuals for the convention, and each operating system's own documentation where it differs.
**Machine:** none; a Windows computer would confirm it and does not exist. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16, and the one-place premise is false.** All three systems use `~/.ssh` (`%USERPROFILE%\.ssh` on Windows), but OpenSSH's manuals call it the **default** location and never the only one. Four documented routes put a usable key elsewhere: `IdentityFile` and `-i` can name any path and may appear many times; a key can exist only inside `ssh-agent` with no file behind it; a PKCS#11 token supplies keys with no file at all; and the home directory is itself a variable. Windows has a second Microsoft-named location, `C:\ProgramData\ssh`, and it is **the host-key and server-configuration directory rather than a second home for a user's own identities — corrected 2026-08-22, where this stood as a bare second location under a heading about where keys live.** Microsoft, *Key-Based Authentication in OpenSSH for Windows*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/openssh/openssh_keymanagement`, read 2026-08-20: *"Because there's no user associated with the `sshd` service, the host keys are stored under C:\ProgramData\ssh."*, with `administrators_authorized_keys` in the same directory, while a user's own key is offered at `C:\Users\<name>\.ssh`. **And the unresolved this entry carried onward — that whether the files there include private key files is not stated — is closed, 2026-08-22.** *OpenSSH Server Configuration for Windows*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows-server/administration/openssh/openssh-server-configuration`, read 2026-08-20, gives the `HostKey` defaults as `__PROGRAMDATA__/ssh/ssh_host_rsa_key` and its three siblings, and OpenSSH's own `sshd_config` manual, `https://man.openbsd.org/sshd_config#HostKey`, read 2026-08-20, says a `HostKey` *"Specifies a file containing a private host key used by SSH."* **So private key files do live there and they are the machine's.** The directory's default ACL is unread and nothing here depends on it. **The consequence for the delete promise: Smoozy cannot assume a listed key's file is `~/.ssh/<name>`, and must track per key whether a file backs it at all.** The Keys spec already decides ownership by location — managed against referenced — so no promise breaks. Windows is documentation alone. See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

### Fact 34 — What else ordinarily lives in that location besides key pairs, and how a key file is told from anything else there.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 3. The answer decides whether a file that was never a key appears to the user as a broken key.
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own manuals. **Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16, with one trap that matters.** Besides key pairs, the location ordinarily holds `config`, `known_hosts`, `authorized_keys` and `authorized_keys2`, `environment`, `rc`, `-cert.pub` certificates, arbitrary `Include` fragments — so **no closed filename list is possible** — and an `agent/` subdirectory of sockets. **The trap, observed:** `ssh-keygen -l -f` correctly rejects `config`, plain text and empty files at exit 255, but **accepts `known_hosts` at exit 0 and fingerprints it**, because a `known_hosts` line differs from a public key line only by a leading hostname field. **Any fingerprint-based scan must exclude `known_hosts` and `authorized_keys` by name before reading.** **The `authorized_keys` half was asserted by analogy here and has since been run: observed 2026-08-20 on the Creator's Mac, OpenSSH 10.2p1, `ssh-keygen -l -f` fingerprints an `authorized_keys` file at exit 0, an options-prefixed line included** (`Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Keys.sources.md`). One build; every other build is still unobserved. **`Keys` 4.1 does not lean on the exclusion at all** — its listing rule discriminates by the private-key container test and never by a successful fingerprint — so the answer confirms the trap rather than being relied on. See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

### Fact 35 — What can be read out of a private key file without its passphrase — its type, its fingerprint, and whether it is passphrase-protected at all — and whether a connection attempt can be made that cannot fall back to asking for one.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 4 — "shape-deciding for this module". Two promises fail outright on a no: that keys under a passphrase are supported exactly as ones Smoozy made itself, and `Preparation/Specs/Servers.md`'s rule that Smoozy does not attempt a connection where the key needs a passphrase it is not holding. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 10 — "whether a key file can be seen to need a passphrase without having the passphrase, and whether a connection attempt can be made that cannot fall back to asking for one".
**Merge note — flagged.** Servers 10 has two halves. The first is Keys 4 exactly. The second — a connection attempt that cannot fall back to prompting — is a fact about the SSH client's behaviour rather than about the key file. Merged because Servers itself poses them as one fact and both are answered from OpenSSH's own manuals in one sitting; the second half is named explicitly so it cannot be lost.
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own manuals, on the private key file format, on `ssh-keygen -l`, and on the client's own prompting behaviour.
**Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** for one module. **Posable:** now.

**HALF A ANSWERED 2026-08-16; HALF B ANSWERED 2026-08-20.** **Half A:** type, bit size and fingerprint are all readable from an encrypted private key file with no passphrase, and stay readable with the `.pub` deleted; the **comment is not recoverable** without the passphrase. Passphrase-protection is detectable **without prompting**, by `ssh-keygen -y -P '' -f` — exit 0 against 255 — or by reading the cipher and KDF fields out of the key container. Both are observed behaviour on one build, not a documented contract: **the manual documents no command whose stated purpose is to report whether a key has a passphrase.** **HALF B ANSWERED, 2026-08-20, by the second of the two tests this entry itself named — and the entry went on calling it *the important gap* until 2026-08-21.** The documentation half of the complaint stands and is unchanged: `ssh_config(5)` still names only *"password prompts and host key confirmation requests"*, and a passphrase is neither. **What is no longer unestablished is the behaviour.** Run on the Creator's own Mac against a throwaway server on the loopback address, **with a terminal deliberately attached**, because with no terminal the test proves nothing. **Two controls first:** an encrypted key with batch mode off prompted *"Enter passphrase for key"* twice and then refused; the same rig with the correct passphrase supplied authenticated. **Then the test:** the same encrypted key, the same terminal, batch mode on — **no prompt at all**, refused at exit 255, with the verbose trace showing the client offering the key and the server accepting it before the attempt died. **A negative would have been the prompt appearing exactly as in the control.** It did not. **So the construction is observed rather than inferred**, on one build, and the label comes off in `Security and licence` §4. **What the entry got wrong is worth keeping in view:** it named a test against a real server as one of the two things that would settle it, then carried the question as open for four days without anyone running it. An earlier run of the same shape had been declined for having no control; this one has two. See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

### Fact 36 — How the public file and a certificate file are named relative to a private key file.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 5. **Partly cited already and not enough**: `Preparation/Specs/Sources/Keys.sources.md` quotes `ssh-keygen(1)`'s FILES section on `id_*.pub` and cites CERTIFICATES for what a certificate is, but neither entry carries the naming rule this needs, and the certificate half is the thinner of the two.
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own manuals, FILES and CERTIFICATES. **Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16.** The public file is the private key path plus `.pub`. The certificate is the private key path plus `-cert.pub` — **the `.pub` is dropped rather than doubled**, so a key at `id_ed25519` has `id_ed25519.pub` and `id_ed25519-cert.pub`. Documented in three places and confirmed by signing a throwaway key. See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

### Fact 37 — Whether a wrong passphrase can be told apart from every other reason a key could not be used or a connection refused.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 7 — the one fact in that document whose answer may not be settled by documentation alone.
**Answered by:** reading first — the documentation of whatever makes the connection, and OpenSSH's own manuals. Where reading does not settle it, **a throwaway on the Creator's own computer against any reachable SSH server**, not a rental.
**Machine:** none rented. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16, and the answer is mostly negative.** Documentation settles almost nothing: `ssh(1)`'s entire exit-status contract is "255 if an error occurred", `ssh-keygen(1)` has no EXIT STATUS section at all, and **no OpenSSH manual page documents a single one of the diagnostic strings the tools emit** — verified by searching seven pages. Every distinction therefore rests on parsing undocumented text. **Loading a key locally separates the causes** by message: a wrong passphrase gives `incorrect passphrase supplied to decrypt private key`; malformed, truncated, empty and public-as-private all give `invalid format`; too-open permissions give `bad permissions` and pre-empt the passphrase entirely. **Connecting does not.** At default log level a wrong passphrase, an empty passphrase, no passphrase available, a key not authorised, and a wrong account name **all collapse into `Permission denied (publickey).` at exit 255**, separating only at `-vv`. **And `incorrect passphrase` is not proof of a wrong passphrase:** the correct passphrase against a one-bit-corrupted key produced the identical message, and a flip inside the blob produced **exit 0 with correct-looking output** — silent corruption of the secret half. **A further hazard:** retrying a passphrase against a real server tripped the default source penalty, after which attempts *with the correct passphrase* failed as a closed connection, indistinguishable client-side from a broken server. All observed on one build against one throwaway server; nothing here is documented, so nothing is guaranteed stable. **This supports rather than breaks the whole-app rule that Smoozy does not guess at a cause it cannot establish.** See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

### Fact 38 — What each of Mac, Windows and Linux offers for holding a secret on the user's own computer; what the user has to do to grant it; what they meet when it is refused, moved or lost; and what using it costs them — a place to look, something to type, or a thing that can be lost.

**Named by, three sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 8 — "not this module's to answer alone"; `Preparation/Specs/Whole app.md` and Open Threads both say the six things Smoozy holds are **one question rather than six**. The cost half is written in deliberately, because that is the clause that sends the answer back to the Creator.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 11 — "how each of the six things Smoozy holds is kept safely, on each operating system"; three rows in that document cannot be answered before it.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 3 — "the whole of the biggest single question in this stage"; the clause that sends it back to Specs turns on the answer's cost to the user.

**Merge note.** Clean merge — all three sweeps say outright it is one question.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation, and trying it on a computer running each.
**Machine:** none rented. The Creator has an Apple-silicon Mac; no Windows machine has run it, and none is rented today.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but it is the largest single question in the stage and it feeds back into Specs where the cost lands on the user.
**Posable:** now by reading, on all three; the Windows third closes as unresolved for confirmation unless a computer appears.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16, and the cost half goes to the Creator.** **macOS** has two stores behind one interface and the choice decides everything: the file-based keychain is governed by per-item access lists, so a caller not on the list triggers a system dialog, and Apple documents that the grant is lost when the application is changed or updated; the data protection keychain does not prompt but needs an entitlement authorised by a provisioning profile inside an application bundle. **Windows** never asks — no prompt is documented on the generic-credential path — and the price is Microsoft's own sentence that any program running as that user can read the store, so there is **no per-application boundary and therefore no refusal state to distinguish**. **Linux has no single answer**: the specification is a draft that mandates no access control at all, availability depends on which desktop is installed, and it is the only one of the three with a documented memory-only collection. **Three limits bound all of it.** Windows is documentation alone and its two load-bearing pages are archived, one dated 2001. Linux was never observed — the only machine available is a Mac. And **one observation is a recorded disagreement**: a keychain write that returned a missing-entitlement error on the first run returned success on the same day's repeat, so that behaviour is **not established**, both runs stand side by side, and three claims that leaned on the first were cut. **Ruled 2026-08-31: permanently unresolved — nothing recorded distinguishes the two runs, no side is picked, and it will not be re-run. Guard: the struck claim that every unentitled write is refused must never be quietly recovered; one write succeeded, and that observation stands whatever caused the difference.** **The cost half is the third escalation** — a place to look, something to type, a thing that can be lost, on all three — and it goes to the Creator rather than being written. See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

**The cost half was ruled on 2026-08-16 and is now a spec promise.** Mac remembers, accepting the system's own dialog after an update and the loss of everything remembered on a login-password reset; Windows remembers silently, accepting that another program running as that user can read the store; Linux remembers where the computer has somewhere to remember, and where it has nowhere Smoozy asks each time and says so once rather than pretending. Label: **Creator-stated** (2026-08-16). It is written into [Whole app](../Specs/Whole%20app.md)'s Security concern with its decisions-record line, and **an architecture document takes what the user meets from there rather than choosing it again** — what remains architecture's is which store on each system and how it is reached. The technical half of this fact stands unchanged, disagreement and all.

### Fact 39 — What each of Mac, Windows and Linux offers for generating an SSH key pair of a given type, with a given comment and an optional passphrase, and whether it is present without Smoozy installing anything.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Keys.md` Fact 9.
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own manuals and each operating system's own documentation. **Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16, and it must be probed rather than assumed.** `ssh-keygen` is the tool on all three, with `-t`, `-C`, `-N ""` and `-f`. **macOS**: present at `/usr/bin/ssh-keygen`, confirmed by generating a throwaway key; it sets the file modes itself — observed 0600 private and 0644 public on 2026-08-20 — but **does not create a missing parent directory** — **challenged 2026-08-20 and the challenge failed, so this clause now stands on four observations rather than one.** The claim put to it was that the tool creates `~/.ssh` at 0700 and prints *"Created directory"* when the target sits inside it, and that the project only saw otherwise by pointing the target elsewhere. Posed four ways on the Creator's Mac — target inside a missing directory literally named `.ssh`, target two levels deep in missing directories, target one level deep in a differently-named directory, and the path typed at the tool's own interactive prompt rather than given with `-f`. **All four failed at exit 1 with `No such file or directory` and created nothing, and the phrase *"Created directory"* never appeared.** One variant could not be posed and does not matter: the tool takes its default path from the password database rather than from `HOME` — observed — so its *own default* path on an account with no `.ssh` is untested, and `Keys` G9 always supplies the path itself. *(That second run also observed the tool refusing an existing `-f` target with no terminal available — `already exists… Overwrite (y/n)?`, exit 1, the file byte-identical after — which is what `Keys` 6.4 uses as its collision backstop. One build; `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Keys.sources.md`.)* **Windows**: **corrected 2026-08-20, on the Creator's challenge, and the correction reverses the headline.** The install-state table this fact was answered from is on Microsoft's OpenSSH **overview** page, is headed *"SSH install state"*, **does not distinguish the client from the server**, and has no Windows 11 row; its Windows Server 2025 row reads *"Installed but not enabled"*, a state only the server service has. Microsoft's **manufacturing** documentation — *Available features on demand*, `ms.date` 2026-02-20, monikers windows-10 and windows-11 — lists **OpenSSH Client under its own heading "Preinstalled FODs"**, defined as *"come preinstalled in a Windows image"*, available *"Windows 10, version 1709 and later"*, while **OpenSSH Server sits in that page's "FODs that are not preinstalled" section**. **`ssh-keygen` ships in the client**, which the overview page's own command list places beside `ssh` and apart from `sshd`. **So `ssh-keygen` is present on an ordinary Windows 10 or 11 installation**, and the earlier reading — that a fresh Windows computer cannot make a key — was the server's state applied to the client. **Smoozy still probes rather than assumes**, because the manufacturing page speaks about the shipped image and an OEM or enterprise image can have the capability removed, and it still **must not silently run the elevated install command**. Both pages are quoted in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Keys.sources.md`; no Windows machine has run it and neither reading is confirmed against a real installation. **Linux**: **corrected 2026-08-21, and it is the Windows half's error a second time — the page read was about the server.** An install command is what documentation gives whether or not the thing is already there, and it is not a statement of absence. Debian's own package database puts `/usr/bin/ssh-keygen` in **`openssh-client`**, and the openssh packaging files in **both Debian and Ubuntu** carry `Priority: standard` with no override on that binary — which Debian Policy defines as *"a reasonably small but not too limited character-mode system. This is what will be installed by default if the user doesn't select anything else."*, against `optional` for everything a user must ask for. **So the ordinary Debian or Ubuntu installation has it.** **Probe, do not assume still stands, on the changed reason** — a priority is a claim about the archive's default selection rather than a guarantee about one machine, and a minimal container image or a trimmed appliance can lack it. **Red Hat and Fedora are unread**, their documentation sites having refused retrieval. Windows and Linux are documentation alone. See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

**Added 2026-08-21 — a passphrase need never appear on the command line, and this project had already been doing it.** `ssh-keygen(1)`'s ENVIRONMENT section documents only `SSH_SK_PROVIDER`, so the page really does not offer an alternative to `-N` — **but the mechanism exists and works.** Run on the Creator's Mac, `OpenSSH_10.2p1`, 2026-08-20, with a two-line askpass helper in a scratch directory and **no `-N` and no `-P` anywhere on the command line**, standard input from `/dev/null`: `env -u DISPLAY SSH_ASKPASS=<scratch>/ask.sh SSH_ASKPASS_REQUIRE=force FAKEPASS='correct horse' ssh-keygen -q -t ed25519 -f <scratch>/k1` exited 0, and reading the key back through the same helper printed its public half at exit 0. **Three negative controls:** the wrong passphrase through the same helper gave *"incorrect passphrase supplied to decrypt private key"*, exit 255; askpass turned off with no terminal gave the interactive prompt and then the same failure; and asserting an empty passphrase on the command line failed too — so the key really is protected and the helper really is what supplies the value. Label: **observed, not documented** — `ssh-keygen(1)` names neither variable. **Why this matters architecturally:** a passphrase in `argv` is visible to every process on the machine that can run `ps`, and the architecture had a hazard with no route out. **It has a route: `SSH_ASKPASS` with `SSH_ASKPASS_REQUIRE=force`.** This project's own Fact 37 tables had already used it seventeen times and written down that they had.

## Facts 40 to 44 — the queue on the machine, from `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md`

### Fact 40 — Whether a rented machine can be read, over the connection Smoozy holds, for the work sitting on it and for the note it keeps against each job.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact C, wanted by thirteen rows and carrying both that document's `[PENDING]` markers. The sources file treats the two as one ability — "it is the same ability as reading a machine for the work sitting on it" — and Open Threads parks them as one item.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental. **Ask the disk-full form too** — the sources file calls this the fact most likely to fail exactly when a job needs it.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but a negative answer takes thirteen rows with it.
**Posable:** the plain half now — whether a machine can be listed and read at all under whatever login Smoozy holds, on the Fact 1 throwaway. The full question **only once the shape exists**, since there is nothing on the machine yet to write a note or hold a line.

**ANSWERED IN FULL, 2026-08-18 — yes, including the disk-full form, on both machine kinds.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §15.1. Each tester wrote its own negative-result line first. Over the connection, on both boxes: the line answered every job with its state, its admission point, its operation and its unreleased hold; the note answered attempt count, ending with outcome and detail, re-run marks and label — **and a null ending for a job that has not ended rather than inventing one**; the history answered its count and its entries.

**With the filesystem at a genuine zero, all of them answered normally and truthfully**, before the refused writes and again after them, including free space as `0` rather than a stale figure. **No read reported success from nothing.** **Difference between the machine kinds: none either tester could point to** — these are reads of files the login owns over an endpoint the login owns. **This is the case the sources file calls the most likely to fail exactly when a job needs it, and it did not.**

### Fact 41 — Whether the machine's own copy of a history entry can be removed over a connection.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact D — deleting reaching both copies. Labelled unresolved, checked and standing.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** as Fact 1, and posed **after** Fact 40, since there is nothing to remove until something writes it.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** only once the shape exists.

**ANSWERED IN PART, 2026-08-18, on both machine kinds independently — the subject ends and the bytes stay.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §15.2. Each tester wrote its own negative-result line first.

**What ends:** the history entry, the note, the collection record and the queue entry, all of them, with the removal journalled and **no other job's material touched.**

**What does not:** the captured streams, the rendered frame and the launch material **all survive on disk under the removed job's own identity, fully readable, while the machine reports nothing about it.** Both boxes, independently, and both testers noted that the throwaway's own expectation was that the streams end with the subject.

**What it means for whoever answers the row:** *removing the machine's own copy* is answered for everything the machine will afterwards **tell** you about, and unanswered for what the machine still **holds**. **Thin: one removal per box, not repeated.**

### Fact 42 — Whether a rented machine bills by the hour rather than more finely.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact G — wanted by the idle half hour and by nothing else. **Nothing about the feature turns on it** — only the spec's stated reason, which would have to be corrected rather than left standing.
**Answered by:** reading a provider's own pricing page, quoted, with the address and the date read.
**Machine:** none. Do not put it in a rental batch.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now, once a provider is named. The date matters here more than anywhere.

**ANSWERED for Vast.ai by reading, 2026-08-21, at no cost and with no machine.** Vast.ai, *Billing*, `https://docs.vast.ai/guides/reference/billing`, read 2026-08-20: *"You are charged the base active rental cost for every second your instance is in the active/connected state."* and *"There are separate prices and charges for: Active rental (GPU) (in $/hr), Storage costs (in $/GB/hr), Bandwidth costs (in $/TB)."* **The rate is quoted per hour and the charge accrues per second.** The project got it wrong by reading an hourly **rate** on a listing as hourly **billing**. **Scope, and it matters here more than anywhere:** this settles Vast.ai and nothing else. Sesterce and Hyperstack have both been rented from and neither was read. **What is refuted is the blanket form** — *a rented machine bills by the hour* as a fact about rentals in general. **What it cost:** one sentence in `Preparation/Specs/Queue & History.md`, its sources file, and the quote in `Rows/Queue & History.md`. **The half-hour idle message is untouched** — a machine doing nothing costs money at any granularity — which is what that spec's own sources file predicted before anyone read anything.

### Fact 43 — Whether a card's live memory use can be read over a connection under whatever login Smoozy holds.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact H, card half — the readings taken from the machine. Parked at Architecture twice: as the live card reading raised by that spec, and inside the wider question of whether a machine's hardware can be read over a connection at all.
**Split note:** see Fact 28. Kept apart from 28 and 46, answered in the same batch.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** a rental with a working card. One card answers the reading itself; the **several-card case is separately not established** — the runs behind the printed half kept one line of each render and threw the rest away — so a machine with more than one card answers more.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-15/16, and the several-card case this register records as never established is now established.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 43. With a render pinned to card 0, `nvidia-smi` returned per card: identity, model, total, used and free memory, processor and memory-bus utilisation, temperature and power draw — card 0 at `2801 MiB` used, 97% and 229.47 W against card 1 at `0 MiB`, 0% and 7.80 W. **The idle card reading zero at the same instant rules out every way this could have been a machine-wide figure**, and both read zero once the render stopped. The compute-apps listing names **which process is on which card**, by the card's UUID and the process's own path and pid. **It works unprivileged**, because `/dev/nvidia*` are `crw-rw-rw-`.

**Two figures, not one.** Card-level `2801 MiB` against process-level `2792 MiB` at the same instant — **9 MiB of driver context not attributed to the process** — so anything comparing what a render is using against what the card has must know which it is reading. Add the idle floor: **483 MiB of each card was already held by the driver**, so free is not total.

**Still owed:** two renders on two cards at once, and one render spanning both, were not posed; nor whether a non-root login can see another user's process in the compute-apps list, which matters for attribution.

### Fact 44 — What a machine losing power outright leaves at a job's output path.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact I — the power-cut row, and behind it every row that reads a frame's presence. Labelled unresolved, checked and standing.
**Answered by:** a hand test on a machine whose power can be cut at the wall.
**Machine:** **not a rental.** The sources file says outright that "the power of a rented machine cannot be cut over the network." Do **not** put it in a rental batch. Open Threads already carries it as a hand test due "whenever a machine that can be switched off at the wall is available", and **the Creator has declined to have their own Mac's power cut; a guest machine killed at the hypervisor, which never touches the Mac's own power, was never put to them and is now with them — corrected 2026-08-22, where this said only that the Creator has declined to have their own Mac used for it.** The nearest reachable case, a reset at the kernel, is already answered (*Frames 13*) and is not this.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** **not at all today.** **No machine this project runs today can pose it — corrected 2026-08-22, where this said *No machine in this project can pose it*.** The blocker is what is installed rather than what exists: the Creator's Mac has no hypervisor and no container runtime installed at all, and installing free virtualisation software is the Creator's own act.

**PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED unless the Creator takes the hypervisor route, 2026-09-01.** Added out of scenario replay *The provider reboots the machine*, finding 1. There will be no further rental (the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-31) and a rental could never pose this one anyway; the hypervisor route is the only one open and it is with the Creator, at `What Build inherits` item 4. **Until they take it this fact does not arrive, and nothing may be written as though it were coming.**

**A provider's forced hardware maintenance is the ordinary occasion for this fact, rather than a corner case — which is the thing the entry did not say and the replay's main contribution.** The scenario's machine *"powers down abruptly"*, and an unannounced hardware maintenance is likelier to be a hard power cycle than an orderly shutdown. **What breaks, in three parts, none of it silent to the same degree.** The render **operation** record's attempt count not landing is soft: reconciliation reads attempt 1 and re-runs, which is the same outcome. The **job** record not landing is not: the line comes back short and the job becomes `Queue & History` D2's *no ending Smoozy ever learned*, for work the machine was paid for. And **a frame's file coming back short but not empty** is the worst: `Job files and outputs` C15 counts it as already there, the re-run skips it, and the user pays for a hundred frames of which several have holes nobody can see — the harm C13's zero-byte rule exists to prevent, arriving by the one route it does not cover. **Corrected 2026-09-02: that is true of a mid-range frame and false of the boundary frame, which this line did not separate.** `Job files and outputs` **C17** re-renders the last frame Smoozy asked for that counts as already there, whatever state its file is in, and **C21** states why — *"without C17 the frame in flight would pass as done"* — so the frame the power cut happened under is re-rendered rather than skipped, and `Rendering` **A4** was corrected the same day to open a carried-on job **at** that frame rather than after it, so the desktop's arithmetic and the machine's agree. **The exposure is therefore a short file at a frame that is not the last one asked for that is present** — an earlier frame whose write had not reached the disk when the power went — and those, and only those, are counted as already there and skipped. The same narrowing was already applied to `Job files and outputs` [Section 14](Job%20files%20and%20outputs.md#14-unresolved) item 23 on the same day, for the disk-fill route, on the same reasoning. **What holds without it:** `State, identity and recovery` §6's rule that after a boot the supervisor did not initiate it trusts no record to be more recent than the last one it can read, and C15's limit being stated rather than hidden. **The promise stands weaker, not unsupported.**

## Facts 45 and 46 — reading the machine's Blenders and its cards

### Fact 45 — Whether a rented machine can be searched for every Blender installed on it, over the connection Smoozy holds.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Render Settings.md` Fact 2 — the automatic Blender pick; states that with no list no job can be sent from that machine at all. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 10 — the search row, handed to Architecture outright; labelled an assumption with no source in this project; a negative answer is a hole rather than a narrowing, and it takes `Preparation/Specs/Render Settings.md`'s version match with it.
**Merge note.** Clean merge — both sweeps state the same question in the same words.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with **more than one Blender on it, placed several different ways** — one unpacked into a plain folder, one installed through the machine's own software, one installed as a snap, and at least one put somewhere Server Setup did not put it, such as a self-compiled build. The case that fails is the one nobody installed, and the plain folder is invisible to every route but a file search. The one box on record happened to have exactly that mix.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED in part, 2026-08-15/16, and the failure is the one the fact predicts plus one worse.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 45.
