Five Blenders were planted five ways against known ground truth. **A plain unpacked folder is invisible to every route but a whole-disk file search**, exactly as predicted — and **a renamed binary defeats the file search too**, found only by running each candidate and reading its version output. That probe is a heuristic rather than a search: it would miss a small build and costs an execution per candidate. The whole-disk search cost **5.3 seconds** on a 126 GB disk 12% full.

**Three traps that break the obvious implementation.** The apt build **prints two lines of colour-management noise before its version**, so a reader taking the first line gets none — every build must be parsed by finding the line beginning `Blender `, never by position. **Deduplication by resolved path is wrong for snaps**: `/snap/bin/blender` resolves to `/usr/bin/snap`, so it would collapse every snap into one entry and could name the snap launcher as a Blender — the version each path reports is the reliable discriminator. And **restricting the search to a single filesystem, the ordinary way to keep it cheap, silently loses the snap**, which is mounted from a loop device.

**A ceiling no route lifts:** a non-root login found three of four on the other machine and was **refused the fourth outright**. A real instance of a case the specs care about turned up by accident — the apt build is **3.0.1, below the floor**, which `Render Settings` says must appear in the list and never be the automatic pick.

**Still owed:** flatpak was not installed on either box; neither tested a non-root login against a renamed binary; a Blender inside a container, on a network mount, or in a user's home directory is untested.

### Fact 46 — Whether a machine's GPUs — how many, which they are, and how much memory each holds — and the machine's own memory can be read over the connection Smoozy holds, on a machine with no Blender on it, and whether a machine that has no card at all can be told from one that has.

**POINTER ADDED 2026-08-31, and it changes a condition rather than an answer.** Read this entry against ***The machine 5***, where the **cold-machine condition** now lives: **every reading behind this fact was taken after a refresh was asked for**, and **asked cold, a four-card machine and a cardless one give the same answer.** `Servers` H16 declines the route anyway, so nothing breaks — **the repair strengthens H16's third reason rather than weakening it.** The pointer was routed twice by the downstream reading of the repaired Blender research before anybody carried it.

**And one conflict travels with it, stated rather than resolved, per the Architecture front door.** `Rendering` L21a and L24 and `Servers` H16 rest on a batch record saying that from factory settings the individual devices arrive already switched on. **The repaired *The machine 5* says the windowless runs came back empty in all four versions with an untouched settings folder, and empty again under factory settings, filling only once a refresh was asked for.** **The batch's own record does not say whether its script called a refresh**, so the two cannot be reconciled from what is written down, and neither is doubted.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 2 — labelled unresolved, checked, standing and narrowed; the Blender half is partly answered by *The machine 6* for a machine that already has a Blender, and the card-memory half is answered nowhere. `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Render Settings.md` Fact 5 — "whether a rented machine's graphics cards and memory can be read over the connection", wanted by saying that a scene will render on the processor on a machine with cards; owned by `Preparation/Specs/Servers.md`.
**Merge note.** Clean merge; Render Settings names Servers as the owner. Render Settings adds the requirement below, which Servers does not.
**Split note:** see Fact 28. Kept apart from 28 and 43, answered in the same batch.

**The no-card half cannot be posed on Vast.ai at all, established 2026-08-16.** Every offer that search returns with no GPU is a **storage** ask — `resource_type: "disk"`, `cpu_ram: 0` — and two attempts to rent one were refused with `error 404/3603: no_such_ask`. **Every no-GPU offer that search returned was a storage ask, and Vast's own field list has no CPU-only filter to ask with — corrected 2026-08-22, where this read *No offer exists with no GPU and real memory at any price*, which is a statement about what the provider sells drawn from two refused rentals.** `https://docs.vast.ai/cli/reference/search-offers.md`, read 2026-08-20, lists `cpu_arch`, `cpu_cores`, `cpu_ghz`, `cpu_cores_effective`, `cpu_ram` and `has_avx` — the processor on a GPU machine — and no field naming a machine kind. **The batch that closes this half must go to another provider**, and no further Vast rental should be bought to try; the conclusion follows from the missing filter alone. **What would close the narrowed sentence costs nothing and has not been run — added 2026-08-22, because a narrowed claim with no route to settle it is what this correction was about**: one search for `num_gpus=0` with `cpu_ram` above zero, which is the query the two fields above permit even though no machine-kind field exists. It settles whether the marketplace lists such an offer at all, as against whether one search happened not to return one. It does not settle whether one could be rented — that stays with the two `no_such_ask` refusals.

**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** a rental with **more than one card** — count, per-card identity and per-card memory are three different questions with one card and three harder ones with four — no screen and no display server, and posed **before anything is installed**, since the promise covers a server the user has just added. **And a second rental with no card at all**, since the warning's condition is *a machine that has GPUs* and the case that has to be told apart is a machine that has none.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

---

**ANSWERED in part, 2026-08-15/16, for count, per-card identity and per-card memory — on a two-card box with no Blender, no screen and no display server, which is the form the fact asks for.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 46. **The no-card half is not answered**, and the Telegram box of 2026-08-16 established it is **unposable on Vast**, whose search has no CPU-only filter to ask with. *(This said *which sells no cardless offer* until 2026-08-22 — what was observed is what could be asked for, not what the provider sells.)*

**The several-card trap the fact exists to catch was caught.** `nvidia-smi --query-gpu=count --format=csv,noheader` returns **the value once per card**, so a caller reading the first line is right by luck and one parsing the whole output as an integer gets `22`. **It passes every test ever written on a one-card machine.** Safe readings are `nvidia-smi -L | wc -l` or counting rows of any per-card query. Identity needs the **UUID and bus location**, because both cards were the same model and `lspci` **listed them without a model name on that image, which is that image's `pci.ids` database being older than the card rather than a property of the tool — corrected 2026-08-22, where this was written as *`lspci` lists them without a model name*.** The PCI ID Repository gives vendor `10de` device `2684` as *"AD102 [GeForce RTX 4090]"*, and the tell is in the observation itself: the vendor half resolved to *NVIDIA Corporation* from the same lookup on the same run. **Per-card memory has exactly one route and it is `nvidia-smi`** — `/proc/driver/nvidia/gpus/*/information` gives model, UUID and bus location and **no memory figure at all**.

**Three states exist, not two.** A card present with no driver makes `nvidia-smi` simply absent, **byte-identical to what a cardless machine gives**; a driver with no card gives `No devices were found`, exit 6. **So the discrimination is possible only by reading PCI** — `lspci -d 10de:`, empty with exit 0 for an absent vendor. **Anything concluding "no GPU" from `nvidia-smi` failing will misclassify a perfectly good rented A6000, and on two of these three providers that is what they shipped.** Counting `/sys/class/drm/card*` is worse still: it reports two cards on a one-card machine, because of an emulated Cirrus console adapter.

**On a container the machine's own memory reading is wrong.** `nproc` said 4 and `/proc/meminfo` said 15.57 GiB against real allocations of **`cpu.max` 3.84 CPUs and `memory.max` 14.95 GiB**. **The reading to take on a container is the cgroup's**, falling back to `/proc` only when it reads the literal `max`. And **in a view with genuinely no cards, `/proc/driver/nvidia` and the PCI sysfs scan lie**, both inherited from the host kernel.

**REPRODUCED AND EXTENDED, 2026-08-18, and the half that matters is explicitly still open.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §14. The cgroup figures reproduced exactly — 15.57 GiB against 14.95, 4 processors against 3.84 — on cgroup v2 with the v1 paths absent, **and the high-water reading answers the literal `max`, so a reader taking that instead falls back to the wrong figure.** The provider's own API reported the processor count as `4` and `4.0` effective, **disagreeing with the cgroup's 3.84.**

**The card readings all agreed on that box, and that is the trap passing rather than the question closing.** The driver's own answer, two count-as-a-count routes, the kernel listing and the bus scan **all gave 1, because the host had exactly one card**. What *is* established is the mechanism: **the bus scan is unmistakably the host's unfiltered view** — it listed the host's own storage controller, its audio device and its network card, none of which belong to the container. **So it would lie on a multi-card host; only the arithmetic consequence is invisible here.** **This needs a multi-card host and must not be treated as closed.**

**ANSWERED ON TWO MULTI-CARD HOSTS, 2026-08-22, and it lies exactly as predicted.** Machines E and F of the multi-provider batch, each a **two-card slice of a four-card host**, on two different sellers' hosts in two countries. **`nvidia-smi` reported two — the slice's own, correctly, with the right UUIDs and bus locations. `/proc/driver/nvidia/gpus` reported four on both**, the extra two carrying UUIDs that are another tenant's. **`nproc` reported 40 on a slice sold as 20 cores, and memory reported the host's 64 GiB**, with **no cgroup quota readable** to correct either. **And the device nodes were inconsistent between the two hosts** — four nodes on one for a two-card rental, two on the other, with `Device Minor` values of 2 and 0 for the cards `nvidia-smi` called 0 and 1, so node number is not index. **On both virtual machines in the same batch every reading was honest**, so this is a container property rather than a rented-machine property. **`nvidia-smi` was the only reading correct on all four boxes.** Evidence in [The multi-provider batch](The%20multi-provider%20batch%20—%20the%20surface%20that%20did%20not%20vary,%20the%20Debian%20route%20that%20exists,%20and%20the%20cards%20a%20slice%20cannot%20see.md).

**Two more host-inherited readings nobody had asked about:** free-memory reporting shows the **host's** 4 GiB of swap, and the uptime reading gives the **host's** 8.2 days on a container created that morning. **Both would lie to a reader.**

## Facts 47 to 56 — making a machine ready, from `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md`

### Fact 47 — What a rented machine needs beyond a graphics driver before a render will run on it.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 1 — the ready action. Named by that document's `[PENDING]` marker as Architecture's and not a Blender question.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** a **freshly rented, untouched** machine with a card. The question is about what a provider leaves undone, so a machine already used for other work cannot answer it. **This must be the first thing asked on whatever box it goes to.**
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED on four machines, 2026-08-15/16, and there is no single answer: it is a property of the image the provider ships, not of rented machines.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 47. The four results were **nothing, two things, four things and seven things** — **that spread is the finding.** A fat provider image with the driver already in place needed only an unpacked Blender tarball; a bare `ubuntu:22.04` container needed an `xz` decompressor and nine X11/OpenGL libraries, 33 packages and 178 MB behind them; Sesterce needed four; Hyperstack seven, including two traps of its own — `ubuntu-drivers install --gpgpu` **does not install `nvidia-smi`** and **omits `libnvoptix`**, so OptiX fails with `Error: Found no Cycles device of the specified type` while CUDA works.

**All four agree on the mechanism, and that is what a ready action is built on.** The blocker is the X11 and OpenGL shared-library chain, and **`libgl1` is needed even for a background render**, because the executable links `libGL.so.1` at load time — the failure is at launch, not at render. **And Blender names only the first missing library**, so fixing them one at a time takes as many rounds as there are libraries: **a ready action must read the whole list at once, from `ldd`, and never work from the launch error.**

**One trap that hides the problem rather than showing it.** On Sesterce the NVIDIA metapackage dragged in a whole X stack and **incidentally supplied six of the nine missing libraries**; had the driver not gone in first, five would have been missing rather than three. **So a ready action must install these explicitly and never rely on the driver's dependencies.** No CUDA toolkit was ever needed on any machine — `libnvoptix.so.1` and `libcuda.so.1` come with the driver package.

**Still owed:** four images on three providers. The mechanism is settled; the list is not, and cannot be, since it is per image. **And one of the four broke the fact's own precondition** — it requires an untouched machine and says the question must be asked first, and on Hyperstack it was asked sixth. The other three did ask it first, so the answer stands, **but a strict reading would want Hyperstack's re-posed.**

### Fact 48 — Whether a rented machine can be a locked container whose drivers are already in place and which Smoozy cannot change.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 2 — the nothing-to-prepare row. Labelled an assumption, inherited from `Preparation/Specs/Servers.md`.
**Split note — flagged.** Kept apart from Fact 61, which asks from providers' documentation whether one machine commonly hands out several containers. Server Setup says the two are to be confirmed together, but they are answered different ways — this one wants an actual rental of that kind, that one wants a page. Kept apart so neither is closed by the other.
**Answered by:** a rented machine, of that kind.
**Machine:** a rental **of that kind**, which may mean a different provider or a different plan from every other batch. The one box on record was sold as a container and was a whole virtual machine, so posing this may need looking for the case rather than assuming it will turn up.
**Shape-deciding:** gates the spine. Round 1 on `Preparation/Architecture/The shape.md` found that the answer can overturn the supervisor's boot-return arrangement, so it is settled before any spine document is written.
**Posable:** now, if such a rental can be found — which is itself part of the answer.

**ANSWERED: yes, 2026-08-15/16, on a container rented specifically to pose it — and the *cannot change* half holds under every route tried.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 48. **This is one of the two facts that gated the spine, and it is settled.**

**One seller states that such rentals are the norm on its own service.** *(Headline narrowed by the architecture runner, 2026-08-23, out of the 6.8 re-run repair. It read **Such rentals are the norm rather than a corner case**, which states as a property of rentals what is one seller's documentation about that seller's own instances — and this register entry is where the wider form spread from: it was restated as a property of rentals in `Server Setup` in three places, in `Rented-server runtime` in two, in `Sources/The shape.sources.md` and in `Six machines`, all narrowed the same day. The three-seller batch of 2026-08-22 recorded the other two sellers shipping virtual machines. **Nothing anywhere turns on which kind is commoner** — every rule that reads this fact is written per machine kind off a reading taken at every placement — so no answer moves with the headline. The quotation, its source, its date and the observation below are unchanged.)* Vast.ai's own documentation, read 2026-08-15: *"Instances are almost always Docker containers; a small subset are virtual machines."* The three Vast boxes in the batch were not containers only because they were rented from **VM templates**.

**Confinement is by capability dropping plus seccomp, not a user namespace.** Root inside holds **13 of 41 capabilities** (`CapPrm/CapEff/CapBnd = 00000000a80405fb`); the 28 missing include **`CAP_SYS_MODULE`**, `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, `CAP_SYS_RAWIO`, `CAP_SYS_PTRACE`, `CAP_SYS_BOOT`. `/sys` is read-only, five paths under `/proc` are read-only and four more masked.

**Four routes to the driver, all closed.** Installing a driver package fails on the bind mounts — `unable to make backup link ... Invalid cross-device link` — **and poisons apt afterwards**. Loading or unloading a kernel module returns `EPERM` on every route including the raw syscalls; **`init_module` was handed a deliberately malformed image, which a caller holding the capability would answer `ENOEXEC` or `EINVAL`, so `EPERM` proves the capability is absent rather than the image bad**, and the unload targets both showed 0 users so `EBUSY` was not available as an excuse. Writing `/proc/driver/nvidia` gives `EACCES` **from the driver's own write handler**. Replacing the user-space libraries in place gives `EROFS`.

**The one gap is dangerous.** The `.so.1` symlinks everything actually opens are ordinary files in the writable overlay, **so the user-space half can be shadowed** by anything controlling `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`. Under a deliberate mismatch, `cuInit` failed but `cuDriverGetVersion` succeeded and reported CUDA 9.1 — **a version read from the CUDA library is not evidence the pairing works** — and Blender **rendered on the processor and exited 0** with the device setting still reading `GPU`, 2:23 against 1:29. **A broken driver pairing produces a slow, quiet, apparently successful render rather than a refusal.**

**And on a container a restart is not a recovery action.** Across two container restarts the host's uptime rose monotonically, so **the host never rebooted and the driver was never reloaded**. **A card that has stopped answering is terminal for that server.** One command escapes the container entirely: `nvidia-smi -pm` is served by the **host's** `nvidia-persistenced` through a bind-mounted socket, changing a host-wide setting.

**Still owed:** no actual card hang was induced — what is established is that if one occurs, none of the four repair routes is open. **The observed capability set is Docker's default; a provider running `--privileged`, or adding `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, would answer this fact differently, and this test cannot rule that out.**

### Fact 49 — Whether a machine can be read for the setup state it is in: how far a half-finished setup got.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 3 — the setup-state row, the resume row, the part-finished row, the run-it-again row. Labelled unresolved, checked and standing.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, **posed after the ready action exists**, with a setup deliberately cut off part-way.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** **not yet.** The sources file states outright it cannot be answered ahead of knowing what the ready action does.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-18 — yes, with one reading named as wrong.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §9. The stub setup ran against five recorded boundaries. **Cut at a recorded safe boundary, the machine named the third of five by name with the moment it was reached**, and the recorded outcome. **Cut hard, mid-step rather than at a boundary, it named the second of five** — which is right: it names the last boundary actually *passed*, not the one it died in. **A job submitted while the state stood was never admitted**, before or after a reboot, and **the state survives a reboot with no worker behind it**, still naming its boundary and its moment.

**One reading is wrong and it is the defunct-entry hole.** Immediately after the hard cut, with **no setup worker alive at all**, the machine reported the recorded pair as still validating; only the reboot, which reaped the entry, flipped it. **So between the cut and the next supervisor start, the machine says a dead setup group is live.** The cause is that the supervisor never waits on the workers it starts — the same hole that blocked the forced identifier reuse under Fact 91.

### Fact 50 — Whether making a machine ready can be run again on a half-finished machine without harm.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 4 — the run-it-again row. Labelled unresolved; the promise it holds up is one that spec says Architecture has to deliver.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** a rental **nobody needs afterwards** — a half-finished ready action may involve a driver whose reinstall becomes an upgrade the running kernel does not match, and the recorded outcome of that is a card that stops answering. Run it last on a box being given up.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** **not yet** — needs the ready action to exist, as Fact 49.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-18 — NO.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §16. Run last, on the box being given up, against the machine Fact 49 had left half-finished with two of five steps genuinely done.

**A second ready action was not refused.** No error and no refusal — a second setup worker started and got its own recorded execution group, **while the machine's own state still named only the first run.** Two setup runs in flight at once, and the machine's state naming one of them.

**And then it did the harm.** The unauthorised second run walked **all five boundaries from the beginning** and, on reaching the end, **cleared the machine-level state itself** — leaving a machine that had genuinely completed two of five steps **marked ready**, by a run nobody authorised. **On a real machine those steps are package and driver installation**, which is exactly the work that arrived broken on this very rental and exactly the work that is not free to repeat.

**The card was unharmed** — checked after the second run and again after the clear, it answered normally — **so the harm this fact is about is the state, not the hardware, and it is real.** The documented recovery behaved correctly: clearing under the *original* identity worked, and a fresh run afterwards started cleanly with a new identity.

### Fact 51 — Whether Smoozy can restart a rented machine over the connection it already holds, whether it comes back reachable, how long that takes, and whether the connection can be re-established afterwards.

**Named by, three sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 5 — the restart row, the resume row, the ordering row, the run-it-again row. Labelled an assumption; **that sweep calls it shape-deciding**; `Preparation/Architecture/Method.md` names it as its example of the first escalation to the Creator.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 8 — a restart Server Setup caused; how many attempts and how long the pause, whose wait figure the spec parks with it. A negative answer is stated to be a hole rather than a narrowing.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 2 — the restart row; "the Method's named candidate for the first escalation to the Creator".

**Merge note.** Clean merge.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental **whose provider console is reachable**, so a machine that does not come back can be told from one that came back on a new address, and **one that nobody needs afterwards**, since the whole question is whether it returns. The one reboot on record was triggered from that console rather than over the connection — which is exactly the case this fact is about — and it brought an unattended kernel upgrade with it. `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` says outright the box was not rebooted over a connection.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Shape-deciding:** claimed by one sweep. The Method names only two stage-shape facts and this is not one of them; it is named instead as the first escalation to the Creator. **Reported as claimed rather than ruled.**
**Posable:** now — and it must be run **last** on whatever box carries it, because a box that does not come back ends the batch.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-13** — yes. `systemctl reboot` over the connection Smoozy already held; the connection reported `closed by remote host` and the machine answered again in about ten seconds at the same address and port, with a new boot id and the disk intact.


### Fact 52 — Whether the shop-front listing for Linux software and Blender's own downloads agree: whether a release the listing names always has a folder on the downloads that can be worked out from it.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 6 — the list-and-fetch row. Stated in the sources file as not established.
**Answered by:** reading the listing and the download server's own published pages, with the date read.
**Machine:** none, beyond one machine to re-run the listing read. **Asking that listing for a named channel has never been run** — only its unnamed default.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED BY READING, 2026-08-19, and the answer is *for the stable channels, yes; for the others, no*.** Posed while `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md` was written; the citation, the exact values and what each source does not cover are in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Server Setup.sources.md`. Every stable-risk channel the listing named had a folder and a file at the derivable address on Blender's own downloads, confirmed by direct request for 5.2.0, 5.1.2, 5.0.1 and 4.3.1 and by folder listing for the 4.5 and 4.2 lines. **The disagreement the row's promise exists for was observed the same day rather than supposed**: `5.2lts/candidate` named 5.2.1 and its download answered 404, and `latest/edge` named 5.3.0 with no folder for it at all. **So the promise fires on the unstable risks and holds on the stable ones**, which is what the quick path reads.

**One shape a builder needs:** the checksum is **one file per release covering all platforms**, `blender-<version>.sha256`; a per-file `…tar.xz.sha256` does not exist and answers 404.

**Still owed:** any other day — the frequency of disagreement over time is unknown; **asking that listing for a named channel and actually installing from it has still never been run**, only the read; and whether the sha256 naming holds for every historic folder.

### Fact 53 — Whether blender.org itself publishes anything a machine can read, and where daily builds sit.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 7 — the list-and-fetch row, for daily builds only. Labelled unresolved; Open Threads adds that nothing now rests on it.
**Answered by:** reading Blender's own published pages, with the date read. **Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED BY READING, 2026-08-19 — yes on both halves.** Posed while `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md` was written; the citation is in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Server Setup.sources.md`. Blender's own build service publishes a structured listing a machine can read, and **daily builds sit there** — 131 entries on the day read, each carrying the version, the risk kind, the branch, the platform, the size, the file moment and its own address, spanning alpha, candidate and stable rebuilds. **So the two kinds the Design marker assumes can be told apart are marked in the data rather than inferred.**

**One trap observed:** the listing carried a **stale entry from June 2024 beside current ones**, so a reader must read the file moment and must not assume every entry current.

**Still owed:** how long entries persist; whether the listing's shape is stable over time; and anything about the separate experimental listings the same service may carry.

### Fact 54 — Whether a rented machine can reach Blender's own downloads and the extensions site over its own network, and whether the fetch happens on the machine or on the user's computer.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 8 — the list-and-fetch row, the add-on repository row. Nothing in this project establishes it either way.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental. The one box on record had `curl`, `wget` and `git` present, its own outbound network working, and its firewall off. Best asked **early on a fresh box**, before anything is installed.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED: yes on every machine that posed it, 2026-08-15/16, and the fetch happens on the machine.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 54. One box pulled 360 MB and 366 MB in 10.3 and 9.9 seconds — **about 37 MB/s on the machine's own line, with nothing passing through the Mac** — and another reached six blender.org hosts, all 200, fetching a 335 MB archive in 2 seconds. Both ran deliberate negative controls in the same session — `curl: (6) Could not resolve host` and `curl: (28) ... Connection timed out` — so the test could fail.

**Two things worth carrying.** The downloads **honour byte ranges** — `http_code=206`, returning the archive's real first bytes `fd 37 7a 58 5a 00`, the XZ magic number — so a resumable fetch is available from a rental. And **a plain alphabetical sort of the release listing picks 4.2.9 as the newest when 4.2.23 exists**: anything reading that index must sort by version, not by string.

**Still owed:** one moment on each machine, reach over hours or days untested, no proxy or egress-filtered box tested, and **an actual extension package was never fetched — only the listing.**

### Fact 55 — What a restart stops on the machine — a transfer, a dependency check and a render — and whether a running render dies plainly rather than being left neither finished nor gone.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 13 — the what-a-restart-stops row, the ordering row, the busy-machine warning. Labelled an assumption, both halves.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with a card, **a render in flight and a transfer in flight at the moment of the restart**. The reboot already on record answers the render half **from the provider's console only**, which is not the case in question. Run it immediately before Fact 51, on the same box.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED for a transfer, 2026-08-13.** An 8 GB write in flight at the moment of a restart died and did not come back, leaving 4.7 GB **under the file's final name with nothing marking it incomplete** and an empty error stream. A render in flight was not tried on this machine.

**THE RENDER HALF IS ANSWERED, 2026-08-15/16, against a real Cycles render — and this entry carried it as untried for four days.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), *Fact 55 — what a restart stops, posed against a real render*. **Two real machine restarts with a render in flight**, on machines restarted over the connection as root, so it is the route this fact is about rather than the provider console.

**Both feared outcomes are real and which one arrives cannot be chosen.** A frame ran about 16.8 seconds — roughly 15.4 sampling, 1.4 writing. **Interrupted during the write, a partial sits under the frame's real final name with nothing to mark it** — 52,514,816 bytes carrying OpenEXR's real magic number, not sparse — **which settles that Blender does not write to a temporary name and rename.** **Interrupted during sampling, about 92% of a frame, there is nothing at the path at all**: no partial and no zero-byte file. *(No placeholder either, but that is a setting rather than a property.)* **A polite stop behaves identically to a hard kill at exit 143, and that is the signal a shutdown sends.** Both restarts landed in sampling despite the second being aimed deliberately at a write, because **the shutdown latency varied from 9 seconds to 6.4 between runs minutes apart** — so a restart leaves a partial about 8% of the time and nothing about 92%, and cannot be aimed even on purpose.

**Nothing announces the death.** The system log records only that it sent a termination signal to remaining processes, naming neither Blender nor the render nor its output. **The only evidence a render was killed rather than finished is the absence of the frames it had not written** — and, in the 8% case, a partial that looks like a finished small file.

**Still owed, and it is now the only clause open:** **a dependency check in flight was not posed at all**, which is that section's own *Not covered* line. Also not covered: one output format and one picture size.

**Why this entry was stale, recorded rather than glossed.** The six-machine batch answered the render half on 2026-08-15/16 and this entry was never brought up. `Preparation/Architecture/Server Setup.md`'s writing brief, `Preparation/Groundwork/Open Threads.md` and the board all inherited the stale reading, and the document written from them **stated the answered clause as untried and the unposed clause as fact** — the two exactly swapped. Caught by that document's before-audit gate on 2026-08-19 and corrected in all six places. **The lesson is the register's own: a fact answered by a batch is not filed until its entry says so.**


### Fact 56 — Whether a copy of an add-on already set up on the user's computer carries licence material inside it.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 15 — the copy-from-the-user row, under that document's own *What is not settled*, which says what the module says about licences has to change if it does.
**Answered by:** reading a particular seller's own documentation. **Neither a machine nor Blender's documentation can answer it** — nothing in any version's documentation describes pricing, purchase, keys, activation or entitlement, and the sources file records that no test on a computer can answer it.
**Machine:** none. Do not put it in a rental batch.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** now, with a warning carried from the sources file: **this question was raised by an agent from its own knowledge and must be reworded blind before anyone is asked.**

**ANSWERED BY READING, 2026-09-02, and the answer is yes — the licence material a copy carries is licence text and a copyright notice, not an entitlement.** The entry names no seller; the seller read is **Superhive (formerly Blender Market)**, the marketplace whose rules bind the paid Blender add-ons this question is about, on its own support pages, both read **2026-09-02**. *Available Licensing Options*, `https://support.superhivemarket.com/article/54-available-licensing-options`: *"All scripts, add-ons, and other code-based products may be licensed as either GPL or MIT."* and *"No other licensing options are currently permitted for code-based products."* That page's own table gives, for GNU GPL 3.0 and for MIT alike, **Allow Redistribution: y**, **Allows Modification: y**, and **Requires License & Copyright Notice Be Included with Software: y**. *How to Make Your Blender Add-on GPL Compliant*, `https://support.superhivemarket.com/article/294-how-to-make-your-blender-addon-gpl-compliant`: *"Along with the other files in your add-on, you should include one named LICENSE"*, whose content *"should be the entire text of the GPL"*; *"every Python file in your add-on should also include a license notice at the top of the file"*; and *"The best thing to do is pack your add-on into a folder. That way, you can include a proper LICENSE, README and other helpful files"*. **So by the seller's own instruction a set-up add-on's folder carries licence material twice over** — a LICENSE file beside the code and a notice at the head of every Python file — and a copy of that folder carries both. Label: **documented**. **Neither ruling of 2026-08-31 reached this: no machine was used, nothing was rented and nothing was spent.** **The citation is carried here in full — exact words, address and date read — and a companion entry is owed at `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Server Setup.sources.md`**, which was not written from here because that file and its answer document were both in another implementer's hands on the day.

**What it settles, and it settles it in the direction the promise already stood.** Both permitted licences allow redistribution and modification, so packing the folder and installing it on a machine the user rents is inside what the seller's own terms allow, and **neither licence requires a seller-issued key for the copy to work**. `Server Setup` **A3** needs no change — Smoozy does not ask for, read, or check a licence key — and what the module *says* about licences can now cite this reading instead of deferring to an unread question.

**What it does not settle, and the residue is narrower than the question was.** Neither permitted licence forbids an add-on making a runtime activation check of its own, so **whether any individual add-on additionally stores a seller-issued key inside its folder is not established**, and this is one marketplace's rules rather than every seller's. **Breaks:** nothing Smoozy does. A3's mechanism is unchanged and the copy either works on the rented machine or refuses in the add-on's own words. **The silence is removed by the failure being the add-on's own** — an add-on that will not run unactivated says so itself — and Smoozy claims nothing about licences it has not read.

**The blind-rewording warning is honoured rather than spent.** Nobody was asked anything; a publication was read. The warning stands for the day the residue above is put to a person.

---

## Facts 57 to 61 — the connection itself, from `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md`

### Fact 57 — Whether the end of a connection is something the side still holding it learns of by itself, rather than finding out the next time something needs it — and whether that differs by how the connection ended.

**Named by, three sweeps:**
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 1 — "shape-deciding for this document"; five rows rest on it: noticing a connection has ended, the connected period, how many attempts, transfers across a repaired drop, and the list showing the true state. Labelled unresolved.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Server Setup.md` Fact 14 — "whether Smoozy notices a dropped connection by itself"; owned in Servers rather than there.
- `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 10 — lists what breaks on a no: the list showing the true state, the reconnecting state, the repair that carries on for as long as the app is open, the whole-app notification for a connection Smoozy could not restore, and every module written for the moment Smoozy knows it is offline.

**Merge note.** Clean merge.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental posed **three ways** — the user's own line pulled at their end, the machine powered off from the provider's console, and the machine closing the connection politely. The middle one needs **console access**, not just a login. `Preparation/Architecture/The rented machine.md` records that box sending no keepalive of its own (`clientaliveinterval 0`), so anything the answer relies on has to come from the caller's side.
**Shape-deciding:** for one module, claimed by Servers.
**Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED ALL THREE WAYS, 2026-08-16**, by the six-machine batch. `Preparation/Architecture/Six machines — the container, the webhook and the ready action.md` holds the answer of record and its table; this entry is the index to it and does not restate the figures. **The spread is large, and it inverts the question.**

- **The machine closed it politely** — the caller was told, in the same second.
- **Powered off from the provider's console** — the caller was told, in the same second, with keepalives on or off. A provider's stop is an **orderly shutdown rather than a power cut**: the machine's own service lived long enough to close its sockets politely.
- **The caller's own line pulled, keepalives on** — detected in 56 seconds.
- **The caller's own line pulled, keepalives off** — **not detected at all**, silent for 21 minutes 45 seconds.

**So the danger is never the machine going away; it is the caller's own connection failing.** Only a broken path needs keepalives, and without them a caller sits for twenty minutes believing a dead connection is a slow one. **The machine had not learnt within the 5 minutes 10 seconds it was watched** — it held the socket open with the job still running. **Corrected 2026-08-21: this entry said *never learns at all*.** That is contradicted by the manual of the software the run observed. The box carried `tcpkeepalive yes`, and `sshd_config(5)` (macOS 26.5 man page dated 2025-10-04, read on the Creator's Mac 2026-08-20) says *"The default is yes (to send TCP keepalive messages), and the server will notice if the network goes down or the client host crashes."* The ceiling is the kernel's own defaults — kernel.org `ip-sysctl`, read 2026-08-20: `tcp_keepalive_time` *"Default: 2hours"*, then 9 probes at 75 s, *"connection will be aborted after ~11 minutes of retries"*; and `tcp_retries2` *"yields a hypothetical timeout of 924.6 seconds and is a lower bound"* where the machine has anything to send. **The run measured what those defaults already predict and nothing more.** Nothing this fact decides moves: two hours, or fifteen minutes, is no use to a caller either.

**Two side-findings.** A ten-minute silent gap left a connection **unrecoverable even after the path was restored**, almost certainly because the provider's address translation forgets faster than the protocol does — the actual timeout was not established. And **one provider answers no ping at all** while its connection works perfectly, so any ping-based health check would report it dead.

**Two things stand open, and neither is one of the three ways.** First, everything observed ran through a provider's proxy — the earlier console test was on a Vast rental whose SSH ran **only** through `sshN.vast.ai`, so what was seen is what the proxy does when the machine under it goes away rather than what a machine held straight to does. A direct connection may behave completely differently: silence rather than a close. Second, **the genuinely abrupt case is not posable** — a real power cut would look like the pulled-line row, and no rented machine can produce one.

**Correcting the earlier entry, 2026-08-17.** This entry read *answered in part* and named the pulled-line way as still owed, months after the six-machine batch answered it in the same file. The staleness was caught by the before-audit gate on `Preparation/Architecture/State, identity and recovery.md`, whose unresolved list had inherited it. **Nothing waits on the two remaining ways that the four answered ways do not already decide.**

### Fact 58 — Whether an open connection survives the key file it was opened with being deleted, moved, or changed under it.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 3. Labelled unconfirmed in the spec's own marker.
**Answered by:** a machine, but not a rented GPU machine.
**Machine:** **any reachable SSH server** — the cheapest thing available, or a ride on any other batch. Servers flags this deliberately because it is currently parked with questions that do need hardware.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED: yes, 2026-08-15/16.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 58. A copy of the key was made, a session opened with the copy, and the copy deleted; **the live session kept working, and a fresh connection with the missing key failed** — which proves the key gated authentication and that the survival is because the session no longer needs the file. The real key was never touched.

### Fact 59 — What a machine that is not Linux answers on a first connection, and how Smoozy tells.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 4 — a machine that is not Linux.
**Answered by:** a machine that is not Linux, reachable over SSH.
**Machine:** **not Linux** — a Windows or macOS host. A rented Linux machine cannot pose it. Servers flags it here rather than in the batch request, because adding it to a rental's list would waste the rental.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** the macOS half now, on the Creator's own machine. **The Windows half was recorded here as *not at all*, and that was false — corrected 2026-08-21.** It was posable by reading, on the vendor's own published source, at no cost and with no Windows machine, and this project has closed facts that way before (Fact 61, *by reading, with no machine and nothing spent*). The route was available and nobody took it. Only one clause of it wants a machine, and it is named in the answer below.

**MOVED RATHER THAN CLOSED, 2026-08-19, and the useful half is a negative.** Observed on the Creator's own Mac, macOS 26.5.2, with nothing installed and no setting changed: the SSH version banner is `SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_10.2` — **no operating-system marker of any kind** — against a rented Ubuntu box's `SSH-2.0-OpenSSH_8.9p1 Ubuntu-3ubuntu0.16`. **A bare banner is therefore what macOS gives and also what an unsuffixed Linux build would give, so the banner cannot separate *those two*.** That closes **that pair** by observation rather than by caution, which is worth more than leaving it untested. Written up in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Servers.sources.md`.

**THE WINDOWS HALF IS ANSWERED IN THE MECHANISM, 2026-08-20, by reading, with no machine and nothing spent — and the banner separates Windows from both of the above.** Microsoft names its own fork: its OpenSSH overview page calls `https://github.com/PowerShell/openssh-portable` *"The Microsoft fork of this project"*, so what is in that repository is first-party. That repository's `version.h`, read 2026-08-20: `#define SSH_WINDOWS_VERSION "OpenSSH_for_Windows_10.0"`, and `SSH_VERSION` is built from it. Its `kex.c`, in `kex_exchange_identification`, composes the string as `"SSH-%d.%d-%s%s%s\r\n"` with `SSH_VERSION` in it. And RFC 4253 §4.2 puts that string on the wire *"When the connection has been established"*, with key exchange beginning after — **so the marker arrives before authentication and nothing has to be logged into.** **Cross-checked against this project's own macOS reading rather than taken on trust:** the macro carries no portable suffix, which predicts a banner of `10.2` from a binary reporting `10.2p1` — and that is exactly what was observed. A negative would have been a banner carrying the suffix.

**One clause is genuinely unread and it is the only part wanting a machine.** What was read is the branch behind the **GitHub** release of the Windows build — its own banner constant says `Win32-OpenSSH-GitHub` — and Microsoft distinguishes that from the **in-box** build shipped with Windows, which its own troubleshooting page says *"often lags behind the latest Win32-OpenSSH releases that are available on GitHub"*. The marker sits in the Windows version macro rather than in the GitHub-specific suffix, so it is very likely present in both; **very likely is not this project's bar.** **What would settle it:** one TCP connection to a Windows machine with the **in-box** server feature enabled, reading the first line it sends — no login, no credential, no change to the machine. It rides in the standing Windows batch.

**What this opens is a design question and not a fact.** Moving Windows from `Servers` Q13's outcome 3 to outcome 2 is a change of route and goes to the Creator with this evidence. A second reading points the same way: Microsoft documents that *"The initial default in Windows is the Windows command prompt (cmd.exe)"*, so a stock Windows server answers Q12's naming reading with a shell that has no such command — outcome 3 exactly, which is the case the banner would move.

**The authenticated half was declined rather than merely unposed, and the reason is a bound rather than a difficulty.** Posing it needs a key written into the Creator's own `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`, which is a change to their own SSH configuration — forbidden by `Security and licence` §7's stance and by this stage's rule that nothing pre-existing is ever touched. **So this half needs the Creator's own act or a macOS host this project made**, and nothing schedules either. **The authenticated half is unposed on Windows too, for the same reason — corrected 2026-08-22, where this clause read *The Windows half is unchanged and unposable*, which is the retracted claim this entry's own header strikes ten lines above.**

**What waits on it:** `Servers` Row 35 is answered without it, in three outcomes rather than two; what the fact would settle is which machines land in the second rather than the third — a machine that is not Linux and does not answer the reading is honestly reported as a connection that did not complete rather than as *not a Linux machine*.

**THE IN-BOX SERVER'S FIRST LINE IS PERMANENTLY UNRESOLVED, 2026-09-02. This is not a pending item and must not be read as one.** *"It rides in the standing Windows batch"* is the sentence the Creator's ruling of **2026-08-31** voids: **there is no standing batch and there will be no rental to answer a question.** This is Windows-list item 12 and `Servers` §16 item 1 must say the same thing.

**Breaks — as it stood:** which machines land in Q13's outcome 2 rather than outcome 3, and nothing more; the mechanism is answered by reading Microsoft's own fork and the standard. **What holds:** Q13's three outcomes, and the macOS pair closed by observation. **The silence is already removed, and it is the right trade rather than a stopgap:** Row 35 is answered in **three** outcomes rather than two, so a machine that does not answer is reported as *a connection that did not complete* rather than as *not a Linux machine* — honest and less useful, which is the permanent answer here and not a placeholder for one.

**The authenticated half is with the Creator rather than closed by the ruling**, since it needs their own act on their own machine, and it is recorded above rather than swept in here.

### Fact 60 — Whether a render started under one login can still be seen and stopped after Smoozy logs in as another.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 5 — editing a server that has jobs on it; work Smoozy is not tracking. Open Threads states that until it is answered nothing states what the user gets.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with **two logins that are not both root** — on the box already recorded, root ignores file permissions and root is real root, so posing this needs a second ordinary account made first.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** the plain half now, with a bare render. Properly **only once the shape exists**, since what has to be seen and stopped is whatever Smoozy puts on the machine.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-15/16: seen yes, stopped no.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 60. From a second ordinary login the process is visible with its owner and command line, but **both a polite and a forceful stop are refused with `Operation not permitted`**; the same login then stopped it cleanly. **So Smoozy can watch another login's render across a re-login, but to stop it, it must be the same account or hold elevated access.**

**Still owed, and stated plainly in the finding: no real Blender render was used.** That box had none and the driver work that would have supplied one was blocked. A stand-in process is signal-identical, but this leaves open whether a real Blender's child processes or its hold on the card change the answer. **The machines that did have Blender were not used for this, so it should be re-posed on the next batch that has one.**

**RE-POSED AGAINST A REAL RENDER AND CLOSED, 2026-08-18 — the answer does not change.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §18. A real 400-frame Cycles render with **44 threads**, seen from a second ordinary login. **Seen: everything** — both processes with owner and command line, the process name, the thread count, and even the start-time field. **Stopped: nothing** — polite and forcible stops against the render and against the worker all refused with `Operation not permitted`, a pattern kill of the render refused, and a pattern kill of every process of that login refused seven times over. **All 44 threads survived and the render kept going.** **A real render's children do not change the answer**, and the fact is closed.

**Two details worth carrying.** The existence probe by signal is refused too, so a second login **cannot even test liveness that way**. And the supervisor's endpoint holds independently: from the second login the endpoint's directory and the client binary were both `Permission denied`.

### Fact 61 — Whether any provider the Creator would actually use puts a server behind a gateway needing its own details, and whether one rented machine commonly hands out several containers each on its own port.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Servers.md` Fact 9 — the four connection details. Parked at Architecture as one check with two halves.
**Split note:** kept apart from Fact 48; see there.
**Answered by:** reading the providers' own documentation, with the address and the date read.
**Machine:** none. Fast-changing, so the date matters.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED BOTH HALVES, 2026-08-19, by reading, with no machine and nothing spent.** The citations — exact words, addresses and dates read — are in `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Servers.sources.md`, written by `Servers`. **The gateway half is no on every provider read:** none of the three fronts SSH with a gateway needing its own details; where a proxy route exists it is an address and a port rather than a second set of credentials. **The several-containers half is documented rather than assumed**, which is what the `Servers` spec had been carrying as a labelled assumption and leaning on in two places — each open internal port mapped to a random external port on the machine's usually shared public address, and a second provider assigning a different external port for access. **So a connection identity is four fields**, and `Servers` Row 24 is answered on documented ground rather than on one box's observed port mapping.

**What the reading does not cover, and it is named rather than glossed:** three providers on one date; **a fourth provider this project has rented from was not read**; and a bastion at a corporate or academic host is outside it entirely. `Servers` Section 16 item 8 carries what breaks — a provider that does front SSH that way has servers Smoozy cannot add at all, failing at the first connection with a refusal it cannot explain. **A fifth field would be a Specs change rather than an architecture one.**

---

## Facts 62 to 69 — Telegram, from `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md`

`Preparation/Architecture/Method.md` says outright: **the Telegram facts come before the Telegram work**, not during it — and if either of the two that hold the module up fails, the module goes back to a different promise rather than being reinterpreted.

### Fact 62 — Whether a rented machine can reach Telegram from wherever it is rented, and go on reaching it.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 2 — wanted by every row in the document. **Partly answered already**: the 2026-08-12 run held one token on three rented Linux machines at once and every call behaved. That is one provider on one day, and Telegram is a service reachable from some places and not others.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental. Worth re-posing on any provider the Creator actually intends to use, since it costs nothing to ask. Rides on every batch.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-13** — yes. Three requests to `api.telegram.org` each answered `HTTP 401`, rejecting a deliberately invalid token, which is what proves the service was reached. **Not covered**: whether it goes on reaching it over a day rather than a minute.

**Re-posed on a fourth provider location, 2026-08-16** — a Vast box listed in Oregon called `getMe` every five minutes for the length of the sitting; every call answered, round trip 0.46–0.48 seconds. The window achieved was **under three hours**, so *over a day* is still not covered.

**ANSWERED IN FULL, 2026-08-17** — the German box reached **24.022 hours** and the *goes on reaching it* clause is closed. **578 calls, every one HTTP 200 with `ok: true`**, no refusal, no rate-limit reply and **no gap over eight minutes** against a five-minute cadence. No drift either: the `getMe` median is 0.062–0.066 s in every one of the six four-hour blocks, and the two slowest answers of the day (1.248 s and 1.423 s) are isolated inside a block whose medians are unchanged. A negative result would have been any refusal or any silent gap; there is none. See [Two more boxes](Two%20more%20boxes%20—%20the%20volume,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20a%20day%20left%20running.md).


### Fact 63 — Whether a webhook can be used instead of the exclusive receiving slot, and what a rented machine would have to be for one.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 3 — the listening slot, and through it the shared note and the routing. The sources file records that webhooks "were not exercised at all", and Telegram's documentation states exclusivity between `getUpdates` and a webhook, which is a different question from the one the tests answered.
**Answered by:** a rented machine, and reading Telegram's own documentation for what it requires.
**Machine:** a rental with **a public address, an inbound port reachable from outside, and whatever certificate Telegram requires**. That is a different rental from the ordinary one.
**Shape-deciding:** returns to `Preparation/Architecture/The shape.md` and to the security scope before any spine document hardens the deployment boundary. A positive answer permits the one machine currently holding the registered webhook to bind an inbound, internet-reachable endpoint; no other prepared server binds one and no rental becomes ineligible. It therefore changes that boundary rather than only Telegram's receiving algorithm. The whole arbitration problem exists only because receiving is exclusive.
**Posable:** now, and Telegram's sweep asks that it be **settled before the batch is chosen rather than after**.

**ANSWERED FURTHER, 2026-08-16** — and the answer is now provider-shaped rather than certificate-shaped. A Vast rental asked for all four ports Telegram accepts was given `45104`, `45181`, `45149` and `45191` instead; the mapped port is genuinely open to the internet and answered a self-signed HTTPS request from outside, and Telegram still refused it: `Bad Request: bad webhook: Webhook can be set up only on ports 80, 88, 443 or 8443`. **So a URL naming a Vast container's own mapped port cannot be a webhook address** — the provider's port allocator decides the number before the machine boots. **Narrowed 2026-08-21: this said *cannot host a webhook at all, whatever it is rented as*.** Telegram's page constrains the URL, not the machine, so a URL naming some other address on one of the four ports is excluded by nothing observed. **Whether a relay can be put in front of such a container is not settled**, and nothing needs it — `Telegram.md` J1 says the whole route is optional. What would settle it: one container, one relay, one `setWebhook` against the relay on 443, one message. About an hour and a few cents. The earlier Hyperstack answer stands; the difference is a provider that lets the machine choose its own port. **A Vast virtual-machine rental was not tried.** See [One box](One%20box%20—%20the%20Telegram%20capture%20and%20the%20port%20that%20cannot%20be%20chosen.md).

~~**Still not covered, and it now needs a different provider:** the webhook POST wrapper, the `X-Telegram-Bot-Api-Secret-Token` header, and the retry cadence against a failing endpoint.~~ **TWO OF THOSE THREE ARE ANSWERED BY READING, 2026-09-02, and they were never a rental question at all.** Telegram's own *Bot API* page, `https://core.telegram.org/bots/api`, Bot API 10.3, the page's own date **August 24, 2026**, read **2026-09-02**. No machine, no provider and nothing spent. **The wrapper**, from `setWebhook`: *"Whenever there is an update for the bot, we will send an HTTPS POST request to the specified URL, containing a JSON-serialized Update."* **The header**, from the same method's `secret_token` row: *"A secret token to be sent in a header “X-Telegram-Bot-Api-Secret-Token” in every webhook request, 1-256 characters. Only characters A-Z, a-z, 0-9, _ and - are allowed."* and *"If specified, the request will contain a header “X-Telegram-Bot-Api-Secret-Token” with the secret token as content."* **So the one exceptional inbound endpoint has a documented authentication**, and `Security and licence` §1.3 no longer waits on one. Both citations already have companion homes — `Sources/Security and licence.sources.md`, and `Sources/Telegram.sources.md` entry 45c for the header and entry 45d for the wrapper, all written 2026-09-02.

**The retry cadence is the third, it stands open, and no reading will ever close it.** The same page's whole statement is *"In case of an unsuccessful request (a request with response HTTP status code different from `2XY`), we will repeat the request and give up after a reasonable amount of attempts."* — **no interval, no count, no window**, and Telegram's own webhook guide, `https://core.telegram.org/bots/webhooks`, read 2026-09-02, says nothing about retries at all (`Sources/Telegram.sources.md` entry 51a). **Only a run against a deliberately failing endpoint settles it — and that is cheaper than this entry has been assuming, on Fact 103's second run of 2026-09-02.** Telegram registered an ordinary resolvable HTTPS address that answers no update at all, with no rental, no provider and no certificate of this project's own; **what a retry run additionally needs is an update to deliver**, which is the same single typed message Fact 103's staleness half is waiting on and is not a rental either. **They stopped blocking**: the receiving call answers everything the module needs on any rental, and that is where the remaining Telegram facts were closed instead. **What is left unbounded is how long a machine that has stopped answering goes on being sent the same update**, which `Telegram` bounds from its own side.

### Fact 64 — How deep the pinned stack goes, and whether an older pin can be read without unpinning the newer one.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 4 — the pinned-slot row. Recorded in the sources file as not tried.
**Machine:** none — a bot and one Telegram account.
**Answered by:** a throwaway on the Creator's own computer against a live bot, and Telegram's own documentation.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**NOT ANSWERED, and `Telegram.md` is written so that nothing rests on it** — Section 3 B17 states as a check rather than an assumption that nothing ever reads beneath the top pin, that the stack is emptied at every hand-over and popped to the board by the janitor, and that the machinery works at the depth of two already observed. **What the unmeasured depth could still break is transient only**: a pin refused at some unknown cap would delay a reply until it is resent. That clause is Section 21 item 1.

**The key arrived on 2026-08-19** — a bot token is in `~/.smoozy-keys/telegram.txt` — **so this is posable by any agent the moment it has a conversation to work in**, which needs the user to have written to the bot at least once. The runner tried on 2026-08-19 and found the bot's update queue empty, so no conversation identifier was available.

**ANSWERED BY READING, 2026-09-02 — the second half is no, and the first half is answered negatively: Telegram publishes no depth at all.** Read at `https://core.telegram.org/bots/api` and at `https://core.telegram.org/api/pin`, both 2026-09-02. No machine, no conversation and nothing spent. **The citation is carried here in full and a companion entry is owed at `Preparation/Architecture/Sources/Telegram.sources.md`**, which was not written from here because that file was being edited by another implementer on the day.

- **An older pin cannot be read without unpinning the newer one, by a bot.** Every route the Bot API publishes reaches the newest pin and no other: `ChatFullInfo.pinned_message` is *"Optional. The most recent pinned message (by sending date)"*, and `unpinChatMessage`'s message identifier is *"If not specified, the most recent pinned message (by sending date) will be unpinned."* **No method on that page returns a list of pinned messages, or any pin but the newest.**
- **The stack is real and is readable — but not by a bot, which is what makes the negative a real answer rather than a silence.** Telegram's own client-protocol page states *"Telegram allows pinning multiple messages on top of a specific chat"* and then, plainly, *"The pinned_msg_id of userFull, chatFull, channelFull contains the ID of only the latest pinned message. To obtain a full list, use messages.search with inputMessagesFilterPinned filter."* **`messages.search` is a client-protocol call with no Bot API counterpart**, so the full list exists and Smoozy — a bot — cannot reach it.
- **The depth is published nowhere.** `pinChatMessage` is *"Use this method to add a message to the list of pinned messages in a chat"* and names no cap; neither page states a number; and `unpinAllChatMessages`, *"Use this method to clear the list of pinned messages in a chat"*, is the only bulk operation published.

**What that does to B17.** `Telegram` Section 3 **B17**'s check — that nothing ever reads beneath the top pin — stops being a discipline the design keeps and becomes **the only behaviour a bot has**, so the emptying at every hand-over and the janitor's pop are what keep the unpublished depth away from the machinery. **Breaks, unchanged and transient:** a pin refused at some unpublished cap delays a reply until it is resent. **What holds:** everything B17 rests on, now documented rather than assumed. **There is no silence to remove:** a refused pin is a refused call answered in Telegram's own words.

**What remains is a measurement rather than a question, and nothing rests on it:** the cap itself, which wants a live conversation the bot can see. **Neither ruling of 2026-08-31 closes it** and Section 21 item 1 carries it.

### Fact 65 — Whether Telegram's pace holds over hours and days rather than over a minute.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 5 — the clock, a picture per frame, several machines in one conversation. Every run behind the comfortable answer was under a minute, and the sources file says the ceiling was never actually reached.
**Answered by:** a machine that stays up for a day and keeps sending.
**Machine:** a rental is the honest place, since that is where it will run, but the question is **time rather than hardware**. Ride it on whichever box is up longest, or on the Fact 63 box.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED IN PART, 2026-08-16** — the sending half is exercised and **nothing was ever refused**. Sixty texts in a row took 35.2 seconds, about half a second each. Thirty fired at the same instant were all accepted and **serialised** — ids in strict order, twenty-eight answered within 1.26 seconds and the last two at 5.6 — so the service absorbs a burst by delaying it rather than refusing it, at least at that depth. Ten 1,465,514-byte 1920×1080 pictures took **1.36 to 1.82 seconds each**, steady. **Still owed: hours rather than minutes**, which is what the fact actually names.

**ANSWERED for a day at a light rate, 2026-08-17, and the limit is stated rather than hidden.** Over **24.022 hours** on the German box the pace held with no drift at all — medians flat to within 0.004 s across every four-hour block, five full-HD pictures at **0.485–0.765 s** at the 0, 6, 12, 18 and 24-hour marks, nothing refused. **But that box sent lightly**: two small calls every five minutes and one picture every six hours. **A day at a rendering job's rate was not posed.** So the fact splits: *does the pace survive a day* is answered yes, and *does it survive a day of heavy sending* rests on the burst readings above and on nothing longer. See [Two more boxes](Two%20more%20boxes%20—%20the%20volume,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20a%20day%20left%20running.md).

**The refusal record belongs here, filed 2026-08-21.** This entry's *nothing was ever refused* is true of **sends** and was read across the project as a property of the service. It is not. **Two batches recorded a refusal**, both on `setWebhook`: `{"ok":false,"error_code":429,"description":"Too Many Requests: retry after 1","parameters":{"retry_after":1}}` — `Six machines`, under this fact's own heading, and [One box](One%20box%20—%20the%20Telegram%20capture%20and%20the%20port%20that%20cannot%20be%20chosen.md). The field is documented on the reference page this module's sources file already read, under `ResponseParameters`: *"Optional. In case of exceeding flood control, the number of seconds left to wait before the request can be repeated"* (`https://core.telegram.org/bots/api`, read 2026-08-20). **And the rate itself is published**, on a page nobody had opened — *"In a single chat, avoid sending more than one message per second. We may allow short bursts that go over this limit, but eventually you'll begin receiving 429 errors."* (`https://core.telegram.org/bots/faq`, read 2026-08-20). **So the heavy-day half is no longer an unobserved gap**: it is a published number the design is at 62% of at rest and over under load. `Telegram.md` §21 item 4 carries the arithmetic and C6a the back-off rule this forced. **This entry's own qualified sentence survives whole** — the service absorbed a burst by delaying it, *at least at that depth*, and Telegram's FAQ says the same in its own words.

### Fact 66 — Whether a stranger can reach the bot at all, and what the bot sees when they do.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 6 — proving the conversation is the user's. **The one half of the 2026-08-12 run that could not be done**, for want of anybody else's Telegram account. The filtering is known to be possible; the thing being filtered has never been produced.
**Answered by:** a throwaway against a live bot.
**Machine:** none. *(This said **a second Telegram account**, which the Creator must supply. **Corrected 2026-08-21: the documented half needs no account at all.**)*
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** the documented half was always posable by reading; only the arrival itself wants an account, and nothing turns on it.

**ANSWERED for the documented half, 2026-08-21, on two of Telegram's own pages, and the request for a second account is withdrawn.** *Whether a stranger can reach the bot at all:* `https://core.telegram.org/bots`, read 2026-08-20 — *"Bots can't start conversations with users. A user must either add them to a group or send them a message first. People can search for your bot's username or start a chat via its unique t.me/bot_username link."* **People, unqualified**, with nothing on that page restricting who. *What the bot sees:* `https://core.telegram.org/bots/features`, read the same day — *"All bots will also receive, regardless of privacy mode: All service messages. All messages from private chats."* **And no @BotFather command restricts who may message a bot** — that page's complete command list contains none. **The update's shape is documented from both ends and already captured by this project**: a `from` and a `chat`, the chat's `type` *"private"*. **What stays unobserved is the arrival itself**, and `Telegram.md` P5 says the filter is complete without it. **The bot token of 2026-08-19 was never what was missing, and neither, it turns out, was the account.** **`Telegram.md` Section 7 answers the promise without it**: the proof is possession at setup, the filter is one comparison against the proved conversation identity, and it **fails closed** — an update whose conversation cannot be established is ignored rather than answered, because answering would tell a stranger the bot is alive. What is unobserved is the thing being filtered ever arriving. Section 21 item 2.

### Fact 67 — What a machine still holding a replaced token does, and whether it can tell it has been replaced.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 7 — the deferred-delivery row. The whole promise of *a way back* is that a machine Smoozy has not reached stops being able to act as the bot.
**Answered by:** a throwaway against a live bot.
**Machine:** none — a bot and two holders of its token.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-16** — the old holder is cut off at once and **cannot tell why**. A poll in flight when the Creator revoked the token came back short, and the next call 0.47 seconds later was refused, with a 58-byte body: `{"ok":false,"error_code":401,"description":"Unauthorized"}` — **byte for byte what a token that never existed answers**. So a retired machine learns only that it is out, never that it was replaced, and nothing names a cause or a moment. **Whatever tells a machine it has been retired must come from Smoozy.** The bot itself survives whole: under the new token the id and username are unchanged and the update position the old holder had confirmed still holds.

### Fact 68 — Whether a render running on a rented machine can be reached and told to end **by something running on that same machine, with nobody connected**.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 8 — the stop from a phone.
**Split note — ruled, and this is the split the brief asks about by name.** Telegram argues this is **not** Fact 2, because the phone's stop arrives with Smoozy closed, so it is issued locally by the piece of Telegram on the machine rather than over a connection. **I keep them apart, and here is why.** Three things differ, not one:
1. **The mechanism.** Fact 2 is an inbound connection driving a command. This is one process on the machine signalling a sibling.
2. **The failure modes.** Fact 2 can fail because a connection cannot reach a detached process. This can fail because the thing Smoozy left on the machine has no handle on the render at all, or no permission to signal it — a failure Fact 2 cannot expose.
3. **What a yes proves.** A yes to Fact 2 may rest entirely on the connection's own session control, which is exactly what is absent here. A yes to Fact 2 therefore does not establish this, and merging them would rest the phone's stop on a question nobody posed.

Telegram's own recommendation stands and is adopted: **pose it on the same machine at the same moment as Fact 2, since it costs one more command.** Separate number, same batch, same sitting.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental. Rides on the Fact 1 and 2 throwaway.
**Shape-deciding:** no, but it sits directly on the shape pair.
**Posable:** the plain half now; the full form once the shape exists, as with Fact 2 — **on Batch E**, which carries it from 2026-08-18.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-13** — yes, with nobody connected. The first attempt failed because it found its target by pattern and matched two processes, so **a stop must be aimed at a recorded identity, never at a pattern**. Redone against the identity the service manager holds, it worked.

**ANSWERED IN ITS FULL FORM, 2026-08-18 — yes.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §4. This is the form the split argued for: **the whole stop sequence taken by something on the machine, rather than a service manager's own identity.** A detached script scheduled the stop 90 seconds out and recorded the conditions at the moment it acted; the tester disconnected entirely and did not touch the box. **At the moment of the stop the logged-in list was empty and the only connection process was the listener.** The stop went through the ordinary command path, wrote the desired state, issued the signal to the recorded render process, and Blender was alive before and gone after, with a recorded outcome afterwards carrying counts and exit status.

**One honest limit, recorded rather than smoothed over:** the machine's session manager still listed one session for the supervisor's login, with no seat and no terminal, **because the detached script inherited it** — not because anyone was connected. **Thin: one scheduled stop.**


### Fact 69 — What Telegram's bot-making chat does when a unique name is already taken.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Telegram.md` Fact 10 — the setup walkthrough. Recorded in the sources file as not provoked when a bot was made on 2026-08-12.
**Answered by:** a throwaway against the live service.
**Machine:** none — a Telegram account and one deliberately colliding name.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**NOT ANSWERED, and a bot token cannot answer it.** The bot-making chat only talks to people, so no key in `~/.smoozy-keys/` reaches it — **this one needs the Creator's own hands on Telegram**, which the runner established on 2026-08-19 after the token arrived. **It costs one sentence's wording**: `Telegram.md` S1 has Smoozy generating another candidate name on a tap, which needs nothing from the bot-making chat, so what is missing is how the collision is described to the user. Section 21 item 3.
