**This is not one of the facts the ruling of 2026-08-31 closes.** It needs no rental and no Blender research — one message typed into the bot-making chat by a person. **It is on the Creator's list at `What Build inherits` item 6**, and it must not be written as permanently unresolved. *(Added 2026-09-02.)*

---

## Facts 70 to 76 — what a render leaves, and the app on the user's computer

### Fact 70 — Whether both of a render's output streams can be captured on the machine, kept, read back afterwards with nobody connected, and searched for a line naming a path — and whether *the render printed nothing* can be told from *nothing was collected*.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/What a job writes.md` Fact 6 — "whether what a render printed can be captured, kept and searched afterwards for a line naming a path, for a job that ran detached with nobody connected and finished before anyone looked"; it is the whole of the signal a lost write leaves, and the document says a program driving the render "cannot be told, only read". `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 7 — "whether both of a render's output streams can be captured on the machine and read back afterwards with nobody connected, and whether *the render printed nothing* can be told from *nothing was collected*".
**Merge note.** Clean merge; each contributes a clause the other lacks — the searching, and the two-streams-apart-and-nothing-versus-nothing pair.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental.
**Shape-deciding:** no.
**Posable:** the plain half now — whether a detached run's two streams can be kept apart and read back later — on the Fact 1 throwaway. The full question **only once the shape exists**, since there is nothing on the machine to collect anything until Fact 1 is settled.

**ANSWERED at the plain half, 2026-08-13** — both streams are captured separately, kept, read back with nobody connected, and searchable, with the ending status recorded. **The second half stands**: whether *the render printed nothing* can be told from *nothing was collected* was not tested.

**THE SECOND HALF IS ANSWERED, 2026-08-18 — yes, on all four outcomes.** See [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md) §7.1. Six cases were run and **read back with nobody connected** from a detached script, the one exception named in the finding. *Collected and empty* was **not** written where the worker was killed — that case gave *collection incomplete*, written by reconciliation, with the reason *"the worker never reported an end of stream"*. **A sink that was never established is plainly distinct from an empty one**, answering *never established* with its own reason. **And every count agreed exactly with what the sinks held**, in the ordinary case and in every other. A refused write to a sink gave *collection incomplete* with errno 28 and a count stopped at the refusal, **while the render itself was not touched.**

**THE MEASUREMENT NOBODY HAD TAKEN, same day.** Not a numbered fact and the one that could have cost a render, since the captured streams were given no cap when it was taken — they now carry a finite per-attempt ceiling above a 64 MiB control-state reserve (`Rented-server runtime` M4a), with any loss stated by M13's `collection incomplete` outcome. On real 100-frame animations through the throwaway's own counting loop: **about 5,143 bytes per frame on the ordinary stream under Blender 4.5.9, about 3,713 under 5.0.1 for the identical scene** — roughly a 28% reduction across the version boundary — **and 0.0 bytes per frame on the complaints stream in every run**, including a scene with a deliberately missing texture. An EEVEE run gave 426 bytes per frame. **Thin: one render each, one scene family, processor-only, on an otherwise idle box**; the complaints figure is a measured zero rather than a demonstrated capacity, and the wall-clock gap between versions is a warm cache rather than a measurement. The missing-texture silence is a **Blender observation routed as a question owed elsewhere**, not a claim made here.


### Fact 71 — Whether Smoozy can drive a Blender on a rented machine to make a small viewable copy of a frame written in a working format, over the connection it holds, and what that costs in the machine's time.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/What a job writes.md` Fact 7 — what the frame preview reads. Open Threads notes that `Preparation/Specs/Telegram.md` already has the bot make copies on the machine, so whether that and the app's own copies are one piece of work or two is this stage's — a **shape** question, not part of this fact.
**Split note.** Kept apart from Fact 5. Fact 5 is driving a Blender to *read a value out*; this is driving it to *produce a file*, and it carries a cost question Fact 5 does not.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental with a Blender on it. Same box as Fact 5.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-17, with one named gap.** See [One box with Blender on it](One%20box%20with%20Blender%20on%20it%20—%20the%20reading,%20the%20listing,%20and%20the%20names%20that%20do%20not%20come%20back.md) §6. Driving a Blender headless over the connection to make a small viewable copy works, and **the cost is about 0.8 seconds idle and about 2.4 seconds with all four cores taken by a render**, the output byte-identical between the two and the render still progressing. **Blender's own startup dominates; the work itself is about 0.2 seconds.**

**The colour choice is the whole difficulty and it was posed rather than assumed.** The file records no treatment, so something must choose; the tester chose the job's own view transform read out of the `.blend`. Under a plain choice **23.7% of the picture blows out**; under the job's own and one other, none of it does. Every preview was fetched and looked at: all three read plainly as the render with textures visible, **and a copy with no treatment chosen does not** — shading gone, surfaces flat clipped colour. **That is the failure mode and it is obvious rather than subtle.**

**The gap.** Single-layer OpenEXR and Radiance HDR behave identically in all three versions. **Multilayer OpenEXR does not**: loading it gives no pixels and **the scaling call silently succeeds and changes nothing**, the saving call writes at full size, and the compositor's own interface differs across the version boundary. **4.5.9 produced the small copy through the compositor; 5.0.1 and 5.2.0 did not** — a gap in that sitting rather than a demonstrated impossibility. A route that does work for multilayer in all three is the image library bundled inside Blender's own Python, **which reproduces the job's treatment only when pointed at Blender's own colour configuration** and otherwise guesses wrong.

**What else the machine carries: outside Blender, nothing at all** — no image tool of any kind, and no imaging library in the system Python. Inside Blender's Python, two. Installing an outside tool pulls six further packages; nothing was installed.

### Fact 72 — Whether a transfer over the connection Smoozy holds already tells a whole arrival from a damaged one, or whether that has to be added — and what the user's own end is capable of.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 4 — the whole-or-plainly-not-there row.
**Split note — flagged.** Kept apart from Fact 18. *Damaged* and *truncated* are different conditions and a check for one need not catch the other. Same box, same sitting, so the split costs nothing.
**Answered by:** a rented machine.
**Machine:** an ordinary rental, **with the transfer posed from the Creator's own Mac rather than from anywhere else** — the finding on record is that the Mac's `rsync` is Apple's `openrsync` and refuses the flags normally used for resuming, while the rented machine had the ordinary one. A question answered from a Linux box at both ends gives the wrong answer for the only end that is not rented.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED in part, 2026-08-15/16, and the split is sharp.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 72. **Damage in the pipe is caught for free and hard.** With a relay flipping exactly one bit — and a control run first, flip disabled, proving the relay faithful — the connection died in both directions: `message authentication code incorrect` downloading, `Broken pipe` uploading. **But the failure presents as a dropped connection rather than a corruption message**, indistinguishable from an ordinary drop without reading the exit text, **and it leaves the same nameless partial under the final name.** The machine's own log said nothing at all, so an integrity failure cannot be learned by reading the rental's log.

**Damage at rest is invisible.** A file with four bytes overwritten in place **and its modification time put back** travelled through `scp` perfectly at exit 0, and a default `rsync` **called it done** — 48 bytes sent, exit 0, file still corrupted, and with a change summary requested it printed nothing at all.

**The check need not be built, but it must be asked for.** Apple's `rsync` supports checksum comparison, caught the four-byte wound, repaired it by sending 36,314 bytes and reported it machine-readably. The user's own end has nothing else: **`sftp`'s 33 commands include no checksum, verify, hash or digest command of any kind**, and `scp` has nothing either.

**Still owed:** compression was never on, only the default cipher suite was exercised, disk-caused corruption was not posed, and nothing was posed against Windows.

### Fact 73 — What each of Mac, Windows and Linux lets an application post as a notification: whether one can be posted at all, what the user must grant first, what happens when they have not, and whether a notification can carry a picture.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 5 — the notification row.
**Answered by:** reading each operating system's own documentation, and trying it on a computer running each.
**Machine:** none rented.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** Mac and Linux now; Windows not confirmable.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-18, by the reading batch, and the clause most likely to break the promise bites on all three.** See [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §4.

**A plain downloaded binary cannot post a notification on any of the three systems without extra machinery**, and in each case the machinery is ordinary. **macOS requires a bundle identity** — Apple documents that precondition nowhere and it is decisive: an unbundled executable is killed by an uncaught exception on the very first call, exit 134, nothing to catch; inside a bundle with an identifier it works, and **an ad-hoc signature was enough**, no Developer ID and no notarization. Reproduced exactly by the check, including the distinct failure paths for *no bundle* and *bundle with no identifier*. **Windows needs either a runtime package an unpackaged app must deploy through its installer** — Microsoft's own page says this "can compromise the simplicity of self-contained deployment" — **or a Start-menu shortcut carrying an application identifier**: "Without a valid shortcut installed in the Start screen or in All Programs, you cannot raise a toast notification from a desktop app." **And the same page says who may install that shortcut, in the two sentences before the one quoted — recorded here 2026-08-22, where this entry carried the requirement and not the route.** *How to enable desktop toast notifications through an AppUserModelID*, `https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/shell/enable-desktop-toast-with-appusermodelid`, page footer *"Last updated on 2021-01-07"*, read 2026-08-20: *"This topic shows you how to create a shortcut for your app, assign it an AppUserModelID, and install it in the Start screen. We strongly recommend that you do this in the Windows Installer rather than in your app's code."* **That is a recommendation and not a precondition**, and the page's worked example is the app-code route: `TryCreateShortcut` is a method of the application, reading `APPDATA` and writing into `\Microsoft\Windows\Start Menu\Programs`, per-user, with no elevation and no installer in either listing. **An elevated Windows app cannot post at all, and the post fails silently.** **Linux has no identity precondition at all**, but needs a session message bus and a process owning the notification name, and **the specification says outright that clients "must not assume that it is available"** — so Linux is the one system where the promise can fail for a reason Smoozy cannot fix.

**The picture clause, which Row 284 rests on.** **macOS: yes** — JPEG, GIF or PNG only, 10 MB maximum, **and it must be a file on disk**, with no in-memory supply. **Windows: two Microsoft pages disagree and the disagreement is real** — one lists three image sources, two of which need a package identity an unpackaged app lacks; another documents a local file path "Supported only for desktop apps". **Neither page acknowledges the other**, both were verified verbatim on both runs, and the contradiction is deliberately left unresolved. **Linux: yes, and it is the only one documenting pixels supplied in memory** — but **there is no capability string for it**, so a client cannot ask in advance whether the server will render it.

**Three permission models rather than one.** macOS: the app requests, the system prompts, a denial is **silent** and the state is readable back. Windows: no prompt and no request interface found, only a read-only setting. Linux: **no permission model in the specification at all**, so no denied state to read.

**Replace-by-identity works on all three** and is atomic on Linux by specification. *(The clause that used to follow — that this is what lets one repeating fact be said once — is struck on 2026-08-18. `Desktop app and delivery` Round 1 established that a later failure resolving to a standing identity needs no post at all, so replace-by-identity guarantees that any post which does happen adds no second, and is not what makes *said once* true. Whether a replace re-alerts or restores a dismissed notification is established on none of the three: that document's §22 item 27.)* Withdrawal works on all three. **Only Windows documents a true in-place update**, progress data only. **Grouping is absent on Linux.** **No rate limit or coalescing is documented anywhere.**

**STILL OWED:** **no notification was posted and no permission requested, deliberately**, so image rendering, banner placement and the exact denied-state error are unobserved even on macOS. All Windows and all Linux claims are documentation alone.

### Fact 74 — How a binary is produced, signed and hosted for each of Mac, Windows and Linux, and what an application must do to replace itself on each while it is running.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 9 — the update row. Open Threads gives this stage the substance and Build the doing.
**Answered by:** reading each vendor's own documentation, and trying it on a computer running each.
**Machine:** none rented. **The signing halves cannot be tried on a machine that is not the platform in question.**
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** Mac and Linux now; Windows not at all for the signing half.

**ANSWERED on all three, 2026-08-18, and the citation check reversed the answer.** See [The user's own computer](The%20user's%20own%20computer%20—%20where%20a%20download%20lands,%20what%20it%20will%20take,%20what%20it%20will%20show,%20and%20how%20the%20app%20replaces%20itself.md) §5.

**The promise is reachable on all three systems, and not by one shared mechanism.** The first run reported that no Microsoft page documents a plain downloaded Windows app replacing itself unattended, which would have been `Method`'s first escalation — a promise the architecture cannot keep. **Two current Microsoft pages refute it**, and the surviving answer is narrower and different in kind.

**macOS — documented yes.** Apple publishes a page about an updater replacing a file containing signed code: modifying in place is the wrong route, because "macOS caches information about the code's signature in the kernel" and it "can cause a hard-to-reproduce code-signing crash"; write to a temporary file and rename over the original is the right one, with a supported API carrying a **named backup for rollback**. **No prompt, elevation or administrator appears anywhere on that page**, confirmed by reading it end to end. **Observed both ways on the Mac**: rename-over, unlink and move-aside-then-replace all succeed on a running executable, and with the hardened runtime enabled by an ad-hoc signature the in-place route left a signature the verifier called valid on disk and a binary killed at every launch, while write-then-rename ran.

**Windows — the outcome is documented, the in-place file operation is not.** Microsoft documents an unpackaged per-user executable that "downloads the update to a hidden folder without any user interaction", "verifies and installs it", "stops and then restarts", with users who "don't need administrative rights", plus an interface a third party can call to update and relaunch with no administrative rights. **So an unattended, unelevated, self-relaunching update of an unpackaged Windows app is documented.** But **every documented route puts the new build somewhere else** — one says "a new side-by-side folder" — and in-place replacement is blocked by everything Microsoft does document: deletion "fails if an application attempts to delete a file that has other handles open"; a rename "will still fail if a file with the same name already exists and is … a currently executing file"; deferred rename "can be used only if the process is in the context of a user who belongs to the administrators group or the LocalSystem account". **The Windows answer is side-by-side, not in-place.** Two conditions: the third-party interface "is not supported in .NET Core and .NET 5 and later versions", and on current runtimes "checking updates before the application starts is the only update option supported".

**Linux — narrowed 2026-08-22 and the narrowing is Fact 79's, not this entry's. Added 2026-08-29.** The route below argues the Linux update from `rename(2)` and a self-update library, and **that is not how Smoozy updates on Linux**: the release and update artifact is a signed, dependency-declaring package installed through the distribution's own package mechanism, which `Preparation/Architecture/Desktop app and delivery.md` E4 and Z10 fix and `Fact 79`'s narrowing paragraph carries. `rename(2)` underwrites ordinary data publication there rather than the arrival of a new version. **Read Fact 79's narrowing paragraph before this one.** *(Routed from the `The shape` stage-close gate, which found that document pointing at Fact 74 for both narrowings when Fact 74 carries the macOS one alone — so a reader checking the Linux update clause against the register landed on a paragraph arguing the opposite. The paragraph below is kept as what was read at the kernel layer, which is true of what it describes.)*

**Linux — documented yes at the kernel layer, on the thinnest documentation of the three.** `rename(2)`: an existing path "will be atomically replaced, so that there is no point at which another process attempting to access newpath will find it missing", and "Open file descriptors for oldpath are also unaffected"; only in-place writing is refused, with `ETXTBSY`. **The project-level half is weaker than it first looked** — the self-update library's page is copyrighted to 2022, three of the four claims first quoted from it appear nowhere on it, its promise never to overwrite before "all validation mechanisms report success" covers **integrity only** because signature validation is stated as unimplemented, and the flags it says to respect still need defining.

**The cost is not technical and it lands on Windows.** Microsoft's managed signing is documented as open to individuals **located in the United States or Canada only**; organizations need "a verifiable tax history of three or more years"; a paid subscription is mandatory; validation takes "from 1 to 20 business days" with three document attempts before the door closes; **and the two Microsoft country lists genuinely disagree with each other**, reproduced. The fallback is an ordinary certificate at "$300–500/year". **And a signature does not buy silence:** "Even when signed, a newly created binary could still show a SmartScreen warning until its hash or publisher certificate accumulates sufficient evidence of positive reputation", taking "several weeks and hundreds of clean installs from a wide audience", with **"no need (or mechanism) to manually submit a file for SmartScreen reputation review for consumer endpoints"** — *the quotation stopped at "manually submit a file" until 2026-08-22, dropping the qualifier that makes it true rather than absolute; a submission portal does exist, and the same page scopes it to enterprise IT administrators accelerating trust for internal or managed deployments* — and **extended-validation certificates no longer bypass it**. At this project's scale the documentation says the warning does not go away on its own. **macOS keeps a Mac in the pipeline** — notarization can be done off one, signing is documented nowhere as being able to be. **Linux requires nothing.**

**The no-account promise holds and is documented.** Apple's download checks "have never included the user's Apple Account or the identity of their device", and Windows' verification call takes a value documented as meaning "There is no interactive user".

**STILL OWED, and it is the clause that could cost the user something no spec promises:** **whether a quarantined macOS app re-triggers first-open approval for the copy it writes for itself is documented on no first-party page read.** The checker attacked it and every source that appeared to answer it was a forum. If it re-triggers, macOS users meet a dialog on every update, and **what clearing it costs is unestablished — corrected 2026-08-22, where this said *clearing it costs them their login password*, which rests on no first-party page.** Apple's *Safely open apps on your Mac*, `https://support.apple.com/en-us/102445`, read 2026-08-20, describes the override for a **blocked** app — a different case — as Settings, *"Privacy & Security"*, *"Open Anyway"*, then *"Open"*, and names no password anywhere. **What was measured, on the Creator's Mac, macOS 26.5.2 build 25F84, 2026-08-20:** a file a program fetched over its own connection and wrote with ordinary file calls carried `com.apple.provenance` and **no `com.apple.quarantine`**, identically to a plain locally created file, and a file written inside a directory carrying `com.apple.quarantine` did not inherit it — with a positive control showing browser downloads on the same machine do carry it. **That does not close the question**: Apple's *Gatekeeper and runtime protection*, `https://support.apple.com/guide/security/gatekeeper-and-runtime-protection-sec5599b66df/web`, published 2024-12-19, read 2026-08-20, names a second mechanism over the same case — *"Gatekeeper also tracks the provenance of files written by downloaded software."* — and *"all software in macOS is checked for known malicious content the first time it's opened, regardless of how it arrived on the Mac."* Everything Windows is documentation alone.

**What `Desktop app and delivery` added to that clause on 2026-08-18, and it is new:** **the architecture's own choice bears on it.** ~~That document replaces the executable **inside the already-approved bundle** rather than installing a new bundle at a new path, which is the choice least likely to trip the approval~~ — **superseded 2026-08-22 and corrected here 2026-08-23; the paragraph had gone on saying the 2026-08-18 mechanism after the choice moved.** **The replaceable unit is the complete signed and notarized `.app` bundle, replaced at the installed path the user already approved, and the updater never writes, deletes or renames a member inside it** — because a modern signature seals a bundle's resources and a signed bundle is to be treated as read-only (`Desktop app and delivery` Z5, quoting Apple's TN2206; the same correction is recorded at Fact 79's narrowing paragraph). **The bearing on this fact is unchanged in kind and if anything stronger:** the installed path is the approved one either way, so the question stays *does the copy the app writes for itself re-trigger first-open approval* rather than becoming *does a new path*. The document states outright that if the fact closes the other way it is a change to what the user gets and returns to Specs then. **One residual travels with the new unit** — the directory form of the named-backup replacement is unread (`Desktop app and delivery` §22 item 43). **The Windows twin of the same question is now Fact 97**, and it is not a twin in shape: the Windows update is side-by-side by construction and cannot take that precaution.

### Fact 75 — Whether a total rate cap can be held across concurrent transfers to more than one machine at once, and whether the rate actually achieved can be read.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 11 — the speed-cap row.
**Split note:** see Fact 25. Kept apart — a cap being *held* is not the same as throughput being *measured*.
**Answered by:** a rented machine, or rather two.
**Machine:** **two rentals running at the same time**, because the promise is stated across servers and a single machine cannot pose it. Neither needs a card or a screen; the cheapest two boxes the provider offers will do, and the answer wants the Creator's own home connection at the other end rather than a fast one.
**Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED: yes to both halves, 2026-08-15/16, with a catch that decides the design.** See [Six machines](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md), Fact 75. Caps hold in both directions and across machines — 409 KB/s against a requested 400 on concurrent uploads to two boxes, and 402.8 and 405.5 KB/s on concurrent downloads against 9.44 MB/s uncapped on the identical transfer.

**The catch: the limit is per invocation**, so dividing a total budget statically only holds while every transfer is still running. With uneven sizes, a two-way 400+400 split against an 800 KB/s budget achieved **421.9 KB/s** — **half the budget forfeited the moment the small transfer finished** — while a single shared budget in one process, same work, achieved **820 KB/s** and cut 21.85 seconds to 11.24. **So a total cap needs one shared budget, not per-transfer caps.**

**Still owed:** the shared-budget mechanism was tested on **uploads only**, and the download case differs materially — a receiver enforces a cap by not reading, so buffering can let the wire rate overshoot.

### Fact 76 — What a key fingerprint is, which form of it is taken, whether it is stable across the three operating systems and across key types, and whether two distinct keys can ever share one.

**Named by:** `Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Whole app.md` Fact 1 — the fingerprint row. Says to settle it with the key-type question Open Threads parks beside it, since the answer may differ by type.
**Split note — flagged.** Kept apart from Facts 32 and 35, which touch fingerprints. Fact 35 asks whether a fingerprint can be *read* from a protected private key; this asks what a fingerprint *is* and whether two keys can collide, which is the property the Whole app promise rests on. Answered in the same sitting from the same manuals.
**Answered by:** reading OpenSSH's own documentation.
**Machine:** none. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

**ANSWERED IN PART, 2026-08-16.** SHA-256 is the documented default hash on four pages, with md5 the only alternative; the printed form is `SHA256:` plus unpadded base64, or `MD5:` plus colon-hex. The digest is taken over the decoded wire-format public key blob, so it is **independent of the comment and of file whitespace**, and is identical whether taken from the private file or the public one. **Both open clauses were worked on 2026-08-20; one closes and the other turns out to be the wrong question.**

**Cross-system stability — ANSWERED, and it needs no other machine.** The figure is taken over bytes the **protocol** fixes, not the operating system: RFC 4253 §6.6 defines a public key's encoding as *"string certificate or public key format identifier"* followed by *"byte[n] key/certificate data"*. So the input is protocol-fixed and the operation is a published hash, leaving nothing for a system to vary. **Posed by taking the figure apart on the Creator's Mac:** the base64 field of a key file was decoded and hashed directly, and both the SHA-256 and the MD5 forms **reproduced the tool's printed line exactly**, at 32 and 16 digest bytes. **A negative would have been either recomputation differing from the printed line**, which would have meant the tool mixes in something of its own — a path, a comment, a system value. It mixes in nothing.

**The collision clause is the wrong question, and the right one has an answer.** It is not *can two keys share a fingerprint*; it is **which hash is the identity**, because the answer flips between the two forms this entry already records. RFC 6151, read 2026-08-20: *"The attacks presented in [KLIM2006] can find MD5 collision in about one minute on a standard notebook PC (Intel Pentium, 1.6GHz)."* and *"MD5 is no longer acceptable where collision resistance is required such as digital signatures."* **So two distinct keys sharing one MD5 fingerprint is a documented, cheap construction, and the printed form is selectable** — `FingerprintHash` takes *"md5 and sha256 (the default)"*. **The rule nobody had written down is: pin the hash to SHA-256 explicitly, and never treat an MD5 fingerprint as an identity.** It is now in `Keys` and in `Security and licence`. **What stays not settled, and it is a citation rather than a doubt:** no first-party sentence says a SHA-256 collision does not exist, because an absence of published collisions is not a citable statement. **Whether the Whole app promise's world-side clause may close on that basis is the Creator's**, and it is filed as such — this project carries no equivalent worry about someone guessing a private key. See [The manuals](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md).

---

## Named by a sweep, and not this stage's

**`Audits/Architecture/Sweep/Queue & History.md` Fact J — whether a whole job's renderlet total can be worked out where the view-layer switch is driven rather than keyframed, or changed by a script inside the file as each frame comes round.** Deliberately unnumbered. The keyframed half is already answered — *Reading a file 3*, in all four versions on both machines — and the remainder is a Blender question that no question in the register asks. **It goes back to Blender Research as a question of its own.** No rented machine answers it, and nobody here answers it from memory. This is the 121st named fact and the only one that leaves the stage.

**Exposed by the six-machine batch, 2026-08-16 — what a render does when a script handed to it at launch cannot be read.** Also deliberately unnumbered, and it leaves the stage for the same reason.

Observed once, on VAST-2, and **recorded as one machine's behaviour rather than as an established fact**: a run whose `--python` script sat in a folder the login could not read printed `OSError: Python file "…" could not be opened: Permission denied`, then **rendered anyway** — the factory scene, at 1920×1080, on EEVEE — wrote a file, and **quit at exit status 0**.

**Why this cannot be settled here.** It is a Blender behaviour, and [Rule 2](../Groundwork/Rules.md) allows Blender facts only from the dedicated research. Nothing in the question register asks it: *Settings 9* covers a stray argument taken as a file name, and *Reports 1* covers what a run prints, but neither asks what happens to a **settings script that cannot be opened**. One observation on one box in one version is not the four-version, two-machine answer this project's research bar requires.

**Why it matters more than its size suggests.** The launch-script route is what Row 120 and several Rendering rows lean on for imposing settings without touching the artist's file. If a script Smoozy hands over is unreadable — a permission slip, a wrong path, a folder the login cannot traverse — the evidence here says the job does not fail. **It silently renders the wrong scene at the wrong size with the wrong engine, and reports success.** Every gate this project has built for catching a bad render reads the exit status or the frames on disk, and both would pass.

**Where it goes.** To Blender Research as a question of its own, in the **Settings** or **Reports** topic. Until it is answered there, no architecture document may rest on a launch script failing loudly.

**Exposed by `Rendering`'s Round 1, 2026-08-19 — whether a value delivered by a command-line argument is in force by the time a script handed in at the same launch runs.** Deliberately unnumbered, and it leaves the stage for the same reason as the two above. **It is the twin of the unreadable-script question and it was held nowhere a later stage would meet it**, which is why it is written here: three architecture documents carried it in their unresolved lists — `Job files and outputs` §14 item 19, `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 11 and `Rendering` §9 item 1 — and an unresolved list is the one place [Method](Method.md) says a reader will not look.

**What rests on it.** `Rendering` L10 delivers the regular output path by its own command-line argument and has the launch material **read the value back**, failing the attempt where it does not match the destination map's pair. That read-back is the positive check standing between the app and a render that writes where nobody asked. **If the argument is not yet in force when the material runs**, the read-back sees the file's own recorded path, mismatches, and **every attempt fails before the render instruction — no job renders anywhere.**

**Why this cannot be settled here.** It is a Blender behaviour and [Rule 2](../Groundwork/Rules.md) allows Blender facts only from the dedicated research. Nothing in the register asks it: *Settings 1* and *What a render demands 3* cover which values have arguments, *Writing 1* covers imposing an output path, and *Settings 16* and *Dependencies 17* cover the window an argument must sit in — none asks about the **order** in which an argument's value and a handed-in script take effect within that window.

**What holds meanwhile, and it is why no document escalated it.** The failure is **safe and total rather than silent**: nothing renders to the wrong place, the marker names what was read, and an ordering that holds costs nothing at all. That is the opposite shape from the unreadable-script question above, which fails quietly at success.

**Where it goes.** To Blender Research as a question of its own, in the **Settings** or **Reports** topic, beside the unreadable-script question it is the twin of.

## The six-machine batch, 2026-08-15 and 2026-08-16

**Six machines across three providers, run together, covering Batches B, C and D.** The Creator supplied five already running and authorised one more rental to pose Fact 48. All six were destroyed afterwards and all three provider accounts verified empty — no instances, no volumes, no snapshots.

**The answers of record are in [Six machines — the container, the webhook and the ready action](Six%20machines%20—%20the%20container,%20the%20webhook%20and%20the%20ready%20action.md).** That document describes each machine exactly and carries what was run and what came back. This section is the register's index into it, so a reader of this file can see at a glance what moved.

**Every fact this section names now carries its own answer in its own entry above, as of 2026-08-18.** For two days it did not: the index listed thirty facts answered while **eighteen of their entries said nothing at all**, and Facts 19 and 46 were in the same state from the answered-in-part list. **A reader looking a fact up in its entry — which is how the register is meant to be used — would have read an answered fact as open.** That is the fault a close gate caught on Fact 21 on 2026-08-17, found here at scale by the runner's own check before writing the sixth spine document. **The findings document was always the answer of record and nothing in it changed**; what was missing was the write-back.

**Answered, and the promise stands.** Facts **3**, **5**, **6**, **7**, **15**, **17**, **18**, **20**, **21**, **25**, **27**, **28**, **29**, **43**, **45**, **47**, **48**, **51**, **54**, **55**, **57**, **58**, **60**, **62**, **71**, **72**, **75**, **77**, **78**, **80**. Thirty facts.

**Answered in part, with what remains named.**

- **Fact 1** — the narrow non-root boot-registration clause is answered, on **both** a virtual machine and a container, by **different arrangements**. ~~Still owed: the remaining promise level on Batch E, and crash return, which was posed on neither kind.~~ **Both were answered by Batch E on 2026-08-18** — crash return by a real kernel panic on the virtual machine and by the container's own restart, which is a crash on that provider whether or not anyone asked for one. **The promise level came back yes for the reboot and the crash and NO for the disk-full form**; see this fact's own entry above.
- **Fact 4** — the batch accidentally answered the half this register records as uncovered: a driver **mismatched** rather than removed does **not** produce the loud refusal. Blender rendered on the processor and exited 0. Still owed: the other three Blender versions.
- **Fact 19** — answered, but the render was deliberately run on the processor; the card case was not posed.
- **Fact 46** — the several-card half is closed properly on a two-card box. **The no-card half is not**: none of the six was cardless, and all four attempts used a proxy. It needs the cheap cardless box Batch B2 always specified.
- **Fact 63** — the machine half and the documentation half are answered, and a webhook was registered and connected to for real. **The payload was never observed**, because no message ever reached the bot. See below.
- **Fact 65** — the sending half the fact actually names **was never exercised**, for the same reason.

**Not answerable on these machines, recorded rather than discovered later.** *(Written on 2026-08-16 and partly overtaken the same day — the disk clauses below were answered that afternoon on a rental with a volume attached, and Fact 62's window was re-posed. See the section above.)* Fact **6**'s disk-choice half and Fact **28**'s more-than-one-disk clause — **every one of the six was single-disk**, and the Batch B1 specification of "more than one disk" was never met. Fact **3** for any card make other than NVIDIA. Fact **62**'s *goes on reaching it* over a day; the longest window achieved was 38 minutes. Fact **77** on 5.0 and 5.1. Fact **66**, still wanting a second Telegram account.

**Blocked, and it costs a rental to recover — but the reason turned out to be a finding.** Every reading during the run showed no updates waiting and an empty result from the ordinary receiving call, which was first written up as *no message ever reached the bot*. **That was wrong.** The Creator confirmed afterwards that they had sent many messages to `@smoozy_example_bot` and that Telegram showed them as **read**, which for a bot means an update was **consumed** rather than never delivered.

**So the updates existed and something in the test consumed them without capturing them.** Which thing is not established — the receiving call was made several times, and a webhook was registered and later deleted; any of those consumes updates and none recorded a body.

**The lesson is worth more than the payload would have been.** The Telegram module rests on several machines holding one token, and this demonstrates the hazard underneath it: **an update belongs to whoever takes it first, and a holder that takes one removes it for every other holder, silently, leaving nothing behind to show it happened.** Fact **67** — what a machine still holding a replaced token does — should be read beside this.

Still unobserved: the delivered Update body, the secret-token header, the retry cadence, and **whether updates queued while a server is down survive or are lost**. **Closing them needs one rental with an open inbound port, and a rule: nothing else may hold the token while the capture runs.**

**A new fact this batch exposed, not yet numbered: Secure Boot.** The Sesterce box had legacy BIOS with no EFI, and its driver build logged `EFI variables are not supported on this system` when it tried to sign the module. **On a Secure Boot rental that step could fail outright, or demand a human enrol a key at the console — which Smoozy cannot do over SSH.** Fact 80 as worded does not cover it. It wants a number and a machine with Secure Boot on.

## The Telegram box, 2026-08-16

**One Vast.ai rental, alive under two hours, destroyed, and the account verified empty.** It cost about nine cents and it existed to close what the six-machine batch had left open on Telegram. The answers of record are in [One box](One%20box%20—%20the%20Telegram%20capture%20and%20the%20port%20that%20cannot%20be%20chosen.md); this is the register's index into it.

**Answered or moved on:** Facts **62** (a fourth location, still short of a day), **63** (now provider-shaped: no Vast rental can host a webhook), **65** (the sending half, minutes not hours), **67** (fully), **57** (the console way, through a proxy), and **46**'s no-card half established as unposable on this provider.

**Four things nobody had asked for, and three of them bear on the Telegram module directly.**

- **Updates queued while nothing listens survive.** Five messages sat 4,002–4,008 seconds — sixty-seven minutes — and arrived whole, in order, in one answer, the moment a poller started.
- **Two holders polling at once deadlock.** Forty seconds, fourteen calls, **every one** answered `409 Conflict: terminated by other getUpdates request` on **both** sides, and neither ever received an update. The previous batch's lesson — an update belongs to whoever takes it first — holds only for a holder that takes **and confirms**. Two that merely poll make the bot deaf, loudly.
- **An update taken but not confirmed comes back identically.** It is lost only when something takes it *and* asks past it. So the receiving arrangement must **confirm after the work is done, never on receipt**.
- **A button press arrives as a `callback_query`** carrying the bot's whole original message and the exact string the bot put on the button. Two presses five seconds apart arrived as two separate updates with **neither answered**, so an unanswered press does not swallow the next.

## The runner's second ruling, 2026-08-16 — attaching a volume is permitted

The Creator left this one to the runner. **A volume may be attached to a rental**, on three conditions: it is the **smallest size that poses the question**, it is **destroyed with the rental** and the account verified empty afterwards as every batch already does, and **nothing the project needs afterwards is ever put on it**.

The reasoning is that it is the only way left to pose Fact **6**'s disk-choice half and Fact **28**'s more-than-one-disk clause — seven rentals across three providers have all been single-disk — and it costs cents against a rental already being paid for by the hour.

**One limit belongs in the answer rather than in this ruling.** A volume answers *does a rental offer a choice of where to write*, which is the half the specs care about. It does **not** answer *does the machine have two physical disks*. Whichever was posed must be said in the answer, in those words.

## The reading batch, 2026-08-16

**No machine was rented and none was needed.** Nine facts — **32, 33, 34, 35, 36, 37, 38, 39 and 76** — were answered by reading each thing's own documentation, with throwaways on the Creator's own Apple-silicon Mac where reading did not settle it. Four agents answered, four fresh agents citation-checked one file each, and the answers of record are in [The manuals — keys, secrets, and what cannot be told apart](The%20manuals%20—%20keys,%20secrets,%20and%20what%20cannot%20be%20told%20apart.md). This section is the register's index into it.

**Answered outright:** Facts **32**, **33**, **34**, **36**, **37** and **39**. Six.

**Answered in part, with what remains named:**

- **Fact 35** — Half A answered 2026-08-16; **Half B answered 2026-08-20** by a run with two controls, the documentation half standing: the manual still does not state that `BatchMode=yes` suppresses a private-key passphrase prompt, so the construction is observed on one build rather than guaranteed. *(Corrected 2026-08-22 — this index line said Half B was open, four days after the entry itself recorded it answered.)*
- **Fact 38** — answered per operating system, but **Windows is documentation alone**, **Linux was never observed**, and one macOS observation is a **recorded disagreement** rather than a result. Its cost half is with the Creator.
- **Fact 76** — form, independence and **cross-system stability** answered, the last on 2026-08-20 from RFC 4253's protocol-fixed encoding plus a recomputation that reproduced the tool exactly. **The collision clause was the wrong question**: the answer turns on which hash, MD5 collisions are documented as cheap and SHA-256's absence of them is not a citable sentence, so the rule is to pin the hash and the residue is the Creator's.

**What the checks changed, which is why they are worth their cost.** 453 claims were ruled across the four files: 334 supported, 94 narrowed, 24 struck, 1 not found. The failures were nearly all of one kind — a real quote made to carry more than it says — plus several labels moved from documented to inferred, which everything downstream treats differently. Two claims had been supplied from an agent's own knowledge rather than any source and were cut.

**Three limits stand over the whole batch**, and they are stated at the top of the answer document as well: no Windows machine has run it and none is rented today; everything observed was observed on one Mac on one build, with Linux not observed at all; and two of the Windows sources are archived pages, one dated 2001, while three Linux distribution documentation sites refused to serve pages at all and the refusals reproduced.

**One caveat reaches beyond this batch.** `man.openbsd.org` serves OpenBSD-current, which runs ahead of shipping systems — it documents a key type the observed OpenSSH does not offer. **The supported type list must be read from the installed binary at runtime, never hardcoded from the manual.**

## The rental batches

**46 of the 81 facts wanted a rented machine when these batches were drawn**, and the live figure is in *Where the register stands* at the end of this file rather than here. They fall into five batches and imply **six separate rentals**, two of which run at the same time. **This plan has been largely overtaken by what actually happened**, and the record of that is above rather than here: Batch A ran on 2026-08-13 and answered nine; six machines ran on 2026-08-15 and 2026-08-16 and answered thirty outright; three more ran on 2026-08-16 for Telegram and the disk questions. **What the batches below are still good for is the specifications** — what a machine has to be for the questions nobody has managed to pose yet.

Facts wanting no machine at all, or a machine that is not a rental, are listed at the end so nobody puts them on a rental's list and wastes it.

### Batch A — the shape throwaway. **Go first.**

`Preparation/Architecture/Method.md` says the two shape facts cannot wait for the sweep to finish collecting everything else, so they get their own machine. This batch is that machine, plus only the riders that cost one more command each.

**One rental.** Ordinary: one NVIDIA card, no screen, an ordinary connection, a Blender on it, **provider console reachable**, and **nobody needs it afterwards** — it ends destroyed.

| Order | Facts |
|---|---|
| First | **1** (plain), **2** (plain), **68** |
| Riders, cheap | **8** (plain half), **40** (plain half), **70** (plain half), **31**, **58**, **62** |
| Terminal — run last, in this order | **55**, then **51**, then **4** |

The terminal three are ordered deliberately. Fact 55 needs a render and a transfer in flight at the moment of a restart; Fact 51 is that restart and may not come back; Fact 4 destroys the card. Anything else on the box must be finished before them.

### Batch B — the workhorse pair. **Two rentals at the same time.**

**B1, the workhorse.** An ordinary rental specified with: **more than one disk** — and note that *more than one disk* now means **more than one physical disk**, since the choice-of-place half was answered on 2026-08-16 with a volume attached instead — **more than one card**, no screen and no display server, a **non-root login** made on it, several Blenders placed several different ways (a plain unpacked folder, one through the machine's own software, one as a snap, one nobody installed), a **genuinely large asset**, files of several sizes, and **the Creator's own Mac at the other end** of every transfer.

**B2, the cheap companion.** The cheapest box the provider offers, **with no card at all**, running at the same time as B1. **Not from Vast.ai**, established 2026-08-16: every no-GPU offer that search returned there is a storage ask that cannot be rented as a machine, and its search carries no CPU-only filter to ask with. *(This said *it sells no cardless rental at any price* until 2026-08-22; the observation is about what could be asked for, and the conclusion — go elsewhere — is unchanged.)*

Order matters at the start of B1: Facts 47 and 54 must be asked on the **freshly rented, untouched** box, before the non-root login is made and before any Blender is placed.

| Where | Facts |
|---|---|
| B1, on arrival, untouched | **47**, **54** |
| B1, before anything is installed | **46** (the reading half) |
| B1, once set up | **3**, **5**, **77**, **78**, **80**, **6**, **7**, **15**, **17**, **18**, **19**, **20**, **21**, **27**, **28**, **29**, **43**, **45**, **60** (plain half), **71**, **72** |
| B2 | **46** (the no-card case), **57** (including the powered-off-from-console way) |
| B1 and B2 together | **25** (the sharing half), **75** |

Fact 3 answers one card make per rental. If the Creator expects users on more than one make, that is a further rental or a further card in B1.

### Batch C — the container rental. **One rental, possibly a different provider or plan; before the spine.**

Fact **48**, plus **Fact 1's narrow non-root boot-registration and reboot-return clause**. The one box on record was sold as a container and was a whole virtual machine, so finding the case may be part of the answer. The machine must expose the non-root login Smoozy would hold. Nothing else goes on this box because nothing else needs this rental shape. This batch runs after the shape and before any spine document: a negative answer changes the supervisor's boot-return arrangement those documents would otherwise inherit.

### Batch D — the Telegram rental. **One rental, a different specification.**

Fact **63**: a public address, an inbound port reachable from outside, and whatever certificate Telegram requires. Fact **65** rides here if the box is left up for a day, and Fact **62** rides on any box.

Telegram's sweep asks that Fact 63 be **settled before the batch is chosen rather than after**, and `Preparation/Architecture/Method.md` puts the Telegram facts before the Telegram work. Its answer returns to `Preparation/Architecture/The shape.md` and the security scope before any spine document hardens the deployment boundary. So this batch is early — after Batch A, and not held for Batch B.

### Batch E — the post-shape batch. **Two boxes, run last. DONE, 2026-08-18.**

**It ran on 2026-08-18 and it was the stage's last machine work.** Two boxes — a Vast container in Spain and a Vast virtual machine in Quebec — **both destroyed, the account verified empty afterwards: no instances, no volumes.** Whole-batch machine spend about **twenty cents**, including one virtual machine destroyed after twenty minutes because it never finished pulling its image.

**The answers of record are in [The last two boxes](The%20last%20two%20boxes%20—%20the%20hook%20that%20cannot%20be%20read%20back,%20the%20full%20disk,%20and%20the%20unit%20that%20kills%20a%20render.md).** That document describes both boxes exactly and carries what was run and what came back, plus the provider-documentation reading that ran beside them. **Every fact this section names carries its own answer in its own entry above** — written back on 2026-08-18, the same day, which is the discipline the six-machine batch's index failed for two days.

**Answered, and the promise stands.** Facts **2**, **8**, **27**, **40**, **49**, **60**, **68**, **70**, **78**, **92** — and **91** and **83** re-posed against the arrangement rather than a stand-in. Twelve.

**Answered NO, and these three are the batch's real value.**

- **Fact 1's disk-full form.** The writes all refuse loudly and every read stays truthful, **but the supervisor cannot start on a full state mount** — its first act needs a directory entry. On a container the keeper loops and heals when space returns; **on a systemd machine the unit did not recover merely because space was freed.** Two of the four named negatives fired, and the serious one is that **the arrangement's own death is recorded nowhere Smoozy writes.** Fact 1's other halves — reboot **and a genuine kernel panic** — are answered yes on both kinds.
- **Fact 50.** A second ready action was **not refused** on a half-finished machine, ran every step from the beginning, and **cleared the interrupted run's own state**, leaving a machine that had genuinely done two of five steps reading as ready.
- **Fact 41, in part.** Removing a history entry ends the entry, the note, the collection record and the queue entry — **and leaves the bytes.** The streams, the frame and the launch material all survive under the removed job's identity while the machine reports nothing about it. Both boxes, independently.

**Fact 95 came out of it** — the container boot hook across providers — **and Fact 46's card readings are explicitly not closed**, because the host had exactly one card and the trap that passes every test on a one-card machine passed here too.

**The measurement that had never been taken is under Fact 70**: about **5 kB per frame** on the ordinary stream under Blender 4.5 and about **3.7 kB** under 5.0 for the same scene, and **nothing at all** on the complaints stream.

**Not reached, and each is named rather than glossed:** Fact 78's rollback boundary, blocked twice over with each blockage a finding of its own; the card readings' *they lie* half, which needs a **multi-card host**; a recycle's effect on a hook Smoozy wrote; and a genuine power cut, which no rental can pose.

**Two repairs to a closed document came out of it**, both applied outside a round on 2026-08-18 with their decisions-record lines: the boot registration said nothing about what its unit does to a running render, and the container placement's read-back cannot reach the hook the root step installs. Neither is a fact and neither is recorded here.

**The specification, for the record.** It was written on 2026-08-18 by `Rented-server runtime`'s own report and had never been written down anywhere until the runner recovered it that day and filed it at `Audits/Architecture/Batch E — the specification.md`. Everything below this line is that specification as it stood before the batch ran, kept because it carries what a negative result would have looked like for each of the six.

**Two rentals rather than one** — one Vast virtual machine and one Vast container, cheapest available, each with one card, no screen, an ordinary connection, **two non-root logins**, and a Blender. The kinds differ on the boot arrangements, the elevation routes, the identifier-reuse demonstration and the memory readings, and this project has already had one answer that would have been correct on one kind and silently wrong on the other. The container answers the boot half and the cgroup-readings half; **the virtual machine is the only place a forced process-identifier reuse can be posed.** **Nobody needs either afterwards**, since Fact 50 may leave a box with a card that has stopped answering.

Fact **1** (remaining promise level after the separate Batch C clause, **including the disk-full form**), Fact **2** (promise level, against a real Blender render and again with the supervisor killed first), Facts **8** (full), **40** (full), **41** (after 40), **49**, **50** (last), **60** (full, re-posed against a real render rather than a stand-in), **70** (full), **78** (interrupted at each commit boundary on **ext4, xfs and overlayfs**), and **27** (the ready-action weight).

**Riding free on whichever box stays up:** Fact **92**; ~~**whether a virtual machine's host keys survive its own recycle**, which the container answer cannot carry because a virtual machine generates its own at first boot~~ — **ANSWERED, and the premise was wrong. Corrected 2026-08-22.** The virtual machine observed was **shipped** its keys in the image: files dated months before the rental and **byte-identical after a recycle** that removed both logins, Blender, the throwaway and the lingering flag. **It does not generate them at first boot**, so the container answer and the machine answer point the same way — an unchanged host key names neither the rental nor the machine. `Servers` Q10 carries the re-decision; **crash return**, posed on neither machine kind by the six-machine batch; and **Fact 68's full form**, whose own entry says *"the full form once the shape exists, as with Fact 2"* and which had sat on no batch.

**One measurement that is not a numbered fact and is the one that could cost a render:** how much a real animation prints on each stream, in bytes per frame. `Rented-server runtime` bounds the captured streams by **a finite per-attempt ceiling above a 64 MiB control-state reserve (M4a), with loss reported by M13**, and the volume was measured on 2026-08-18.

**And one unchecked fact, added 2026-08-18 by that document's Round 1: whether a container rental's boot hook is a thing every provider offers, and where.** **It is now Fact 95 and it is answered in part**; what follows is how it stood when the batch was specified. The container boot arrangement rests on the provider running a hook of its own, observed on **one** provider. The systemd branch already carries the matching caveat for the lingering permission at that document's Section 16 item 2; this is the container half of the same doubt and it is worse, because the two branches fail differently — a systemd machine whose policy refuses lingering **fails the placement loudly**, since the read-back there is of a value the machine set, while a hook written at a path a provider never runs **reads back as confirmed**, since that read-back is of Smoozy's own content. **What breaks if it comes back wrong:** a container rental that reports a good placement and never returns after a restart, with nothing in the confirmation to reveal it. **Nothing may be assumed about any provider that has not been posed**, and this entry deliberately records no expectation. It is a reading of each provider's own documentation and then a restart on a rented box, so it rides on Batch E's container and justifies no rental of its own.

**What must stand up on the boxes first**, and it goes nowhere near `App/`: the placed package with its pointer and boot registration, the supervisor with its run identifier and endpoint, the durable stores, a render worker with stream capture, and a stub setup worker.

### Rental count

| Batch | Rentals | When |

### Facts that must never be put on a rental's list

Adding any of these to a batch wastes the rental, and in two cases the rental is the one place the question cannot be posed.

- **9, 10, 14, 22, 26, 32, 33, 34, 35, 36, 39, 42, 52, 53, 56, 61, 64, 66, 67, 69, 73, 74, 76** — answered by reading a thing's own documentation, or by the Creator, or against a live service from any computer.
- **11, 12, 13, 16, 23, 24, 30, 37, 38, 79** — want a computer of each operating system Smoozy runs on. **Fact 38 was listed in both this group and the reading-only group above; this is its home**, since what each system offers for holding a secret has to be tried on that system and not only read about. A rented Linux machine answers the Linux third of some of them and nothing else; Smoozy does not run on the rented machine at all.
- **14** — a rented server sits in a data centre with an unobstructed connection, which is the one place the question of a blocked or filtered check cannot be posed.
- **44** — a machine whose power can be cut at the wall. A rented machine's power cannot be cut over the network.
- **59** — a host that is not Linux.
- **58** — wants a machine, but any reachable SSH server will do; it should ride on a batch rather than justify one.
- **82** — the same: any reachable SSH server answers it, so it rides on a batch and justifies none. **83 is the opposite and is easy to confuse with it** — that one needs two rentals in sequence behind one proxying provider's address, and no other fact needs that.
- **84** — wants a Linux rental and, for its second half, a container. Rides on a batch and justifies none.

---

### Fact 124 — Whether installing a distribution package on the supported Linux families is a privileged operation, and therefore whether an unprivileged desktop application can install its own update unattended.

**Named by:** the third sanity sweep's verification round, 2026-08-22, out of `Desktop app and delivery` Z10's choice of the package mechanism as the Linux release and update route. **The register held no entry for it**, and the document meanwhile asserted the opposite in T4 — *"no route asks for administrator"* — which had passed every gate this stage has.

**Answered by:** reading the mechanism's own manual page. **No rental and no machine.** **Shape-deciding:** no for the mechanism, yes for what the user gets — it produced `Method`'s third escalation and a Creator ruling the same day. **Posable:** yes, as documentation.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-22, and it is privileged.** `dpkg(1)`, Debian trixie, read 2026-08-22 at `https://manpages.debian.org/trixie/dpkg/dpkg.1.en.html`.

- **The requirement, in the SECURITY section:** *"Any operation that needs write access to the database or the filesystem is considered a privileged operation that might allow root escalation."*
- **And its own force option confirms the normal case**, among the force options: *"Try to (de)install things even when not root."* An option existing to attempt an install when not root is the plainest statement that installing is otherwise a root act.
- **Recorded honestly beside it:** `apt(8)`'s own page, same site and date, says nothing about root at all. That is absence of documentation rather than absence of the requirement, which is the exact error the first sanity sweep exists because of; the citation that carries this fact is `dpkg`'s.
- **Not covered:** how privilege is obtained by a desktop application, and how long an obtained authorisation lasts — `Desktop app and delivery` §22 item 47.

**What rests on it:** `Desktop app and delivery` **T4** (corrected), **Z10a**, §22 item 47, §23, and the Creator's ruling of 2026-08-22 recorded in `Whole app`.

### Fact 125 — Whether the file-transfer subsystem offers an atomic at-most-one claim to a caller with no program on the machine.

**Named by:** the third sanity sweep's verification round, 2026-08-22, when `Rented-server runtime` G1a was found to name a kernel-lock holder that cannot exist before hand-over.

**Answered by:** the protocol's own specification, plus a run on the Creator's own Mac. **No rental and no machine.** **Shape-deciding:** yes for one module — the package transaction has no other exclusion instrument in that phase. **Posable:** yes.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-22, and it does.** `draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02` §6.3, the version this project negotiates, read 2026-08-22: `SSH_FXF_EXCL 0x00000020` — *"Causes the request to fail if the named file already exists. SSH_FXF_CREAT MUST also be specified if this flag is used."*

**Observed** on OpenSSH's own server subsystem driven over pipes, macOS 26.6.2, `OpenSSH_10.3p1, LibreSSL 3.3.6`, 2026-08-22, with a control either side: an exclusive create on an absent path returned a handle; the same request from a second server on the now-existing path returned status **4, `Failure`**; a plain create on that same path returned a handle, so the refusal is the flag and not a permission; and the exclusive create succeeded again once the file was removed. **A negative would have looked like the second exclusive create returning a handle.**

- **Not covered:** any liveness property. An existing claim file says a claim was made and never that its writer is alive — `Rented-server runtime` §16 item 23. And this was read on the caller's own end rather than on a rented machine (`Server protocol and transfers` §12 item 3).

**What rests on it:** `Rented-server runtime` **G1b**, and through it G17, G18 and `Server Setup` R2's in-progress report.

### Fact 126 — Whether a file-transfer-subsystem handle survives the channel it was opened on.

**Named by:** the third sanity sweep, 2026-08-22, against `Server protocol and transfers` T5's cap-of-one lease, which closes the reading channel for a command while §1.1 promises a walk in flight is not abandoned or rewound.

**Answered by:** a run on the Creator's own Mac, the specification being silent. **No rental and no machine.** **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** yes.

**ANSWERED, 2026-08-22, and it does not.** Two `/usr/libexec/sftp-server` processes driven directly over pipes, macOS 26.6.2, `OpenSSH_10.3p1, LibreSSL 3.3.6`, both announcing protocol version 3. A server that had opened a file returned `SSH_FXP_DATA` for a read at offset 0 with its own handle; a second server handed the same handle returned `SSH_FXP_STATUS` code **4, `Failure`**; and after the first channel was closed, a third server handed it returned the same. The handle was four bytes and read as an index into the answering server's own table. **A negative would have looked like either of the last two returning data.**

- **The specification does not settle it**, which is why it was run: `draft-ietf-secsh-filexfer-02`, read 2026-08-22, says the handle is opaque and that a client *"MUST NOT send requests the server with bogus or closed handles"*, and states nothing about validity across sessions.
- **Not covered:** any server other than OpenSSH's, and the same question on a rented machine (`Server protocol and transfers` §12 item 3).

**What rests on it:** `Server protocol and transfers` **§1.1 item 2** and **T5** — the lease change's resume-by-reopening rule and the one directory re-listed that it costs.

### Fact 127 — What Blender's sample-count setting accepts, and what it does with a number outside that.

**Named by:** the Rows 435–438 implementer, 2026-08-23, against `Render Settings` J11's closed set of three refusals. **Numbered by the runner the same day.** It is a **Blender question and belongs to Blender Research rather than here** — recorded in this register only so the row that waits on it has a number to name, in the same position as Fact 104.

**Why it could not be answered in place.** The map names no question that establishes what the count setting accepts. *Settings 15* answers the same shape for the **percentage** setting — out of range is silently corrected at assignment to 1 or 32767, nothing printed, exit 0, and the render paid for at the corrected size, in all four versions on both machines. **Carrying that evidence across to a different setting would be the assembling `Method` forbids**, and the one time this project did that, a claim true of its own test conditions and false as a general rule reached a spec.

**Answered by:** the Blender Research route, asked blind. **No rental and no machine** — it is a local Blender question, in the shape *Settings 15* was already posed in. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

~~**NOT ANSWERED.**~~ **ANSWERED 2026-08-30, and what J11 declined to invent a range against is exactly what Blender does.** Registered on 2026-08-29 as ***Settings 18***, one of seven questions in six topics carried through all seven steps of the round the Creator opened that day and closed on 2026-08-30 — answered by version-isolated passes in all four versions, hand-tested, comparison-checked, and repaired the same day by the cross-sweep and by the mechanism sweep. **The answer is at `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/Settings.md` question 18, and that text is the one to read**: it moved on 2026-08-30 under both sweeps, so any quotation of it taken earlier is stale. **Settled by test in all four versions on two machines** — the Creator's Mac (4.5.12, 5.0.1, 5.1.2 and 5.2.1 on hash `9e2066aef7ef`, macOS 26.6.2) and the rented RTX 3060 Linux machine, whose four builds were checked against the Mac's build for build before anything ran. **No Windows machine ran any part of the Settings topic.**

**The answer, in the terms `Render Settings` §13 item 26 and Row 437 need it.** Both halves are answered, and the first correction this entry's own wording needs is to its singular: **there is no one sample-count setting.** Each count declares its own accepted range and **each is corrected against its own limits**, so the same number is cut down for one engine and taken whole by another — 99,999,999 is above Cycles' ceiling and below EEVEE's, Cycles renders 16,777,216 and EEVEE renders 99,999,999, and neither says a word.

- **What each count accepts**, read out of each running Blender with no file open, identical in all four versions on both machines. **Cycles' final-render count — `scene.cycles.samples`, the name established at *Settings 17* — takes 1 to 16,777,216**, declared start 4,096. **EEVEE's final-render count, `scene.eevee.taa_render_samples`, takes 1 to 2,147,483,647**, declared start 64. **A view layer's own override, `scene.view_layers["…"].samples`, takes 0 to 2,147,483,647, where 0 means no override.** The remainder, all in the comparison's table: Cycles' viewport count 0 to 16,777,216 where 0 means unlimited, its sample-subset offset 0 to 16,777,216 and subset length 1 to 16,777,216, its adaptive minimum 0 to 4,096, its path-guiding training count 0 to 2,147,483,647, its viewport denoising start sample 0 to 16,777,216; EEVEE's viewport count 0 to 2,147,483,647; Grease Pencil's anti-aliasing count 1 to 2,147,483,647. **Workbench's is not a number at all** — `scene.display.render_aa` is a fixed enumeration (off, single-pass, 5, 8, 11, 16, 32), matched case-sensitively, and a choice outside the list is refused with every real choice named. **The bare `inf` several of these upper ends are printed as on the API pages is 2,147,483,647**, established by asking each running Blender and defined nowhere in any version's documentation. **Two dragging limits are in no version's documentation and neither is a limit on assignment** — the view-layer override cannot be dragged past 10,000, nor the Grease Pencil count past 256.

- **Out of range, and this is the hazard.** **A count outside the accepted range is silently corrected to the nearest limit at assignment, nothing whatever is reported — no error, no warning, no line of any kind — and the corrected number is what the render works to and is paid for.** Cycles asked for 99,999,999 stores 16,777,216 and settles in to render that many, Blender's own remaining-time estimate running roughly nine to fourteen hours across the four versions, a figure the comparison bounds to the scale and no finer. The run at the other end is the control on the correction itself: a count of 0, stored as 1, rendered its frame, saved it and exited 0. **The control that makes the silence a result was run** — the same runs made one deliberately wrong assignment and it was refused loudly, so the output was being captured and does carry complaints. **This is the same shape *Settings 15* records for the resolution percentage, now established on the count settings' own evidence rather than carried across**, which is the assembling this fact was numbered to avoid.

- **Where the corrected figure can be read, and it is not everywhere.** **Two homes were seen carrying it and neither is announced**: the setting read straight back, and the ordinary progress line's number after the slash. **The progress line is conditional twice over** — a sample subset or a view layer's own count makes that number count against itself instead (*Settings 16*), and neither was ever put on the same file as an out-of-range count; and on the render-operator route no progress line is printed at all in 5.0, 5.1 and 5.2 (*Reports 3*), leaving the read-back the only reading left. A written PNG's `cycles.ViewLayer.samples` field (*Reports 11*) has never been read on a picture whose count had been corrected.

- **What is refused rather than corrected, and the line between them is exact.** Above the setting's own ceiling but **inside a machine whole number**, the value is silently corrected. **Past what a whole number can hold**, it is refused outright with an error naming the setting, and the value it already had stays. A decimal, a word, text that looks like a number, or nothing at all is likewise **refused with an error naming the setting and the kind of value it expected** — nothing rounded and nothing truncated. **A true-or-false value is the one exception and stores 1.** The whole-number refusal is one behaviour written two ways — ordinary figures on macOS, a shorter number notation on Linux — and it is the same message *Settings 15* records for the percentage, so anything reading Blender's output to catch it must match both shapes; a third is possible on Windows and nothing has tried it.

- **The route is Smoozy's own.** Every result came off a Blender started with no window and driven by a script handed in at launch, which is the launch shape `Rendering` composes and `Render Settings` C1 assigns through.

**What is not established, and it travels with the answer:** a driver or a keyframe carrying a count out of range; what happens when a file *holding* such a count is loaded rather than assigned; what a field does when the number is typed into it or dragged; and Windows.

**What it unblocks, and neither is moved here.** `Render Settings` **§13 item 26**, whose lookup is now performed, and **Row 437**'s third place. **The statement the design sentence's second half wants is per engine rather than one line for the page**, because C1 imposes the chosen engine's own count and the two engines' ceilings differ by nearly two orders of magnitude. **Whether J11 now imposes a range in the box, as Y1 already does for the percentage, is a decision and belongs to the runner** rather than to this register.

*(The original entry's remainder follows, unchanged, since what it says breaks is what the answer settled.)*

**What breaks without it:** `Preparation/Design/Render Settings.md`'s line loses its second half for the samples box, and — because J11 invents no range where none is established — **a typed count outside whatever Blender accepts could be silently corrected and render at a count nobody asked for, at success.** That is the same silent-and-paid-for shape the percentage evidence records, which is what makes it worth asking rather than assuming either way.

**What holds without it:** the other two refusals of J11 and the whole of J12, which rest on *Settings 15* and *Frames 7* directly.

**What rests on it:** `Render Settings` **§13 item 26**, and Row 437's second half.

### Fact 128 — Whether an artist's ordinary save over their own `.blend`, with no reference edited, leaves an untouched reference's recorded bytes exactly as they were.

**Named by:** the fresh reader's pass over `Dependency Upload & Remap`, 2026-08-23, as U3 of the scenario replays' Pile 3. **Numbered by the runner the same day.** It is a **Blender question and belongs to Blender Research rather than here** — recorded in this register only so the clauses that wait on it have a number to name, in the same position as Facts 104 and 127.

**Why it could not be answered in place.** `Dependency Upload & Remap` M4 composes every remap pair on **the recorded location's expected bytes, exactly as the check read them**, and M8 matches on those bytes across exactly the re-upload `Specs/Whole app` step 11 promises — *a later change re-renders by sending only the `.blend` again*. Nothing in the map, in a joined answer or in any spec's sources file establishes what a plain save does to a reference the artist never touched. That document's own M14 records a related trap in the other direction — saving remaps paths relative by default — which is the ground for doubting rather than an answer to this.

**Answered by:** the Blender Research route, **asked blind**. **No rental and no machine** — it is a local Blender question. **Shape-deciding:** no. **Posable:** now.

~~**NOT ANSWERED.**~~ **ANSWERED 2026-08-30, and it does not: a plain save moves the recorded text in two ways, and neither is announced.** Registered on 2026-08-29 as ***Dependencies 20***, in the same seven-step round, and folded into the comparison, comparison-checked, machine-swept, build-swept, cross-swept and mechanism-swept on 2026-08-30. **The answer is at `Preparation/Blender Research/Comparison/Dependencies.md` question 20, and that text is the one to read**: it moved on 2026-08-30 under those sweeps, so any quotation of it taken earlier is stale. **Settled by test and not by reading** — no version's sources answer it and every version says so at length — on three machines whose Blenders match build for build at 4.5.12 `84afd5f785f7`, 5.0.1 `a3db93c5b259`, 5.1.2 `ec6e62d40fa9` and 5.2.1 `9e2066aef7ef`: the Creator's Mac, the rented RTX 3060 Linux machine in Poland, and a rented Windows machine carrying the four official Windows x64 builds, **which reported itself as Microsoft Windows Server 2025 Standard, build 26100.32995, 24H2**. Eight tests in all four versions on the Mac and again on that Linux machine, seven on the Mac alone, one on the Linux machine alone, one crossing between them in 4.5 and 5.2, and six on the Windows machine — five in all four versions and the crossing in 4.5 and 5.2. **Nothing was rendered; every result is text read out of Blender or out of a file's own bytes, and all of it is labelled observed.**

**The answer, in the terms M4, M8 and §12 item 22 need it.**
